USMLE STEP 2 CK Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Head lag is largely resolved when the infant is pulled to a sitting position): At 4 months, head control has matured enough that head lag is minimal when the infant is pulled to sit, along with rolling front to back and voluntary reaching. Independent sitting is a 6-month milestone. The pincer grasp emerges around 9 months. Two-word phrases appear around 2 years.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reassure the family that this represents normal development for her age): Combining words into short phrases is a 2-year milestone, not an 18-month one, so this child's language is age-appropriate and reassurance with continued routine surveillance is correct. Early intervention referral, imaging, and audiology testing are not indicated for development that falls within the expected range for age.
  3. The correct answer is B (Refer for a comprehensive vision screening examination): School-age children who squint at distant objects and struggle academically most commonly have an uncorrected refractive error, so vision screening is the appropriate first step before pursuing more invasive workups. Genetic testing is not indicated because his early milestones were normal. Stimulant medication should not be started without a formal attention deficit evaluation, and this presentation is more consistent with a vision problem. Reassurance would delay a correctable cause of poor school performance.
  4. The correct answer is A (Menarche typically occurs about 2 to 2.5 years after thelarche, so this is within a normal but later range): A 15-year-old with no menarche and normal secondary sexual development does meet the age-based threshold for a primary amenorrhea evaluation (no menses by age 15 with normal pubertal development), which is why she is being seen. However, her history of normal-age thelarche at 11, with menarche typically following by roughly 2 to 2.5 years, supports a constitutional or otherwise benign delay rather than an underlying structural or chromosomal cause, so the initial evaluation should proceed in a stepwise fashion rather than jumping to invasive imaging or assuming a chromosomal abnormality. Delayed puberty refers to absent pubertal signs by age 13 in girls, which does not apply since thelarche already occurred at 11.
  5. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly for comprehensive developmental and neurologic evaluation): Loss of previously acquired skills, known as developmental regression, is never a normal finding and warrants prompt evaluation for autism spectrum disorder, a neurodegenerative condition, or a metabolic disorder.
  6. The correct answer is D (The pincer grasp typically matures slightly later, around 9 to 12 months): A mature pincer grasp, using the thumb and index finger, typically emerges near the end of the first year, often between 9 and 12 months, so a raking grasp at 9 months can still be within the normal maturing range and does not by itself indicate delay.
  7. The correct answer is C (Decreased renal clearance and reduced lean body mass are expected findings of normal aging): Normal, non-pathologic aging is associated with predictable physiologic changes including decreased lean body mass, reduced renal clearance, presbyopia, presbycusis, and modestly slower reaction time, all of which should be distinguished from disease. A Timed Up and Go under 10 seconds is actually reassuring, not concerning, for fall risk.
  8. The correct answer is A (A weight loss of about 10% from birth weight can be within the expected range in the first week): Healthy newborns commonly lose up to about 10% of their birth weight in the first week of life and are expected to regain it by about 2 weeks of age, so a loss of roughly 10% in this vigorous, well-hydrated infant is within the expected range rather than a sign of dehydration. Hospitalization is not automatically required for weight loss at this level in an otherwise well infant.
  9. The correct answer is A (Place the infant on her back on a firm mattress with no loose bedding or soft objects): Supine positioning on a firm, flat surface free of soft bedding, pillows, or loose blankets is the cornerstone of safe sleep counseling and is associated with the lowest risk of sudden infant death syndrome.
  10. The correct answer is C (Honey should be avoided until after the first birthday because of the risk of infant botulism): Honey can contain Clostridium botulinum spores that an infant's immature gastrointestinal tract cannot yet clear, risking infant botulism, so honey should be withheld until after the first birthday.
  11. The correct answer is B (Rear-facing seating better protects a young child's head, neck, and spine in a crash): Current guidance recommends keeping children rear-facing until at least age 2, or until they exceed the seat's rear-facing height or weight limit, because this position better distributes crash forces away from the relatively large head and immature cervical spine of a young child.
  12. The correct answer is D (Stating a full first and last name reliably is generally expected closer to age 4): Reliably stating both first and last name is typically a 4-year milestone, so a 3-year-old who can state only a first name is still developing appropriately.
  13. The correct answer is D (Resolution of the Moro reflex by this age reflects normal maturation of the central nervous system): Primitive reflexes such as the Moro reflex are expected to disappear by around 4 to 6 months as cortical inhibition matures, so its absence at 6 months, alongside age-appropriate sitting and babbling, reflects normal development.
  14. The correct answer is C (Folic acid supplementation to reduce the risk of neural tube defects): Folic acid supplementation before conception and during early pregnancy substantially reduces the risk of neural tube defects such as spina bifida and anencephaly, which is why it is universally recommended for women of reproductive age. High-dose vitamin A supplementation is actually teratogenic and should be avoided rather than recommended.
  15. The correct answer is A (Blood pressure measurement): Blood pressure should be checked at essentially every clinical encounter across the lifespan because hypertension is common, largely asymptomatic, and highly modifiable, making it the one test performed at every visit rather than on a periodic interval.
  16. The correct answer is B (Ask the mother to step out so the adolescent can be interviewed confidentially): Confidential time alone with an adolescent patient, without a parent present, substantially increases the likelihood of honest disclosure about sensitive topics such as sexual activity, substance use, and mental health, and is a standard component of adolescent preventive visits.
  17. The correct answer is B (Perform a more detailed clinical assessment to confirm the diagnosis and evaluate severity): A positive depression screen should prompt a more thorough clinical evaluation of symptoms, duration, functional impact, and safety before deciding on management, which may then include counseling, medication, or referral as appropriate.
  18. The correct answer is D (Bicycle helmet use and screen time limits): Anticipatory guidance for a school-age child should focus on age-relevant safety issues such as helmet use during bicycling and appropriate screen time limits.
  19. The correct answer is C (Begin lipid screening now rather than waiting until the usual average-risk starting age): A strong family history of early cardiovascular disease is a recognized indication to begin lipid screening earlier than the usual average-risk starting age of around 40 to 45, since it identifies elevated risk before symptoms occur.
  20. The correct answer is A (Administer a brief validated cognitive screening tool such as the Mini-Cog): A reported functional change in memory, such as new difficulty managing finances and repeating questions, warrants an objective cognitive screen such as the Mini-Cog or Mini-Mental State Examination to help distinguish normal aging from a pathologic process such as mild cognitive impairment or dementia.
Case C1

An 81-year-old woman is brought to clinic by her son for her annual wellness visit. She lives alone and has a history of hypertension and osteopenia. The son reports she seems more withdrawn lately and has several new bruises on her upper arms in different stages of healing. When asked about the bruises, she looks toward her son before answering and says she 'must have bumped into something.' Her son insists on answering most questions for her. She takes a thiazide diuretic, a statin, and an over-the-counter sleep aid. Vital signs are normal.

  1. The correct answer is A (Interview the patient alone, without her son present, before proceeding further): Bruises in different stages of healing, a withdrawn affect, and a caregiver who insists on answering for the patient are classic red flags for elder abuse, so the appropriate next step is to separate the patient from the potential abuser and interview her privately to allow honest disclosure.
  2. The correct answer is D (Physical abuse): Physical abuse refers to the intentional use of force resulting in injury, pain, or impairment, which matches grabbing hard enough to cause bruising.
  3. The correct answer is B (Report the suspected abuse to adult protective services as required by law): Clinicians are mandated reporters for suspected elder abuse in most jurisdictions, so once abuse is suspected based on a credible disclosure and physical findings, a report to adult protective services is required to ensure a safety assessment and appropriate intervention.
  4. The correct answer is C (A Timed Up and Go test completed in more than 12 seconds): A Timed Up and Go time greater than about 12 seconds is associated with increased fall risk and should prompt further gait and balance assessment along with a home safety review.
  5. The correct answer is C (Administer thiamine before giving any intravenous glucose): This presentation of confusion, nystagmus, and ataxia is classic for Wernicke encephalopathy from thiamine deficiency, and thiamine must be given before or together with glucose because glucose administered alone can precipitate or worsen the encephalopathy by accelerating depletion of remaining thiamine stores.
  6. The correct answer is B (Korsakoff syndrome with confabulation and anterograde amnesia): Korsakoff syndrome is the often-irreversible chronic sequela of untreated or inadequately treated Wernicke encephalopathy, characterized by anterograde amnesia and confabulation with relative preservation of other cognitive domains.
  7. The correct answer is D (Niacin (vitamin B3) deficiency): This presentation of dermatitis in sun-exposed areas, diarrhea, and dementia is the classic triad of pellagra from niacin deficiency, which is associated with corn-based diets because corn niacin is poorly bioavailable and low in tryptophan. Vitamin B12 and folate deficiency cause megaloblastic anemia and, for B12, neurologic findings distinct from this triad.
  8. The correct answer is A (Vitamin B12 deficiency): Elevated methylmalonic acid together with elevated homocysteine is specific for vitamin B12 deficiency, since methylmalonic acid metabolism depends on B12 as a cofactor while homocysteine metabolism depends on both B12 and folate, and B12 deficiency also explains the peripheral neuropathy from subacute combined degeneration.
  9. The correct answer is D (Folate deficiency): Folate is required for neural tube closure in early embryonic development, and folate deficiency during the periconceptional period is directly linked to neural tube defects such as spina bifida, which is why folic acid supplementation is recommended before conception.
  10. The correct answer is A (Vitamin D deficiency): Bowed legs, delayed fontanelle closure, and a rachitic rosary at the costochondral junctions are classic features of rickets from vitamin D deficiency, which impairs bone mineralization in a growing child.
  11. The correct answer is C (Osteomalacia from vitamin D deficiency): Chronic fat malabsorption impairs absorption of fat-soluble vitamin D, and in adults this produces osteomalacia, characterized by bone pain, proximal muscle weakness, and poor mineralization rather than simply reduced bone density.
  12. The correct answer is B (Vitamin K deficiency impairing synthesis of factors 2, 7, 9, and 10): Vitamin K is required for hepatic synthesis of clotting factors 2, 7, 9, and 10, and newborns are born with low vitamin K stores and a sterile gut, making them vulnerable to hemorrhagic disease of the newborn if the routine vitamin K injection is withheld, with factor 7's short half-life explaining early prothrombin time prolongation.
  13. The correct answer is A (Scurvy from vitamin C deficiency): Perifollicular hemorrhages, bleeding gums, and poor wound healing in a patient with minimal fruit or vegetable intake are classic for scurvy, which results from impaired collagen cross-linking due to vitamin C deficiency.
  14. The correct answer is B (Vitamin A): Chronic vitamin A toxicity causes headache, blurred vision from pseudotumor cerebri, dry skin, alopecia, and hepatotoxicity, matching this presentation in a patient who began high-dose supplementation.
  15. The correct answer is C (Zinc deficiency): Zinc deficiency classically causes a perioral and acral dermatitis, impaired wound healing, and hypogeusia (reduced taste), and is a recognized complication of prolonged parenteral nutrition without adequate zinc supplementation, as seen in acrodermatitis enteropathica as well.
  16. The correct answer is D (Vitamin A deficiency causing xerophthalmia with Bitot spots): Night blindness together with dry conjunctiva and Bitot spots is a classic and early presentation of vitamin A deficiency, which can progress to corneal ulceration and permanent blindness if untreated, and remains an important cause of preventable childhood blindness in resource-limited settings.
  17. The correct answer is B (Pica): Pica refers to persistent craving and consumption of non-nutritive substances such as ice, clay, or starch, and is a well-recognized association with iron deficiency, sometimes resolving once the deficiency is corrected.
  18. The correct answer is A (Excess tryptophan diversion to serotonin synthesis leading to niacin deficiency): In carcinoid syndrome, the tumor diverts large amounts of dietary tryptophan toward serotonin production, leaving insufficient tryptophan available for niacin synthesis, which can produce pellagra with its characteristic photosensitive dermatitis, diarrhea, and dementia.
  19. The correct answer is D (Maternal vitamin B12 deficiency transmitted through breast milk): Exclusively breastfed infants of mothers with unsupplemented vegan diets are at risk for vitamin B12 deficiency because breast milk B12 content reflects maternal stores, and infantile B12 deficiency can cause failure to thrive, hypotonia, macrocytic anemia, and developmental regression.
  20. The correct answer is C (Vitamin B12 deficiency): Loss of vibration and position sense with a positive Romberg sign points to dorsal column involvement, which along with glossitis and fatigue is characteristic of vitamin B12 deficiency causing subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord, a well-known long-term risk after gastric bypass surgery without adequate supplementation.
  21. The correct answer is D (Niacin (vitamin B3)): Hartnup disease impairs absorption of tryptophan, an amino acid precursor for endogenous niacin synthesis, so affected patients can develop pellagra-like symptoms including a photosensitive rash and diarrhea similar to dietary niacin deficiency.
  22. The correct answer is C (Wet beriberi from thiamine deficiency): Wet beriberi refers to the cardiovascular manifestation of thiamine deficiency, producing high-output heart failure with peripheral edema and a wide pulse pressure, distinct from dry beriberi, which affects peripheral nerves rather than the heart.
  23. The correct answer is B (Acrodermatitis enteropathica): Acrodermatitis enteropathica is a hereditary defect in intestinal zinc absorption that produces a characteristic perioral and acral dermatitis along with alopecia and diarrhea, closely mirroring the clinical picture of acquired zinc deficiency.
  24. The correct answer is A (Koilonychia): Koilonychia refers to concave, spoon-shaped nails and is a recognized finding in chronic iron deficiency anemia.
  25. The correct answer is B (Dry beriberi from thiamine deficiency): Dry beriberi refers to the peripheral neuropathy form of thiamine deficiency, producing symmetric weakness and sensory loss without the cardiac findings seen in wet beriberi, and is classically associated with diets heavily dependent on polished rice, which is low in thiamine.
Case C2

A 5-month-old male infant is brought to clinic because he has become increasingly lethargic and has stopped reaching new motor milestones over the past 6 weeks. He is exclusively breastfed. His mother, who is otherwise healthy, has followed a strict vegan diet for the past 4 years without any vitamin supplementation. On examination the infant is hypotonic, has poor eye contact, and is pale. A complete blood count reveals macrocytic anemia.

  1. The correct answer is C (Vitamin B12 deficiency from inadequate maternal intake reflected in breast milk): An infant exclusively breastfed by a mother with an unsupplemented vegan diet is at high risk for vitamin B12 deficiency, since maternal B12 stores directly determine breast milk B12 content, and the resulting deficiency in the infant produces macrocytic anemia along with hypotonia, lethargy, and developmental regression.
  2. The correct answer is D (Elevated methylmalonic acid in addition to elevated homocysteine): Both vitamin B12 and folate deficiency raise homocysteine, but only vitamin B12 deficiency also raises methylmalonic acid, since methylmalonic acid metabolism specifically requires B12 as a cofactor, making an elevated methylmalonic acid level the key distinguishing laboratory finding.
  3. The correct answer is A (Subacute combined degeneration affecting dorsal columns and corticospinal tracts): Vitamin B12 deficiency, if prolonged, can progress to subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord, affecting both the dorsal columns, causing loss of vibration and position sense, and the lateral corticospinal tracts, causing spasticity, a combination unique to B12 deficiency among the common nutrient deficiencies.
  4. The correct answer is D (The mother should take a vitamin B12 supplement while breastfeeding any future infants): Because the underlying problem is inadequate maternal B12 intake reflected in breast milk, the most direct and appropriate long-term solution is for the mother to take B12 supplementation while breastfeeding, which corrects the deficiency at its source while preserving the benefits of breastfeeding.
  5. The correct answer is B (Continue intensive lifestyle intervention and consider adding pharmacotherapy): For a patient with obesity and an inadequate response to lifestyle measures alone, guidelines support continuing intensive lifestyle intervention while considering the addition of pharmacotherapy, such as a GLP-1 receptor agonist, reserving bariatric surgery for patients who do not achieve adequate results with these measures or who meet higher-risk criteria.
  6. The correct answer is C (Anorexia nervosa): Significantly low body weight, intense fear of weight gain, and secondary amenorrhea in an adolescent describe the classic presentation of anorexia nervosa.
  7. The correct answer is A (Bulimia nervosa): Recurrent self-induced vomiting at a normal body weight, with dental erosion, knuckle calluses (Russell sign), and hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis, is characteristic of bulimia nervosa.
  8. The correct answer is A (Start feeding slowly while proactively monitoring and repleting phosphate, potassium, and magnesium): In a severely malnourished patient, reintroducing nutrition triggers an insulin surge that drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells, and starting feeding slowly while proactively monitoring and repleting these electrolytes is the standard approach to prevent refeeding syndrome, which can cause fatal arrhythmias and respiratory failure.
  9. The correct answer is C (Albumin is also a negative acute-phase reactant, so it can fall with inflammation independent of nutrition): Albumin has a long half-life and its level is also influenced by systemic inflammation, since it behaves as a negative acute-phase reactant and falls during acute illness independent of true nutritional status, which limits its specificity as an isolated nutrition marker in an acutely ill patient.
  10. The correct answer is D (Binge eating disorder): Recurrent binge eating episodes without regular compensatory behaviors such as purging, laxative misuse, or excessive exercise define binge eating disorder, which is now recognized as the most common eating disorder overall and is often associated with overweight or obesity.
  11. The correct answer is B (Refeeding syndrome causing an intracellular shift of phosphate): In a severely malnourished patient, reintroducing calories stimulates insulin release, which drives phosphate, along with potassium and magnesium, into cells, producing profound hypophosphatemia that can cause muscle weakness and cardiac arrhythmia, the hallmark of refeeding syndrome.
  12. The correct answer is A (Chronic estrogen deficiency from prolonged low body weight and amenorrhea): Prolonged low body weight in conditions such as anorexia nervosa suppresses hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal function, producing amenorrhea and chronic estrogen deficiency, which in turn impairs bone formation and accelerates bone loss, explaining the low bone density seen here.
  13. The correct answer is B (Refer for evaluation for bariatric surgery): Bariatric surgery is appropriate for patients with a body mass index of 40 or above, or a body mass index of 35 or above with a significant obesity-related comorbidity such as type 2 diabetes (some newer guidance lowers this comorbidity threshold to a body mass index of 30 to 35). This patient's body mass index of 42 already meets the standard threshold on its own, particularly after an inadequate response to lifestyle and pharmacologic measures, making surgical referral the appropriate next step here.
  14. The correct answer is C (Calluses on the dorsal surface of the fingers used to induce vomiting): Repeated use of the fingers to induce vomiting produces characteristic calluses or scarring on the dorsal knuckles, known as Russell sign, which is relatively specific to this behavior.
  15. The correct answer is D (Waist circumference helps estimate visceral adiposity and cardiometabolic risk independent of body mass index): Waist circumference provides information about central, visceral fat distribution that is linked to cardiometabolic risk beyond what body mass index alone conveys, since two patients with the same body mass index can have very different fat distribution and risk.
  16. The correct answer is B (Parenteral nutrition through a central venous catheter): When the gastrointestinal tract is nonfunctional, as with a prolonged ileus, enteral feeding cannot be safely used, making parenteral nutrition delivered intravenously the appropriate route until gut function returns.
Case C3

A 15-year-old girl is brought to clinic by her mother, who is concerned about a 15 kilogram weight loss over the past 8 months. The patient states she feels 'fat' despite her mother's protests that she is very thin. She has not had a menstrual period in 5 months. Vital signs show a heart rate of 48 beats per minute and blood pressure of 88/56 mmHg. Examination reveals fine, soft hair over her back and arms, and her body mass index is calculated at 15.5.

  1. The correct answer is D (Anorexia nervosa): A significantly low body weight, an intense fear of being fat despite objective thinness, amenorrhea, bradycardia, hypotension, and lanugo hair together form the classic presentation of anorexia nervosa.
  2. The correct answer is A (Physiologic adaptation to chronic starvation with reduced metabolic demand): Chronic starvation lowers metabolic rate and cardiac workload as an adaptive response, producing bradycardia and hypotension that are common and expected findings in significantly underweight patients with anorexia nervosa, and that generally improve with weight restoration.
  3. The correct answer is C (Refeeding syndrome with hypophosphatemia, hypokalemia, and hypomagnesemia): Severely malnourished patients like this one are at high risk for refeeding syndrome when nutrition is reintroduced, since an insulin surge drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells, producing electrolyte derangements that can cause cardiac arrhythmia and respiratory failure if not anticipated and managed with slow refeeding and proactive electrolyte repletion.
  4. The correct answer is D (Chronic hypoestrogenism from prolonged amenorrhea and low body weight): Prolonged low body weight suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to amenorrhea and a chronic hypoestrogenic state that impairs bone formation and increases bone resorption, explaining the osteopenia commonly seen in anorexia nervosa.
  5. The correct answer is C (Begin mammography screening now and continue every 1 to 2 years): For average-risk women, mammography screening is generally recommended starting somewhere in the range of age 40 to 50 and continuing every 1 to 2 years through at least the mid-70s, so beginning now at age 45 is appropriate and consistent with recommended timing.
  6. The correct answer is B (Cytology alone every 3 years): For women age 21 to 29, cervical cytology alone every 3 years is the recommended screening strategy, since HPV testing in this younger age group has a high rate of transient, clinically insignificant infections that would lead to unnecessary further testing.
  7. The correct answer is A (Colonoscopy every 10 years or annual fecal immunochemical testing): Average-risk adults should begin colorectal cancer screening at age 45, so this 50-year-old man is already within the recommended window, and accepted strategies include colonoscopy every 10 years or annual fecal immunochemical testing, among other validated options.
  8. The correct answer is A (Annual low-dose computed tomography of the chest): Adults age 50 to 80 with a significant smoking history, such as this patient's 35 pack-year history, who currently smoke or quit within the past 15 years, are recommended to undergo annual low-dose computed tomography of the chest for lung cancer screening, which has been shown to reduce lung cancer mortality in this population.
  9. The correct answer is B (Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry screening is recommended starting at age 65): Routine dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry screening for osteoporosis is recommended for all women starting at age 65 regardless of additional risk factors, since bone loss accelerates after menopause and early detection allows for preventive treatment before a fragility fracture occurs.
  10. The correct answer is C (A one-time screening abdominal ultrasound for abdominal aortic aneurysm): Men age 65 to 75 who have ever smoked are recommended to undergo a one-time screening ultrasound for abdominal aortic aneurysm regardless of how long ago they quit, since prior smoking exposure remains a major risk factor for aneurysm development.
  11. The correct answer is D (She should begin more intensive breast cancer screening earlier than average-risk women): Women with a confirmed BRCA mutation and a strong family history of early-onset breast cancer are at substantially elevated risk and are recommended to begin more intensive screening, often including earlier initiation and supplemental imaging such as breast magnetic resonance imaging, rather than following the average-risk schedule.
  12. The correct answer is C (His family history supports beginning lipid screening now rather than waiting for a later average-risk starting age): A father with a myocardial infarction at an unusually young age is a recognized risk factor that supports earlier lipid screening than the general average-risk starting age, allowing for earlier identification and management of dyslipidemia to reduce his own cardiovascular risk.
  13. The correct answer is A (Engage in shared decision-making that weighs potential benefits and harms before testing): Prostate-specific antigen screening involves a more individualized decision than many other screening tests because of the balance between potential mortality benefit and harms such as overdiagnosis and overtreatment, so shared decision-making that reviews these tradeoffs with the patient is the recommended approach for an average-risk man around this age.
  14. The correct answer is D (She should have begun screening earlier than the average-risk starting age given her family history): A first-degree relative diagnosed with colorectal cancer at a young age is an established indication to begin screening earlier than the standard average-risk age and often to use shorter surveillance intervals, reflecting her elevated personal risk.
  15. The correct answer is B (Cervical cancer screening is no longer needed since the cervix has been removed for benign disease): When the cervix is removed as part of a hysterectomy performed for benign disease, and there is no history of high-grade cervical dysplasia, routine cervical cancer screening is no longer necessary since there is no cervix remaining to screen.
  16. The correct answer is B (High-risk HPV testing alone every 5 years): For women age 30 to 65, accepted strategies include high-risk HPV testing alone every 5 years, cytology alone every 3 years, or co-testing every 5 years, so HPV testing alone at a 5-year interval is an appropriate option in this age group.
  17. The correct answer is C (She may reasonably stop routine cervical cancer screening once she reaches age 65 with adequate prior negative results): For women with a history of adequate prior screening and no history of high-grade dysplasia, screening can reasonably be discontinued around age 65, so this patient with consistently normal results approaching that age fits the criteria to stop routine screening once she reaches that threshold.
  18. The correct answer is A (His age falls below the recommended starting age of 50, so screening is not yet indicated based on age alone): Lung cancer screening recommendations specify an age range, generally 50 to 80, in addition to smoking history criteria, so a 47-year-old does not yet meet the age criterion even with a qualifying smoking history, and should become eligible once he reaches the appropriate starting age assuming his quit-time criterion (within 15 years) is still met then.
  19. The correct answer is D (At 38, she has reached the age around which routine screening reasonably begins per ADA guidance, even without additional risk factors): The ADA recommends routine diabetes screening in average-risk adults starting around age 35 regardless of weight, while USPSTF restricts routine screening below that age to adults who are overweight or obese or who have other risk factors. This patient is 38, normal weight, and has no additional risk factors, so she has already reached the age at which routine screening is reasonable to begin under ADA guidance, rather than needing to have started screening years earlier as a young adult.
  20. The correct answer is C (This T-score meets the diagnostic criteria for osteoporosis): A T-score of negative 2.5 or below at any measured site meets the standard diagnostic threshold for osteoporosis, so a value of negative 2.6 falls into this diagnostic category.
  21. The correct answer is A (She should be screened earlier than the routine age 65 starting point given her risk factors): Early menopause and chronic glucocorticoid use are both established risk factors for accelerated bone loss, and postmenopausal women under 65 with such risk factors are recommended to undergo earlier bone density screening rather than waiting for the routine age of 65.
  22. The correct answer is D (It helps guide the decision of whether to initiate statin therapy for primary prevention): Calculating a patient's estimated atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease risk score is a standard part of primary prevention counseling, helping determine whether the potential benefit of starting statin therapy outweighs its risks for a given patient before any cardiovascular event has occurred.
  23. The correct answer is B (Her risk factors support earlier diabetes screening than would be recommended for an average-risk woman her age): Polycystic ovary syndrome and a family history of type 2 diabetes are both recognized risk factors that support earlier initiation of diabetes screening compared with an average-risk woman of the same age without these factors.
  24. The correct answer is D (Osteopenia): A T-score between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5 defines osteopenia, a category of reduced bone density that is not as severe as osteoporosis but still warrants attention to modifiable risk factors and possible preventive treatment depending on overall fracture risk.
  25. The correct answer is B (Diphtheria, tetanus, and acellular pertussis vaccine): The DTaP series routinely begins at 2 months of age as part of the standard childhood immunization schedule, along with several other vaccines given at this visit.
  26. The correct answer is A (They should generally be avoided in patients with significant immunosuppression): Both the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine and the varicella vaccine are live attenuated vaccines, and live vaccines are generally contraindicated in patients with significant immunosuppression because of the theoretical risk of vaccine-strain disease, as well as during pregnancy.
  27. The correct answer is C (An association between the vaccine and intussusception when given outside the recommended age window): The rotavirus vaccine series has strict age limits for starting and completing doses because of an observed association with intussusception when the vaccine is given outside its recommended age window, making the risk-benefit balance less favorable if started too late.
  28. The correct answer is D (Starting before age 15 allows for a shorter 2-dose vaccine series): When the human papillomavirus vaccine series is started before age 15, only 2 doses are needed to complete the series, whereas starting at 15 or older, or in immunocompromised patients, requires a 3-dose series, so this 12-year-old is in the age range that allows for the shorter regimen.
  29. The correct answer is C (Recombinant zoster (shingles) vaccine): The recombinant zoster vaccine is recommended starting at age 50, so this patient at 68 without any additional qualifying conditions should be offered it based on her age alone, along with the pneumococcal vaccine series once she reaches 65.
  30. The correct answer is A (Tdap vaccine): Tdap is recommended during every pregnancy, ideally in the early third trimester, regardless of the patient's prior vaccination status, because maternal antibodies transferred to the fetus help protect the newborn from pertussis before the infant's own vaccine series begins.
  31. The correct answer is B (Administer a Td or Tdap booster now given the wound type and time since last dose): For a contaminated or high-risk wound, a booster is recommended if it has been 5 or more years since the last tetanus-containing vaccine, rather than waiting for the standard 10-year interval used for clean, minor wounds, making a booster now appropriate given both the wound type and the 9-year interval.
  32. The correct answer is D (Consult with an allergy specialist to determine which vaccines can be safely given): A history of a severe allergic reaction to a vaccine warrants careful evaluation, often including allergy specialist consultation, to identify the specific culprit component and determine which future vaccines, if any, require modification, spacing, or medical supervision, rather than either abandoning all future vaccination or ignoring the history.
  33. The correct answer is A (Refer him for specialized allergy evaluation before considering yellow fever vaccination): Because some yellow fever vaccine formulations are produced using egg-based methods, a severe egg allergy with a history of anaphylaxis requires careful evaluation, often with allergy specialist input and possible desensitization protocols, before proceeding with vaccination, rather than either administering it without precaution or assuming no risk exists.
  34. The correct answer is C (Her chronic kidney disease qualifies her for pneumococcal vaccination before the routine age of 65): Certain chronic medical conditions, including chronic kidney disease, qualify younger adults for pneumococcal vaccination before the routine age 65 recommendation that applies to the general population, reflecting their elevated risk for invasive pneumococcal disease.
  35. The correct answer is B (Patients with significant immunosuppression): Rotavirus, measles-mumps-rubella, and varicella vaccines are all live attenuated vaccines, and live vaccines share a common contraindication in patients with significant immunosuppression because of the risk of vaccine-strain disease in a host who cannot adequately control even an attenuated pathogen.
  36. The correct answer is C (He should receive an annual influenza vaccine as recommended for essentially all adults without contraindication): Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for essentially everyone 6 months of age and older without a specific contraindication, regardless of age group or the presence of hypertension, since influenza strains change yearly and immunity wanes over time.
  37. The correct answer is B (The hepatitis B vaccine series can be safely completed later in life if missed in childhood): Vaccines missed in childhood, including the hepatitis B series, can generally be given later in life through an appropriate catch-up schedule, and this is particularly important for healthcare workers given their occupational exposure risk to bloodborne pathogens.
Case C4

A 34-year-old man presents to a travel medicine clinic 6 weeks before a planned 3-week trip to a rural region of sub-Saharan Africa for humanitarian work. He is generally healthy, takes no medications, and has no known allergies. He reports he will be staying in areas with limited access to reliably treated water and will be visiting a country that requires proof of a specific vaccination for entry.

  1. The correct answer is D (Yellow fever vaccine): Several countries in sub-Saharan Africa and South America require proof of yellow fever vaccination for entry, reflecting the disease's endemic presence and the vaccine's role in preventing importation, making it the vaccine most likely tied to a specific entry requirement in this scenario.
  2. The correct answer is A (Atovaquone-proguanil, doxycycline, or mefloquine depending on regional resistance): Malaria prophylaxis is selected based on regional Plasmodium resistance patterns, with atovaquone-proguanil, doxycycline, and mefloquine among the most commonly used oral prophylactic regimens, started before travel and continued for a defined period after return depending on the specific drug.
  3. The correct answer is D (Counsel on safe food and water precautions and consider hepatitis A vaccination if not already immune): Travelers heading to areas with unreliable water treatment should receive counseling on safe food and water practices, such as avoiding untreated water and raw produce washed in it, and hepatitis A vaccination is recommended for most travelers to such destinations who are not already immune, since it is transmitted through contaminated food and water.
  4. The correct answer is B (Maintain hydration and consider carrying an antibiotic for self-treatment of moderate to severe traveler's diarrhea as advised by his travel clinic): Standard traveler's diarrhea counseling includes maintaining oral hydration and, for moderate to severe cases, considering a short course of an appropriate antibiotic that the travel clinic may prescribe in advance for self-treatment, allowing prompt management without needing immediate access to a local hospital.
  5. The correct answer is A (The CAGE questionnaire, and 2 or more positive answers suggest a possible alcohol use disorder): The CAGE questionnaire (Cut down, Annoyed, Guilty, Eye-opener) is exactly the set of questions described, and answering yes to 2 or more items suggests a possible alcohol use disorder warranting further evaluation.
  6. The correct answer is C (The 5 A's: Ask, Advise, Assess, Assist, Arrange): The 5 A's framework, Ask about tobacco use, Advise to quit, Assess willingness, Assist with a plan, and Arrange follow-up, is the standard structured approach for brief tobacco cessation counseling in a primary care visit.
  7. The correct answer is B (Combining behavioral counseling with pharmacotherapy is more effective than either approach used alone): Evidence consistently shows that combining behavioral counseling with pharmacotherapy, such as varenicline, bupropion, or nicotine replacement therapy, roughly doubles quit rates compared with either approach used alone, making combined treatment the most effective evidence-based strategy.
  8. The correct answer is D (Administer a more detailed depression assessment such as the PHQ-9): A positive result on the brief PHQ-2 screening tool should prompt a more detailed follow-up assessment, such as the PHQ-9, to better characterize symptom severity, duration, and any safety concerns before deciding on a specific management plan.
  9. The correct answer is A (Screening for intimate partner violence, which should be part of routine preventive care): Direct, private questions about safety at home and any history of a partner's threats or violence represent standard screening for intimate partner violence, which should be incorporated into routine preventive visits given how common and often hidden this issue can be.
  10. The correct answer is C (Use a nonjudgmental, motivational approach to discuss his drinking and options for support): A nonjudgmental, motivational approach that explores the patient's own perspective, provides information, and offers a range of support options respects patient autonomy while still addressing a clear pattern of unhealthy alcohol use and prior unsuccessful attempts to cut back.
  11. The correct answer is D (Regular moderate-intensity physical activity): Regular moderate-intensity physical activity has strong, consistent evidence for reducing cardiovascular events and all-cause mortality, making it one of the most broadly beneficial lifestyle interventions available for a sedentary patient with hypertension.
  12. The correct answer is B (Further explore the frequency, context, and any functional impact of her use in a nonjudgmental manner): A disclosed pattern of substance use should prompt further, nonjudgmental exploration of frequency, context, associated risks, and any functional impact, which helps determine whether this represents low-risk experimentation or a pattern warranting more active intervention or referral.
  13. The correct answer is A (Reduce intake of refined carbohydrates and added sugars while increasing whole foods such as vegetables, fruits, and whole grains): Reducing refined carbohydrates and added sugars while emphasizing whole, minimally processed foods such as vegetables, fruits, and whole grains is a broadly supported dietary approach for reducing cardiometabolic risk in a patient with obesity and prediabetes.
  14. The correct answer is C (Even moderate alcohol consumption carries some health risks, and counseling should reflect an individualized risk discussion): Current evidence increasingly recognizes that even moderate alcohol consumption carries some level of health risk rather than being risk-free, so counseling should be individualized to a patient's overall health, personal risk factors, and preferences rather than presenting alcohol as either protective or entirely harmless.
Case C5

A 52-year-old man with a 20-year history of heavy daily alcohol use is brought to the emergency department by his roommate, who reports the patient has been eating very little for the past week and has become increasingly confused. On examination he is disoriented to place and date, has horizontal nystagmus, and walks with a wide-based, unsteady gait. His roommate reports the patient often drinks in the morning to avoid feeling shaky and irritable.

  1. The correct answer is B (The CAGE questionnaire): The CAGE questionnaire, which asks about cutting down, annoyance at criticism, guilt, and morning eye-opener drinking, directly corresponds to this patient's history and would have been an appropriate brief tool to identify his risk for alcohol use disorder before this acute presentation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Administer thiamine before or together with any intravenous glucose): His presentation of confusion, nystagmus, and ataxia is classic for Wernicke encephalopathy, and thiamine should be given before or together with glucose because glucose alone can worsen the encephalopathy by further depleting already low thiamine stores.
  3. The correct answer is D (Korsakoff syndrome): Korsakoff syndrome is the chronic, often irreversible amnestic disorder that can follow inadequately treated Wernicke encephalopathy, characterized by anterograde amnesia and confabulation with relative preservation of other cognitive functions.
  4. The correct answer is A (Earlier recognition of his alcohol use disorder and thiamine supplementation during any hospitalization or clinic visit could have reduced this risk): Earlier identification of his alcohol use disorder through routine screening, along with proactive thiamine supplementation, particularly during any hospitalization or clinical encounter for a patient with heavy alcohol use, are recognized strategies to reduce the risk of progressing to Wernicke encephalopathy and subsequent Korsakoff syndrome.
  5. The correct answer is D (Perform a comprehensive medication review to identify and address contributors to fall risk): Polypharmacy, particularly with sedating or blood pressure lowering medications such as a benzodiazepine, diuretic, and alpha-blocker, is a major modifiable contributor to fall risk in older adults, making a comprehensive medication review an essential next step, especially when a medication appears to have been continued in error.
  6. The correct answer is B (This result suggests increased fall risk and should prompt further gait and balance evaluation): A Timed Up and Go time greater than about 12 seconds is associated with an increased risk of falls and should prompt further assessment of gait, balance, strength, and home safety, along with review of contributing medications.
  7. The correct answer is C (Remove loose rugs and improve lighting throughout her home, particularly on stairways): Removing tripping hazards such as loose rugs and improving lighting, especially on stairways, are well-established, evidence-based components of home safety modification to reduce fall risk in older adults.
  8. The correct answer is A (Evidence that his basic hygiene and nutritional needs are not being met despite adequate resources being available): Neglect refers specifically to a caregiver's failure to provide necessary care, such as adequate nutrition and hygiene, despite having the resources or responsibility to do so, which is distinct from the direct physical force implied by bruising or the misuse of funds implied by unexplained withdrawals.
  9. The correct answer is C (Financial exploitation of a vulnerable older adult): Large, unexplained financial withdrawals managed by a caregiver, combined with the patient's inability to account for them, raise concern for financial exploitation of a cognitively vulnerable older adult, which is a recognized and often underreported form of elder mistreatment.
  10. The correct answer is A (Politely but firmly arrange to interview the patient without the son present): When a caregiver's behavior, such as repeatedly answering for the patient and appearing irritated, raises concern for potential mistreatment, the appropriate initial step is to find a tactful way to separate the patient from that caregiver so she can be interviewed privately and safely.
  11. The correct answer is B (Enteral nutrition through a feeding tube): When the gastrointestinal tract is functional but oral intake is unsafe, as in severe dysphagia after a stroke, enteral nutrition delivered through a feeding tube is preferred over parenteral nutrition because it preserves gut mucosal integrity, reduces infectious complications, and is generally less costly.
  12. The correct answer is D (Initiate parenteral nutrition given her nonfunctional gastrointestinal tract): A bowel obstruction that has not resolved represents a nonfunctional gastrointestinal tract, making enteral feeding unsafe, so parenteral nutrition delivered intravenously is the appropriate strategy to meet her nutritional needs during this period of anticipated prolonged bowel rest.
  13. The correct answer is C (Hepatic steatosis related to long-term parenteral nutrition use): Long-term parenteral nutrition is a recognized cause of hepatic steatosis and other forms of liver dysfunction, likely related to a combination of the composition of the nutritional formula and the absence of enteral stimulation of normal gut and biliary physiology, making it the most likely explanation for new liver test abnormalities in this setting.
  14. The correct answer is D (The central venous catheter used to deliver the parenteral nutrition): Catheter-related bloodstream infection is one of the most significant and well-recognized risks of long-term parenteral nutrition, since the central venous catheter required for its delivery provides a direct route for bacterial entry into the bloodstream.
  15. The correct answer is A (Start feeding at a low rate with planned gradual advancement while monitoring electrolytes closely): In a severely malnourished patient, feeding should be initiated at a low rate with gradual advancement while electrolytes, particularly phosphate, potassium, and magnesium, are closely monitored and proactively repleted, since this approach reduces the risk of triggering refeeding syndrome.
  16. The correct answer is B (Enteral feeding helps maintain gut mucosal integrity and carries a lower risk of serious infection): Enteral nutrition helps maintain the structural and immunologic integrity of the gut mucosa and avoids the need for an indwelling central venous catheter, resulting in a lower risk of serious infectious complications compared with parenteral nutrition, which is why it is preferred whenever the gastrointestinal tract can be safely used.
  17. The correct answer is A (She will likely require long-term home parenteral nutrition given her significantly reduced absorptive capacity): Extensive small bowel resection resulting in short bowel syndrome often leaves insufficient absorptive surface area to meet nutritional needs through oral or enteral intake alone, making long-term parenteral nutrition a likely necessary component of her care, sometimes combined with some oral or enteral intake depending on her remaining bowel length and adaptation.
  18. The correct answer is D (Enteral nutrition through a feeding tube while his esophagitis is treated): Because his gastrointestinal tract distal to the esophagus is functional and his primary problem is painful swallowing rather than an inability to digest or absorb nutrients, enteral nutrition through a feeding tube is the appropriate initial approach while his esophagitis is treated, avoiding the risks associated with parenteral nutrition when it is not necessary.
  19. The correct answer is B (Refer for further developmental and motor skill evaluation given this apparent delay): Hopping on one foot and drawing a person with at least 3 body parts are both expected skills by around age 4 to 5, so a 5-year-old who cannot yet perform these tasks shows an apparent gross and fine motor delay warranting further evaluation, even if other domains such as language appear appropriate.
  20. The correct answer is C (Advise against high-dose vitamin A supplementation given its teratogenic potential in future pregnancy): High-dose vitamin A supplementation carries teratogenic risk and should be avoided, particularly in a woman who may become pregnant again soon, since excess vitamin A intake during early pregnancy is associated with fetal malformations.
  21. The correct answer is A (Address blood pressure control, appropriate age-based screening, and lifestyle counseling together): A well-rounded preventive visit integrates chronic disease management, such as blood pressure control, with appropriate age- and risk-based screening recommendations and lifestyle counseling on topics such as diet, exercise, and substance use, rather than narrowly focusing on a single issue.
  22. The correct answer is C (Identifying risk factors before a fall occurs allows for preventive intervention): A central purpose of preventive fall-risk assessment is to identify modifiable risk factors, such as certain medications, vision impairment, or home hazards, before a fall occurs, allowing for intervention that can meaningfully reduce future fall risk.
  23. The correct answer is B (Screening is performed in asymptomatic average- or elevated-risk individuals to detect disease before symptoms develop): Screening tests, such as colonoscopy or fecal immunochemical testing for colorectal cancer, are applied to asymptomatic individuals at average or elevated risk with the goal of detecting disease at an earlier, more treatable stage, in contrast to diagnostic testing performed to evaluate a specific symptom or finding.
  24. The correct answer is D (Blood pressure measurement, cervical cancer screening if due, and counseling on diet, exercise, and substance use): For a healthy young adult woman, an appropriate preventive visit includes blood pressure measurement, cervical cancer screening if she is due based on her age and prior results, and lifestyle counseling on diet, exercise, and substance use, since these apply broadly regardless of specific risk factors.
  25. The correct answer is B (These elements together form a coordinated assessment of overall functional status and modifiable risk in older adults): The components of the Medicare Annual Wellness Visit, including fall-risk assessment, cognitive screening, and medication reconciliation, are intentionally grouped because together they provide a coordinated picture of an older adult's overall functional status and identify overlapping modifiable risks, such as a sedating medication contributing to both fall risk and cognitive complaints.
  26. The correct answer is D (Overweight or obesity is itself a recognized risk factor supporting earlier diabetes screening): Overweight and obesity are well-established risk factors for type 2 diabetes and are explicitly incorporated into screening guidance to support earlier initiation of screening in adults with these risk factors compared with lower-risk peers.
  27. The correct answer is C (He remains eligible for screening since he quit within the past 15 years and falls within the recommended age range): Lung cancer screening recommendations apply to adults age 50 to 80 with a significant smoking history who currently smoke or who quit within the past 15 years, so this patient, who quit 8 years ago and remains within the recommended age range, is still eligible for screening.
  28. The correct answer is A (A body mass index of 25 to 29.9): A body mass index of 25 to 29.9 defines the overweight category, placing this patient's value of 26 within that range.
  29. The correct answer is D (Allow him to leave after documenting the capacity assessment and the risks discussed): the patient demonstrates decision-making capacity because he can state his diagnosis, appreciate the risk of leaving, reason through the tradeoff, and communicate a consistent choice. A history of alcohol use disorder alone does not remove capacity. Adults with capacity may leave against medical advice, and the physician's role is to document the discussion, not restrain or delay the patient.
  30. The correct answer is C (Consent is invalid because informed consent requires disclosure of which specific surgeon will operate): the identity of the physician performing an invasive procedure is considered material information that a reasonable patient would want before agreeing to surgery. Substituting an undisclosed surgeon without the patient's knowledge can constitute a battery even when the procedure is performed competently. The uncomplicated outcome does not retroactively make the original consent valid.
  31. The correct answer is A (Assess the patient's capacity directly and proceed with her consent if she demonstrates capacity): a psychiatric diagnosis does not automatically remove decision-making capacity. Capacity must be assessed based on the patient's actual ability to understand, appreciate, reason, and communicate a choice about this particular procedure, all of which she demonstrates. Competence is a separate legal status determined by a court, not by a family member's opinion.
  32. The correct answer is B (Implied consent under the emergency exception): when a patient cannot communicate, no surrogate is immediately available, and delay would cause serious harm, the law presumes a reasonable person would consent to necessary lifesaving treatment. Therapeutic privilege and waiver both apply to a patient who can communicate but is having information withheld or declined, and substituted judgment describes how a surrogate should decide, neither of which fits this unrepresented, unconscious patient.
  33. The correct answer is D (Prescribe the contraceptive without notifying the parents): most states allow minors to independently consent to contraceptive services, and disclosing this information to parents without the adolescent's permission would violate confidentiality protections that specifically apply to reproductive health care for minors. Legal custody over general decisions does not override this recognized exception, and requiring parental involvement would likely discourage the adolescent from seeking necessary preventive care at all.
  34. The correct answer is B (Explain that decisions will continue to involve the patient directly as long as he retains capacity for each decision): a diagnosis of early dementia does not itself remove capacity, and this patient currently demonstrates the ability to understand, appreciate, and communicate his preferences about his own care. Capacity should be reassessed over time as the disease progresses, but it is not appropriate to bypass a currently capacitated patient out of anticipated future decline.
  35. The correct answer is A (Respect the patient's informed refusal of surgery): a patient with decision-making capacity has the right to refuse any treatment, including a treatment that could prevent a life-threatening complication, as long as the refusal is informed and voluntary. The wife's wishes, however understandable, do not override the patient's own autonomous decision. Neither an ethics committee nor a court has authority to compel treatment on a capacitated adult who has refused it.
Case SOC-C1

A 72-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department after a fall at home and is found to have a subdural hematoma with a rapidly declining level of consciousness. She is intubated for airway protection shortly after arrival and can no longer participate in decision-making. She has no documented advance directive and no court-appointed guardian. Her husband died three years ago. She is survived by two adult children, a son and a daughter, who both arrive at the hospital and disagree about how aggressively to proceed with neurosurgical intervention.

  1. The correct answer is C (Either adult child, since both are equally ranked default surrogates in the absence of a spouse): with no living spouse, no advance directive, and no guardian, adult children occupy the next tier of the default surrogate hierarchy and are equally ranked with one another. Disagreement between two equally ranked surrogates should first be addressed through discussion rather than automatically escalating to an ethics committee or the courts, which is a slower process poorly suited to a time-sensitive surgical emergency.
  2. The correct answer is C (Substituted judgment, based on what the patient herself would have wanted): when a patient's own previously expressed wishes are known, even informally, surrogates are ethically obligated to apply substituted judgment rather than substitute their own preferences. The son's recollection of a specific statement about not wanting to be sustained by machines without real hope of recovery is directly relevant evidence of the patient's values and should be weighed carefully.
  3. The correct answer is B (Convene the hospital ethics committee to help the family reach consensus): when equally ranked surrogates disagree and the medical facts and the patient's own values need to be weighed together, ethics committee consultation is the appropriate mechanism rather than unilaterally acting on only one surrogate's preference. The committee can help clarify the prognosis and mediate the disagreement rather than the medical team choosing a side.
  4. The correct answer is A (It represented appropriate application of the patient's own previously expressed wishes to withhold a non-beneficial intervention): withholding an intervention that would not meaningfully change a very poor prognosis, based on the patient's own previously stated values, is standard, accepted end-of-life practice. It differs fundamentally from physician-assisted death or euthanasia, since no lethal medication was given and the underlying hemorrhage caused death, not any action by the physicians.
  5. The correct answer is D (Take steps to protect the identified third party, which may include warning him or notifying police): under the Tarasoff principle, when a patient makes a specific, credible threat against an identifiable victim, the treating clinician has a duty to take reasonable protective action, which can include warning the intended victim, notifying law enforcement, or arranging hospitalization. This duty overrides ordinary confidentiality because it involves a specific, named individual at risk of serious harm.
  6. The correct answer is D (Decline to provide any information without the patient's written authorization): under HIPAA, protected health information, including whether a patient has attended appointments, cannot be disclosed to an employer without the patient's specific authorization. The employer's stated reason for asking does not change this requirement, and a spouse cannot authorize release of another adult's medical information.
  7. The correct answer is C (Report the case to the local public health department regardless of the patient's request): active tuberculosis is a reportable communicable disease in every state because of its risk to public health, and this legal reporting duty is a recognized exception to ordinary confidentiality protections. The patient's preference not to have the diagnosis reported does not override a mandatory public health reporting requirement, though public health reporting is itself subject to strict confidentiality.
  8. The correct answer is A (Report suspected elder abuse to the appropriate protective services agency): suspected elder abuse or neglect is a mandatory reporting obligation in every state, similar to suspected child abuse, and physicians must report reasonable suspicion even without proof or the patient's own confirmation. Relying solely on the caregiver's explanation or simply documenting the finding without reporting would fail this legal duty, and the patient's consent is not required before a mandatory report is filed.
  9. The correct answer is B (Report the patient to the state department of motor vehicles as required by law): counseling a patient to stop driving voluntarily is a reasonable first conversation, but in a jurisdiction with a mandatory reporting statute for uncontrolled seizure disorders, the physician's legal duty once aware of a reportable condition is to file the required report, not simply rely on voluntary compliance or wait for a second accident.
  10. The correct answer is B (A HIPAA violation because the access was not for treatment, payment, or health care operations): simply working at the same institution does not authorize access to a patient's record. HIPAA permits access only for legitimate purposes such as direct treatment, billing, or operational functions like quality review. Accessing a record purely out of personal curiosity is an impermissible disclosure and a violation even if the information is never shared further.
  11. The correct answer is C (Help the patient request that sensitive-service communications go directly to her rather than the policyholder, where permitted): many insurers and states allow a dependent to request that communications about sensitive services be sent confidentially to the patient rather than routed to the policyholder as a routine benefits statement. This lets the patient receive necessary testing while reducing the chance that a routine insurance statement inadvertently discloses the visit.
  12. The correct answer is D (Release to a state licensing board conducting an official investigation is generally permitted without additional patient authorization): HIPAA specifically permits disclosures to health oversight agencies, including state licensing boards, conducting investigations related to the health care system, without requiring separate patient authorization. This falls within one of HIPAA's recognized exceptions for oversight and legal compliance activities.
  13. The correct answer is A (Decline to disclose the specifics of the substance use conversation without the patient's permission): most states allow adolescents to consent to confidential substance use counseling, and this confidentiality generally extends to disclosures made in that context. Because there is no immediate safety concern such as suicidality or a medical emergency requiring disclosure, the physician should protect the confidentiality of this conversation rather than sharing it with the parent without the adolescent's agreement.
  14. The correct answer is C (Notify the patient and consult legal counsel before releasing any records, since a subpoena alone may not authorize disclosure): a subpoena is a legal request, but unlike a court order signed by a judge it does not automatically compel disclosure of protected health information, especially sensitive psychiatric records. The appropriate step is to notify the patient, allow an opportunity to object, and seek legal guidance rather than releasing records reflexively or refusing outright.
  15. The correct answer is A (Report suspected abuse of a vulnerable adult to protective services despite her request): individuals with intellectual disabilities are considered a vulnerable population, and financial exploitation by a caregiver falls under mandatory reporting requirements for abuse or exploitation of a vulnerable adult in most states. This duty exists precisely because vulnerable individuals may fear retaliation and be reluctant to report abuse themselves.
  16. The correct answer is B (All eighteen HIPAA-specified identifiers must be removed from the data set): HIPAA defines deidentified information according to a specific safe harbor standard that requires removal of eighteen categories of identifiers, including name, geographic detail smaller than a state, dates related to an individual, and other identifying numbers. Once information meets this standard, it is no longer protected health information and generally can be used without separate patient authorization.
  17. The correct answer is D (Respect the father's confidentiality while encouraging him to consider informing his daughter himself): a patient's genetic diagnosis is his own protected health information, and confidentiality generally should be maintained even when the information has implications for a relative's own risk. The appropriate approach is to encourage the patient to communicate this himself, potentially with genetic counseling support, rather than the physician unilaterally disclosing it to another patient.
  18. The correct answer is D (The disclosure is permitted because the patient provided a signed authorization): disclosures for purposes outside treatment, payment, and health care operations, such as sharing records with a life insurance company for underwriting, generally require a specific, signed patient authorization, which is exactly what was provided. Because a valid authorization exists, the office may release the requested records consistent with its scope.
  19. The correct answer is A (A breach of confidentiality because protected health information was disclosed where unauthorized individuals could overhear it): even when both staff members are legitimately involved in a patient's care, discussing identifiable clinical information in a public space where others can overhear it violates the obligation to safeguard confidentiality. Clinical discussions involving identifiable information should occur only in private settings appropriate to the sensitivity of the information.
  20. The correct answer is C (Either a signed patient authorization or institutional review board approval of a waiver of authorization): using protected health information to recruit patients for research generally requires the patient's specific authorization, unless an institutional review board has reviewed and approved a waiver under specific regulatory criteria, such as minimal risk research that could not practicably be conducted otherwise. Working within the same health system does not substitute for either pathway.
  21. The correct answer is B (Obtain specific patient consent for publication or further modify the details to prevent identification): removing a patient's name is not sufficient to protect confidentiality if enough distinctive combined details remain that colleagues, family, or the patient's own community could reasonably identify the individual. The appropriate step is either obtaining informed consent for publication or further altering identifying details while preserving the clinically relevant information.
  22. The correct answer is D (Decline unless she is formally added to the care team or given specific authorization by the patient): being a licensed nurse and a family member does not by itself authorize access to a patient's record outside an assigned clinical role. Access should be limited to individuals with a genuine treatment, payment, or operations purpose, or to those the patient has specifically authorized, regardless of the requester's own credentials or family relationship.
  23. The correct answer is B (Report the behavior through the appropriate institutional compliance or privacy channel): unauthorized access to patient records is a HIPAA violation and a professionalism concern that should be reported through the hospital's formal privacy or compliance process, which can properly investigate and address the pattern. An informal conversation or simply waiting does not address the underlying breach or prevent recurrence for other patients.
  24. The correct answer is C (Screen for immediate danger, offer resources, and respect the couple's autonomy if there is no mandatory reporting trigger): most states do not mandate reporting of intimate partner violence involving a competent adult victim in the absence of specific triggers such as a weapon injury, which preserves the adult victim's autonomy over her own safety decisions. The appropriate approach is to screen for immediate danger, document findings, and offer resources such as a hotline, rather than overriding autonomy or ignoring the issue entirely.
  25. The correct answer is A (Report suspected child abuse to child protective services): reporting suspected child abuse is a mandatory legal duty for physicians in every state, triggered by reasonable suspicion based on clinical findings inconsistent with the stated history, not by obtaining a confession or proof. Relying on a child's direct denial, which is common even in genuine abuse due to fear, or simply monitoring informally, would fail this legal and protective obligation.
  26. The correct answer is C (Provide only the return-to-work clearance the patient has specifically authorized): disclosure of protected health information to an employer must be limited to what the patient has specifically authorized, following the minimum necessary standard. Even a well-intentioned request from an employer for additional diagnostic detail does not expand what may be disclosed beyond the scope of the patient's own authorization.
  27. The correct answer is A (Reporting requirements and seizure-free intervals required for driving vary significantly by state): some states require physician reporting of any seizure disorder diagnosis directly to the department of motor vehicles, while others rely on patient self-reporting with a specified seizure-free interval, commonly around six months to one year, before driving may resume. Because this varies by jurisdiction, physicians should know their own state's specific requirement rather than assuming a single national rule.
Case SOC-C2

A 15-year-old girl is brought to a pediatrician's office by her mother for a routine sports physical. During the private portion of the visit, without her mother present, the patient discloses that she has been sexually active with her 17-year-old boyfriend for the past three months, that they inconsistently use condoms, and that she has not told her mother because she is afraid of getting in trouble. She also mentions that her boyfriend sometimes grabs her arm hard enough to leave marks when he is angry, though she says she is not afraid of him and does not want anyone to intervene.

  1. The correct answer is B (Maintain confidentiality about the sexual activity disclosure, consistent with adolescent reproductive health confidentiality protections): most states protect an adolescent's ability to receive confidential reproductive health counseling and contraceptive services without automatic parental notification. A two-year age difference between two adolescents in a consensual relationship does not, by itself, trigger a mandatory report, and disclosing the relationship to the mother without the patient's consent would undermine the confidentiality this visit depends on.
  2. The correct answer is D (Contraceptive counseling for a minor can generally be provided confidentially in most states): the majority of states recognize a minor's ability to independently consent to contraceptive services and to receive that counseling confidentially, one of the well-established exceptions to requiring parental consent for a minor's medical care. This protection does not depend on the number of partners the patient has had or wait until age 18.
  3. The correct answer is C (Screen further for the pattern and severity of the behavior, assess safety, and offer resources while respecting her stated wishes if no imminent danger is identified): even for a minor, a single vague description of grabbing without other signs of imminent danger requires careful further assessment rather than an automatic report or automatic parental disclosure. Taking no action at all, however, would fail the physician's duty to at least screen further and offer resources appropriate to a potentially unsafe relationship.
  4. The correct answer is D (Provide a more thorough safety assessment, safety planning, and referral to appropriate resources given the escalation and expressed fear): the disclosure of escalating physical aggression, a threat, and expressed fear represents a meaningful change from the initial vague description and warrants a more thorough safety assessment and connection to resources such as a domestic violence advocate, done collaboratively with the patient rather than unilaterally informing her mother without her knowledge or simply waiting for a future visit.
  5. The correct answer is A (The designated health care agent's judgment about the patient's likely wishes in this specific situation): when a patient has both an older living will and a subsequently designated health care agent, the agent's judgment about how the patient's known values apply to the specific situation at hand generally takes priority, because the agent can weigh new clinical information, such as a potentially reversible pneumonia, that the original written document could not have anticipated.
  6. The correct answer is B (The DNR order applies only to cardiopulmonary resuscitation and does not preclude appropriate antibiotic treatment): a DNR order specifically addresses whether cardiopulmonary resuscitation should be attempted in the event of cardiac or respiratory arrest. It does not, by itself, indicate that other appropriate treatments, such as antibiotics for a treatable infection, should be withheld, unless those treatments have separately been declined by the patient or surrogate.
  7. The correct answer is C (Best interest standard, since the patient's own wishes were never directly expressed): substituted judgment requires some documented or previously stated evidence of what the patient himself would have wanted. When no such evidence exists, even when family members share a general sense of what he might have wanted, surrogates should fall back on the best interest standard, choosing the option a reasonable person in his situation would want, using their collective input as his equally ranked default surrogates.
  8. The correct answer is A (Explain that the agent's role is to represent the patient's own previously expressed wishes, which specifically declined this intervention, and offer support in this decision): a health care agent's authority is meant to carry out the patient's own known wishes, not to substitute the agent's personal preferences, especially when the patient has left clear, specific, written instructions on exactly this scenario. The appropriate response is a compassionate conversation reorienting the husband to his role and offering emotional support, rather than either blindly following his request or dismissing him.
  9. The correct answer is B (Continue resuscitation according to standard advanced cardiac life support protocols in the absence of a documented DNR order): in the absence of any documented advance directive or DNR order, the default presumption is in favor of attempting resuscitation, regardless of the patient's age or a dementia diagnosis, since neither age nor dementia alone determines a patient's wishes about resuscitation.
