Age and comorbidity can inform prognosis after resuscitation begins, but they do not justify withholding care absent a documented directive.
  10. The correct answer is D (In physician-assisted death, the patient self-administers the medication prescribed by the physician): this self-administration requirement is the key legal and ethical distinction between physician-assisted death, legal in a limited number of states under strict statutory conditions, and euthanasia, in which another person directly administers a lethal agent to the patient. Euthanasia remains illegal throughout the United States regardless of any state's physician-assisted death statute.
  11. The correct answer is D (Continue current treatment while involving the hospital ethics committee to help mediate the disagreement): when physicians and a surrogate disagree about whether an intervention is futile and initial conversations and second opinions have not resolved the conflict, the appropriate next step is ethics committee involvement to help mediate, clarify the medical facts, and explore the underlying values driving the disagreement, rather than the team unilaterally withdrawing treatment against the surrogate's wishes.
  12. The correct answer is A (An appropriate withdrawal of a non-beneficial intervention consistent with the patient's expressed values): withdrawing a treatment that is not achieving a meaningful benefit, based on a surrogate's substituted judgment about the patient's own previously expressed wishes, is standard and legally accepted end-of-life practice. It is fundamentally different from euthanasia or physician-assisted death because the underlying stroke, not any lethal act, causes death, and no court approval is required for this type of decision.
  13. The correct answer is B (Increasing the morphine dose to relieve pain is ethically appropriate under the principle of double effect, even if it might incidentally shorten life): the principle of double effect holds that an action with a good intended effect, here, relieving severe pain, is ethically permissible even if it carries a foreseen but unintended risk of a harmful secondary effect, such as respiratory depression, as long as the intent is symptom control rather than causing death. This is fundamentally different from euthanasia, where causing death is the direct intent.
  14. The correct answer is C (The POLST form should be interpreted in the context of the patient's goals, and the team should clarify with the patient or surrogate whether it was intended to apply to a reversible acute illness like this one): a POLST form reflects a patient's goals of care, which were often documented with a particular clinical trajectory in mind, such as progressive decline from a chronic illness. When a patient develops an acutely reversible condition that may not match the scenario originally envisioned, the appropriate step is to clarify intent with the patient or surrogate rather than either rigidly applying or dismissing the document.
  15. The correct answer is A (Respect his informed decision and discuss alternative approaches to his goals of care): a patient with decision-making capacity may decline even a potentially curative treatment for reasons that reflect his own values, such as prioritizing voice preservation over survival. Family members cannot override a capacitated patient's informed refusal, and an involuntary psychiatric hold is not an appropriate response to a well-reasoned, values-based treatment refusal absent evidence of an underlying psychiatric condition impairing his judgment.
  16. The correct answer is B (Continue to engage the son in supportive conversations about the prognosis and his mother's likely wishes, involving the ethics committee or palliative care team if needed): a surrogate's initial reaction of wanting everything done, especially amid acute grief, does not by itself justify overriding his authority. The appropriate approach is continued compassionate communication, often supported by palliative care or ethics consultation, to help him apply substituted judgment about what his mother herself would have wanted, rather than unilaterally overriding him or deflecting to hospital policy.
  17. The correct answer is D (Most states automatically revoke a former spouse's designation as health care agent upon divorce unless the patient has specifically reaffirmed it): many states have statutes that automatically revoke a former spouse's authority as a designated health care agent once a divorce is finalized, on the theory that the patient would generally not want an ex-spouse retaining this role. This does not usually void the entire document, since a properly executed advance directive can still have valid provisions, such as a living will, but decision-making authority would typically default to the next-ranked surrogate.
  18. The correct answer is C (Explore what the patient likely meant through further discussion with family and, if needed, ethics consultation, since the directive's language is ambiguous): general or vague language in an advance directive requires interpretation in light of the patient's broader values and any other statements she made, which is best accomplished through further family discussion and ethics consultation rather than either extreme of automatically continuing or automatically discontinuing the intervention, or defaulting immediately to court involvement for what is fundamentally a clinical and family conversation.
  19. The correct answer is A (Honor his informed refusal of blood products): a capacitated adult has the right to refuse any medical intervention, including a lifesaving blood transfusion, based on sincerely held religious beliefs, even when death may result. This right does not disappear if the patient later loses consciousness, since his prior clear, informed refusal continues to apply, and a court order compelling treatment over a competent adult's informed refusal is not appropriate.
  20. The correct answer is D (Reassess her current wishes and proceed with evaluation for the device if she has capacity and desires it now): patient preferences about treatment can change over time, and a patient with capacity retains the right to revise a previous decision at any point. A prior written refusal does not permanently bind a patient who later changes her mind while still possessing capacity, and family consent is not an additional legal requirement for a capacitated adult's own decision.
  21. The correct answer is B (To help facilitate communication, clarify values, and offer a recommendation, without removing decision-making authority from the patient or surrogate): ethics committees generally serve a consultative and mediating role, helping clarify the clinical and ethical issues and facilitate communication among disagreeing parties, rather than issuing a binding order that overrides the patient's or surrogate's own decision-making authority. Reimbursement determinations and licensing board referrals are outside the scope of this consultation.
  22. The correct answer is C (Follow the terms of her advance directive and withdraw life-sustaining treatment as she specified): a validly executed advance directive remains legally effective indefinitely unless the patient revokes or changes it, and the passage of time alone does not invalidate it. Once the clinical situation clearly matches the specific condition she described, her advance directive should be followed as an accurate expression of her own wishes, without requiring court verification of a clinical diagnosis that the medical team has already confirmed through appropriate evaluation.
  23. The correct answer is D (To allow the patient's own wishes to guide care and to designate a trusted decision-maker for the time when she can no longer participate directly in decisions): an advance directive completed while a patient has capacity serves to document her own values and preferences and to name a trusted surrogate for the future, but it does not transfer authority away from the patient while she still retains capacity to participate in her own care decisions, and it does not eliminate the need for ongoing conversation as her disease progresses.
  24. The correct answer is C (Shared decision-making, incorporating the patient's own values alongside the medical facts): this scenario reflects the modern standard of shared decision-making, in which the physician presents the relevant clinical facts, risks, and benefits of each option, and the patient applies his own values and goals to choose among reasonable alternatives. There is no requirement that a patient choose the option with the statistically higher chance of a particular outcome, and no information was withheld here, so therapeutic privilege does not apply.
  25. The correct answer is A (Facilitate ongoing discussion between both parents, supported by palliative care or ethics consultation, since both hold equal legal authority): parents who share legal custody generally have equal decision-making authority for their minor child, and neither parent's wishes automatically take precedence over the other's based on birth order of statements or gender. When parents disagree, the appropriate approach is facilitated discussion, often supported by ethics or palliative care consultation, rather than the hospital's legal department unilaterally selecting a side.
  26. The correct answer is B (Discuss with the patient beforehand whether he wants the order suspended for the specific, time-limited context of the procedure): many institutions require an explicit conversation before an elective procedure with sedation to clarify whether a patient wants their DNR or DNI order to remain in effect during a brief, reversible, procedure-related event, since this is a different context from the original goals behind the order. Neither automatically suspending nor automatically continuing the order without this conversation respects the patient's actual wishes, and sedation itself is not incompatible with a DNR order.
  27. The correct answer is C (Using an opioid to relieve dyspnea at the end of life, with the intent to relieve suffering rather than to cause death, is standard, ethically appropriate palliative care): opioids are an accepted and effective treatment for dyspnea as well as pain in the setting of terminal illness, and using them with the primary intent of relieving suffering, even if a secondary, unintended effect could theoretically shorten a very short remaining lifespan, is ethically supported by the principle of double effect and is not equivalent to euthanasia or physician-assisted death.
  28. The correct answer is A (Explore the family's concerns, and disclose the diagnosis to the patient in a sensitive, supportive manner unless the patient has specifically stated he does not want this information): patients generally have the right to know their own diagnosis, and family members cannot waive this right on the patient's behalf. Therapeutic privilege, the narrow exception allowing withheld information, applies only when disclosure itself would cause serious, direct psychological harm, not simply because family members anticipate the news will be difficult, and family consent is not required to inform the patient of his own diagnosis.
  29. The correct answer is D (Provide an honest range based on typical outcomes, acknowledging uncertainty, in a manner tailored to how much detail he wants): patients generally have a right to honest information about their prognosis when they ask for it, delivered compassionately and calibrated to how much detail the individual patient wants. Refusing to answer, giving a falsely reassuring answer, or deflecting the question to outside research all fail to respect his request for honest information from his own physician.
  30. The correct answer is B (A valid exercise of the patient's autonomy known as waiver, in which she is choosing to delegate information and decision-making to another person): a patient with capacity has the right to waive her own right to receive detailed medical information and to ask that information and decisions instead be directed to a chosen person, such as a spouse. This is an affirmative exercise of autonomy rather than evidence of impaired capacity, and honoring it is fully consistent with the underlying purpose of informed consent, which is to respect the patient's own choices about her care.
Case SOC-C3

An 81-year-old man with severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease is admitted for his fourth exacerbation this year, requiring intubation and mechanical ventilation shortly after arrival due to progressive respiratory failure despite noninvasive support. He has a living will from eight years ago stating he does not want to be kept on a ventilator long-term if there is little chance of returning to his baseline function, and he has designated his wife as his health care agent. He has been successfully weaned from the ventilator after each of his prior three admissions this year, though each recovery has taken slightly longer and his baseline functional status has been gradually declining.

  1. The correct answer is A (Continuing the ventilator for now is reasonable, since there remains a meaningful chance of returning close to his recent baseline, which is consistent with the intent of his directive): his living will specifically references a low chance of returning to his baseline function as the trigger for withdrawing long-term ventilation, and because there remains a reasonable chance of weaning him again as in his prior admissions, continuing current treatment is consistent with rather than contrary to his stated wishes. The living will remains valid regardless of its age, and his wife, as his designated agent, appropriately participates in applying its intent to this specific situation.
  2. The correct answer is C (A goals-of-care conversation now, while he has capacity, can help clarify how his values and directive should apply as his overall trajectory continues to decline): a documented pattern of declining recovery after each exacerbation is exactly the kind of new clinical information that should prompt a proactive goals-of-care conversation with the patient himself while he retains capacity, refining how his general directive should be interpreted going forward, rather than either applying a rigid identical approach each time or leaving the decision to his wife alone without his own updated input.
  3. The correct answer is D (Substituted judgment, applying his own previously expressed wishes from the updated conversation to this current, more clearly unfavorable clinical situation): because the patient had a specific, recent goals-of-care conversation addressing exactly this kind of declining trajectory, his wife has clear evidence of his own wishes to apply through substituted judgment, which is a stronger evidentiary basis than the best interest standard, and is the appropriate legal and ethical framework for a surrogate acting on documented and recently discussed patient values.
  4. The correct answer is B (An appropriate application of substituted judgment to withhold further escalation of a treatment unlikely to provide meaningful benefit): this outcome reflects the accepted, legally sound process of a surrogate applying substituted judgment, informed by a specific prior conversation with the patient, to decide against escalating an intervention that the clinical team believed was very unlikely to reverse his decline. It is not euthanasia, since no lethal act caused his death, does not violate his living will, since his wishes evolved through a documented later conversation that appropriately refines the original document, and requires no court approval.
  5. The correct answer is D (This is a near miss, and it should still be reported through the hospital's safety reporting system): an error caught before it reaches the patient and causes no harm is classified as a near miss or close call. Near misses reveal the same underlying systems weaknesses, such as ambiguous handwritten orders, that could cause real harm in a future instance, so they are valuable safety data that should be formally reported rather than only discussed informally or dismissed because no harm occurred.
  6. The correct answer is B (A root cause analysis): a root cause analysis is a structured, retrospective, multidisciplinary process used after a serious event or pattern of events to identify all of the underlying systems factors that contributed to the error, continuing past the most obvious proximate cause to ask why repeatedly. A PDSA cycle, by contrast, is a forward-looking tool for testing a specific proposed change on a small scale, not a retrospective investigation of what already went wrong.
  7. The correct answer is A (A Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle): this scenario describes the classic structure of a PDSA cycle, in which a team plans a specific, testable change, implements it on a small scale, studies the results against the original goal, and then decides whether to adopt, adapt, or abandon the change before a larger rollout. This iterative, small-scale testing approach is fundamentally different from a large randomized trial or a retrospective investigation of a single past event.
  8. The correct answer is C (Disclose promptly and honestly what happened, acknowledge the error, apologize, and describe steps being taken to prevent recurrence): current professionalism standards require full and prompt disclosure of medical errors to patients regardless of whether they cause lasting harm, including an honest explanation, an apology, and a description of preventive steps. Waiting for the patient to ask, disclosing only a partial account, or withholding disclosure to limit liability all fail this ethical obligation, and current evidence suggests transparent disclosure is more likely to reduce than increase litigation risk.
  9. The correct answer is D (A systems-based approach to preventing error, recognizing that most errors result from system design rather than individual failure alone): modern patient safety science emphasizes designing better systems and processes, such as standardized communication tools, because most serious errors reflect multiple aligned systemic weaknesses rather than a single clinician's isolated failure. This systems-based framing, rather than an emphasis on individual discipline, has been shown to more effectively and sustainably reduce recurrence of similar errors.
  10. The correct answer is C (Quality improvement surveillance to identify opportunities for improving processes of care): ongoing, systematic measurement of an outcome such as infection rates, compared against benchmarks over time, is a core quality improvement activity used to identify units or processes that may benefit from additional intervention or support. This is distinct from a root cause analysis, which is triggered by a specific serious event, and has no connection to legal proceedings or the informed consent process for an individual line placement.
  11. The correct answer is A (Disclose the error to the family since disclosure is required regardless of whether harm occurred): current professionalism standards require disclosure of medical errors that reach a patient, including this one, regardless of whether the harm was ultimately reversible or resulted in lasting injury. The family has a right to know what happened to their child, and disclosure to only the internal risk management department without informing the family who experienced the event does not fulfill this obligation.
  12. The correct answer is B (The Swiss cheese model of error): the Swiss cheese model illustrates how multiple layers of imperfect defense, such as barcode scanning, pharmacist verification, and computerized order entry, each contain small gaps, and a serious error typically requires several of these gaps to align at once for harm to reach the patient. Adding an additional independent layer of defense, like barcode scanning, reduces the likelihood that any single gap will allow an error through undetected.
  13. The correct answer is C (A systems-based, just culture approach that examines contributing process and system factors): modern morbidity and mortality conferences are generally structured around a just culture framework, which distinguishes between simple human error, at-risk behavior, and reckless conduct, and focuses primarily on identifying and fixing systemic contributing factors such as staffing and protocols rather than assigning blame to an individual clinician for an error that likely had multiple contributing systemic causes.
  14. The correct answer is A (Reviewing near misses like this one helps identify system vulnerabilities, such as an alert design problem, before they cause actual harm to a future patient): a caught error, or near miss, often reveals the same underlying system weakness, in this case a possible alert visibility problem, that could cause serious harm the next time the same gap aligns with other factors. Reviewing these events proactively, rather than waiting for repeated occurrences or actual harm, is a core principle of effective patient safety programs.
  15. The correct answer is D (It allows the team to identify and fix problems with the intervention on a small scale before investing resources in a larger rollout): the core advantage of a small-scale PDSA test is that unforeseen problems, unintended consequences, or the need for adjustments can be identified and corrected while the stakes and resource investment are still low, before deciding whether to expand the intervention. This iterative approach is not a legal requirement, and no quality intervention can guarantee success without ongoing monitoring even after a wider rollout.
  16. The correct answer is B (Report the pattern through the appropriate patient safety or quality channel so the underlying cause can be systematically reviewed): a recurring pattern of a similar error by the same individual, even when consistently caught by another safeguard, suggests either an individual knowledge gap or a systemic issue, such as inadequate renal dosing decision support, that deserves formal review rather than being dismissed simply because the safety net has worked so far, or handled only through an informal, unstructured conversation.
  17. The correct answer is C (A systems-level process change designed to add a redundant safeguard against a recurring type of error): identifying that interrupted or rushed counting processes contributed to prior events, and responding by building in an additional, independent verification step that applies to every case regardless of urgency, exemplifies a systems-based safety intervention intended to close a recurring gap, rather than a punitive response directed at the individuals involved in past incidents.
  18. The correct answer is B (A just culture, which distinguishes human error and at-risk behavior from reckless conduct): a just culture framework recognizes that most errors result from ordinary human fallibility or system-encouraged shortcuts rather than intentional wrongdoing, and it responds to these situations by focusing on learning and system improvement, while still holding individuals accountable when their actions represent genuinely reckless or intentional misconduct. This balance is what encourages honest error reporting while preserving meaningful accountability.
  19. The correct answer is D (To identify the underlying systemic contributing factors and implement changes to reduce the likelihood of a similar future event): a sentinel event, defined as an unexpected occurrence involving serious harm or death not primarily related to the natural course of the patient's illness, requires a thorough root cause analysis focused on identifying the systemic factors that contributed to the event and implementing corrective action, rather than being primarily an exercise in individual blame or litigation preparation.
  20. The correct answer is A (Contact the patient promptly to clarify the correct appointment date and correct the documentation error): once a documentation error affecting a patient's care, such as an incorrect follow-up date, is identified, the appropriate response is to promptly contact the patient to clarify the correct information and to correct the underlying documentation, minimizing any risk of a missed or delayed follow-up visit, rather than waiting passively or assigning blame without first verifying what actually happened.
  21. The correct answer is D (An outcome measurement used to evaluate the effect of a systems-level process change): comparing a specific measurable outcome, here time to antibiotic administration, before and after implementing a new protocol is a standard quality improvement method for evaluating whether a systems-level change achieved its intended effect. This is distinct from a root cause analysis, which investigates a specific past event, and has no direct connection to consent processes or malpractice defense.
  22. The correct answer is A (Identifying a specific recurring pattern in when and why errors occur allows a more targeted and effective systems-level intervention): recognizing that falls cluster around a specific, identifiable systemic vulnerability, incomplete handoff communication at shift change, allows the team to design a targeted intervention addressing that particular gap, which is generally more effective than a generic intervention that does not address the actual pattern revealed by the data.
  23. The correct answer is B (Address the underlying staffing shortage and fatigue-related risk factors in addition to reviewing the specific error): a just culture, systems-based response recognizes that a mandated double shift and resulting fatigue are significant systemic contributing factors to this error, and appropriately addressing them, alongside any individual education needed, is more likely to prevent recurrence than terminating the nurse, ignoring the systemic issue entirely, or imposing a punitive schedule change that does not fix the underlying staffing problem.
  24. The correct answer is C (Complex, multifactorial problems like readmissions usually require input and coordinated change across multiple contributing processes and disciplines): heart failure readmissions typically result from the interaction of many contributing factors, including discharge education, medication reconciliation, and outpatient access, each of which is best addressed by the relevant discipline working together. A single department alone often cannot capture or effectively address every contributing factor to a problem this multifactorial.
  25. The correct answer is C (To ensure a rapid, accurate investigation and allow prompt identification of any ongoing hazard requiring immediate action): a prompt internal reporting and review timeline is intended to capture accurate, timely information while memories and documentation are fresh, and, importantly, to identify quickly whether an ongoing systemic hazard needs urgent correction to prevent additional patients from being harmed. Disclosure to the patient about what happened should occur promptly and is not appropriately delayed until an entire internal investigation concludes.
Case SOC-C4

A 54-year-old woman is admitted for an elective total knee replacement. Due to a mix-up in the surgical schedule, the surgical team briefly prepares the wrong knee for the incision before a nurse performing the final surgical time-out notices that the marked, consented knee does not match the limb currently being prepped, and the error is corrected before any incision is made. The correct knee is then operated on successfully with no complications.

  1. The correct answer is B (A near miss, since the error was caught before any incision occurred and no harm resulted): because the surgical time-out successfully caught the discrepancy before the incision was made, no wrong-site surgery actually occurred. This event is classified as a near miss or close call, an error intercepted before reaching the patient, and remains valuable safety data despite causing no harm, which is different from a true sentinel event where the wrong-site surgery actually happens.
  2. The correct answer is D (Disclose what happened, including the near miss and the safeguard that caught it, in an honest and transparent conversation): even though no physical harm resulted and the correct surgery was successfully performed, transparency and honesty remain important professional values, and many patients want to know when something notable occurred during their care, including a close call that was caught by a safety process. Withholding this information, even absent harm, is inconsistent with a culture of open disclosure.
  3. The correct answer is A (Conduct a root cause analysis examining the scheduling, marking, and verification processes that allowed the initial mix-up to occur): even though the final safeguard, the surgical time-out, worked as designed, understanding why the earlier steps, such as scheduling and limb marking, allowed the error to occur in the first place is valuable for strengthening those upstream layers of defense. Relying solely on the final safeguard, or waiting for recurrence before investigating, misses an opportunity for proactive systems improvement.
  4. The correct answer is D (Adding an additional, earlier, independent layer of verification reduces the chance that a single point of failure allows an error to reach the final time-out unnoticed): consistent with the Swiss cheese model of error, adding another independent layer of defense earlier in the process reduces the likelihood that the same gap will align with a failure at the final time-out to allow an error through. The final time-out remains an important safeguard and is not eliminated by adding this additional earlier check.
  5. The correct answer is B (Medicare Part B): Medicare Part B covers outpatient physician services, durable medical equipment, and preventive services, and requires payment of a monthly premium. Part A covers inpatient hospital stays, skilled nursing facility care, and hospice, Part C is Medicare Advantage, an alternative private-plan structure for the overall benefit, and Part D covers outpatient prescription drugs.
  6. The correct answer is A (Medicare Part A): Medicare Part A covers inpatient hospital care, skilled nursing facility stays, hospice care, and some home health services, and is funded through payroll taxes with generally no monthly premium for most beneficiaries who have sufficient work history. Part B covers outpatient services, Part D covers prescription drugs, and a Medigap policy is a separate supplemental option that helps cover cost-sharing but does not itself substitute for Part A.
  7. The correct answer is C (Medicaid): Medicaid is a joint federal and state program providing coverage for eligible low-income individuals and families, and because states administer their own programs within federal guidelines, both the specific covered benefits and the income eligibility thresholds vary meaningfully from state to state. Medicare, in contrast, primarily serves patients 65 and older or those with specific disabilities or end-stage renal disease, with more uniform national eligibility rules.
  8. The correct answer is B (A health maintenance organization): an HMO typically requires enrollees to select a primary care physician who coordinates care and provides referrals to specialists, and generally does not cover out-of-network care except in emergencies. A PPO, by contrast, offers more flexibility, allowing patients to see specialists without a referral and to receive at least partial coverage for out-of-network care, usually in exchange for a higher premium.
  9. The correct answer is A (A preferred provider organization): a PPO allows enrollees to see specialists directly without a referral and provides at least partial coverage for out-of-network care, generally at a higher cost-sharing level, in exchange for a higher monthly premium than a more restrictive plan. This flexibility contrasts with an HMO, which generally requires a primary care referral and does not cover out-of-network care except in an emergency.
  10. The correct answer is D (Capitation, which shifts financial risk to the practice and creates an incentive to manage utilization and keep patients healthy): capitation pays a fixed amount per enrolled patient per period, independent of the volume of services that patient actually uses, which shifts financial risk onto the provider and creates an incentive to control unnecessary utilization and to keep the patient population healthy, in contrast to fee-for-service, which pays separately for each individual service rendered and can incentivize higher volumes of testing and treatment.
  11. The correct answer is C (An accountable care organization): an accountable care organization brings together groups of providers who share responsibility for the cost and quality of care for a defined population, aligning financial incentives with achieving better outcomes and controlled costs rather than simply the volume of services delivered. This differs structurally from an HMO or PPO, which are insurance plan designs defining network and referral rules for individual enrollees rather than a shared-accountability provider arrangement.
  12. The correct answer is A (Pay-for-performance or value-based payment): tying a portion of reimbursement to measured quality outcomes, such as readmission rates or blood pressure control across a population, rather than to the volume of services delivered, is the defining feature of pay-for-performance or value-based payment models. This is distinct from pure fee-for-service, which pays for each service regardless of outcome, and from pure capitation, which pays a fixed per-patient amount without an explicit quality-based adjustment.
  13. The correct answer is D (Even after formal discharge, professional guidelines generally caution against romantic relationships with former patients, particularly soon after termination, due to residual power imbalance and trust dynamics): professional ethics guidelines, especially in mental health fields, generally advise against or prohibit romantic relationships with former patients for a defined period after termination, or in some specialties indefinitely, because of the lasting power imbalance and trust dynamics inherent in the prior treatment relationship, regardless of formal discharge status.
  14. The correct answer is B (Decline the offer, since it represents an inappropriate conflict of interest tying a personal benefit to a specific prescribing commitment): accepting a substantial personal benefit explicitly conditioned on a commitment to prescribe a specific product creates a direct conflict of interest that can improperly influence clinical judgment, independent of whether the medication happens to be effective for some patients. This differs from accepting a small, non-conditioned token of appreciation or attending genuinely independent continuing education.
  15. The correct answer is C (Report the concern to the appropriate supervising attending or institutional channel promptly): signs of possible substance impairment and medication diversion by a colleague represent a serious patient safety concern and a professionalism obligation to report through appropriate channels, regardless of any discomfort about hierarchy or fear of professional consequences. Waiting, addressing it only informally, or remaining silent all risk continued harm to patients under that colleague's care.
  16. The correct answer is C (An institutional review board): an institutional review board, or IRB, must review and approve any proposed research involving human subjects before it may begin, evaluating the balance of risks and benefits, the adequacy of the informed consent process, and any additional protections required for vulnerable populations such as children. Neither a billing department, a licensing board, nor individual physicians acting alone have this specific regulatory review authority.
  17. The correct answer is A (The financial interest should be disclosed to the institutional review board and appropriately managed, since disclosure and oversight, not automatic exclusion, is the usual remedy): a conflict of interest exists because of the mere potential for the financial interest to bias professional judgment, regardless of whether bias is ever proven to have occurred. The standard approach is transparent disclosure to the appropriate oversight body, such as the institutional review board, along with appropriate management strategies, rather than an automatic, blanket exclusion from participating in the research at all.
  18. The correct answer is B (These elements protect the autonomy of a research participant, who may otherwise feel obligated to continue participating in a study they believe is required for their care): explicitly disclosing that participation is voluntary, that withdrawal will not affect ongoing standard care, and any research-specific risks helps prevent a patient from feeling coerced into continuing participation, particularly one who might otherwise believe that declining or withdrawing from the study would jeopardize her regular clinical treatment. These protections apply broadly to research consent generally, not only to surgical studies or industry-funded trials.
  19. The correct answer is D (Disclose the consulting relationship prominently to the audience and present a balanced review of all reasonable treatment options): the appropriate way to manage this conflict of interest is transparent disclosure of the relationship to the audience, combined with an educational presentation that fairly represents the full range of reasonable treatment options rather than emphasizing only the sponsoring company's device. Allowing the company to control or approve the content, or omitting disclosure, would compromise the educational integrity of the presentation.
  20. The correct answer is B (The physician should specifically address the potential for the patient to feel pressured to participate given the existing treatment relationship, and should clarify that his standard care will not be affected by his decision): when a treating physician is also the researcher recruiting his own patient, there is a heightened risk that the patient may feel obligated to participate to please his physician or fear his regular care will suffer if he declines. This dual role does not automatically prohibit the study, but it does require the consent process to explicitly address this pressure and reaffirm that standard care is unaffected by the patient's research decision.
  21. The correct answer is A (To improve care transitions and reduce the risk of a preventable readmission or adverse event after discharge): coordinated discharge processes, including medication reconciliation, scheduled follow-up, and clear patient-facing instructions, are core systems-based practice interventions designed to improve the transition of care from hospital to outpatient settings and reduce the risk of a preventable readmission or a post-discharge adverse event, rather than being driven primarily by length-of-stay metrics or billing requirements alone.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

Case case_AKI1

A 68-year-old man with type 2 diabetes and hypertension is admitted for a gastrointestinal bleed. He is treated with intravenous fluids and undergoes upper endoscopy, which reveals and cauterizes a bleeding duodenal ulcer. He is started empirically on ceftriaxone for presumed cholangitis and receives a single dose of gentamicin on hospital day 1 before that diagnosis is excluded. On hospital day 3 his urine output falls to less than 20 mL per hour and his creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.0 mg/dL to 2.1 mg/dL. His blood pressure is now 128/78 mmHg and heart rate is 82 beats per minute, and he has received a total of 4 liters of crystalloid since admission with no further evidence of active bleeding.

  1. The correct answer is B (Acute tubular necrosis from a combination of transient ischemia and aminoglycoside nephrotoxicity): Muddy brown granular casts are the classic urine sediment finding in acute tubular necrosis, and a fractional excretion of sodium above 2 percent indicates that the tubules have lost the ability to reabsorb sodium normally. This patient had a period of hypoperfusion from gastrointestinal bleeding plus a nephrotoxic aminoglycoside, a common combination that produces ATN in hospitalized patients. Prerenal azotemia would show a bland sediment and a low FENa, not muddy brown casts.
  2. The correct answer is A (Prerenal azotemia from reduced effective circulating volume during active bleeding): A bland sediment with hyaline casts, a FENa under 1 percent, and an elevated BUN to creatinine ratio all reflect a kidney that is functioning normally but underperfused, so it is avidly reabsorbing sodium, water, and urea in response to active gastrointestinal blood loss. This pattern reverses quickly once effective circulating volume is restored, unlike the tubular damage seen in established ATN.
  3. The correct answer is C (Give intravenous calcium gluconate immediately, followed promptly by insulin with dextrose): Peaked T waves indicate potassium-related cardiac membrane instability, which is a medical emergency regardless of the absolute potassium number. Intravenous calcium gluconate stabilizes the cardiac membrane within minutes and should be given first, followed by measures such as insulin with dextrose that shift potassium intracellularly while a definitive potassium-removing therapy such as dialysis is arranged.
  4. The correct answer is D (Refractory hyperkalemia that persists on repeat testing despite maximal medical therapy): The standard indications for urgent dialysis in AKI, remembered as AEIOU, are refractory acidosis, severe electrolyte abnormalities such as hyperkalemia unresponsive to medical treatment, ingestion of a dialyzable toxin, volume overload unresponsive to diuretics, and uremic symptoms. A modestly elevated creatinine alone, without any of these features, does not by itself require emergent dialysis.
  5. The correct answer is B (Reduced renal production of erythropoietin as her functioning kidney mass has declined): As functioning renal mass declines in chronic kidney disease, erythropoietin production falls, producing a normocytic, normochromic anemia with normal iron studies, exactly as seen here. This is treated with an erythropoiesis-stimulating agent once adequate iron stores are confirmed, since ESAs are ineffective when iron is deficient.
  6. The correct answer is D (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion related to his malignancy): A euvolemic patient with hyponatremia, inappropriately concentrated urine relative to a low serum osmolality, and an elevated urine sodium, in the setting of small cell lung cancer, is classic for SIADH. Small cell lung cancer is a well-known ectopic source of antidiuretic hormone production. Primary polydipsia would instead show a dilute urine osmolality, not a concentrated one, which argues against that diagnosis here.
  7. The correct answer is A (Regular insulin given together with concurrent dextrose to prevent hypoglycemia): After membrane stabilization with calcium, the next step is to shift potassium intracellularly quickly, which is accomplished with intravenous insulin, given with dextrose to prevent hypoglycemia, and can be supplemented with a nebulized beta agonist. Sodium polystyrene sulfonate removes potassium from the body but acts too slowly for this emergency, and dialysis remains the definitive treatment when a patient cannot be managed with medical therapy alone.
  8. The correct answer is C (Elevated anion gap metabolic acidosis from accumulating ketoacids): The anion gap equals sodium minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate, which here is 132 minus (94 plus 10), or 28, well above the normal range of about 8 to 12. Diabetic ketoacidosis generates unmeasured ketoacid anions, producing an elevated anion gap metabolic acidosis, entirely consistent with her hyperglycemia, ketonemia, and missed insulin doses.
  9. The correct answer is C (Focal segmental glomerulosclerosis associated with his heroin use): FSGS is the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in Black adults and is strongly associated with heroin use and HIV infection, though this patient's HIV test is negative. The biopsy finding of segmental sclerosis in only some glomeruli, with foot process effacement, is characteristic. Minimal change disease shows a normal light microscopy appearance, and membranous nephropathy shows diffuse basement membrane thickening rather than segmental sclerosis.
  10. The correct answer is B (Post-infectious (post-streptococcal) glomerulonephritis following his untreated pharyngitis): A latency period of one to three weeks after streptococcal pharyngitis, followed by hematuria with red cell casts, hypertension, and low complement levels that later normalize, is classic for post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis in a child. IgA nephropathy typically causes gross hematuria within a day or two of an upper respiratory infection, a much shorter latency than the two and a half weeks described here.
  11. The correct answer is D (IgA nephropathy, the most common glomerulonephritis worldwide): Recurrent episodes of gross hematuria occurring within one to two days of an upper respiratory infection, with normal complement levels, is the classic presentation of IgA nephropathy. The short latency distinguishes it from post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis, which has a longer delay of one to three weeks and typically shows low complement levels during the acute illness.
  12. The correct answer is A (Anti-glomerular basement membrane disease, also known as Goodpasture syndrome): Pulmonary hemorrhage combined with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and, most specifically, linear immunofluorescence along the glomerular basement membrane, is diagnostic of anti-GBM disease. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis would instead show a pauci-immune pattern with little to no immunofluorescence staining, rather than the smooth linear pattern described here.
  13. The correct answer is A (Class IV lupus nephritis, an aggressive form requiring intensive immunosuppression): Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis involving the majority of glomeruli with subendothelial deposits defines class IV lupus nephritis, the most severe and common aggressive class. It requires induction immunosuppression, typically high-dose corticosteroids plus either cyclophosphamide or mycophenolate mofetil, to preserve kidney function and prevent progression to end-stage renal disease.
  14. The correct answer is C (Calcium oxalate, related to low fluid intake and a high animal protein diet): Calcium oxalate stones are the most common type of kidney stone overall and are strongly promoted by chronically low fluid intake and diets high in oxalate or animal protein, matching this patient's history exactly. Struvite stones require a urease-producing infection, cystine stones suggest an inherited transport defect, and uric acid stones are linked to gout and appear radiolucent on plain film, none of which fit this presentation as well.
  15. The correct answer is B (Struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate), which forms in alkaline urine from urease-producing bacteria): Struvite stones form in alkaline urine generated by urease-producing organisms such as Proteus mirabilis, which splits urea into ammonia and raises urine pH. They can grow rapidly into large staghorn calculi filling the collecting system, exactly as described here, and typically require surgical removal along with treatment of the underlying chronic infection.
  16. The correct answer is D (Begin empiric antibiotics such as nitrofurantoin without routinely obtaining a urine culture first): Uncomplicated cystitis in a healthy, nonpregnant woman with classic symptoms and no red flags can be treated empirically with an agent such as nitrofurantoin, trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, or fosfomycin, based on symptoms and a simple urinalysis alone, without needing to wait for a urine culture to return.
  17. The correct answer is D (Obtain a urine culture and begin outpatient oral antibiotics such as a fluoroquinolone): Unlike simple cystitis, pyelonephritis should always have a urine culture sent to guide therapy. This patient appears clinically stable and is tolerating oral intake, so outpatient treatment with an oral agent is appropriate; hospitalization is reserved for patients who are toxic-appearing, pregnant, or unable to tolerate oral medications reliably.
  18. The correct answer is A (No antibiotic treatment is indicated based on these findings alone): Asymptomatic bacteriuria in an elderly, nonpregnant patient without a planned urologic procedure should not be treated, since treatment does not improve outcomes and only increases the risk of antibiotic resistance and adverse drug effects. Treatment of asymptomatic bacteriuria is reserved specifically for pregnancy and prior to invasive urologic procedures.
  19. The correct answer is C (Pelvic floor muscle (Kegel) exercises taught by a trained provider): Leakage triggered by increased abdominal pressure such as coughing, sneezing, or lifting, without urgency, describes stress incontinence, typically from urethral hypermobility after vaginal delivery. First-line treatment is pelvic floor muscle strengthening exercises, with surgical options such as a midurethral sling reserved for cases that do not respond adequately to conservative measures.
  20. The correct answer is B (Start an antimuscarinic agent such as oxybutynin or a beta-3 agonist): Sudden urgency with leakage before reaching the toilet describes urgency incontinence from an overactive detrusor muscle. After behavioral measures such as bladder training have provided only partial benefit, as in this patient, first-line pharmacologic treatment is an antimuscarinic agent or a beta-3 agonist such as mirabegron, not a sling procedure, which instead treats stress incontinence.
  21. The correct answer is C (An alpha-1 blocker such as tamsulosin for prompt symptom relief): This presentation is classic for benign prostatic hyperplasia, with a smoothly enlarged prostate and typical lower urinary tract symptoms. Alpha-1 blockers relax prostatic and bladder neck smooth muscle and provide rapid symptom relief, making them first line. Finasteride works by shrinking the gland over months and is generally added for larger prostates rather than used for immediate relief.
  22. The correct answer is D (Refer for prostate biopsy to evaluate for underlying malignancy): A hard, asymmetric nodule combined with a rising PSA over time is concerning for prostate cancer rather than benign hyperplasia, which typically produces smooth, symmetric enlargement instead. Biopsy is needed to confirm the diagnosis and obtain a Gleason score, which guides both prognosis and treatment decisions going forward.
  23. The correct answer is B (Arrange emergent urologic consultation for surgical exploration and detorsion): This presentation, sudden severe pain, a high-riding testicle with abnormal transverse lie, and an absent cremasteric reflex, is classic for testicular torsion, a surgical emergency. When clinical suspicion is this high, surgical exploration should not be delayed for imaging, since testicular salvage depends heavily on detorsion within about six hours of symptom onset.
  24. The correct answer is A (Epididymitis, most likely caused by a sexually transmitted organism): Gradual onset of scrotal pain, relief with scrotal elevation (a positive Prehn sign), an intact cremasteric reflex, and urethral discharge in a sexually active young man with multiple recent partners point to epididymitis, commonly caused by Chlamydia trachomatis or Neisseria gonorrhoeae, which requires antibiotics covering both organisms.
Case case_PID1

A 23-year-old sexually active woman presents with three days of lower abdominal pain, fever, and a new, foul-smelling vaginal discharge that started around the same time as the pain. She reports having had two new sexual partners over the past two months and admits she uses condoms inconsistently. On exam she has a temperature of 38.3C, diffuse lower abdominal tenderness without rebound or guarding, and marked cervical motion tenderness with bilateral adnexal tenderness on bimanual exam. A urine pregnancy test performed in the department is negative, and she is tolerating oral intake without difficulty.

  1. The correct answer is A (Pelvic inflammatory disease): Fever, lower abdominal pain, and cervical motion tenderness with bilateral adnexal tenderness in a sexually active young woman with multiple recent partners is the classic clinical picture of pelvic inflammatory disease, an ascending infection of the upper genital tract usually caused by Chlamydia trachomatis or Neisseria gonorrhoeae. A negative pregnancy test helps exclude ectopic pregnancy as the cause of her pain.
  2. The correct answer is B (Chlamydia trachomatis, a leading sexually transmitted cause): Chlamydia trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae are the two organisms most commonly responsible for pelvic inflammatory disease, though anaerobic and other vaginal flora often contribute as polymicrobial co-pathogens once the ascending infection is established, which is why empiric treatment covers multiple organism classes at once.
  3. The correct answer is D (Outpatient antibiotic therapy covering gonorrhea, chlamydia, and anaerobic organisms): A hemodynamically stable patient with pelvic inflammatory disease who can tolerate oral intake and does not have a tubo-ovarian abscess or other complication can be treated as an outpatient with a regimen that covers gonorrhea, chlamydia, and anaerobic organisms, given the polymicrobial nature of most of these infections.
  4. The correct answer is C (Tubal scarring leading to infertility and an increased ectopic pregnancy risk): Untreated or repeated episodes of pelvic inflammatory disease cause scarring and adhesions in the fallopian tubes, which can lead to tubal factor infertility and a substantially increased risk of future ectopic pregnancy, making prompt recognition and treatment of each episode important.
  5. The correct answer is D (It is contraindicated because combining it with his nitrate can cause severe, life-threatening hypotension): Phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitors and nitrates both increase nitric oxide-mediated vasodilation, and combining them can cause profound, refractory hypotension. This combination is an absolute contraindication, and patients on regular nitrate therapy for angina should not be prescribed a PDE-5 inhibitor under any circumstances.
  6. The correct answer is C (Polycystic ovary syndrome meeting standard diagnostic criteria): This patient meets the Rotterdam criteria with oligo-ovulation (irregular cycles), clinical hyperandrogenism (hirsutism and acne) confirmed biochemically, and polycystic-appearing ovaries on ultrasound, after other causes such as thyroid disease and hyperprolactinemia have been excluded, making polycystic ovary syndrome the most likely diagnosis overall.
  7. The correct answer is A (Endometriosis, given the cyclic timing and associated dyspareunia and dyschezia): Cyclic pelvic pain that begins before menses, along with dyspareunia and dyschezia, is the classic triad of endometriosis, caused by endometrial-like tissue implanted outside the uterus. Laparoscopy with visualization remains the definitive diagnostic test, although empiric medical treatment, as she has already partially tried, is often reasonably started before pursuing surgery.
  8. The correct answer is B (Adenomyosis, given the diffuse uterine enlargement and tenderness): A diffusely enlarged, tender, globular uterus with progressively heavy and painful periods, without a discrete mass, is classic for adenomyosis, which is the presence of endometrial glands and stroma within the myometrium. This differs from leiomyoma, which typically produces a discrete, well-defined mass rather than diffuse enlargement of the whole uterus.
  9. The correct answer is B (Uterine leiomyoma, commonly called a fibroid): An irregularly enlarged uterus with a discrete, well-circumscribed mass causing heavy bleeding and bulk symptoms such as pelvic pressure and urinary frequency is characteristic of uterine leiomyomas, the most common pelvic tumor in women. They are estrogen sensitive and typically shrink in size after menopause.
  10. The correct answer is D (Disseminated gonococcal infection despite the absence of local symptoms): The triad of migratory polyarthralgia, tenosynovitis, and pustular or vesiculopustular skin lesions in a sexually active patient is characteristic of disseminated gonococcal infection, which can occur even without prominent genitourinary symptoms and requires blood and often synovial fluid cultures along with targeted antibiotic therapy.
  11. The correct answer is C (Oral metronidazole given to the patient and her partner): A frothy, malodorous discharge with a strawberry cervix is classic for Trichomonas vaginalis infection. Because it is a sexually transmitted infection, both the patient and her sexual partner require treatment, typically with oral metronidazole, to prevent reinfection after her own symptoms resolve.
  12. The correct answer is A (Conservative management with a repeat ultrasound in several weeks to months): A small, simple, thin-walled cyst in a premenopausal woman is almost always a functional cyst and can be safely followed with a repeat ultrasound rather than removed, since most resolve spontaneously within a few cycles. Surgery is reserved for complex, solid, or persistently enlarging masses, particularly in postmenopausal women.
  13. The correct answer is C (Persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus drives cervical dysplasia and cancer risk): Persistent infection with high-risk HPV subtypes is the driving cause of cervical intraepithelial neoplasia and cervical cancer, which is why co-testing with cytology and HPV testing has become central to modern cervical cancer screening, and why HPV vaccination is an effective long-term prevention strategy.
  14. The correct answer is A (Semen analysis for the male partner along with assessment of the woman's ovulatory function): Infertility is defined as failure to conceive after 12 months of regular unprotected intercourse, a threshold this couple has already met. Because male factor contributes to roughly half of infertility cases, a semen analysis should be obtained early and in parallel with assessment of the woman's ovulatory and anatomic factors, rather than assuming the cause is female.
  15. The correct answer is D (Fibroadenoma, a common benign breast tumor of young women): A firm, mobile, well-circumscribed mass in a young woman is the classic presentation of a fibroadenoma. It can be followed clinically with imaging or excised if it enlarges over time, and malignancy is unlikely in this age group given these reassuring clinical and imaging features together.
  16. The correct answer is B (Obtain mammography and ultrasound followed by core needle biopsy): A firm, irregular, immobile mass with skin dimpling in a woman over 40 is concerning for malignancy and cannot be safely observed. Prompt imaging followed by tissue diagnosis with core needle biopsy is required, since clinical exam alone cannot reliably distinguish benign from malignant disease in this setting.
  17. The correct answer is D (Perform a skin punch biopsy to evaluate for inflammatory breast cancer): Breast erythema, warmth, and peau d'orange skin changes that do not improve with antibiotics should raise concern for inflammatory breast cancer, an aggressive malignancy that mimics infection. Biopsy is required to make the diagnosis, since failure to respond to a full course of antibiotics is the key clue that this is not simple mastitis.
  18. The correct answer is B (Paget disease of the breast, often associated with an underlying ductal carcinoma): A persistent eczematous, crusting change of the nipple and areola that fails to respond to topical treatment is characteristic of Paget disease of the breast, which is frequently associated with an underlying ductal carcinoma even when no mass is palpable, and warrants biopsy of the nipple itself rather than continued topical therapy.
  19. The correct answer is A (Prerenal azotemia from volume depletion related to her gastroenteritis): A FENa under 1 percent with a bland urine sediment in a patient with a clear history of volume loss from vomiting points strongly to prerenal azotemia, reflecting appropriate renal sodium and water conservation in response to reduced effective circulating volume, rather than intrinsic tubular or glomerular damage.
  20. The correct answer is C (Myoglobin in the urine causing a positive heme dipstick without true hematuria): In rhabdomyolysis, released myoglobin is filtered by the kidney and reacts with the dipstick's heme-based test, producing a strongly positive result for blood even though few actual red blood cells are seen on microscopy. Myoglobin is directly nephrotoxic to the renal tubules and is a well-recognized cause of acute tubular necrosis in this setting.
  21. The correct answer is B (A transient rise in creatinine peaking within a few days, typically followed by recovery): Contrast-induced nephropathy classically causes a rise in creatinine peaking around 48 to 72 hours after exposure, followed by gradual recovery toward baseline over the following one to two weeks in most patients, particularly those without severe underlying kidney disease. Risk is higher in patients with diabetes and pre-existing chronic kidney disease, both present in this patient.
  22. The correct answer is C (Acute interstitial nephritis triggered by the proton pump inhibitor): Fever, rash, eosinophiluria, and an acute rise in creatinine following initiation of a new drug, particularly a proton pump inhibitor, beta-lactam, sulfa drug, or NSAID, is the classic presentation of drug-induced acute interstitial nephritis, and the offending medication should be identified and stopped promptly.
  23. The correct answer is D (An erythropoiesis-stimulating agent to replace her deficient hormone): Anemia of chronic kidney disease is caused primarily by reduced renal production of erythropoietin. Once iron stores are confirmed adequate, as in this patient, an erythropoiesis-stimulating agent directly addresses the underlying mechanism by replacing the deficient hormone signal needed for normal red blood cell production.
  24. The correct answer is A (Reduced renal activation of vitamin D and phosphate retention lower serum calcium and stimulate the parathyroid glands): Failing kidneys cannot convert vitamin D to its active form and cannot excrete phosphate normally, both of which lower ionized calcium and directly stimulate parathyroid hormone secretion, producing the secondary hyperparathyroidism and eventual renal osteodystrophy seen in advanced chronic kidney disease.
  25. The correct answer is A (It reduces intraglomerular pressure and proteinuria beyond its blood pressure lowering effect alone): ACE inhibitors and ARBs preferentially dilate the efferent arteriole, which lowers intraglomerular pressure and reduces proteinuria independent of their systemic blood pressure effect, making them the preferred agents in patients with diabetic or other proteinuric chronic kidney disease.
Case case_HypoNa1

A 76-year-old man with a long history of heart failure with reduced ejection fraction presents with two weeks of worsening fatigue and new mild confusion noticed by his wife. She reports increasing bilateral leg swelling and abdominal distension over that same period, along with a ten-pound weight gain despite eating less than usual. On exam he has jugular venous distension estimated at 12 cm of water, bilateral lower extremity pitting edema to the knees, shifting dullness on abdominal percussion, and a displaced point of maximal cardiac impulse. Serum sodium returns at 126 mEq/L, and his weight today is notably higher than his documented weight at his last clinic visit one month ago.

  1. The correct answer is D (Hypervolemic, based on his edema, ascites, and weight gain): Jugular venous distension, peripheral edema, shifting dullness consistent with ascites, and a documented weight gain all indicate volume overload, so this patient's hyponatremia is hypervolemic, driven by his underlying heart failure, which activates the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and antidiuretic hormone release despite an overall excess of total body sodium and water.
  2. The correct answer is C (Free water restriction combined with treatment of his underlying heart failure, including diuresis): Hypervolemic hyponatremia from heart failure reflects a dilutional process from excess free water relative to total body sodium, so management focuses on free water restriction and treating the underlying cause with diuresis, not administering additional isotonic or hypertonic saline, which would worsen his existing volume overload.
  3. The correct answer is B (Osmotic demyelination syndrome from an overly rapid sodium rise): Rapid correction of chronic hyponatremia causes a sudden osmotic shift that can damage myelin in the pons and other brain regions, producing osmotic demyelination syndrome, which can cause severe and sometimes permanent neurologic injury. This is why chronic hyponatremia correction is generally limited to about 8 mEq/L in any 24-hour period.
  4. The correct answer is C (A urine sodium that is often low due to avid renal sodium reabsorption): In hypervolemic hyponatremia from heart failure, reduced effective circulating volume triggers avid renal sodium and water reabsorption through activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system and antidiuretic hormone, so urine sodium is often low and urine osmolality is inappropriately concentrated relative to the low serum osmolality, rather than dilute.
  5. The correct answer is A (Administer magnesium repletion in addition to continued potassium repletion): Hypomagnesemia impairs renal potassium conservation and makes hypokalemia refractory to potassium repletion alone. Correcting magnesium alongside continued potassium replacement is necessary to successfully normalize the potassium level whenever both abnormalities are present together like this.
  6. The correct answer is B (Metabolic alkalosis from loss of gastric acid and chloride through vomiting): Vomiting causes loss of hydrochloric acid from the stomach, which raises serum bicarbonate and lowers chloride, producing a hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis. The associated volume depletion and hypokalemia, from renal potassium wasting as the kidney tries to conserve sodium, both help perpetuate the alkalosis until her volume deficit is corrected.
  7. The correct answer is D (Chronic respiratory acidosis with appropriate renal metabolic compensation): A markedly elevated PaCO2 with a near-normal pH and an elevated bicarbonate reflects chronic respiratory acidosis, seen in longstanding COPD, in which the kidneys have had time to compensate by retaining bicarbonate, bringing the pH closer to normal even though the primary respiratory problem persists unchanged over time.
  8. The correct answer is D (A mixed respiratory alkalosis and elevated anion gap metabolic acidosis): Salicylate toxicity classically causes early direct stimulation of the respiratory center, producing a respiratory alkalosis, together with an elevated anion gap metabolic acidosis from accumulating organic acids, giving a near-normal pH despite two opposing primary disturbances, exactly as seen in this patient's blood gas result.
  9. The correct answer is C (Calculate the delta ratio, comparing the rise in anion gap to the fall in bicarbonate): The delta ratio compares how much the anion gap has increased above normal to how much bicarbonate has fallen below normal. A ratio far from the expected 1 to 2 range suggests a second, hidden acid-base process, such as a concurrent normal anion gap acidosis from large-volume saline resuscitation, a common twist tested on harder acid-base questions.
  10. The correct answer is A (Minimal change disease, the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in young children): Sudden-onset nephrotic syndrome in a young child with a normal-appearing glomerulus on light microscopy and podocyte foot process effacement on electron microscopy is classic for minimal change disease, which usually responds well to a course of corticosteroid therapy.
  11. The correct answer is B (Membranous nephropathy occurring secondary to his underlying solid tumor): Diffuse basement membrane thickening with subepithelial deposits is characteristic of membranous nephropathy, which can occur as a primary autoimmune disease, often with positive anti-phospholipase A2 receptor antibodies, or secondary to conditions such as solid tumors, hepatitis B, or certain autoimmune diseases, as is more likely here given his negative antibody result and recent cancer diagnosis.
  12. The correct answer is A (Diabetic nephropathy, showing classic Kimmelstiel-Wilson lesions): Nodular glomerulosclerosis, described as Kimmelstiel-Wilson lesions, is the characteristic biopsy finding in long-standing diabetic nephropathy, which is the leading cause of nephrotic-range proteinuria and chronic kidney disease among adults overall, developing gradually through a stage of microalbuminuria before overt proteinuria such as this appears.
  13. The correct answer is D (Renal vein thrombosis related to his nephrotic syndrome): Patients with nephrotic syndrome, particularly membranous nephropathy, are at increased risk of venous thromboembolism because of urinary loss of antithrombin III and other natural anticoagulant proteins. Sudden flank pain, hematuria, and worsening renal function in this setting should prompt evaluation for renal vein thrombosis with dedicated imaging.
  14. The correct answer is C (Uric acid, consistent with his gout history and acidic urine): Uric acid stones form in persistently acidic urine and are classically radiolucent on plain X-ray, though they are visible on CT, matching this presentation exactly. They are associated with gout, high-purine diets, and conditions with high cell turnover, and can be managed in part by alkalinizing the urine to increase uric acid solubility.
  15. The correct answer is B (An inherited defect in renal tubular amino acid transport): Recurrent stones with hexagonal crystals, a family history suggesting consanguinity, and an unusually young age of onset all point to cystinuria, an autosomal recessive defect in renal tubular transport of cystine and dibasic amino acids, which causes recurrent cystine stone formation throughout life.
  16. The correct answer is D (Proteus mirabilis, a urease-producing organism promoting stone formation): Proteus mirabilis and other urease-producing organisms split urea into ammonia, alkalinizing the urine and promoting precipitation of struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) crystals, which can grow into large staghorn calculi filling the collecting system, exactly as described in this patient with chronic catheter-associated infection.
  17. The correct answer is A (Treat with an appropriate antibiotic even though she remains asymptomatic): Asymptomatic bacteriuria in pregnancy is one of the specific situations where treatment is indicated even without symptoms, because untreated bacteriuria in pregnant patients carries a significant risk of progressing to pyelonephritis and is associated with preterm labor and low birth weight.
  18. The correct answer is B (An underactive detrusor muscle from diabetic autonomic neuropathy causing overflow incontinence): A large post-void residual with constant dribbling rather than a sudden urge suggests overflow incontinence from an underactive, poorly contractile bladder, commonly caused by diabetic autonomic neuropathy affecting the bladder's motor innervation, or alternatively by bladder outlet obstruction.
  19. The correct answer is C (Functional incontinence related to her mobility and cognitive limitations): When the urinary tract itself functions normally but the patient cannot reach the toilet in time because of a physical or cognitive limitation, such as advanced dementia or severe mobility impairment from arthritis, this is classified as functional incontinence, and management focuses on improving access and assistance rather than bladder-directed therapy.
  20. The correct answer is B (A varicocele from dilation of the pampiniform venous plexus): A soft, painless scrotal mass described as a bag of worms that decreases in size with recumbency is the classic description of a varicocele, caused by dilation of the pampiniform venous plexus, and it is far more common on the left side because of how the left gonadal vein drains into the left renal vein.
  21. The correct answer is C (A hydrocele, a fluid collection within the tunica vaginalis): A painless, fluid-filled scrotal swelling that transilluminates brightly is characteristic of a hydrocele, a collection of fluid within the tunica vaginalis surrounding the testicle. It is generally benign, though a new hydrocele appearing in an adult should still prompt an ultrasound to exclude an underlying testicular mass as the cause.
  22. The correct answer is A (Obtain abdominal and pelvic imaging to evaluate for a retroperitoneal mass compressing the gonadal vein): A new varicocele, especially one on the right side or one that fails to decrease in size with recumbency, is atypical for a simple idiopathic varicocele and raises concern for a retroperitoneal or renal mass obstructing venous drainage, which requires imaging such as CT or ultrasound before assuming a benign cause.
  23. The correct answer is D (Send tumor markers and proceed to radical inguinal orchiectomy): A firm, painless, solid intratesticular mass in a young man is presumed to be testicular cancer until proven otherwise. Tumor markers such as alpha-fetoprotein, beta-hCG, and lactate dehydrogenase are checked, and definitive diagnosis and treatment is radical inguinal orchiectomy rather than a transscrotal biopsy, which risks seeding the scrotal tissues with tumor cells.
  24. The correct answer is C (New erectile dysfunction may be an early marker of underlying cardiovascular disease warranting risk assessment): Because the small penile arteries develop atherosclerotic narrowing before larger coronary arteries typically do, new erectile dysfunction in a middle-aged man can be an early warning sign of systemic vascular disease, and evaluation of his cardiovascular risk factors is an important part of a thorough workup at this visit.
  25. The correct answer is B (Chronic unopposed estrogen exposure increases her risk of endometrial hyperplasia and cancer): Women with PCOS who do not ovulate regularly are exposed to continuous estrogen stimulation of the endometrium without the opposing effect of progesterone that normally follows ovulation, increasing the long-term risk of endometrial hyperplasia and endometrial cancer, which is why inducing regular withdrawal bleeding, often with combined oral contraceptives, is recommended for patients like her.
  26. The correct answer is D (Ovulatory dysfunction, once other causes have been excluded): Once pregnancy, structural causes such as polyps and fibroids, and coagulopathy have all been reasonably excluded as in this patient, ovulatory dysfunction becomes the most likely explanation for abnormal uterine bleeding, reflecting irregular or absent ovulation that disrupts the normal cyclic hormonal pattern controlling endometrial shedding.
  27. The correct answer is A (A surge in luteinizing hormone occurring at approximately midcycle): Ovulation is triggered by a surge in luteinizing hormone roughly midway through a normal 28-day cycle, occurring in response to the sustained high estrogen produced by the dominant follicle, and this LH surge causes final oocyte maturation and its release from the ovary.
  28. The correct answer is A (Laparoscopy with direct visualization and biopsy of suspected lesions): Although endometriosis is frequently treated empirically based on clinical presentation, laparoscopy with direct visualization of endometrial implants, ideally confirmed with biopsy, remains the definitive diagnostic gold standard, particularly when a patient has already failed empiric medical therapy and further surgical management is being seriously considered.
  29. The correct answer is C (Chlamydia trachomatis, a common cause of mucopurulent cervicitis): Mucopurulent cervical discharge with a friable cervix and postcoital spotting in a sexually active young woman is a classic presentation of chlamydial cervicitis, which is frequently asymptomatic but, if left untreated, can ascend to cause pelvic inflammatory disease and subsequent infertility.
  30. The correct answer is D (Hysterosalpingogram to assess tubal patency and uterine anatomy): Once ovulatory function has been reasonably confirmed as regular and male factor has already been excluded with a normal semen analysis, a hysterosalpingogram is the standard next test to evaluate the uterine cavity and confirm tubal patency, since tubal scarring or blockage is a common structural cause of infertility that this test can identify.
  31. The correct answer is B (Fibrocystic breast changes related to normal hormonal fluctuation): Bilateral, cyclic breast tenderness and diffuse nodularity that fluctuates with the menstrual cycle, without a discrete dominant mass, is characteristic of fibrocystic breast changes, a common benign condition related to normal hormonal fluctuation across the menstrual cycle.
  32. The correct answer is A (Exercise stress ECG, since he can exercise and the baseline ECG is interpretable): His presentation is classic for stable angina: predictable, exertional, reproducible chest pain relieved by rest, with a normal resting ECG and no rest symptoms to suggest an acute coronary syndrome. When a patient can exercise and the baseline ECG is interpretable, an exercise stress ECG is the appropriate first noninvasive test to evaluate for inducible ischemia before considering catheterization or serial biomarkers, which are reserved for suspected acute coronary syndrome.
  33. The correct answer is C (Right coronary artery occlusion, with risk of right ventricular infarction and preload-dependent hypotension): ST elevation in the inferior leads II, III, and aVF reflects occlusion of the right coronary artery in most patients, which also supplies the AV node (explaining her relative bradycardia here) and, frequently, the right ventricle. Inferior STEMI carries a real risk of right ventricular infarction, which makes the patient preload dependent, so nitrates and morphine can precipitate profound hypotension in this setting and should be used cautiously if at all.
  34.
