The correct answer is C (Give fibrinolytic therapy now if no contraindication exists, since transfer would exceed the acceptable PCI time window): Primary PCI is preferred only if it can be performed within 120 minutes of first medical contact when transfer is required. When that window cannot realistically be met, as in this rural setting, fibrinolytic therapy should be given within 30 minutes of arrival provided there is no contraindication, because time to reperfusion is the dominant factor limiting eventual infarct size and long-term outcome.
  35. The correct answer is B (Chewed non-enteric-coated aspirin, 325 mg): Non-enteric-coated aspirin, chewed rather than swallowed whole, is one of the earliest and most important interventions in suspected acute coronary syndrome because chewing produces rapid, near-complete platelet inhibition within minutes. Enteric coating is designed to slow and delay absorption, which is the opposite of what is needed in this acute, time-sensitive setting.
  36. The correct answer is A (Start a beta blocker or diltiazem for rate control and calculate a stroke risk score): The rhythm strip shows an irregularly irregular rhythm without discrete P waves, consistent with new atrial fibrillation, a common complication after myocardial infarction. Because he is hemodynamically stable, the priority is rate control with a beta blocker or a non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker, along with formal assessment of his stroke risk using the CHA2DS2-VASc score to decide on anticoagulation, rather than emergency cardioversion.
  37. The correct answer is D (Non-ST elevation myocardial infarction (NSTEMI)): Unstable angina and NSTEMI both present with rest pain and may show ST depression or T wave inversion without ST elevation, but they are distinguished by biomarkers: unstable angina has a persistently normal troponin on serial testing, while NSTEMI has a rising, elevated troponin, exactly as seen on this patient's repeat measurement several hours after presentation.
  38. The correct answer is B (Papillary muscle rupture causing acute severe mitral regurgitation): A new holosystolic murmur radiating to the axilla with sudden flash pulmonary edema several days after infarction is classic for papillary muscle rupture causing acute, severe mitral regurgitation. Because the left atrium has had no time to accommodate the abrupt new volume load, pulmonary edema develops rapidly, and this complication is considered a surgical emergency requiring urgent evaluation.
  39. The correct answer is C (Free wall rupture with cardiac tamponade): Free wall rupture typically occurs 3 to 5 days after a transmural infarct and presents with rapid hemodynamic collapse from cardiac tamponade, exactly as seen here with sudden hypotension, jugular venous distension, muffled heart sounds, and echocardiographic evidence of tamponade physiology. This is a surgical emergency requiring immediate pericardiocentesis and definitive repair.
  40. The correct answer is D (Dressler syndrome (post-infarction pericarditis)): Dressler syndrome is an immune-mediated pericarditis that occurs weeks after myocardial infarction, presenting with pleuritic, positional chest pain relieved by sitting forward, low-grade fever, and diffuse ST elevation with PR depression on ECG, a pattern clearly distinct from the localized ST elevation seen with a new, acute infarction in a specific coronary territory.
Case case_a_1

A 66-year-old man with a history of hypertension, type 2 diabetes, and hyperlipidemia presents with 30 minutes of crushing substernal chest pain radiating to his left arm, associated with diaphoresis and nausea. He reports the pain began suddenly while he was watching television and has been constant since onset, rated 9 out of 10 in severity. Vital signs on arrival are blood pressure 142/88 mmHg, heart rate 96/min, respiratory rate 18/min, and oxygen saturation 97 percent on room air. He appears uncomfortable and diaphoretic but is alert and able to answer questions clearly. A 12-lead ECG is obtained within minutes of arrival.

  1. The correct answer is C (Aspirin, and transfer for primary PCI if it can be achieved within 120 minutes): ST elevation in leads II, III, and aVF indicates an inferior STEMI, usually from right coronary artery occlusion. Aspirin should be given immediately and reperfusion pursued as fast as possible, with primary PCI preferred if achievable within the guideline-recommended time window from first medical contact.
  2. The correct answer is C (Right ventricular involvement causing preload-dependent hypotension; give intravenous fluids and avoid nitrates): Inferior STEMI from right coronary artery occlusion frequently involves the right ventricle, since the right coronary artery typically supplies it. Right ventricular infarction produces a preload-dependent state: hypotension with clear lung fields and elevated jugular venous pressure suggests this mechanism rather than pump failure or tamponade, both of which would be expected to show pulmonary edema or other distinguishing signs. Intravenous fluids are given to support preload, while agents that reduce preload, such as nitrates, are avoided because they can precipitate severe hypotension in this setting.
  3. The correct answer is A (Dual antiplatelet therapy, a high-intensity statin, a beta blocker, and an ACE inhibitor): Standard discharge therapy after an acute coronary syndrome treated with stent placement includes dual antiplatelet therapy (aspirin plus a P2Y12 inhibitor) for at least 12 months, a high-intensity statin, a beta blocker, and an ACE inhibitor or ARB, particularly important here given his diabetes and hypertension, all of which meaningfully reduce his long-term cardiovascular risk.
  4. The correct answer is A (At least 12 months): After an acute coronary syndrome, particularly one treated with stent placement, dual antiplatelet therapy is generally continued for a minimum of 12 months to reduce the risk of stent thrombosis and recurrent ischemic events, unless an elevated bleeding risk requires an individualized, shorter course determined jointly with the patient's cardiologist.
  5. The correct answer is C (Ventricular septal rupture): A step-up in oxygen saturation between the right atrium and the right ventricle indicates a left-to-right shunt occurring at the level of the ventricles, which occurs with ventricular septal rupture after infarction. This produces a new harsh murmur at the left sternal border, as described here, and is a mechanical complication requiring urgent surgical evaluation and repair.
  6. The correct answer is D (Give atropine): This patient has a symptomatic, unstable bradycardia, evidenced by his altered mentation, hypotension, and signs of poor perfusion. Atropine is the first-line pharmacologic treatment; if it fails to improve heart rate and perfusion, transcutaneous pacing or an epinephrine or dopamine infusion is the next step while preparing for definitive pacing. A beta blocker would worsen his bradycardia further, and cardioversion treats tachyarrhythmias rather than bradyarrhythmias like this one.
  7. The correct answer is D (Synchronized cardioversion): Any tachyarrhythmia causing hemodynamic instability, such as this patient's altered mental status and significant hypotension, should be treated with immediate synchronized cardioversion rather than escalating medical therapy first, because further delay in an unstable patient risks progression to cardiovascular collapse.
  8. The correct answer is A (Adenosine): This presentation is classic for supraventricular tachycardia from atrioventricular nodal reentry. In a stable patient like this one, vagal maneuvers are tried first, and if unsuccessful, adenosine is the next step because it transiently blocks conduction through the AV node and can terminate the reentrant circuit responsible for the arrhythmia.
  9. The correct answer is A (Anticoagulation with a direct oral anticoagulant such as apixaban): Her CHA2DS2-VASc score is 6 (1 for hypertension, 1 for age 65-74, 1 for diabetes, 2 for prior TIA, 1 for female sex), indicating a markedly elevated stroke risk that warrants long-term anticoagulation. In the absence of a mechanical valve or moderate to severe mitral stenosis, a direct oral anticoagulant is preferred over warfarin for most patients with nonvalvular atrial fibrillation like her.
  10. The correct answer is D (Transesophageal echocardiogram to exclude a left atrial thrombus, or at least 3 weeks of anticoagulation first): When the duration of atrial fibrillation is unknown or exceeds 48 hours, as is the case here, cardioversion carries a real risk of dislodging an atrial thrombus and causing an embolic stroke. Either at least 3 weeks of therapeutic anticoagulation before cardioversion, or a transesophageal echocardiogram to specifically exclude a left atrial thrombus, is required first.
  11. The correct answer is C (Observation, since this pattern is usually benign and does not require pacing): This describes second degree AV block, Mobitz type I (Wenckebach), which is usually benign, often due to increased vagal tone, and typically does not require pacing unless the patient becomes symptomatic. This is in clear contrast to Mobitz type II, which carries a real risk of progressing abruptly to complete heart block and usually warrants pacemaker placement even without symptoms.
  12. The correct answer is B (Torsades de pointes): A markedly prolonged corrected QT interval, especially in the setting of a family history of sudden death at a young age, strongly suggests congenital long QT syndrome, which predisposes affected individuals to torsades de pointes, a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia that can degenerate into ventricular fibrillation and cause sudden cardiac death, particularly during exertion or strong emotional stimuli.
  13. The correct answer is A (Brugada syndrome): The coved ST elevation in the right precordial leads with a right bundle branch block-like pattern, occurring in a young patient with syncope and a family history of unexplained sudden death, is the classic ECG signature of Brugada syndrome, an inherited channelopathy that predisposes affected individuals to ventricular fibrillation and sudden cardiac death, often without any structural heart abnormality on imaging.
  14. The correct answer is C (Begin chest compressions and prepare for immediate unsynchronized defibrillation): This rhythm is ventricular fibrillation, a pulseless arrest rhythm requiring immediate action. It is treated with immediate high-quality chest compressions and unsynchronized defibrillation as soon as the shockable rhythm is confirmed, following the standard advanced cardiac life support algorithm, not synchronized cardioversion, which requires a discernible QRS complex to properly time the delivered shock.
  15. The correct answer is B (Transthoracic echocardiogram): While his elevated BNP supports a diagnosis of heart failure, echocardiography is the key test needed to establish the ejection fraction, which determines whether he has HFrEF or HFpEF, a distinction that directly and substantially changes the pharmacologic approach to his ongoing management.
Case case_b_1

A 71-year-old man with a well-documented history of HFrEF, with an ejection fraction of 25 percent on his most recent echocardiogram, presents to the emergency department with 3 days of worsening dyspnea, new orthopnea, and a 4 kg weight gain that he first noticed after attending a family gathering over the weekend. He admits to eating unusually salty foods at that gathering and forgetting one dose of his home diuretic. Exam reveals jugular venous distension, bibasilar crackles on lung auscultation, and 2+ pitting edema in both lower extremities. His blood pressure is 138/84 mmHg, heart rate is 92/min, and his skin remains warm and well perfused to the touch throughout the exam.

  1. The correct answer is B (Intravenous loop diuretic): This is acute decompensated heart failure triggered by dietary sodium indiscretion and a missed diuretic dose, presenting as volume overloaded ('wet') but well perfused ('warm'). Intravenous loop diuretics are the appropriate initial treatment for this congested, well-perfused presentation. Intravenous fluids would clearly worsen his existing congestion, and inotropes are reserved instead for poorly perfused, low-output states, which he does not currently have.
  2. The correct answer is C (Warm and wet, implying diuresis alone is likely sufficient without the need for inotropic support): He is congested (wet, reflecting his volume overload) but remains well perfused (warm, given his normal blood pressure and lack of any signs of poor perfusion), so diuresis alone is generally sufficient in his case. A 'cold' classification, indicating poor perfusion instead, would prompt consideration of inotropic support, which is not needed here.
  3. The correct answer is B (Add a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist and an SGLT2 inhibitor to complete guideline directed medical therapy): Complete guideline directed medical therapy for HFrEF includes an ACE inhibitor or ARB (or ARNI), a beta blocker, a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist, and an SGLT2 inhibitor. He is already on two of these four pillars, so the mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist and SGLT2 inhibitor should now be added, given his adequate renal function and potassium, to further reduce his future mortality and hospitalization risk.
  4. The correct answer is D (An SGLT2 inhibitor): SGLT2 inhibitors have demonstrated a clear mortality benefit in HFrEF independent of diabetes status and are now considered part of complete guideline directed medical therapy alongside an ACE inhibitor or ARB (or ARNI), a beta blocker, and a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist. Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers are generally avoided in HFrEF because of their negative inotropic effect on an already weakened ventricle.
  5. The correct answer is C (Hydralazine plus isosorbide dinitrate): Hydralazine combined with isosorbide dinitrate has demonstrated an added mortality benefit specifically in Black patients with HFrEF who remain symptomatic on standard guideline directed therapy, and it is also a useful option for patients who cannot tolerate an ACE inhibitor or ARB because of significant renal dysfunction.
  6. The correct answer is A (Give an antiarrhythmic such as amiodarone, or perform elective synchronized cardioversion): This is monomorphic ventricular tachycardia, which in a patient with known structural heart disease like his ischemic cardiomyopathy should be assumed to be ventricular in origin until proven otherwise. Because he remains hemodynamically stable, treatment options include antiarrhythmic drug therapy (amiodarone, procainamide, or sotalol) or elective synchronized cardioversion; adenosine is not appropriate for a wide-complex tachycardia of likely ventricular origin, and defibrillation is unsynchronized and reserved specifically for pulseless rhythms, not this stable, perfusing rhythm.
  7. The correct answer is A (Diuretics for congestion, an SGLT2 inhibitor, and aggressive treatment of her hypertension and obesity): In HFpEF, the ACE inhibitor, beta blocker, and mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist regimen proven effective in HFrEF has not shown the same clear mortality benefit. Management instead centers on diuretics for volume control, SGLT2 inhibitors, which do have demonstrated benefit specifically in HFpEF, and aggressive treatment of underlying contributors such as her hypertension and obesity.
  8. The correct answer is B (Serum potassium and renal function): Mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists such as spironolactone can cause clinically significant hyperkalemia and worsening renal function, particularly when combined with an ACE inhibitor or ARB as in his case, so potassium and renal function should be checked before starting therapy and monitored closely at follow-up thereafter.
  9. The correct answer is D (An S3 gallop, reflecting rapid filling of a volume-overloaded, poorly compliant ventricle): An S3 gallop occurs early in diastole and reflects rapid ventricular filling into an already volume-overloaded, poorly compliant ventricle, making it a classic and fairly specific finding in decompensated systolic heart failure, distinct from an S4, which occurs late in diastole during atrial contraction against a stiff, noncompliant ventricle.
  10. The correct answer is C (A washout period of at least 36 hours off the ACE inhibitor before starting the ARNI, to reduce angioedema risk): Combining an ACE inhibitor with an ARNI significantly increases the risk of life-threatening angioedema because both medication classes increase circulating bradykinin levels. A washout period of at least 36 hours is therefore required between stopping the ACE inhibitor and starting sacubitril-valsartan to meaningfully reduce this risk.
  11. The correct answer is B (Aortic valve replacement, since he now has symptomatic severe aortic stenosis): Once symptoms such as syncope, angina, or heart failure develop in severe aortic stenosis, as they clearly have here, prognosis worsens sharply without intervention, regardless of a preserved ejection fraction. Aortic valve replacement, surgical or transcatheter depending on surgical risk and patient factors, is indicated for symptomatic severe aortic stenosis; valvuloplasty is only a temporizing measure and not considered definitive therapy.
  12. The correct answer is D (Acute severe mitral regurgitation from papillary muscle rupture or ischemic dysfunction): Acute severe mitral regurgitation after myocardial infarction, whether from frank papillary muscle rupture or severe ischemic papillary muscle dysfunction, causes sudden flash pulmonary edema because the left atrium has not had time to dilate and accommodate the new regurgitant volume, quite unlike chronic mitral regurgitation, which is typically well tolerated for years before symptoms develop.
  13. The correct answer is B (Refer for mitral valve surgery, since objective evidence of ventricular decline is now present): In chronic mitral regurgitation, surgery is indicated once the patient becomes symptomatic, or once objective measures of left ventricular size or function begin to decline, as has just occurred here, even in the continued absence of symptoms, because waiting for symptoms to appear can allow irreversible ventricular damage to accumulate in the meantime.
  14. The correct answer is A (Warfarin): This presentation, including her history of rheumatic fever and the classic exam findings, is consistent with rheumatic mitral stenosis, which strongly predisposes to atrial fibrillation because of chronic left atrial enlargement and pressure overload. In atrial fibrillation associated with moderate to severe mitral stenosis, as in her case, or with a mechanical valve, warfarin remains the anticoagulant of choice, since direct oral anticoagulants have not been shown to be safe or effective in this specific patient population.
  15. The correct answer is A (Chronic severe aortic regurgitation): Chronic severe aortic regurgitation produces a widened pulse pressure and hyperdynamic, bounding pulses because a large stroke volume is needed to compensate for backward flow into the left ventricle during diastole, along with the characteristic early diastolic decrescendo murmur best heard with the patient sitting forward in expiration, exactly as described in his exam.
Case case_c_1

A 75-year-old woman with a history of hypertension presents to the emergency department with 2 days of palpitations and mild fatigue that have gradually worsened. She denies any chest pain, syncope, or focal neurologic symptoms. Vital signs on arrival are blood pressure 128/78 mmHg, heart rate 96/min and notably irregular on palpation, respiratory rate 16/min, and oxygen saturation 98 percent on room air. She appears comfortable and is able to provide a clear history without difficulty. A rhythm strip is obtained as part of her initial workup.

  1. The correct answer is C (Calculate her CHA2DS2-VASc score to guide the anticoagulation decision): The rhythm strip shows an irregularly irregular rhythm without discrete P waves, consistent with new atrial fibrillation. Because she is currently hemodynamically stable, the next step is to calculate her CHA2DS2-VASc score, accounting for her age, sex, and hypertension, to determine whether anticoagulation is indicated, rather than assuming her risk based on stroke history alone.
  2. The correct answer is B (Synchronized cardioversion): She has now become hemodynamically unstable, with new confusion and significant hypotension directly attributable to her arrhythmia. Any unstable tachyarrhythmia should be treated with immediate synchronized cardioversion, regardless of her current anticoagulation status, since further delay risks progression to cardiovascular collapse. Adenosine, notably, is not an effective treatment for atrial fibrillation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Continue anticoagulation for at least 4 weeks after cardioversion, then reassess based on her CHA2DS2-VASc score): Even after successful cardioversion restores normal sinus rhythm, atrial mechanical function can lag meaningfully behind the restored electrical rhythm, and there remains a real risk of thrombus formation and subsequent embolization for several weeks afterward. Anticoagulation should therefore be continued for at least 4 weeks post-cardioversion, with the decision on further long-term therapy then guided by her overall CHA2DS2-VASc score.
  4. The correct answer is B (Reassurance, with no further treatment needed): An isolated midsystolic click without an associated murmur is classic for mitral valve prolapse, a condition that is usually entirely benign and often found incidentally, exactly as in her case. In an asymptomatic patient with no complicating features, reassurance alone is appropriate, with no indication for surgery, anticoagulation, or routine antibiotic prophylaxis before procedures.
  5. The correct answer is D (The modified Duke criteria, combining blood culture and echocardiographic findings): Fever, a new regurgitant murmur, and peripheral stigmata such as painful fingertip nodules (Osler nodes), particularly in a patient at elevated risk such as this one given his injection drug use, should raise strong concern for infective endocarditis. The modified Duke criteria combine blood culture results and echocardiographic findings, along with additional clinical criteria, to help establish this diagnosis.
  6. The correct answer is B (Pacemaker placement, given the risk of progression to complete heart block): This rhythm strip shows second degree AV block, Mobitz type II, characterized by a constant PR interval with intermittently and unpredictably dropped beats, exactly as described here. Unlike Mobitz type I, this pattern carries a substantial risk of abrupt progression to complete heart block, so pacemaker placement is generally recommended even in asymptomatic patients like him, ideally before proceeding with elective surgery.
  7. The correct answer is A (Lifestyle modification, including the DASH diet, sodium reduction, and exercise): A blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg or higher, confirmed on repeated proper measurement as it was here, meets the threshold for a diagnosis of hypertension. In a patient without another compelling indication for immediate pharmacotherapy, lifestyle modification, including the DASH diet, sodium reduction, weight loss, exercise, and moderation of alcohol intake, is the appropriate first step.
  8. The correct answer is C (An ACE inhibitor or ARB): In patients with diabetes and evidence of early kidney involvement such as his new microalbuminuria, an ACE inhibitor or ARB is preferred as first-line therapy because of its added benefit in reducing intraglomerular pressure and slowing progression of diabetic kidney disease, well beyond its blood pressure lowering effect alone.
  9. The correct answer is D (A thiazide diuretic or calcium channel blocker): In Black patients without a specific compelling comorbid indication, such as diabetic nephropathy or heart failure, which he does not have, thiazide diuretics and calcium channel blockers tend to produce a greater initial blood pressure response than ACE inhibitors or ARBs used alone, and are generally preferred as first-line monotherapy in this population.
  10. The correct answer is C (Primary hyperaldosteronism): Resistant hypertension accompanied by unexplained hypokalemia and metabolic alkalosis, as seen in her case, is a classic clue for primary hyperaldosteronism, in which excess aldosterone drives renal potassium wasting and hydrogen ion loss, and this finding should prompt measurement of a plasma aldosterone-to-renin ratio for further evaluation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Fibromuscular dysplasia): Fibromuscular dysplasia is the classic cause of renal artery stenosis in young women like her, producing the characteristic 'string of beads' appearance on angiographic imaging, in clear contrast to atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis, which typically affects older patients who have significant vascular risk factors such as long-standing smoking or diabetes.
  12. The correct answer is A (Pheochromocytoma): Episodic headache, palpitations, and diaphoresis accompanied by paroxysmal severe hypertension, exactly as described in his case, is the classic triad-plus-pattern for pheochromocytoma, a catecholamine-secreting adrenal tumor, and should prompt measurement of plasma or urine metanephrines as the next diagnostic step.
  13. The correct answer is B (Gradual reduction of blood pressure over 24 to 48 hours with oral medication, without hospitalization): This is hypertensive urgency: severely elevated blood pressure without any evidence of acute end-organ damage. It is managed with gradual blood pressure reduction over 24 to 48 hours using oral medication, typically without the need for hospitalization, in clear contrast to hypertensive emergency, which requires intravenous therapy administered in a monitored setting.
  14. The correct answer is A (An intravenous beta blocker, such as esmolol or labetalol): In aortic dissection, blood pressure and heart rate should be lowered rapidly with an intravenous beta blocker first, in order to reduce the shear stress, or dP/dt, on the aortic wall, before adding a vasodilator if further blood pressure reduction is still needed. Starting with a pure vasodilator alone can cause reflex tachycardia, which would increase shear stress and could actively worsen his dissection.
  15. The correct answer is A (A 68-year-old man who has smoked cigarettes for 20 years): Current screening guidelines recommend one-time abdominal ultrasound screening for abdominal aortic aneurysm specifically in men aged 65 to 75 who have ever smoked, given their substantially higher risk compared with never-smokers, women without additional risk factors, and younger patients regardless of other cardiovascular risk factors.
  16. The correct answer is D (Ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm): The triad of sudden severe abdominal or back pain, hypotension, and a pulsatile abdominal mass, exactly as seen in his presentation, is classic for ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm, a surgical emergency requiring immediate resuscitation and emergent repair without delay for extensive further workup.
  17. The correct answer is C (Aortic dissection): Sudden, severe, tearing chest or back pain accompanied by a significant blood pressure difference between the two arms, or a pulse deficit, exactly as documented here, is a classic presentation of aortic dissection, reflecting compromised flow to one subclavian artery as the dissection extends along the length of the aorta.
  18. The correct answer is D (Emergency surgical repair): A dissection involving the ascending aorta, classified as Stanford type A, is a surgical emergency because of the risk of rupture into the pericardium causing tamponade, or involvement of the coronary arteries or the aortic valve itself. Emergency surgical repair is indicated regardless of the patient's current hemodynamic stability, since the risk of sudden catastrophic complications remains high without repair.
  19. The correct answer is A (Transesophageal echocardiography): Transesophageal echocardiography can be performed directly at the bedside and provides excellent visualization of the aorta, making it the preferred imaging modality in a hemodynamically unstable patient with suspected aortic dissection, like him, who cannot safely be transported elsewhere for CT angiography.
  20. The correct answer is A (Supervised exercise therapy, smoking cessation, a statin, and an antiplatelet agent): An ABI below 0.9 is diagnostic of peripheral artery disease, and his symptoms are entirely consistent with claudication. First-line management includes supervised exercise therapy, smoking cessation, a statin, and an antiplatelet agent, reserving revascularization procedures for lifestyle-limiting symptoms that fail an adequate trial of conservative therapy, or for critical limb ischemia, which he does not currently have.
Case case_d_1

A 79-year-old woman with a history of hypertension presents after an episode of syncope that occurred while she was walking briskly up a hill during her usual morning walk. On further questioning, she reports several months of exertional chest tightness and progressive shortness of breath that she had attributed to simply getting older, but which have clearly been worsening. On exam, a harsh systolic murmur is heard at the right upper sternal border, radiating clearly to both carotid arteries, with a notably delayed and diminished carotid upstroke on palpation of both carotid pulses.

  1. The correct answer is D (Transthoracic echocardiogram): Her history and exam findings, exertional syncope, angina, and dyspnea together with a harsh systolic murmur radiating to the carotids and a delayed carotid upstroke, strongly suggest aortic stenosis. Echocardiography is the appropriate next step to confirm this diagnosis and assess its severity. Exercise stress testing is generally avoided when severe symptomatic aortic stenosis is already suspected, because exertion in that setting can provoke dangerous hemodynamic collapse.
  2. The correct answer is D (Aortic valve replacement, either surgical or transcatheter based on her risk profile): She has symptomatic severe aortic stenosis with a reduced ejection fraction, both of which are clear indications for aortic valve replacement rather than continued observation or medical therapy alone. The choice between surgical and transcatheter aortic valve replacement depends on surgical risk, valve anatomy, and patient preference, and both remain viable options for a patient at intermediate risk like her.
  3. The correct answer is A (Antiplatelet therapy, without the need for lifelong anticoagulation): Bioprosthetic valves, unlike mechanical valves, do not require lifelong anticoagulation in a patient without another specific indication such as atrial fibrillation, which she does not have. Antiplatelet therapy alone is generally sufficient for long-term management after bioprosthetic valve implantation in a patient in her situation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Urgent vascular imaging and emergent revascularization): This presentation, with pain, pallor, poikilothermia, pulselessness, paresthesias, and paralysis, together forming the classic 'six Ps,' describes acute limb ischemia, most likely embolic given his atrial fibrillation and nonadherence to anticoagulation, a true vascular emergency. Urgent imaging and emergent revascularization are required, since prolonged ischemia risks an unsalvageable limb within just a few hours.
  5. The correct answer is D (Critical limb ischemia, which requires urgent vascular evaluation for revascularization rather than exercise therapy alone): Rest pain and ulceration in a patient with established peripheral artery disease define critical limb ischemia, a considerably more severe stage than claudication that directly threatens limb viability and requires urgent evaluation for revascularization, rather than continuing the conservative, exercise-based approach that was appropriate for his prior claudication alone.
  6. The correct answer is B (NSTEMI is confirmed by the rising troponin pattern, and an early invasive strategy should be considered given his ongoing risk): The chart shows a troponin value that starts low or normal and rises meaningfully over several hours, the expected pattern for myocardial injury when the first sample is drawn early in the course of symptoms. Combined with his rest pain and lack of ST elevation, this is diagnostic of NSTEMI, and depending on his risk features, an early invasive strategy with catheterization within 24 to 72 hours should be considered. STEMI, by contrast, is defined specifically by ECG findings, not by biomarker trends alone.
  7. The correct answer is B (Vasovagal (neurocardiogenic) syncope): A clear situational trigger, here prolonged standing in a hot, crowded environment, combined with a classic prodrome of nausea, warmth, and visual graying, followed by rapid and complete recovery without confusion, is characteristic of vasovagal syncope, the most common and generally benign cause of syncope in young, otherwise healthy patients like her.
  8. The correct answer is A (Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy): A systolic murmur that increases with Valsalva or with standing, maneuvers that decrease venous return and thereby increase dynamic outflow obstruction, and decreases with squatting, which increases venous return and afterload, is characteristic of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, the opposite pattern from aortic stenosis. Combined with her family history of sudden death during exertion, this raises significant concern for this diagnosis and an associated risk of exercise-related sudden cardiac death.
  9. The correct answer is C (No further cardiac testing is needed, given his good functional capacity): A patient who can achieve at least 4 metabolic equivalents of activity, such as climbing two or more flights of stairs without cardiac symptoms as he clearly can, generally does not require further preoperative cardiac testing, regardless of his other cardiovascular risk factors, unless the planned surgery itself carries a very high inherent cardiac risk.
  10. The correct answer is D (Postpone the elective surgery and evaluate and stabilize his active cardiac condition first): Active cardiac conditions, including unstable angina as described here, decompensated heart failure, significant arrhythmia, and severe valvular disease, should be addressed before any elective surgery, even a genuinely low-risk procedure such as cataract surgery, because proceeding without first addressing an unstable cardiac condition poses an unacceptable perioperative risk.
  11. The correct answer is B (Atrial septal defect): A fixed, widely split S2 that does not vary meaningfully with respiration, along with a pulmonic flow murmur resulting from increased right-sided volume, is classic for atrial septal defect, in which the chronic left-to-right shunt causes right heart volume overload that persists independent of the normal respiratory cycle.
  12. The correct answer is D (Eisenmenger syndrome, with reversal of the shunt from left-to-right to right-to-left due to pulmonary hypertension): A chronic, uncorrected left-to-right shunt such as his large ventricular septal defect can lead to progressive pulmonary vascular remodeling and pulmonary hypertension over years. Once pulmonary pressures eventually exceed systemic pressures, the shunt reverses direction to right-to-left, producing cyanosis and clubbing, known as Eisenmenger syndrome, and the murmur often softens considerably as the pressure gradient across the defect diminishes.
  13. The correct answer is C (Acute pericarditis): Pleuritic, positional chest pain that is relieved by sitting forward, an audible pericardial friction rub, and diffuse rather than localized ST elevation with PR depression are all classic for acute pericarditis, clearly distinguishing it from the localized ST elevation pattern seen with myocardial infarction in a specific coronary territory.
  14. The correct answer is D (Cardiac tamponade, supported by an exaggerated inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure (pulsus paradoxus)): Beck triad, consisting of hypotension, jugular venous distension, and muffled heart sounds, together with pulsus paradoxus, an exaggerated inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure of more than 10 mmHg as measured here, strongly supports cardiac tamponade, a complication that can occur with a malignant pericardial effusion compressing the heart in a patient with known metastatic cancer.
  15. The correct answer is B (Constrictive pericarditis, distinguished from restrictive cardiomyopathy by pericardial thickening or calcification on imaging): Constrictive pericarditis, often a late consequence of conditions such as his prior tuberculosis, produces a thickened or calcified pericardium clearly visible on imaging, along with a prominent y descent in the jugular venous waveform and a pericardial knock, all of which help distinguish it from restrictive cardiomyopathy, a condition that instead involves the myocardium itself rather than the pericardium.
  16. The correct answer is A (Initiate a statin for primary prevention, given his intermediate to high calculated risk): Long-term cardiovascular risk calculators, which incorporate age, sex, blood pressure, cholesterol values, diabetes status, and smoking history, are used specifically to guide statin therapy in primary prevention. A calculated risk in the intermediate to high range, as seen in his case, supports starting a statin even in an asymptomatic patient who has no established cardiovascular disease.
  17. The correct answer is D (Permanent pacemaker placement, often combined with rate-controlling medication for the tachyarrhythmia episodes): This describes sick sinus syndrome, also known as tachycardia-bradycardia syndrome, in which alternating bradyarrhythmias and tachyarrhythmias make medical therapy alone genuinely difficult, since rate-controlling drugs given for the tachycardia component would predictably worsen her underlying bradycardia. A permanent pacemaker allows the safe subsequent use of rate-controlling medication specifically for her tachyarrhythmic episodes.
  18. The correct answer is B (Switch to or add intravenous loop diuretic therapy, or add a second diuretic acting at a different nephron segment): Diuretic resistance in decompensated heart failure can result from reduced oral bioavailability due to gut wall edema, as well as reduced renal blood flow limiting drug delivery to its site of action. Switching to intravenous loop diuretic dosing, or adding a second agent acting at a different part of the nephron, such as sequential nephron blockade with a thiazide-type diuretic, can help overcome this resistance and achieve more adequate decongestion.
  19. The correct answer is C (A bioprosthetic valve, which avoids the need for lifelong anticoagulation but has a shorter durability and may require reoperation sooner): Bioprosthetic valves do not require lifelong anticoagulation, making them preferable for younger patients who wish to become pregnant or who strongly wish to avoid anticoagulation, at the cost of reduced durability and a higher likelihood of eventually needing reoperation compared with a mechanical valve, which offers greater durability but requires lifelong warfarin anticoagulation instead.
  20. The correct answer is B (Pharmacologic stress testing with imaging, since the baseline ECG is not interpretable for ischemic changes): A baseline ECG abnormality such as his left bundle branch block obscures the ST segment changes needed to properly interpret an exercise ECG for ischemia. In this situation, pharmacologic (or exercise) stress testing combined with imaging, such as nuclear perfusion imaging or stress echocardiography, is preferred over a standard exercise ECG alone, regardless of his good underlying exercise capacity.
  21. The correct answer is C (Acute pulmonary embolism causing right heart strain): The S1Q3T3 pattern, combined with tachycardia, hypotension, syncope, and a clear risk factor for venous thromboembolism in the form of prolonged immobility during her long flight, strongly suggests a large pulmonary embolism causing acute right heart strain, a diagnosis that should always be actively considered whenever syncope is accompanied by these particular findings together.
  22. The correct answer is A (Colles fracture, from a fall onto an extended, outstretched hand): This patient's mechanism, a fall onto an outstretched hand, combined with dorsal displacement of the distal radial fragment producing a silver-fork deformity, is the classic description of a Colles fracture. It is the most common wrist fracture in older adults with osteoporotic bone. A Smith fracture instead results from a fall onto a flexed wrist and produces volar displacement, the mirror image of this presentation.
  23. The correct answer is B (Smith fracture): Volar displacement of the distal radial fragment after a fall onto a flexed wrist is the classic description of a Smith fracture, essentially the reverse pattern of a Colles fracture, which instead follows a fall onto an extended, outstretched hand and produces dorsal displacement.
  24. The correct answer is D (Immobilize in a thumb spica splint and repeat radiographs or obtain further imaging in 10 to 14 days): Snuffbox tenderness after a fall on an outstretched hand should raise suspicion for a scaphoid fracture even when initial radiographs are normal, since these fractures are frequently occult on first imaging. The correct approach is thumb spica immobilization with repeat radiographs or advanced imaging in ten to fourteen days, because missing this fracture risks avascular necrosis given the scaphoid's retrograde blood supply.
  25. The correct answer is A (The scaphoid's blood supply enters distally and travels retrograde, so proximal fractures more often disrupt perfusion to the proximal fragment): The scaphoid receives its dominant blood supply through vessels that enter at the distal pole and travel retrograde toward the proximal pole. A fracture through the waist or proximal pole can therefore cut off perfusion to the proximal fragment, explaining the elevated risk of avascular necrosis compared with distal pole fractures, which usually retain adequate blood flow.
  26. The correct answer is B (Urgent surgical fixation, given the risk of avascular necrosis from disrupted retrograde blood flow to the femoral head): Displaced femoral neck fractures disrupt the retrograde blood supply carried mainly by the medial femoral circumflex artery along the femoral neck, placing the femoral head at real risk of avascular necrosis. Urgent surgical fixation, often with hemiarthroplasty or total hip arthroplasty for a displaced fracture in an older patient, is required both to address this vascular risk and to allow early mobilization, which sharply reduces mortality from prolonged immobility in this population.
  27. The correct answer is C (The intertrochanteric region receives blood supply from multiple collateral vessels, unlike the femoral neck, which depends heavily on a single retrograde source): The femoral neck depends heavily on retrograde flow through vessels traveling along its surface, so a fracture there can readily disrupt perfusion to the femoral head. The intertrochanteric region, in contrast, is supplied by a richer network of collateral vessels, so avascular necrosis is far less common there even though prompt surgical fixation is still required to allow early mobilization.
  28. The correct answer is B (It is the thinnest and least mechanically supported segment of the bone): Clavicle fractures most often occur at the junction of the middle and distal thirds because this segment is the thinnest part of the bone and lacks the additional mechanical support provided by the surrounding ligamentous and muscular attachments at the medial and lateral ends. Most fractures at this location are managed conservatively with a sling given the clavicle's excellent healing capacity.
  29. The correct answer is B (Emergently reduce the fracture and reassess perfusion, proceeding to vascular exploration if pulses do not return): Supracondylar humerus fractures in children carry a well-recognized risk of brachial artery and median nerve injury because both structures run close to the fracture site. A cool, pulseless hand after this injury is a vascular emergency requiring immediate fracture reduction with reassessment of perfusion, and vascular exploration if pulses do not return, rather than delayed imaging or observation.
  30. The correct answer is A (Closed reduction with ulnar gutter splinting, since some angulation at this joint is well tolerated): This presentation, a fracture through the neck of the fifth metacarpal after a closed-fist punch, is the classic boxer's fracture. Because the fifth metacarpometacarpal joint tolerates a meaningful degree of angulation without significant functional loss, most boxer's fractures are managed with closed reduction and splinting rather than surgery, which is reserved for fractures with excessive angulation, rotation, or intra-articular extension.
  31. The correct answer is C (Obtain ankle radiographs, since he meets criteria for bone tenderness at the malleolus and inability to bear weight): The Ottawa Ankle Rules recommend radiographs when there is bone tenderness at the posterior edge or tip of either malleolus, or when the patient cannot bear weight for four steps both immediately after the injury and in the emergency department. This patient meets both criteria, tenderness at the tip of the lateral malleolus and inability to bear weight, so radiographs are indicated to evaluate for a malleolar fracture.
  32. The correct answer is B (Manage with rest, ice, compression, elevation, and early functional rehabilitation without radiographs): This patient does not meet Ottawa Ankle Rule criteria for imaging, since she has no bony tenderness at the malleoli or fifth metatarsal base and can bear weight for four steps both immediately after injury and on examination. The great majority of ankle sprains without these red flags are managed conservatively with rest, ice, compression, elevation, and early functional rehabilitation, without the need for radiographs.
  33. The correct answer is C (These findings, pain out of proportion to injury and pain with passive stretch, are early signs of compartment syndrome and should not be dismissed because pulses are still present): Pain out of proportion to the injury and pain with passive stretch of the muscles in the affected compartment are the earliest and most reliable signs of compartment syndrome. Pulses and capillary refill often remain normal until late in the process, since compartment pressure compromises capillary perfusion long before it is high enough to occlude a major artery, so normal pulses must never be used to exclude the diagnosis.
  34. The correct answer is D (Emergent fasciotomy to decompress the affected compartments): Once compartment syndrome is diagnosed, whether by clinical findings alone or supported by compartment pressure measurement showing a pressure within 30 mmHg of diastolic pressure, emergent fasciotomy is the definitive treatment. Any circumferential cast or constrictive dressing should also be released immediately while arrangements for surgery are made, since delay converts a reversible ischemic injury into permanent muscle and nerve damage.
  35. The correct answer is A (It is a late finding, and by the time it appears, irreversible muscle and nerve damage has often already occurred): Pulselessness is one of the classic late findings of compartment syndrome, alongside pallor, paresthesia, and paralysis, but relying on it for diagnosis is dangerous because by the time a pulse is lost, irreversible muscle and nerve ischemia has frequently already occurred. Pain out of proportion to injury and pain with passive stretch remain the earliest and most reliable clues, and treatment should never be delayed while waiting for pulses to disappear.
  36. The correct answer is D (Volkmann contracture, resulting from unrecognized or inadequately treated compartment syndrome of the forearm): Volkmann contracture describes the permanent flexion contracture of the forearm and hand that results when compartment syndrome of the forearm is not recognized and treated promptly. Ischemic muscle and nerve tissue is replaced by fibrous scar tissue, producing the fixed, claw-like posturing described here. It is a preventable outcome when compartment syndrome is diagnosed early and treated with emergent fasciotomy.
  37. The correct answer is D (Anterior cruciate ligament tear): A noncontact pivoting injury with an audible pop, rapid effusion, and increased anterior tibial translation on a positive Lachman test is the classic presentation of an anterior cruciate ligament tear. The rapid hemarthrosis reflects bleeding from the torn, richly vascularized ligament, and MRI is typically used to confirm the diagnosis and evaluate for concomitant meniscal injury.
  38. The correct answer is C (Meniscus tear): A twisting injury followed by joint line tenderness, mechanical catching or locking, and a positive McMurray test, pain or a palpable click with combined rotation and extension of the knee, is characteristic of a meniscus tear. Unlike an acute ligamentous tear, meniscal injuries often produce a more gradual effusion over hours rather than immediate swelling.
  39. The correct answer is C (Osteoarthritis): Activity-related joint pain that improves with rest, brief morning stiffness under thirty minutes, and bony enlargement at the distal interphalangeal joints, known as Heberden nodes, are classic features of osteoarthritis. The absence of warmth, synovial swelling, or systemic symptoms further supports a degenerative rather than an inflammatory process.
  40. The correct answer is B (Weight loss, physical therapy, and acetaminophen or topical or oral NSAIDs as needed): Initial management of osteoarthritis centers on nonpharmacologic measures, including weight loss and physical therapy, combined with acetaminophen or NSAIDs, whether topical or oral, for symptomatic relief. Because osteoarthritis is a mechanical rather than a systemic inflammatory disease, disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs have no role, and joint arthroplasty is reserved for patients with significant functional impairment despite adequate conservative treatment.
  41. The correct answer is A (Joint space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and osteophyte formation at the distal interphalangeal joints): This radiographic pattern, joint space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, and osteophyte formation concentrated at the distal interphalangeal joints, is characteristic of osteoarthritis, which corresponds clinically to the bony enlargement felt as Heberden nodes. This is distinct from the marginal erosions and periarticular osteopenia of rheumatoid arthritis or the punched-out erosions with overhanging edges seen in chronic tophaceous gout.
  42. The correct answer is A (Rheumatoid arthritis): Symmetric small joint involvement of the wrists, metacarpophalangeal, and proximal interphalangeal joints with sparing of the distal interphalangeal joints, combined with prolonged morning stiffness that improves with activity, is the classic presentation of rheumatoid arthritis. This inflammatory pattern, worse at rest and better with movement, is the opposite of the mechanical pattern seen in osteoarthritis.
  43. The correct answer is A (Interstitial lung disease and rheumatoid nodules, both recognized extra-articular manifestations of longstanding seropositive disease): Longstanding, seropositive rheumatoid arthritis is associated with several extra-articular manifestations beyond the joints, including rheumatoid nodules, interstitial lung disease, pericarditis, scleritis, and accelerated atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. This patient's combination of reticular lung opacities and subcutaneous nodules reflects two of the more common extra-articular findings in patients with this disease burden.
  44. The correct answer is D (Anti-CCP antibody is more specific for rheumatoid arthritis than rheumatoid factor): Both rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody support the diagnosis of rheumatoid arthritis, but anti-CCP antibody is more specific, since rheumatoid factor can be positive in a variety of other conditions and even in healthy individuals. Neither test is required for diagnosis, and clinical presentation combined with imaging findings remains central to making the diagnosis.
  45. The correct answer is A (Early initiation of a disease-modifying antirheumatic drug, most often methotrexate, to prevent irreversible joint damage): Early initiation of disease-modifying antirheumatic drug therapy, with methotrexate as the anchor agent, is essential in rheumatoid arthritis because delayed treatment allows ongoing synovial inflammation to cause irreversible cartilage and bone erosion. NSAIDs and corticosteroids can help control symptoms but do not halt disease progression and are not substitutes for disease-modifying therapy.
  46. The correct answer is C (Screening for latent tuberculosis and hepatitis B, since biologic therapy can reactivate latent infection): Biologic agents that inhibit tumor necrosis factor or other inflammatory pathways can reactivate latent infections, most notably tuberculosis and hepatitis B. Screening for both is required before initiating these agents, and any evidence of latent infection should be addressed, often with concurrent antimicrobial or antiviral treatment, before or during biologic therapy.
  47. The correct answer is D (Ankylosing spondylitis): Inflammatory back pain that improves with exercise and worsens with rest, occurring in a young patient with sacroiliac joint involvement, is characteristic of ankylosing spondylitis, one of the spondyloarthropathies strongly associated with HLA-B27. This pattern, improvement with movement, is the reverse of typical mechanical low back pain, which tends to worsen with activity.
  48. The correct answer is D (Dactylitis, associated with psoriatic arthritis): Dactylitis, diffuse swelling of an entire digit giving a sausage-like appearance, is a distinguishing feature of psoriatic arthritis. Combined with a history of psoriasis and nail changes such as pitting, this presentation strongly supports a diagnosis of psoriatic arthritis rather than another inflammatory arthropathy.
  49. The correct answer is B (Reactive arthritis): The triad of conjunctivitis, urethritis, and asymmetric arthritis occurring after a genitourinary or gastrointestinal infection is classic for reactive arthritis, one of the seronegative spondyloarthropathies. Unlike septic arthritis, the joint itself is sterile, since reactive arthritis is an immune-mediated response triggered by a preceding infection rather than direct joint infection.
  50. The correct answer is B (Osteoarthritis stiffness is typically brief, under thirty minutes, while rheumatoid arthritis stiffness typically lasts over an hour and improves with activity): Morning stiffness duration is a genuinely useful distinguishing feature: osteoarthritis produces brief stiffness, usually resolving within thirty minutes, that follows a mechanical pattern worsening with activity, while rheumatoid arthritis produces prolonged stiffness lasting more than an hour that improves as the patient becomes more active, reflecting its inflammatory rather than mechanical basis.
  51. The correct answer is D (Perform arthrocentesis with synovial fluid cell count, Gram stain, and culture to evaluate for septic arthritis before attributing the presentation to another cause): A hot, swollen, febrile joint should always prompt arthrocentesis with cell count, Gram stain, and culture to evaluate for septic arthritis, since this diagnosis can be rapidly destructive to the joint and mimics gout, pseudogout, or an inflammatory arthritis flare closely. Even when crystals are identified, septic arthritis must still be excluded by culture, since crystal disease and joint infection can coexist.
  52. The correct answer is A (MRI of the sacroiliac joints, which can detect bone marrow edema before changes are visible on plain film): MRI of the sacroiliac joints can detect active inflammation, seen as bone marrow edema, well before the structural changes such as erosion or ankylosis that are required to see sacroiliitis on plain radiographs. This makes MRI the preferred imaging modality for detecting early axial spondyloarthritis in a patient with a normal or equivocal plain film.
  53. The correct answer is B (Gout, presenting as podagra): Sudden, severe monoarticular pain and inflammation of the first metatarsophalangeal joint, termed podagra, in a patient with a risk factor for hyperuricemia such as thiazide diuretic use, is the classic presentation of acute gout. Recurrent self-limited episodes in the past further support this diagnosis over a first presentation of septic arthritis.
  54. The correct answer is B (A sudden change in serum urate concentration, in either direction, can destabilize existing crystal deposits and trigger an acute flare): Acute gout flares can be triggered not only by rising uric acid levels but also by a rapid fall, as occurs when urate-lowering therapy is initiated. This is why urate-lowering agents are never started or adjusted during an acute attack and are typically covered with prophylactic low-dose colchicine or an NSAID for several months after initiation to prevent this reaction.
  55. The correct answer is A (Linear calcification within the articular cartilage of the joint, known as chondrocalcinosis): Chondrocalcinosis, calcification within the articular cartilage itself visible as a thin linear density paralleling the joint surface, is a supportive radiographic finding in calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease, or pseudogout. This condition is associated with hyperparathyroidism, hemochromatosis, hypothyroidism, and hypomagnesemia, and most often affects the knee and wrist in older adults.
Case case_msk_gout

A 60-year-old man with a history of recurrent podagra and untreated hyperuricemia presents to the emergency department with an acutely swollen, hot, and exquisitely painful right knee that began abruptly overnight. He denies fever, recent trauma, or antibiotic use. Vital signs are within normal limits. Arthrocentesis of the knee is performed for diagnostic and therapeutic purposes.

  1. The correct answer is B (Gout, confirmed by needle-shaped, negatively birefringent crystals under polarized light microscopy): Definitive diagnosis of gout requires arthrocentesis demonstrating needle-shaped, negatively birefringent monosodium urate crystals under polarized light microscopy. Synovial fluid should also be sent for Gram stain and culture in every case, since septic arthritis can closely mimic an acute gout flare and the two conditions can even coexist in the same joint.
  2. The correct answer is D (NSAIDs, colchicine, or corticosteroids, chosen based on renal function and comorbidities, without starting or adjusting urate-lowering therapy): Acute gout flares are treated with NSAIDs, colchicine, or corticosteroids, with the choice guided by the patient's renal function and comorbidities. Urate-lowering therapy such as allopurinol or febuxostat should never be started or adjusted during an acute attack, since a change in serum urate concentration at this time can prolong or worsen the flare.
  3. The correct answer is D (Begin urate-lowering therapy, most often allopurinol, once the acute flare has resolved, typically covered with several months of low-dose colchicine or an NSAID): Recurrent gout attacks and the presence of tophi are indications for chronic urate-lowering therapy, most often with allopurinol, a xanthine oxidase inhibitor. This should be started only after an acute flare has fully resolved, and low-dose colchicine or an NSAID is typically continued for several months afterward to prevent a flare triggered by the change in serum urate levels that urate-lowering therapy itself causes.
  4. The correct answer is D (Hemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism, hypothyroidism, and hypomagnesemia): Calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease in a younger patient should prompt evaluation for an underlying metabolic disorder, since the condition is associated with hemochromatosis, hyperparathyroidism, hypothyroidism, and hypomagnesemia. Identifying and treating one of these conditions may not reverse existing crystal deposits but is important for the patient's broader management.
  5. The correct answer is C (Febuxostat carries a boxed warning for increased cardiovascular mortality in patients with established cardiovascular disease): Febuxostat is an alternative xanthine oxidase inhibitor for patients who cannot tolerate allopurinol, but it carries a boxed warning for increased cardiovascular mortality in patients with pre-existing cardiovascular disease. This risk should be weighed carefully and discussed with the patient, particularly given his history of myocardial infarction, before starting therapy.
  6. The correct answer is C (Serum uric acid is an unreliable marker during an acute attack and can be normal or even fall during a flare): Serum uric acid level is an unreliable marker of an acute gout flare, since it can be normal or can even decrease during an attack due to urate deposition into inflamed tissue and cytokine-mediated renal excretion changes. Diagnosis of an acute flare should rely on the clinical presentation and, when needed, synovial fluid crystal analysis rather than the serum uric acid level alone.
  7. The correct answer is C (Acute attacks are treated similarly to gout with NSAIDs, colchicine, or corticosteroids, but there is no equivalent long-term crystal-lowering therapy for this condition): Acute attacks of calcium pyrophosphate deposition disease are managed similarly to gout, with NSAIDs, colchicine, or intra-articular corticosteroids. However, unlike gout, there is no established long-term therapy analogous to urate-lowering treatment, since calcium pyrophosphate deposition is not driven by a single reversible metabolic pathway in the same way hyperuricemia drives gout.
  8. The correct answer is A (Gout crystals are needle-shaped and negatively birefringent, while pseudogout crystals are rhomboid and positively birefringent): The key distinguishing feature between these two crystal arthropathies is that monosodium urate crystals in gout are needle-shaped and negatively birefringent, appearing yellow when parallel to the axis of the red compensator, while calcium pyrophosphate crystals in pseudogout are rhomboid-shaped and positively birefringent, appearing blue in the same orientation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry screening is recommended starting at age 65 for all women): Dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry screening for osteoporosis is recommended for all women beginning at age sixty-five, regardless of the presence of additional risk factors, since age itself is the dominant risk factor for postmenopausal bone loss. Younger postmenopausal women or men with significant risk factors may warrant earlier screening.
  10. The correct answer is A (This result meets the World Health Organization criteria for osteoporosis, since it is at or below negative 2.5): The World Health Organization defines osteoporosis as a T-score of negative 2.5 or lower at the hip or spine. A T-score of negative 2.8 therefore meets diagnostic criteria for osteoporosis, distinct from osteopenia, which is defined by a T-score between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5.
  11. The correct answer is D (Osteopenia, since the T-score falls between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5): A T-score between negative 1.0 and negative 2.5 is classified as osteopenia, a category of reduced bone density that is not yet severe enough to meet the World Health Organization threshold for osteoporosis, which requires a T-score of negative 2.5 or lower. Osteopenia still warrants attention to modifiable risk factors and calcium and vitamin D intake.
  12. The correct answer is D (Take the medication on an empty stomach with a full glass of water and remain upright for at least thirty minutes afterward): Oral bisphosphonates such as alendronate must be taken on an empty stomach with a full glass of water, and the patient should remain upright, sitting or standing, for at least thirty minutes afterward. This regimen reduces the risk of esophageal irritation and erosive esophagitis, a known complication of these medications when taken improperly.
  13. The correct answer is B (Long-term use carries a small but real risk of atypical femoral fractures and osteonecrosis of the jaw): While bisphosphonates reduce fracture risk overall, long-term use carries a small but real risk of atypical femoral fractures and osteonecrosis of the jaw. Many patients are considered for a drug holiday after several years of therapy, with the decision individualized based on ongoing fracture risk, though calcium and vitamin D intake should continue regardless.
  14. The correct answer is C (Abruptly stopping denosumab can cause a rebound increase in fracture risk and typically requires transition to another antiresorptive agent): Denosumab is a monoclonal antibody against RANK ligand that reduces osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and is often used in patients who cannot tolerate bisphosphonates or who have significant renal impairment. However, abrupt discontinuation can cause a rebound increase in bone turnover and fracture risk, so transition to another antiresorptive agent is typically recommended if denosumab is stopped.
  15. The correct answer is B (The combination of a cancer history and unexplained weight loss alone): Red flag features for low back pain that warrant further evaluation, often with imaging, include age over fifty with new onset pain, a history of cancer, unexplained weight loss, fever, intravenous drug use, prolonged corticosteroid use, significant trauma, and progressive neurologic deficit. This patient's combination of a cancer history and unexplained weight loss, together with new onset pain after age fifty, raises concern for a possible metastatic or other serious underlying cause even without leg radiation or neurologic deficit.
Case case_msk_backpain

A 47-year-old man with a history of chronic low back pain presents with two days of rapidly worsening back pain, new numbness in the perianal region, and difficulty initiating urination. He denies fever or recent trauma. Examination reveals decreased sensation in a saddle distribution and mild bilateral lower extremity weakness with reduced deep tendon reflexes at the ankles.

  1. The correct answer is C (Cauda equina syndrome): Saddle anesthesia, bilateral leg weakness, and urinary retention with overflow incontinence in the setting of low back pain are the classic findings of cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency caused by compression of the lumbosacral nerve roots. Prompt recognition is critical because delayed treatment risks permanent neurologic deficit.
  2. The correct answer is A (Emergent MRI of the lumbosacral spine): Suspected cauda equina syndrome requires emergent MRI of the lumbosacral spine to identify the level and cause of compression, most often a large central disc herniation, so that surgical decompression can proceed without delay. Any delay in imaging or treatment increases the risk of permanent bowel, bladder, and lower extremity neurologic dysfunction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Emergent surgical decompression): Confirmed cauda equina syndrome from significant nerve root compression requires emergent surgical decompression, since the window for reversing neurologic injury is time-sensitive. Conservative management, injections, or delayed elective referral are not appropriate once this diagnosis is established, given the risk of permanent bowel, bladder, and motor or sensory deficits.
  4. The correct answer is C (A positive straight leg raise test, supporting radicular pain most commonly from a herniated disc at the L4-L5 or L5-S1 level): A straight leg raise test that reproduces radicular pain radiating below the knee when the leg is elevated between thirty and seventy degrees is considered positive and supports lumbar nerve root compression, most commonly from a herniated disc at the L4-L5 or L5-S1 level. This finding helps distinguish radicular sciatica from nonspecific mechanical low back pain.
  5. The correct answer is A (Weakness of great toe and foot dorsiflexion): Weakness of great toe and foot dorsiflexion is characteristic of an L5 radiculopathy, while a diminished or absent ankle reflex is more characteristic of an S1 radiculopathy, since the ankle reflex is mediated primarily through the S1 nerve root. Recognizing these distinct motor and reflex patterns helps localize the level of nerve root compression on examination.
  6. The correct answer is D (Cellulitis): Poorly demarcated erythema, warmth, tenderness, and edema, often with an identifiable portal of entry such as tinea pedis, are characteristic of cellulitis, a bacterial infection of the dermis and subcutaneous tissue most often caused by Streptococcus pyogenes or Staphylococcus aureus. The absence of systemic toxicity or findings such as bullae or crepitus makes necrotizing fasciitis unlikely at this stage.
  7. The correct answer is C (An oral antibiotic targeting streptococcal species, without empiric MRSA coverage in the absence of purulence or specific risk factors): Uncomplicated cellulitis without purulent drainage or abscess is most often streptococcal in origin, so an oral antibiotic targeting streptococci is appropriate first-line therapy. Coverage for methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus is added when specific risk factors are present, such as a history of MRSA infection, purulent drainage, or an abscess, none of which are present in this patient.
  8. The correct answer is D (A well-demarcated, erythematous plaque with a thick, silvery scale over the extensor surfaces): A well-demarcated, erythematous plaque with a thick, silvery scale distributed over extensor surfaces such as the elbows and knees is the classic description of a psoriatic plaque. Psoriasis is a chronic, immune-mediated inflammatory skin disease, and this distribution pattern, along with possible nail pitting or onycholysis, helps distinguish it from other papulosquamous conditions.
  9. The correct answer is D (The Auspitz sign, reflecting the thinned epidermis over elongated dermal papillae): The Auspitz sign, pinpoint bleeding when a psoriatic scale is removed, reflects the thinned suprapapillary epidermis overlying elongated, tortuous dermal papillae characteristic of psoriatic plaques. It is a classic, though not always elicited, clinical finding that supports the diagnosis of psoriasis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Nail pitting and onycholysis are common in psoriasis and can precede the development of psoriatic arthritis): Nail changes including pitting, onycholysis, and oil-drop discoloration are common in psoriasis and can precede the development of joint involvement, since a meaningful proportion of patients with psoriasis eventually develop psoriatic arthritis. Recognizing nail involvement can therefore prompt closer monitoring for emerging joint symptoms such as dactylitis or enthesitis.
  11. The correct answer is D (Atopic dermatitis): A chronic, relapsing, intensely pruritic rash favoring the flexural creases in an older child, occurring in the setting of a personal or family history of asthma or allergic rhinitis, the atopic triad, is characteristic of atopic dermatitis. In infants the distribution is typically different, favoring the face and extensor surfaces rather than the flexural creases.
  12. The correct answer is D (Erysipelas, given its characteristically sharp, raised border, classically caused by Streptococcus pyogenes): Erysipelas is a superficial skin infection, classically caused by Streptococcus pyogenes, distinguished from typical cellulitis by its sharply demarcated, raised borders that make it visually distinct from the poorly defined edges of ordinary cellulitis. Recognizing this border pattern helps differentiate the two related but distinct infections on physical examination alone.
  13. The correct answer is A (Emergent surgical consultation for possible necrotizing fasciitis, since debridement is both diagnostic and therapeutic): Pain out of proportion to visible findings, rapid progression, bullae, skin necrosis, and crepitus from gas-forming organisms, along with systemic toxicity, are warning signs of necrotizing fasciitis. Any of these findings in a patient who otherwise appears to have simple cellulitis should prompt emergent surgical consultation, since surgical debridement is both diagnostic and therapeutic, and delay dramatically increases mortality.
  14. The correct answer is B (Secondary bacterial infection, most often with Staphylococcus aureus, given the impaired skin barrier and frequent excoriation): Honey-colored crusting and pustules developing within eczematous plaques suggest secondary bacterial infection, most often with Staphylococcus aureus, which occurs commonly in atopic dermatitis given the impaired skin barrier and frequent scratching and excoriation. This finding often warrants a course of antibiotic therapy in addition to continued management of the underlying atopic dermatitis.
  15. The correct answer is A (Incision and drainage of the fluctuant, purulent collection): A fluctuant, purulent collection is consistent with a cutaneous abscess, and incision and drainage is the essential component of management, since antibiotics alone are often insufficient to resolve a walled-off purulent collection. Antibiotics may be added in addition to drainage depending on the size of the abscess, surrounding cellulitis, and patient risk factors, but drainage itself should not be omitted.
Case case_msk_cellulitis

A 56-year-old man with poorly controlled diabetes presents with one day of warmth, tenderness, and redness over his lower leg following a minor abrasion sustained while gardening. He is afebrile and appears nontoxic on initial evaluation, and oral antibiotics are started for presumed cellulitis. He returns to the emergency department twelve hours later with worsening symptoms.

  1. The correct answer is A (Cellulitis): This presentation, poorly demarcated erythema, warmth, and tenderness following a portal of entry such as a minor abrasion, without bullae, crepitus, or pain out of proportion to examination findings, is consistent with uncomplicated cellulitis. Diabetes is a recognized risk factor for cellulitis and warrants close monitoring for progression given impaired wound healing and immune response.
  2. The correct answer is A (Emergent surgical consultation for debridement, since this presentation is now concerning for necrotizing fasciitis): The development of pain out of proportion to examination findings, hemorrhagic bullae, crepitus, and systemic toxicity with hypotension and tachycardia represents progression to a presentation concerning for necrotizing fasciitis. This requires emergent surgical consultation, since surgical debridement is both diagnostic and therapeutic and delay significantly increases mortality in this condition.
  3. The correct answer is D (Aggressive surgical debridement of all necrotic tissue): The intraoperative findings of necrotic fascia extending beyond the visible skin changes confirm necrotizing fasciitis. Definitive treatment is aggressive surgical debridement of all necrotic tissue, often requiring multiple return trips to the operating room, with broad-spectrum antibiotics serving as an important adjunct rather than a substitute for surgery, since antibiotics alone cannot penetrate poorly perfused necrotic tissue.
  4. The correct answer is B (Basal cell carcinoma): A pearly, translucent papule with rolled, telangiectatic borders, sometimes with central ulceration, arising in a sun-exposed area is the classic description of basal cell carcinoma, the most common skin cancer. It typically grows slowly and rarely metastasizes, though it can cause significant local tissue destruction if left untreated.
  5. The correct answer is C (Squamous cell carcinoma, arising from an actinic keratosis): Squamous cell carcinoma often develops from a precursor lesion called actinic keratosis, a rough, scaly, erythematous patch on chronically sun-exposed skin. Progression to a firm, hyperkeratotic, ulcerated plaque, as described here, is consistent with this evolution, and squamous cell carcinoma carries a real, though still modest, risk of metastasis compared with basal cell carcinoma.
  6. The correct answer is D (This lesion carries a meaningfully increased risk of metastasis compared with squamous cell carcinomas on the trunk): Squamous cell carcinoma carries a real, though still modest, overall risk of metastasis, and this risk is meaningfully higher for lesions located on the lip or ear, or in immunosuppressed patients, compared with lesions on the trunk or extremities. These higher-risk locations warrant closer post-treatment surveillance for recurrence or nodal spread.
  7. The correct answer is A (A pearly, translucent papule with a rolled, telangiectatic border and no overlying scale): A pearly, translucent papule with a rolled border and visible telangiectasias, without the scale typical of squamous cell carcinoma or the asymmetry and color variation of melanoma, is the classic appearance of basal cell carcinoma. Surgical excision is the standard treatment, and Mohs micrographic surgery is often preferred for lesions in cosmetically sensitive areas such as the face.
  8. The correct answer is C (27 percent): The rule of nines assigns eighteen percent to the anterior trunk and nine percent to each arm. Adding the anterior trunk, eighteen percent, to one full arm, nine percent, gives a total of twenty-seven percent total body surface area affected, a figure used to guide fluid resuscitation calculations such as the Parkland formula.
  9. The correct answer is C (Deep partial-thickness burn, extending into the reticular dermis with destruction of many nerve endings): A pale, mottled appearance with decreased sensation reflects a deep partial-thickness burn, which extends further into the reticular dermis than a superficial partial-thickness burn and destroys more nerve endings, explaining the reduced sensation. These burns are more likely than superficial partial-thickness burns to require surgical grafting for optimal healing.
  10. The correct answer is B (Full-thickness burn, characteristically painless because nerve endings have been destroyed): A leathery, dry, white or charred appearance with complete absence of sensation is characteristic of a full-thickness burn, since the entire dermis, including its nerve endings, has been destroyed. These burns always require surgical debridement and grafting and will not heal adequately with conservative wound care alone.
  11.
