The correct answer is B (Melanoma, given features consistent with the ABCDE criteria): Asymmetry, an irregular border, color variation within a single lesion, a diameter greater than six millimeters, and evolution or change over time together make up the ABCDE criteria for melanoma. This lesion demonstrates all five features, and any lesion meeting these criteria, or showing rapid change, bleeding, or a distinctly different appearance from a patient's other nevi, warrants biopsy.
Case case_msk_melanoma

A 50-year-old man is referred to dermatology after his spouse noticed a changing pigmented lesion on his back meeting several ABCDE criteria for melanoma, including recent growth, an irregular border, and color variation. A full-thickness excisional biopsy of the lesion is performed.

  1. The correct answer is A (Full-thickness excisional biopsy of the entire lesion): When melanoma is suspected based on ABCDE criteria, full-thickness excisional biopsy is the appropriate next step, since accurate measurement of Breslow depth, the single most important prognostic factor, requires capturing the entire thickness of the lesion. A shave biopsy risks transecting the base of the lesion, understaging the tumor and altering subsequent management inappropriately.
  2. The correct answer is B (Wide local excision with sentinel lymph node biopsy): For melanomas of intermediate thickness, generally greater than 0.8 to 1 millimeter, wide local excision with sentinel lymph node biopsy is the standard next step, since regional nodal status has important prognostic and treatment implications. Thinner, lower-risk lesions may not require sentinel node biopsy, but this patient's depth places him in a category where nodal staging is indicated.
  3. The correct answer is B (Breslow depth, the tumor's measured thickness, correlates with metastatic risk and overall survival): Breslow depth, the measured thickness of the melanoma from the granular layer of the epidermis to the deepest point of tumor invasion, is the single most important prognostic factor in melanoma, correlating directly with the risk of metastasis and overall survival. This is precisely why accurate depth measurement through full-thickness excisional biopsy, rather than a shave biopsy, is so important at the time of initial diagnosis.
  4. The correct answer is B (Early airway assessment with a low threshold for intubation, since inhalation injury can cause airway compromise from edema developing over the following hours): Singed nasal hair, facial burns, carbonaceous sputum, and a history of confinement in an enclosed burning space should raise suspicion for inhalation injury, which can cause progressive airway compromise from edema developing over several hours even when the patient initially appears to be speaking normally. Early airway assessment and a low threshold for intubation are essential, since waiting for overt respiratory distress risks losing the ability to secure the airway safely.
  5. The correct answer is D (11,200 mL total, with half given over the first 8 hours and the remainder over the following 16 hours): The Parkland formula calculates total fluid over the first twenty-four hours as 4 mL times body weight in kilograms times percent total body surface area burned, which here is 4 times 70 times 40, equal to 11,200 mL. Half of this volume, 5,600 mL, is given in the first eight hours from the time of injury, with the remaining 5,600 mL given over the following sixteen hours, rather than as a constant rate across the full 24 hours.
  6. The correct answer is A (Sertraline): This patient meets criteria for major depressive disorder, with depressed mood, hypersomnia, hyperphagia, and anhedonia present for more than 2 weeks and causing functional impairment. SSRIs such as sertraline are first-line pharmacotherapy for moderate depression. Alprazolam treats anxiety, not depression, and carries dependence risk. Haloperidol is an antipsychotic with no role here. Lithium is a mood stabilizer used for bipolar disorder, not unipolar MDD.
  7. The correct answer is D (Serotonin syndrome): The combination of two serotonergic agents (an SSRI and tramadol), along with autonomic instability, altered mental status, and neuromuscular findings such as clonus and hyperreflexia worse in the lower extremities, is classic for serotonin syndrome. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is associated with antipsychotic use and produces lead-pipe rigidity rather than clonus and hyperreflexia. Malignant hyperthermia occurs with inhaled anesthetics or succinylcholine. Anticholinergic toxicity causes dry skin and mydriasis rather than diaphoresis and clonus.
  8. The correct answer is A (Quetiapine): This presentation meets criteria for a manic episode (elevated/irritable mood for at least 1 week with grandiosity, decreased sleep need, and impulsivity causing marked impairment) in a patient with known bipolar disorder. Atypical antipsychotics such as quetiapine are effective for acute mania. Fluoxetine monotherapy is contraindicated because an antidepressant without a mood stabilizer can precipitate or worsen mania. Buspirone treats generalized anxiety, not mania. Psychotherapy alone is inadequate for an acute manic episode requiring pharmacologic control.
  9. The correct answer is C (NSAID-induced reduction in renal lithium clearance): NSAIDs reduce renal prostaglandin synthesis, which decreases renal blood flow and glomerular filtration, thereby reducing lithium clearance and raising serum lithium to toxic levels. This produces the classic toxicity triad of coarse tremor, ataxia, and confusion. Lithium can cause nephrogenic diabetes insipidus over time, but that would not explain an acute toxic level after starting an NSAID. Lithium is renally cleared, not hepatically metabolized, so hepatic enzyme induction is irrelevant. Lithium has negligible plasma protein binding, so displacement is not a mechanism.
  10. The correct answer is A (Generalized anxiety disorder): Excessive, difficult-to-control worry about multiple life domains for at least 6 months with associated restlessness, fatigue, muscle tension, and sleep disturbance defines GAD; this patient meets that 6-month threshold with the core GAD symptom cluster. Panic disorder requires recurrent unexpected panic attacks, which are absent here. Social anxiety disorder requires fear specific to social or performance situations, not generalized worry. Adjustment disorder requires a clear identifiable stressor within the preceding 3 months, which is not described.
  11. The correct answer is B (Cognitive behavioral therapy and an SSRI): This vignette describes panic disorder, recurrent unexpected panic attacks with at least 1 month of anticipatory anxiety and avoidance behavior. First-line treatment is CBT combined with an SSRI or SNRI. Long-term daily benzodiazepine use is not first-line because of dependence and sedation risk, though a benzodiazepine may be used briefly for acute symptom relief. Haloperidol has no role in panic disorder, which is not a psychotic illness. Propranolol can help situational physical symptoms of anxiety but is not first-line monotherapy for panic disorder.
  12. The correct answer is C (Exposure and response prevention with an SSRI): This presentation of intrusive obsessions paired with time-consuming compulsions performed to reduce anxiety is classic obsessive-compulsive disorder. First-line treatment is cognitive behavioral therapy with exposure and response prevention, combined with an SSRI, often at higher doses than used for depression. Supportive psychotherapy alone is insufficiently targeted. Antipsychotics are not first-line for OCD. Benzodiazepines do not address the underlying compulsive cycle and are not first-line therapy.
  13. The correct answer is B (Posttraumatic stress disorder): Exposure to a traumatic event followed by intrusion symptoms, avoidance, negative alterations in mood or cognition, and hyperarousal persisting beyond 1 month defines PTSD; this patient's symptoms have been present for 8 months. Acute stress disorder is diagnosed when the identical symptom picture lasts between 3 days and 1 month, which has been exceeded here. Adjustment disorder does not require the specific intrusion and hyperarousal symptom clusters tied to a life-threatening trauma. Generalized anxiety disorder does not require an inciting traumatic event or the intrusion/avoidance symptom pattern described.
  14. The correct answer is A (Social anxiety disorder): Marked, persistent fear of social or performance situations in which the person may be scrutinized or embarrassed, present for at least 6 months and causing functional impairment, defines social anxiety disorder. Agoraphobia centers on fear that escape would be difficult if panic-like symptoms occurred, not fear of judgment. Avoidant personality disorder is a pervasive, enduring pattern across the entire personality rather than a discrete anxiety disorder limited to social/performance contexts, though the two can be distinguished mainly by pervasiveness and are sometimes comorbid. Specific phobia involves fear of a single discrete object or situation, not the broad range of social contexts described here.
  15. The correct answer is D (Schizophreniform disorder): Delusions, disorganized speech, and negative symptoms (flat affect) present for 4 months meet the core symptom criteria for schizophrenia, but the diagnosis of schizophrenia requires continuous signs of disturbance for at least 6 months; between 1 and 6 months, the correct diagnosis is schizophreniform disorder. Brief psychotic disorder requires resolution within 1 month, which has already been exceeded. Schizophrenia requires 6 months of continuous disturbance, not yet met at 4 months. Schizoaffective disorder requires a concurrent major mood episode, which is not described.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Tardive dyskinesia): Repetitive, involuntary orofacial movements developing after months to years of antipsychotic exposure, particularly with first-generation agents such as haloperidol, is characteristic of tardive dyskinesia, which may be irreversible even after the offending drug is stopped. Acute dystonia occurs within hours to days of starting or increasing an antipsychotic and presents as sustained muscle contraction, not repetitive orofacial movements. Akathisia is subjective motor restlessness occurring within days to weeks, not involuntary orofacial movements. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome is an acute emergency with fever and rigidity, not a chronic movement disorder.
  2. The correct answer is C (Discontinue haloperidol and provide supportive cooling with dantrolene): The fever, lead-pipe rigidity, autonomic instability, and markedly elevated creatine kinase describe neuroleptic malignant syndrome, an emergency requiring immediate discontinuation of the antipsychotic along with supportive cooling, hydration, and dantrolene or bromocriptine in severe cases. Continuing haloperidol would worsen the syndrome. Switching to clozapine during an acute NMS episode is inappropriate and dangerous. Diphenhydramine treats acute dystonia, not NMS, and observation alone is inadequate given the life-threatening presentation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Initiate clozapine with regular absolute neutrophil count monitoring): Clozapine is indicated for treatment-resistant schizophrenia after failure of at least two adequate antipsychotic trials, and its use requires mandatory regular monitoring of absolute neutrophil count because of the risk of agranulocytosis. Exceeding the maximum recommended dose of a failed agent is unlikely to help and increases side-effect risk. Adding a second first-generation antipsychotic without addressing treatment resistance is not standard practice. Discontinuing all antipsychotics in a patient with ongoing hallucinations would risk clinical deterioration; psychotherapy alone is not sufficient for treatment-resistant schizophrenia.
  4. The correct answer is B (Dialectical behavior therapy): The pattern of unstable relationships, identity disturbance, chronic emptiness, and recurrent self-harm in the context of fear of abandonment describes borderline personality disorder, for which dialectical behavior therapy, combining individual and group skills training in distress tolerance and emotion regulation, has the strongest evidence base. Clozapine is used for treatment-resistant schizophrenia, not personality disorder. Exposure and response prevention is the treatment for OCD, not borderline personality disorder. Lithium monotherapy is used for bipolar disorder and is not the primary treatment for this personality disorder.
  5. The correct answer is D (Schizoid personality disorder): A pervasive pattern of detachment from social relationships, restricted emotional expression, preference for solitary activities, and indifference to others' opinions, without associated distress, defines schizoid personality disorder. Schizotypal personality disorder additionally features cognitive or perceptual distortions and eccentric behavior, which are not described here. Avoidant personality disorder involves social inhibition driven by fear of criticism and feelings of inadequacy, whereas this patient is content with isolation rather than fearful of it. Social anxiety disorder involves fear of scrutiny in specific situations, not a pervasive personality-wide pattern of detachment.
  6. The correct answer is B (Symptom-triggered benzodiazepine dosing and thiamine): This patient has early alcohol withdrawal (autonomic hyperactivity, tremor, anxiety within 6-24 hours of the last drink). Benzodiazepines using a symptom-triggered protocol are first-line, and thiamine should be given, ideally before glucose, to prevent precipitating Wernicke encephalopathy in a chronically alcohol-dependent, likely thiamine-deficient patient. Naloxone treats opioid overdose, not alcohol withdrawal. Disulfiram is used for relapse prevention after detoxification, not during active withdrawal. Haloperidol alone does not adequately treat the autonomic instability and seizure risk of alcohol withdrawal and is not first-line.
  7. The correct answer is B (Delirium tremens): Confusion, disorientation, hallucinations, and severe autonomic instability occurring 48-96 hours after the last drink is the classic presentation of delirium tremens, the most severe form of alcohol withdrawal, which carries significant mortality if untreated and requires aggressive benzodiazepine dosing and supportive care. Alcoholic hallucinosis presents with hallucinations but a clear sensorium, without the global confusion and severe autonomic instability described here. Wernicke encephalopathy presents with the triad of confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia from thiamine deficiency, not primarily with hallucinations and this degree of autonomic instability. Simple alcohol withdrawal does not include confusion or hallucinations.
  8. The correct answer is B (Naloxone precipitated acute opioid withdrawal): In an opioid-dependent patient, naloxone rapidly displaces opioids from receptors, which reverses the overdose but can precipitate acute opioid withdrawal, producing rhinorrhea, lacrimation, piloerection, myalgias, diarrhea, and agitation. Naloxone is an opioid antagonist and does not cause serotonin syndrome. Recurrence of the original overdose would present with recurrent respiratory depression and pinpoint pupils, not an agitated withdrawal picture. Nothing in the vignette suggests concurrent alcohol use or a withdrawal timeline consistent with alcohol.
  9. The correct answer is C (Gradual taper over weeks to months): Long-term benzodiazepine use can produce a withdrawal syndrome resembling alcohol withdrawal, including anxiety, tremor, autonomic instability, and seizure risk, so discontinuation should always be done through a gradual taper, sometimes with substitution of a longer-acting benzodiazepine, rather than abruptly. Abrupt discontinuation is unsafe and can precipitate seizures. Disulfiram is used for alcohol use disorder, not benzodiazepine discontinuation, and has no role here. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no role in benzodiazepine withdrawal.
  10. The correct answer is C (Supportive care and monitoring): Stimulant withdrawal presents with dysphoria, fatigue, increased appetite, and vivid unpleasant dreams, which is generally managed supportively without a specific pharmacologic antidote, and this patient's vital signs and symptoms do not suggest a competing emergency. Naloxone is used for opioid overdose, which is not described. A benzodiazepine taper is used for benzodiazepine or alcohol withdrawal, not stimulant withdrawal. Disulfiram is used in alcohol use disorder and is irrelevant here.
  11. The correct answer is A (Acute stress disorder): Intrusion, avoidance, negative mood, and hyperarousal symptoms following trauma, present for less than 1 month (here, 2 weeks), meet criteria for acute stress disorder. If these same symptoms persist beyond 1 month, the diagnosis would then become PTSD. Adjustment disorder does not require the specific traumatic exposure and symptom cluster described. Generalized anxiety disorder requires 6 months of diffuse worry, not an acute trauma-linked presentation.
  12. The correct answer is B (Slow, gradual caloric advancement with close electrolyte monitoring): This patient has severe anorexia nervosa with bradycardia and hypotension reflecting a hypometabolic starved state. Refeeding must be slow and gradual with proactive monitoring and repletion of phosphate, potassium, and magnesium to prevent refeeding syndrome, in which rapid caloric reintroduction triggers an insulin surge that drives electrolytes intracellularly and can cause cardiac and respiratory failure. Immediate aggressive high-calorie refeeding is dangerous and is the classic precipitant of refeeding syndrome. A stimulant appetite suppressant would worsen her condition. Bariatric surgery is irrelevant in a severely underweight patient.
  13. The correct answer is B (Bulimia nervosa): Recurrent binge eating with compensatory self-induced vomiting at least weekly for 3 months, in a patient of normal body weight, with the classic findings of parotid swelling, dental erosion, knuckle calluses (Russell sign), and hypokalemic hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis from vomiting, defines bulimia nervosa. Anorexia nervosa requires a significantly low body weight, which is absent here. Binge-eating disorder lacks the compensatory purging behavior and its physical stigmata. Illness anxiety disorder involves preoccupation with having a serious illness, not an eating and purging pattern.
  14. The correct answer is A (Somatic symptom disorder): Distressing somatic symptoms accompanied by excessive, disproportionate thoughts, anxiety, and time devoted to the symptoms for more than 6 months defines somatic symptom disorder; the symptoms need not be medically unexplained for this diagnosis. Malingering requires conscious fabrication for a clear external incentive such as disability payments, which is not described. Factitious disorder involves conscious falsification of symptoms to assume the sick role, not genuine symptom experience with excessive worry. Conversion disorder specifically involves neurological symptoms incompatible with recognized neurological disease, not the diffuse somatic complaints described here.
  15. The correct answer is D (Conversion disorder): Neurological symptoms, in this case weakness, that are incompatible with recognized neurological disease (a nonanatomic, give-way pattern that resolves with distraction) and that arise following a psychological stressor define conversion disorder (functional neurological symptom disorder). Malingering requires a clear external incentive, which is not described. Factitious disorder requires conscious symptom falsification to assume the sick role, which is not demonstrated here. Somatic symptom disorder involves excessive worry about somatic symptoms rather than a specific neurological deficit inconsistent with disease.
  16. The correct answer is C (Identify and treat the underlying medical cause): Acute onset, fluctuating course, and impaired attention describe delirium, which is most often caused by an underlying medical condition (in this case, possibly worsening infection, hypoxia, or a metabolic derangement) or medication effect. The first step is always to identify and treat the underlying cause rather than reaching for symptomatic medication. Haloperidol may occasionally be used briefly for severe agitation that poses safety risk, but it is not the initial step and does not address the cause. Dementia has an insidious, progressive onset without the acute fluctuation described, so donepezil is not indicated. Transfer to inpatient psychiatry is inappropriate for a medically caused delirium, which should be managed on the medical service.
  17. The correct answer is D (Lorazepam): Marked psychomotor disturbance with immobility, mutism, and waxy flexibility (maintaining passively placed postures) describes catatonia. A benzodiazepine such as lorazepam is first-line treatment and is also diagnostic when it produces rapid improvement. Haloperidol can worsen catatonia and increase the risk of neuroleptic malignant syndrome in a catatonic patient. Electroconvulsive therapy is reserved for benzodiazepine-refractory catatonia, not first-line. Fluoxetine alone would not address the acute catatonic state.
  18. The correct answer is D (Warn the identified coworker and notify appropriate authorities): A credible, specific threat toward an identifiable person creates a duty to warn or protect that person and to notify appropriate authorities, which overrides usual confidentiality protections under the Tarasoff principle. Maintaining strict confidentiality would leave an identified potential victim unprotected. Referral to a support group with routine follow-up does not address the imminent safety risk. Waiting until the next scheduled session is unsafe given the specificity and immediacy of the threat.
  19. The correct answer is C (Electroconvulsive therapy): ECT is indicated for severe or treatment-resistant depression, depression with psychotic features, and situations requiring rapid response such as a severely malnourished, medically compromised patient who is not eating due to delusions. It has a rapid onset of action compared to further medication trials. Adding a third antidepressant after two adequate failed trials with psychotic features is less effective and slower than ECT in this urgent scenario. Exceeding recommended antipsychotic dosing increases side-effect risk without proven added benefit. Discharge home is inappropriate given the medical urgency of ongoing food refusal and weight loss.
  20. The correct answer is B (Antisocial personality disorder): A pervasive pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others, beginning with conduct disorder before age 15 (here, animal cruelty, truancy, and fire-setting at age 12) and continuing into adulthood with lying, theft, and assault without remorse, defines antisocial personality disorder, which requires the individual to be at least 18 years old for the diagnosis. Conduct disorder is the childhood/adolescent precursor diagnosis and does not apply to this 26-year-old adult. Narcissistic personality disorder centers on grandiosity and need for admiration rather than rights violations and criminal behavior. Borderline personality disorder centers on unstable relationships and self-image rather than the persistent antisocial conduct described.
  21. The correct answer is B (Obsessive-compulsive personality disorder): A pervasive preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism, and control that the individual experiences as appropriate (ego-syntonic) rather than distressing, without true obsessions or compulsions, defines obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. Obsessive-compulsive disorder requires genuine intrusive obsessions and compulsions performed to relieve anxiety, which are explicitly denied here. Generalized anxiety disorder involves diffuse worry rather than a personality-wide pattern of perfectionism and rigidity. Avoidant personality disorder centers on fear of criticism and social inhibition, not perfectionism and control.
Case BHP-C1

A 19-year-old woman is brought to student health services by her roommate, who reports increasing concern over the past 2 months. The patient has lost 25 lb, now weighs 92 lb at a height of 5'5" (BMI 15.3), and restricts her intake to under 600 calories daily. She runs 10 miles every day regardless of weather or injury and insists she is "still too big." She has not menstruated in 4 months. Vital signs show a heart rate of 42/min, blood pressure of 82/54 mm Hg, and temperature of 35.6C.

  1. The correct answer is C (Anorexia nervosa, restricting type): Restriction of energy intake leading to a significantly low body weight (BMI 15.3), an intense fear of weight gain despite being underweight, and disturbance in body image, without binge eating or purging behavior, defines anorexia nervosa, restricting type. Bulimia nervosa requires binge eating and compensatory behavior with typically normal body weight, neither of which is described. Binge-eating disorder involves binge episodes without the severe caloric restriction and low body weight seen here. While depression can cause weight loss, the specific fear of weight gain, body image disturbance, and degree of restriction point specifically to anorexia nervosa.
  2. The correct answer is A (Bradycardia and hypotension): Severe caloric restriction produces a hypometabolic state with reduced cardiac workload, causing bradycardia and hypotension, both present in this patient. Parotid gland enlargement, knuckle calluses (Russell sign), and dental enamel erosion are physical stigmata of recurrent self-induced vomiting seen in bulimia nervosa or the purging subtype of anorexia, and none of these are described in this patient, who has the restricting subtype.
  3. The correct answer is C (Refeeding syndrome from an insulin-driven intracellular electrolyte shift): Reintroducing nutrition in a severely malnourished patient triggers an insulin surge that drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium into cells, producing hypophosphatemia and associated muscle weakness, the hallmark of refeeding syndrome. This is why caloric advancement must be gradual with proactive electrolyte monitoring and repletion. Acute renal failure would present with elevated creatinine and typically hyperphosphatemia, not hypophosphatemia. Primary hyperparathyroidism causes hypercalcemia and hypophosphatemia through a different mechanism unrelated to the timing of refeeding. Laxative-induced malabsorption is not described in this vignette and does not fit the temporal relationship to refeeding.
  4. The correct answer is B (Advance caloric intake slowly with proactive electrolyte monitoring and repletion): Refeeding syndrome is prevented by advancing caloric intake gradually while proactively monitoring and repleting phosphate, potassium, and magnesium before and during refeeding, since the electrolyte shifts occur as an insulin response to reintroduced glucose. A single large caloric bolus is precisely the practice that precipitates refeeding syndrome and must be avoided. Withholding electrolyte supplementation would worsen the complication rather than prevent it. Nutritional support should not be delayed indefinitely, since this patient requires stabilization; rather, it should be introduced cautiously and gradually alongside electrolyte monitoring.
Case BHP-C2

A 47-year-old man with schizophrenia has been maintained on haloperidol for 8 years with reasonable symptom control but persistent mild residual auditory hallucinations. He returns to clinic today reporting a new symptom that started 3 days ago: an unpleasant, uncontrollable urge to move his legs and pace around the room, which he describes as an inability to sit still that is worse than his psychiatric symptoms ever were.

  1. The correct answer is D (Akathisia): A subjective sense of inner restlessness and an inability to sit still, developing within days to weeks of antipsychotic use, describes akathisia, one of the extrapyramidal side effects associated with first-generation antipsychotics such as haloperidol. Tardive dyskinesia presents as involuntary repetitive movements, typically orofacial, after months to years of exposure, not a subjective urge to move after days. Worsening psychosis would present with hallucinations or delusions, not a specific motor restlessness. While restless legs syndrome exists independently, the clear temporal relationship to ongoing haloperidol use makes a medication-induced akathisia far more likely.
  2. The correct answer is A (Lower the antipsychotic dose or add a beta-blocker): Akathisia is managed by lowering the causative antipsychotic dose when feasible or adding a beta-blocker (or a benzodiazepine), both of which reduce the subjective motor restlessness. Increasing the haloperidol dose would worsen akathisia. Anticholinergics such as benztropine are the primary treatment for acute dystonia and parkinsonism, not the first choice for akathisia, though they are sometimes used adjunctively. Switching to clozapine is reserved for treatment-resistant psychosis after failure of at least two other agents and is not the appropriate response to a manageable side effect.
  3. The correct answer is A (A second-generation (atypical) antipsychotic): Atypical antipsychotics such as risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, or aripiprazole generally carry a lower risk of extrapyramidal symptoms such as akathisia and tardive dyskinesia compared with first-generation agents, making them a more appropriate consideration for a patient who has demonstrated sensitivity to these side effects, though they require metabolic monitoring. A second first-generation antipsychotic at a higher dose would increase, not decrease, extrapyramidal risk. Benzodiazepines are not effective monotherapy for psychotic symptoms. Discontinuing all antipsychotic therapy would risk relapse of his underlying schizophrenia and is not indicated by a manageable side effect.
  4. The correct answer is D (Bipolar II disorder): At least 4 days of elevated mood with increased energy, decreased sleep need, and increased goal-directed activity, without marked impairment or need for hospitalization, describes a hypomanic episode; combined with a history of major depressive episodes, this meets criteria for bipolar II disorder. Bipolar I disorder requires a full manic episode with marked impairment or hospitalization, which is not described here (her performance and functioning remained intact). Cyclothymic disorder requires numerous periods of hypomanic and depressive symptoms that do not meet full episode criteria over at least 2 years, whereas this patient has met full criteria for discrete hypomanic and depressive episodes. Major depressive disorder alone would not account for the hypomanic episode described.
  5. The correct answer is D (Liver function tests): Valproate carries a risk of hepatotoxicity, so liver function tests should be monitored at baseline and periodically during treatment. Thyroid-stimulating hormone and renal function are specifically monitored with lithium, not valproate, because lithium can cause hypothyroidism and nephrogenic effects. Fasting lipid panels are relevant to atypical antipsychotics because of metabolic side effects, not specifically to valproate's primary monitoring concern.
  6. The correct answer is D (Titrate the dose slowly according to the recommended schedule): Rapid dose escalation of lamotrigine increases the risk of Stevens-Johnson syndrome, a severe and potentially life-threatening skin reaction, so the medication must be titrated slowly following the recommended schedule. Taking the medication with or without food, sun avoidance, and timing of the dose do not meaningfully affect this specific risk.
  7. The correct answer is C (Antidepressant monotherapy without a mood stabilizer can precipitate a manic or hypomanic episode): In patients with bipolar disorder, using an antidepressant without a concurrent mood stabilizer or antipsychotic carries a recognized risk of triggering a manic switch, so antidepressants are generally used cautiously and in combination with mood-stabilizing therapy rather than as monotherapy. Antidepressant monotherapy is not preferred in bipolar disorder regardless of side-effect profile. Antidepressants are not universally contraindicated in all mood disorders; they are simply not used as unopposed monotherapy in bipolar disorder specifically. Being currently euthymic does not eliminate the risk of destabilization if an antidepressant is added without a mood stabilizer.
  8. The correct answer is B (Administer prophylactic benzodiazepines and monitor closely for withdrawal symptoms): A patient with heavy, chronic daily alcohol use undergoing a procedure is at significant risk for alcohol withdrawal within hours of his last drink, so perioperative benzodiazepine prophylaxis with close monitoring for withdrawal symptoms is appropriate to prevent escalation to seizures or delirium tremens. Proceeding with no special precautions ignores a well-recognized and preventable risk. Naloxone is used for opioid overdose, not alcohol withdrawal prophylaxis. Delaying necessary surgical care and sending the patient home to detoxify independently is unsafe given his risk of severe withdrawal and does not address his surgical need.
  9. The correct answer is D (Binge-eating disorder): Recurrent binge-eating episodes with a sense of loss of control and marked distress, occurring without any compensatory behavior such as vomiting, laxative misuse, fasting, or excessive exercise, defines binge-eating disorder, which is often associated with obesity as seen in this patient's BMI of 34. Bulimia nervosa requires recurrent compensatory behavior, which is explicitly absent here. Anorexia nervosa, binge-eating/purging type, requires a significantly low body weight, which contradicts this patient's elevated BMI. Night eating syndrome is characterized by eating predominantly after the evening meal or after waking at night, a distinct temporal pattern not described in this vignette.
  10. The correct answer is C (The absence of an external incentive and the motivation to assume the sick role): Factitious disorder involves conscious falsification or induction of symptoms (here, self-injected insulin) in the absence of an external incentive, with the underlying motivation being to assume the sick role itself. Malingering, in contrast, involves conscious symptom fabrication for a clear external incentive such as financial compensation, avoiding work, or obtaining a controlled substance. Both conditions can produce genuine physiologic derangement (as in this case, true hypoglycemia from injected insulin), so the presence of a true physical abnormality does not distinguish the two. Physician involvement and chronicity are not the defining distinguishing features between the two diagnoses.
  11. The correct answer is C (Discontinue clozapine and evaluate for agranulocytosis): A markedly low absolute neutrophil count with fever in a patient on clozapine is concerning for clozapine-induced agranulocytosis, a serious and potentially life-threatening complication that requires immediate discontinuation of the drug and further evaluation and monitoring. Continuing clozapine risks progression of agranulocytosis and serious infection. Increasing the dose would worsen marrow suppression. Switching to haloperidol without addressing the acute hematologic emergency and infection risk first is not the appropriate immediate step; the agranulocytosis must be addressed and the patient stabilized before selecting a subsequent antipsychotic.
  12. The correct answer is A (Schizotypal personality disorder): Cognitive or perceptual distortions (magical thinking, unusual perceptual experiences), eccentric behavior and speech, and acute discomfort with close relationships, without ever meeting full criteria for a frank psychotic episode, defines schizotypal personality disorder. Schizoid personality disorder involves detachment and restricted emotion without the odd perceptual and cognitive features described here. Schizophrenia requires an episode with true delusions, hallucinations, or disorganized speech/behavior meeting full symptom and duration criteria, which has not occurred in this patient. Delusional disorder requires a fixed, non-bizarre delusion without the broader eccentric personality pattern described.
  13. The correct answer is D (This may represent major depressive disorder given the persistence, severity, and functional impairment): While grief is a normal and expected response to loss, when symptoms are unusually severe, persistent well beyond expected cultural norms, and cause significant functional impairment, a diagnosis of major depressive disorder should be considered and evaluated rather than assumed to be normal bereavement (prolonged grief disorder is a distinct DSM-5-TR diagnosis but requires at least 12 months of persistent grief in adults, so it does not apply this early). Dismissing this presentation as normal grief without further evaluation risks missing a treatable condition. Nothing in the vignette suggests bipolar disorder, which requires a manic or hypomanic episode. Grief does not always resolve by a fixed time point, and assuming spontaneous resolution without evaluation is not appropriate given the severity described.
  14. The correct answer is D (Continue monitoring metabolic parameters and consider switching to an agent with a more favorable metabolic profile): Atypical antipsychotics, including risperidone, are associated with metabolic side effects such as weight gain, hyperglycemia, and dyslipidemia, which require baseline and periodic monitoring; when significant metabolic changes develop, switching to an agent with a more favorable metabolic profile (such as aripiprazole) should be considered while managing the metabolic abnormalities directly. Abruptly discontinuing all antipsychotic therapy risks psychotic relapse and is not the appropriate first response to a manageable side effect. These metabolic changes are a well-documented antipsychotic side effect and should not be ignored. Adding a stimulant is not an appropriate or evidence-based approach to antipsychotic-induced weight gain and metabolic changes.
  15. The correct answer is C (Involuntary psychiatric hospitalization if he refuses voluntary admission): A patient with a recent serious suicide attempt, a history of prior attempts, and ongoing active suicidal intent represents an imminent danger to himself, warranting hospitalization; if he refuses voluntary admission, involuntary hospitalization is appropriate to ensure safety. Discharge home, even with follow-up or a safety plan, does not adequately address active, ongoing suicidal intent with a recent lethal attempt and prior history. Referral to substance use counseling alone does not address the acute, imminent suicide risk requiring immediate safety intervention.
  16. The correct answer is B (Insidious onset and a stable, progressive course without fluctuating attention): Dementia is characterized by an insidious onset and a generally stable, progressive decline in cognition, with attention typically preserved (at least in earlier stages), which distinguishes it from delirium, which has an acute onset, a fluctuating course, and prominent impairment of attention and awareness. Hallucinations can occur in both dementia and delirium and do not by themselves distinguish the two. An identifiable acute medical trigger is characteristic of delirium, not dementia, so its absence, not presence, supports this patient's diagnosis. Fluctuation in level of consciousness is a hallmark of delirium, not the stable course seen in this patient's dementia.
  17. The correct answer is D (She does not meet criteria for any eating disorder): This patient has a normal BMI, denies fear of weight gain, and has no binge eating, purging, restriction, or body image disturbance; there are no historical or clinical elements suggesting an eating disorder. Atypical anorexia nervosa requires significant weight loss and body image disturbance in the absence of low weight, neither of which is described. Binge-eating disorder requires recurrent binge episodes, which are denied. Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder involves food avoidance based on sensory characteristics or lack of interest in eating without body image disturbance, which is also not described in this reassuring history.
  18. The correct answer is D (Substance-induced psychotic disorder): Psychotic symptoms that develop in clear temporal relationship to substance use and that are attributable to the physiologic effects of that substance define substance-induced psychotic disorder, which is the most likely diagnosis here given the direct temporal link to synthetic cannabinoid use. Schizophrenia requires 6 months of continuous disturbance and is not substance-attributable. Brief psychotic disorder is a primary psychiatric diagnosis of exclusion used when psychosis is not attributable to a substance or another medical condition; here, the substance link should be identified first. Schizophreniform disorder similarly requires the psychosis not be attributable to a substance or medical condition.
  19. The correct answer is D (Switch to a different antidepressant or augment therapy): After an adequate trial (adequate dose for 4 to 8 weeks) without response, appropriate next steps include switching to a different antidepressant (potentially of a different class) or augmenting the current regimen (for example, with an atypical antipsychotic or another agent), along with reassessing psychotherapy. ECT is generally reserved for severe, treatment-resistant, or psychotic depression, or when rapid response is needed, not as the immediate next step after one failed trial. Discontinuing all treatment ignores other effective options that have not yet been tried. Combining two SSRIs at full dose is not standard practice and increases the risk of serotonin syndrome without established added benefit.
  20. The correct answer is A (Add exposure and response prevention therapy): For OCD, the combination of an SSRI (often at a higher dose than used for depression) with cognitive behavioral therapy using exposure and response prevention is the most effective evidence-based approach, and adding ERP when medication alone is insufficient is standard practice. Benzodiazepines are not a primary or recommended augmentation strategy for OCD and do not address the underlying compulsive cycle. Bupropion has minimal serotonergic activity and is generally not effective for OCD. Stimulants have no established role in the treatment of OCD.
  21. The correct answer is A (Non-suicidal self-injury used as a maladaptive coping mechanism): Self-harm behavior without suicidal intent, often used to regulate overwhelming emotional states (described here as helping her "feel real again"), is common in borderline personality disorder and is classified as non-suicidal self-injury, best addressed with dialectical behavior therapy skills for emotion regulation and distress tolerance rather than by assuming suicidal intent. This behavior does not meet criteria for a suicide attempt given the explicitly denied intent to die, though risk should still be carefully assessed. There is no indication of an external incentive suggesting malingering. This is not a psychotic symptom and does not require antipsychotic treatment as the primary intervention.
  22. The correct answer is B (Buspirone): Buspirone is a non-sedating anxiolytic without abuse or dependence potential, making it appropriate for a patient with GAD who needs to avoid sedation, such as someone operating heavy machinery, though it takes several weeks to become effective and does not help acute anxiety. Alprazolam, diazepam, and lorazepam are all benzodiazepines that carry sedation risk and dependence potential, making them less appropriate for a patient specifically concerned about safe operation of machinery and long-term dependence.
  23. The correct answer is C (Delusional disorder): A single fixed, non-bizarre delusion (a plausible though false belief) persisting for at least 1 month, with otherwise preserved functioning and the absence of other psychotic symptoms such as hallucinations or disorganized speech, defines delusional disorder. Schizophrenia requires at least two of the five characteristic symptoms (delusions, hallucinations, disorganized speech, disorganized/catatonic behavior, negative symptoms) along with functional decline, which is not seen here given his intact work performance. Schizoaffective disorder requires a concurrent major mood episode, which is not described. Brief psychotic disorder resolves within 1 month, whereas this patient's belief has persisted for 7 months.
  24. The correct answer is A (Gradual dose tapering to minimize discontinuation symptoms): SSRIs should generally be tapered gradually rather than stopped abruptly to reduce the risk of discontinuation symptoms such as dizziness, paresthesias, irritability, and flu-like symptoms, sometimes called SSRI discontinuation syndrome. Abrupt discontinuation increases the likelihood and severity of these symptoms. Switching to a benzodiazepine introduces dependence risk and is not an appropriate substitute for a controlled taper of an effective medication. Doubling the dose before stopping has no established benefit and is not standard practice.
  25. The correct answer is B (Schedule regular follow-up visits with a single consistent provider and avoid unnecessary repeat testing): The recommended approach to somatic symptom and related disorders is regularly scheduled visits with a single, consistent primary provider, which builds a therapeutic alliance and reduces the anxiety-driven cycle of seeking repeated testing, while avoiding unnecessary procedures that carry their own risks and rarely provide reassurance. Repeating already-negative imaging reinforces the maladaptive cycle rather than helping her. Referring to numerous specialists on demand similarly reinforces excessive care-seeking without benefit. Dismissing her symptoms as not real and discharging her from care abandons a patient who requires ongoing, structured support and risks missing a true underlying condition.
  26. The correct answer is D (Brief psychotic disorder): Psychotic symptoms (hallucinations and a fixed false belief) with sudden onset following an identifiable severe stressor, lasting less than 1 month with an expected full return to baseline functioning, defines brief psychotic disorder. Schizophrenia requires 6 months of continuous disturbance, far longer than the 3 weeks described. Major depressive disorder with psychotic features requires a full major depressive episode with psychotic features occurring specifically during the mood episode, which is not clearly established here as a full depressive syndrome versus an acute grief-triggered psychotic reaction. Schizoaffective disorder requires 2 weeks of psychosis in the absence of a mood episode plus a concurrent major mood episode, a more complex pattern not described in this acute presentation.
  27. The correct answer is C (Buprenorphine): Buprenorphine, a partial opioid agonist, can be prescribed in an office-based outpatient setting without requiring daily observed dosing at a specialized clinic, making it appropriate for a patient seeking flexible outpatient treatment. Methadone for opioid use disorder is dispensed through federally regulated opioid treatment programs typically requiring daily observed dosing, at least initially, which does not match this patient's stated preference. Naloxone is used for overdose reversal, not maintenance treatment, and is not taken daily for this purpose. Disulfiram is used for alcohol use disorder, not opioid use disorder.
  28. The correct answer is C (Borderline personality disorder): Frantic efforts to avoid real or imagined abandonment, unstable and intense relationships, identity disturbance, chronic emptiness, and recurrent self-harm behavior together define borderline personality disorder. Histrionic personality disorder centers on excessive emotionality and attention-seeking rather than abandonment fears and self-harm. Dependent personality disorder involves excessive need to be cared for and submissive, clinging behavior, but not the identity instability and self-harm pattern described. Narcissistic personality disorder centers on grandiosity and lack of empathy, not fear of abandonment or self-harm.
  29. The correct answer is A (Tricyclic antidepressants): Tricyclic antidepressants carry anticholinergic effects and can prolong cardiac conduction (QT prolongation, arrhythmia risk), making them relatively contraindicated or requiring particular caution in patients with recent cardiac events such as myocardial infarction. SSRIs are generally considered safer in cardiac patients and are often preferred in this population. Bupropion has minimal cardiac conduction effects compared with tricyclics, though it lowers seizure threshold. Buspirone is used for anxiety, not depression, and does not carry the same cardiac conduction risk profile as tricyclics.
  30. The correct answer is A (Cognitive behavioral therapy with an SSRI or SNRI): This presentation meets criteria for generalized anxiety disorder (excessive worry across multiple domains for more than 6 months with associated somatic symptoms and a normal medical workup ruling out secondary causes), for which first-line treatment is cognitive behavioral therapy combined with an SSRI or SNRI. Long-term daily benzodiazepine monotherapy is not first-line because of dependence and sedation risk. Antipsychotics have no role as first-line treatment for GAD. A normal medical workup supports the diagnosis of a primary anxiety disorder requiring treatment, not the absence of a treatable condition.
  31. The correct answer is B (Second-generation (atypical) antipsychotics): Atypical antipsychotics are generally considered to have somewhat greater efficacy against negative symptoms (flat affect, avolition, alogia) compared with first-generation agents, which are primarily effective against positive symptoms such as hallucinations and delusions, making atypical agents a more appropriate choice when negative symptoms predominate. First-generation antipsychotics are less effective for negative symptoms. Benzodiazepines have no established role in treating negative symptoms of schizophrenia. Tricyclic antidepressants are not used to treat negative symptoms of schizophrenia.
  32. The correct answer is B (Inpatient psychiatric hospitalization given severe impairment and risk): This presentation describes a severe manic episode with marked functional impairment (financial recklessness, days without sleep) and impaired judgment posing risk to himself and his family's welfare, which typically requires inpatient hospitalization for safety and rapid stabilization. Outpatient follow-up in a week is inadequate given the acuity and risk of this presentation. Manic episodes of this severity do not reliably resolve spontaneously without treatment and pose ongoing risk if untreated. Couples counseling does not address the acute psychiatric emergency requiring medical stabilization.
  33. The correct answer is B (Disulfiram): Disulfiram inhibits aldehyde dehydrogenase, causing acetaldehyde accumulation if alcohol is consumed, producing an aversive reaction of flushing, nausea, vomiting, palpitations, and hypotension, which serves as a deterrent to drinking. Naltrexone reduces alcohol cravings and the rewarding effects of alcohol through opioid receptor antagonism but does not cause this aversive reaction. Acamprosate helps maintain abstinence by modulating glutamatergic and GABAergic activity but similarly does not produce an aversive reaction with alcohol. Buprenorphine is used for opioid use disorder, not alcohol use disorder.
  34. The correct answer is A (Establish a clear temporal relationship between substance use and psychotic symptoms, with symptoms expected to improve with abstinence): Given the clear temporal link between heavy methamphetamine use and the onset of psychotic symptoms, the most appropriate approach is to consider substance-induced psychotic disorder, confirming that symptoms developed during or shortly after intoxication and are expected to improve with sustained abstinence; if psychotic symptoms persist well beyond the expected period of intoxication and withdrawal, an independent primary psychotic disorder would need to be reconsidered. Diagnosing schizophrenia immediately ignores the substance-use context and the duration requirement for schizophrenia. Schizophreniform disorder similarly requires that symptoms not be attributable to a substance. Brief psychotic disorder is a diagnosis of exclusion once substance-induced and medical causes have been ruled out, not the immediate default given a clear substance trigger.
  35. The correct answer is A (Administer intravenous thiamine): The triad of confusion, ataxia, and gaze palsy (ophthalmoplegia) in a chronically alcohol-dependent patient with poor nutritional intake describes Wernicke encephalopathy from thiamine deficiency; thiamine must be administered before or concurrently with any glucose-containing fluids, since giving glucose first in a thiamine-deficient patient can precipitate or worsen Wernicke encephalopathy by consuming remaining thiamine stores in glucose metabolism. Naloxone treats opioid overdose and has no role here. Oral folate does not address the acute thiamine deficiency causing this presentation. A benzodiazepine would be appropriate for alcohol withdrawal symptoms but does not address the specific and urgent need to replete thiamine before glucose administration in this vignette.
Case BL1-561

A 68-year-old man presents for evaluation of six weeks of fatigue and exertional dyspnea. He reports no chest pain and denies hematemesis or melena, though he admits his stools have looked darker than usual on a few occasions. He takes low-dose aspirin daily for coronary artery disease and denies alcohol use. Vital signs are stable. Physical examination reveals conjunctival pallor and a soft, nontender abdomen with no organomegaly.

  1. The correct answer is C (A low serum ferritin along with a low serum iron and an elevated total iron binding capacity): a low ferritin, low serum iron, and a high total iron binding capacity is the classic pattern of true iron deficiency, since the body upregulates transferrin production as its iron stores are depleted. Option A describes thalassemia trait, which produces a normal iron panel and a compensatory normal-to-high red cell count for the degree of microcytosis, not a picture that supports iron deficiency. Option B describes the pattern seen in anemia of chronic disease, where ferritin (an acute phase reactant) stays normal or rises while both serum iron and TIBC fall together. Option D describes sideroblastic anemia, in which iron is present but cannot be incorporated into heme, so ferritin and iron are both elevated rather than low.
  2. The correct answer is B (Referral for colonoscopy and upper endoscopy to evaluate for an occult gastrointestinal bleeding source): new iron deficiency anemia in an adult man with no other clear cause represents occult gastrointestinal blood loss until proven otherwise, and colonoscopy plus upper endoscopy is the appropriate next step to locate the source, particularly given his age and aspirin use, which raises concern for a colonic lesion. Option A is wrong because aspirin use does not exclude the need to search for a structural lesion such as a colonic malignancy. Option C is wrong because empiric acid suppression does not substitute for identifying the bleeding source. Option D is wrong because delaying endoscopic evaluation risks missing a treatable lesion, including colorectal cancer.
  3. The correct answer is A (Unexplained iron deficiency anemia in an older adult should prompt evaluation for colorectal cancer as a source of chronic occult blood loss): a right-sided colon cancer often bleeds slowly and occultly rather than causing overt hematochezia, so iron deficiency anemia can be the first and only clue, which is exactly why unexplained iron deficiency in an older adult mandates colon cancer screening. Option B is wrong because colorectal cancer causes microcytic anemia from chronic blood loss, not macrocytic anemia. Option C is wrong because aspirin can contribute to bleeding but a structural lesion must still be excluded rather than assumed. Option D is wrong because a normal upper endoscopy only excludes an upper source, not a colonic one, which is why both studies were performed.
  4. The correct answer is A (A rise in the absolute reticulocyte count within one to two weeks after starting iron therapy): an appropriately responding marrow increases its output of young red cells once adequate iron becomes available, so a brisk rise in the absolute reticulocyte count within one to two weeks is the earliest and most direct sign of an effective response to iron replacement. Option B is wrong because ferritin reflects iron stores rather than active erythropoiesis and can rise from repletion without confirming marrow response. Option C is wrong because TIBC changes lag well behind the reticulocyte response and are not the primary marker used to confirm early treatment efficacy. Option D is wrong because RDW normalization occurs gradually over weeks to months as new, uniformly sized cells dilute the older population.
  5. The correct answer is D (Autoimmune destruction of gastric parietal cells causing vitamin B12 deficiency with subacute combined degeneration): loss of parietal cells removes the source of intrinsic factor, producing pernicious anemia and vitamin B12 deficiency, which impairs both DNA synthesis in the marrow (macrocytic anemia) and myelin synthesis in the dorsal columns and corticospinal tracts (subacute combined degeneration), explaining both the hematologic and neurologic findings together. Option A is wrong because folate deficiency causes the identical megaloblastic blood picture but does not cause subacute combined degeneration or any neurologic disease. Option B is wrong because alcohol causes macrocytosis through a different, non-megaloblastic mechanism and does not classically produce dorsal column and corticospinal findings together. Option C is wrong because iron deficiency produces microcytic, not macrocytic, anemia and does not cause this neurologic syndrome.
  6. The correct answer is B (A direct toxic effect of alcohol on erythrocyte membrane and marrow precursor development): chronic alcohol use causes macrocytosis through a direct toxic effect on developing red cell precursors and altered membrane lipid composition, producing round macrocytes without the hypersegmented neutrophils and other megaloblastic features seen with true B12 or folate deficiency. Option A is less likely given normal B12 levels and the absence of hypersegmented neutrophils or neurologic findings that would suggest pernicious anemia. Option C is wrong because reticulocytosis from blood loss would raise the MCV only mildly and would be accompanied by evidence of anemia severity and a high reticulocyte count, not described here. Option D is possible in chronic alcohol use but is a less likely first explanation than the simpler and far more common direct toxic effect described.
  7. The correct answer is D (Reduced renal production of erythropoietin limiting marrow red cell output): the kidneys are the primary source of erythropoietin, and advanced chronic kidney disease reduces erythropoietin production, leading to a normocytic, hypoproliferative anemia with an inappropriately low reticulocyte count despite normal iron stores, exactly as described here. Option A is wrong because this patient's laboratory picture shows decreased production rather than the elevated reticulocyte count, bilirubin, and LDH expected with hemolysis. Option B is wrong because normal iron studies argue against ongoing blood loss as the primary driver of this anemia. Option C describes anemia of chronic disease, which can coexist with renal disease but does not best explain a picture with normal iron studies and no described inflammatory illness.
  8. The correct answer is A (Beta thalassemia trait): thalassemia trait classically produces a disproportionately low MCV relative to a mild degree of anemia, a normal or even mildly elevated red cell count, a normal RDW because the cells are uniformly small, and a normal iron panel, all of which fit this asymptomatic child and would be confirmed with hemoglobin electrophoresis. Option B is wrong because iron deficiency would show an abnormal iron panel and typically a high RDW from variably sized cells. Option C is wrong because there is no described inflammatory illness and ferritin would be expected to be normal or elevated, not simply normal iron studies overall. Option D is wrong because sideroblastic anemia would show an elevated serum iron and ferritin, not a normal iron panel.
  9. The correct answer is A (Folate deficiency from increased demand and poor dietary intake): pregnancy dramatically increases folate requirements to support rapid cell division, and a diet poor in folate-rich vegetables readily produces folate deficiency, which explains the megaloblastic picture of macrocytosis with hypersegmented neutrophils; her prior alcohol use further impairs folate absorption and storage. Option B is less likely without a description of a strict vegan diet, terminal ileal disease, or another specific risk factor for B12 deficiency, and B12 deficiency in pregnancy is considerably less common than folate deficiency. Option C is wrong because iron deficiency produces microcytic, not macrocytic, anemia. Option D is wrong because vitamin B6 deficiency does not cause megaloblastic anemia or hypersegmented neutrophils.
  10. The correct answer is C (Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura): the combination of fever, neurologic changes, thrombocytopenia, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia with schistocytes, and renal involvement, together with a negative Coombs test and normal coagulation times, is classic for thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, caused by a deficiency of the ADAMTS13 enzyme that normally cleaves large von Willebrand factor multimers. Option A is wrong because immune thrombocytopenic purpura causes isolated thrombocytopenia without hemolysis or schistocytes. Option B is wrong because DIC characteristically prolongs the PT and aPTT and consumes fibrinogen, which are not described here, and typically has an identifiable trigger such as sepsis. Option D is wrong because warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia has a positive direct Coombs test, which is negative in this patient.
  11. The correct answer is B (IgG-mediated extravascular hemolysis with splenic macrophage clearance of antibody-coated red cells): warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia, frequently associated with lymphoproliferative disorders such as chronic lymphocytic leukemia, is caused by IgG antibodies coating red cells, which are then partially phagocytosed by splenic macrophages, producing spherocytes and a positive direct Coombs test for IgG, exactly as described here. Option A describes microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, which produces schistocytes rather than spherocytes and a negative Coombs test. Option C describes cold agglutinin disease, which is IgM-mediated and worsens with cold exposure, not the pattern described in this patient. Option D describes hereditary spherocytosis, an inherited condition that would also produce a negative, not positive, direct Coombs test.
  12. The correct answer is A (Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency): G6PD deficiency is an X-linked enzyme defect that impairs the red cell's ability to neutralize oxidative stress, so exposure to an oxidant drug such as a sulfonamide antibiotic precipitates acute hemolysis with bite cells (from macrophage removal of oxidized hemoglobin) and Heinz bodies (denatured hemoglobin precipitates), exactly as described in this patient. Option B is wrong because hereditary spherocytosis produces spherocytes rather than bite cells and Heinz bodies and is not typically drug-triggered. Option C is wrong because paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria is an acquired clonal disorder that is not triggered by a specific oxidant medication and does not produce bite cells. Option D is wrong because cold agglutinin disease is triggered by cold exposure and produces agglutinated red cells on smear, not bite cells.
  13. The correct answer is D (Functional asplenia from repeated splenic infarction due to sickling): repeated vaso-occlusive sickling episodes progressively infarct the spleen over early childhood, leading to functional asplenia; because the spleen normally removes nuclear remnants called Howell-Jolly bodies from circulating red cells, their appearance on smear is a direct marker of this autosplenectomy and also signals increased risk for infection with encapsulated organisms. Option A is wrong because Howell-Jolly bodies are a splenic function marker, not a coagulation finding. Option B describes hereditary spherocytosis, which is unrelated to sickle cell disease and to Howell-Jolly bodies specifically. Option C describes G6PD deficiency, which produces bite cells and Heinz bodies rather than Howell-Jolly bodies.
  14. The correct answer is A (Flow cytometry demonstrating absence of CD55 and CD59 on red cell surfaces): the combination of episodic intravascular hemolysis with morning hemoglobinuria and unusual-site venous thrombosis, including hepatic vein (Budd-Chiari) thrombosis, is classic for paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria, an acquired clonal disorder from a PIGA gene mutation that removes the protective complement regulatory proteins CD55 and CD59, confirmed by flow cytometry. Option B describes the test for hereditary spherocytosis, which does not classically cause hepatic vein thrombosis. Option C describes the test for a hemoglobinopathy such as sickle cell disease, which would not explain this clinical picture. Option D describes the test for autoimmune hemolytic anemia, which is not associated with this pattern of unusual-site thrombosis.
  15. The correct answer is C (IgM antibody binding at cold temperatures with complement-mediated intravascular hemolysis): cold agglutinin disease is mediated by IgM antibodies, often triggered by Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection, that bind red cells preferentially in cooler peripheral tissues and fix complement, producing complement-mediated intravascular hemolysis that worsens with cold exposure, consistent with a positive C3 and negative IgG Coombs result. Option A describes warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia, which is IgG-mediated and would show a positive IgG Coombs test, not the C3-only pattern described here. Option B describes microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, which is not antibody-mediated and would have a negative Coombs test. Option D is incorrect because this presentation is antibody-mediated rather than an inherited structural defect.
  16. The correct answer is D (Mechanical shearing of red cells as they pass across the prosthetic valve surface): a mechanical heart valve creates high shear stress across its surface that physically fragments passing red cells, producing schistocytes and the classic laboratory picture of intravascular hemolysis (elevated LDH, low haptoglobin) with a negative Coombs test, since no antibody is involved. Option A is wrong because a negative Coombs test argues against an antibody-mediated process. Option B describes paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria, which is unrelated to a prosthetic valve. Option C describes cold agglutinin disease, which would show a positive Coombs test for complement, not a negative result.
  17. The correct answer is B (IgG autoantibody binding red cell surface antigens with extravascular clearance): systemic lupus erythematosus is a recognized cause of warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia, in which IgG autoantibodies coat the red cell surface and lead to partial phagocytic removal by splenic macrophages, producing spherocytes and a positive IgG-positive direct Coombs test, exactly as seen here. Option A describes microangiopathic hemolysis, which would show schistocytes and a negative Coombs test rather than the spherocytes and positive IgG result described. Option C describes hereditary spherocytosis, an inherited condition unrelated to autoimmune disease activity. Option D describes cold agglutinin disease, which is IgM- and complement-mediated rather than IgG-mediated.
  18. The correct answer is D (His hemolysis will substantially improve because the primary site of red cell destruction has been removed): in hereditary spherocytosis, the underlying membrane protein defect (commonly affecting spectrin or ankyrin) itself is not corrected by splenectomy, but because the spleen is the principal site where abnormally shaped red cells are trapped and destroyed, removing it substantially reduces the rate of hemolysis and improves anemia, which is why splenectomy is the definitive treatment for severe disease. Option A is wrong because the underlying red cell fragility persists; splenectomy treats the consequence, not the membrane defect itself. Option B is wrong because hereditary spherocytosis is a membrane protein disorder, not a hemoglobinopathy, so electrophoresis is not the relevant test and would not change. Option C is wrong because the abnormal protein originates in the red cell membrane itself, not in the spleen.
  19. The correct answer is B (G6PD deficiency impairs generation of NADPH, limiting the red cell's ability to neutralize oxidative damage): G6PD is the rate-limiting enzyme of the pentose phosphate pathway, which generates NADPH; NADPH is required to regenerate reduced glutathione, the red cell's main defense against oxidative stress, so a deficiency leaves red cells vulnerable to hemoglobin denaturation and hemolysis whenever an oxidative trigger such as fava beans, certain drugs, or infection is encountered. Option A describes the mechanism of hereditary spherocytosis, not G6PD deficiency. Option C describes the mechanism of paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria. Option D is unrelated to G6PD deficiency and instead relates to vitamin B12 absorption.
  20. The correct answer is D (Acute promyelocytic leukemia with disseminated intravascular coagulation): Auer rods are pathognomonic for acute myeloid leukemia, and their presence together with a florid consumptive coagulopathy (prolonged PT and aPTT, low fibrinogen, elevated D-dimer) at presentation is the classic picture of acute promyelocytic leukemia, the AML subtype driven by t(15;17) that is well known for triggering DIC, and which is treated urgently with all-trans retinoic acid. Option A is wrong because CML blast crisis would show a background of maturing myeloid cells with a prior chronic phase, not the described acute presentation with Auer rods. Option B is wrong because ALL involves lymphoblasts rather than myeloblasts with Auer rods. Option C is wrong because CLL is an indolent lymphocytic disease seen with smudge cells, not blasts with Auer rods.
  21. The correct answer is D (Acute lymphoblastic leukemia): acute lymphoblastic leukemia is the most common childhood malignancy and classically presents exactly as described here, with marrow replacement by lymphoblasts causing pancytopenia, bone pain from marrow expansion, and organomegaly from leukemic infiltration, all in a young child. Option A is wrong because AML is far less common in young children and typically presents with Auer rod-containing myeloblasts rather than lymphoblasts. Option B is wrong because CML is a disease of middle-aged adults with marked leukocytosis and a full spectrum of maturing myeloid cells, not blasts predominating in a child. Option C is wrong because Hodgkin lymphoma presents with painless lymphadenopathy rather than marrow replacement and pancytopenia.
  22. The correct answer is A (A tyrosine kinase inhibitor targeting the BCR-ABL fusion protein): the t(9;22) Philadelphia chromosome produces the BCR-ABL fusion protein, a constitutively active tyrosine kinase that drives chronic myeloid leukemia, and imatinib and other BCR-ABL tyrosine kinase inhibitors are the targeted first-line therapy that directly addresses this molecular driver. Option B is the specific therapy for acute promyelocytic leukemia, an AML subtype unrelated to the Philadelphia chromosome. Option C targets CD20, a B-cell marker relevant to B-cell lymphomas and CLL, not the myeloid lineage described here. Option D is a component of ALL induction therapy, not the targeted approach for CML.
  23. The correct answer is A (Chronic lymphocytic leukemia): chronic lymphocytic leukemia is the most common leukemia in adults and classically presents as an indolent, often incidental finding of lymphocytosis with smudge cells on smear, reflecting fragile malignant lymphocytes damaged during slide preparation, along with a CD5-positive monoclonal B-cell population on flow cytometry. Option B is wrong because ALL involves lymphoblasts, an acute and typically symptomatic presentation, rather than mature small lymphocytes found incidentally. Option C is wrong because hairy cell leukemia shows cells with fine cytoplasmic projections and typically presents with pancytopenia and splenomegaly rather than this smear pattern. Option D is wrong because CML is a myeloid, not lymphoid, malignancy.
  24. The correct answer is C (Reed-Sternberg cell): the binucleate, owl-eye appearing cell described, together with alcohol-induced lymph node pain, a classic and distinctive symptom, and a mixed inflammatory background, is the Reed-Sternberg cell, the diagnostic hallmark of Hodgkin lymphoma. Option A describes a finding of chronic lymphocytic leukemia on peripheral smear, not a lymph node biopsy finding. Option B describes acute leukemia, not the indolent lymphadenopathy pattern described here. Option D describes the histologic appearance of Burkitt lymphoma, a rapidly growing non-Hodgkin lymphoma with a very different clinical picture than this indolent presentation with alcohol-induced pain.
  25. The correct answer is C (Translocation-driven overexpression of the c-myc oncogene): endemic Burkitt lymphoma, strongly associated with Epstein-Barr virus infection, is driven by a t(8;14) translocation that places the c-myc oncogene under the control of the immunoglobulin heavy chain promoter, causing massive overexpression of c-myc and extremely rapid cell proliferation, producing the classic starry sky histology and rapidly growing jaw mass described. Option A describes the mechanism of CML, an unrelated myeloid disease. Option B describes follicular lymphoma, an indolent disease with a very different clinical course than this rapidly growing mass. Option D describes the mechanism disrupted in acute promyelocytic leukemia, unrelated to Burkitt lymphoma.
  26. The correct answer is B (Direct tubular toxicity from filtered free light chains and hypercalcemia): in multiple myeloma, excess free light chains (Bence Jones protein) are filtered by the glomerulus and precipitate with Tamm-Horsfall protein within the renal tubules, causing direct tubular injury (myeloma cast nephropathy), and this injury is compounded by the hypercalcemia also present, together explaining her renal impairment. Option A describes a mechanism relevant to lupus nephritis or post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis, not myeloma. Option C describes the mechanism of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura or atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome, unrelated to this presentation. Option D describes Goodpasture syndrome, a distinct autoimmune process not associated with the monoclonal gammopathy described here.
  27. The correct answer is B (It places BCL-2 under control of the immunoglobulin heavy chain promoter, blocking apoptosis): the t(14;18) translocation characteristic of follicular lymphoma juxtaposes the BCL-2 gene with the immunoglobulin heavy chain enhancer, causing BCL-2 overexpression; because BCL-2 normally inhibits programmed cell death, its overexpression allows malignant lymphocytes to evade apoptosis and accumulate over time, producing the indolent clinical course typical of this lymphoma. Option A describes the mechanism of CML rather than follicular lymphoma. Option C describes Burkitt lymphoma's t(8;14) mechanism, which drives rapid proliferation rather than apoptosis evasion. Option D describes the mechanism disrupted in acute promyelocytic leukemia, unrelated to follicular lymphoma.
  28. The correct answer is D (Arterial and venous thrombosis from increased blood viscosity): the hallmark risk in polycythemia vera is thrombosis, both arterial and venous, driven by the markedly increased red cell mass raising blood viscosity, which is why therapeutic phlebotomy to lower the hematocrit and low-dose aspirin form the backbone of management. Option A describes a possible late progression of myeloproliferative disease (myelofibrosis) rather than the most immediate risk from an elevated red cell mass itself. Option B describes chronic granulomatous disease, an unrelated immunodeficiency. Option C describes essential thrombocythemia, a distinct myeloproliferative neoplasm defined by platelet overproduction rather than red cell overproduction.
  29. The correct answer is A (Marrow fibrosis forcing compensatory extramedullary hematopoiesis in the spleen and liver): primary myelofibrosis replaces normal marrow with fibrous tissue, which both explains the dry tap on aspiration and forces the body to shift blood cell production to extramedullary sites such as the spleen and liver, producing massive splenomegaly along with a leukoerythroblastic smear containing teardrop cells, immature myeloid forms, and nucleated red cells. Option B describes multiple myeloma, which does not typically cause massive splenomegaly or a leukoerythroblastic smear. Option C would not produce marrow fibrosis or a dry tap and does not explain this degree of splenomegaly. Option D describes paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria, unrelated to this fibrotic marrow picture.
  30. The correct answer is B (Both thrombotic and, at very high platelet counts, paradoxical bleeding complications): essential thrombocythemia produces a markedly elevated platelet count with an associated increased risk of both thrombosis and, at very high counts, paradoxical bleeding from acquired von Willebrand factor dysfunction, which is why this dual risk profile is a defining and heavily tested feature of the condition. Option A describes a complication of multiple myeloma, unrelated to essential thrombocythemia. Option C describes a complement deficiency, unrelated to platelet overproduction. Option D describes vitamin B12 deficiency, an unrelated nutritional cause of macrocytic anemia.
  31.
