The correct answer is C (Richter transformation): Richter transformation describes the relatively uncommon but well recognized progression of chronic lymphocytic leukemia into an aggressive, high-grade lymphoma, most often diffuse large B-cell lymphoma, and it should be suspected whenever a previously indolent CLL patient develops rapidly growing, asymmetric adenopathy with new systemic symptoms, as seen here. Option A describes progression of chronic myeloid leukemia to an acute leukemia phase, a distinct process in a different disease. Option B describes a metabolic emergency from rapid tumor cell death, typically after starting chemotherapy, not a change in the tumor's underlying histology. Option D describes symptomatic hyperviscosity from an extremely elevated white blood cell count, not a histologic transformation.
  32. The correct answer is D (Disseminated intravascular coagulation triggered by tissue factor release from the abrupted placenta): placental abruption releases large amounts of tissue factor into the maternal circulation, triggering widespread activation of the coagulation cascade that consumes platelets and clotting factors while simultaneously activating fibrinolysis, producing the classic DIC picture of prolonged PT and aPTT, low fibrinogen, low platelets, and elevated D-dimer seen on this panel. Option A would show an isolated prolonged aPTT with a normal PT and normal platelet count and fibrinogen, not this global consumptive picture. Option B would not produce the combined prolongation of both PT and aPTT with low fibrinogen described here. Option C classically prolongs the aPTT alone in vitro and does not cause this acute consumptive bleeding picture.
  33. The correct answer is D (X-linked recessive): hemophilia A, caused by factor VIII deficiency, is inherited in an X-linked recessive pattern, which explains why it almost always affects boys, who have only one X chromosome, while female carriers are typically asymptomatic, and it presents with deep bleeding into joints such as recurrent hemarthrosis after minor trauma, exactly as described here. Option A is wrong because hemophilia does not follow an autosomal dominant pattern; von Willebrand disease, a different bleeding disorder, is the one typically inherited in an autosomal dominant fashion. Option B is wrong because hemophilia is X-linked, not autosomal recessive. Option C is wrong because hemophilia involves a nuclear gene on the X chromosome, not mitochondrial DNA.
  34. The correct answer is D (Von Willebrand disease): von Willebrand disease, the most common inherited bleeding disorder and typically autosomal dominant, presents with mucocutaneous bleeding such as epistaxis and menorrhagia because von Willebrand factor is required for platelet adhesion, and because it also stabilizes circulating factor VIII, deficiency can mildly prolong the aPTT while the platelet count and PT remain normal, exactly as described here. Option A is wrong because hemophilia A is X-linked and would essentially never present with an affected mother and daughter both symptomatic in this pattern, and it does not cause mucocutaneous bleeding. Option B is wrong because ITP causes a low platelet count, which is normal in this patient. Option C is wrong because Factor V Leiden causes a hypercoagulable, not a bleeding, phenotype.
  35. The correct answer is B (Immune thrombocytopenic purpura): immune thrombocytopenic purpura in children classically follows a viral illness by one to several weeks and presents with an isolated, often severe, drop in platelet count with an otherwise normal blood count and no organomegaly, and it is typically self-limited in this age group, sometimes managed with observation alone or corticosteroids or IVIG if bleeding is significant. Option A would also show hemolytic anemia with schistocytes and elevated LDH, not an isolated platelet drop. Option C would show abnormal PT and aPTT with low fibrinogen, not an isolated thrombocytopenia. Option D would show accompanying renal impairment and microangiopathic hemolysis, neither of which is described here.
  36. The correct answer is D (Antiphospholipid antibodies interfering with the phospholipid reagent used in the aPTT assay in vitro): antiphospholipid syndrome, often seen alongside systemic lupus erythematosus and presenting with recurrent thrombosis and pregnancy loss exactly as described, paradoxically prolongs the aPTT in the laboratory because the antiphospholipid antibodies interfere with the phospholipid reagent used in the assay itself, even though the patient is clinically prothrombotic rather than at risk for bleeding, which is confirmed by a mixing study that fails to correct. Option A would not produce a stable, chronic aPTT prolongation with a normal bleeding history and would instead be an acute consumptive process. Option B does not explain a thrombotic clinical history. Option C would present with a bleeding, not a thrombotic and pregnancy-loss, history.
  37. The correct answer is C (Factor V Leiden mutation): Factor V Leiden is the most common inherited thrombophilia and results from a point mutation in the factor V gene that renders it resistant to cleavage and inactivation by activated protein C, allowing unchecked procoagulant activity and a substantially increased risk of unprovoked venous thromboembolism, often with a strong family history as described here. Option A describes a different, less common inherited thrombophilia that does not involve factor V resistance to cleavage. Option B describes another distinct inherited thrombophilia unrelated to the specific mechanism described in this question. Option D describes a different mutation that increases prothrombin levels rather than causing factor V resistance to cleavage.
  38. The correct answer is A (A transient hypercoagulable state from a faster decline in protein C and protein S than in procoagulant factors): protein C and protein S are vitamin K-dependent anticoagulant proteins with shorter half-lives than the procoagulant vitamin K-dependent factors, so starting warfarin alone causes their levels to fall first, creating a transient hypercoagulable state that can cause microvascular thrombosis and skin necrosis, particularly in patients with an underlying protein C or S deficiency, which is why an overlapping heparin bridge is standard practice when initiating warfarin for acute thrombosis. Option B is wrong because warfarin-induced skin necrosis is a procoagulant, not an allergic, mechanism. Option C is possible in principle but does not explain why this specific complication is linked to warfarin initiation without a bridge. Option D is wrong because the mechanism is a coagulation protein imbalance, not direct capillary toxicity.
  39. The correct answer is C (Protein C deficiency): protein C is a vitamin K-dependent anticoagulant protein that, once activated, cleaves and inactivates factors Va and VIIIa to limit clot propagation, so a deficiency of protein C removes this natural brake on coagulation and predisposes to recurrent venous thromboembolism, matching the mechanism described here. Option A describes a mutation that makes factor Va resistant to inactivation by protein C, which is a different mechanism than a deficiency of protein C itself. Option B describes a deficiency of a different natural anticoagulant that inhibits thrombin and factor Xa directly rather than inactivating factors Va and VIIIa. Option D is an acquired autoimmune condition rather than a deficiency of a specific vitamin K-dependent protein.
Case BL2-600

A 29-year-old woman presents with a two-month history of painless swelling in her left axilla along with drenching night sweats and an unintentional 12-pound weight loss. She reports intermittent low-grade fevers. Examination reveals a firm, rubbery, nontender 4 cm axillary lymph node. Chest imaging reveals a mediastinal mass. There is no hepatosplenomegaly.

  1. The correct answer is B (Excisional lymph node biopsy for histopathologic examination): a firm, rubbery, persistently enlarged lymph node accompanied by drenching night sweats, significant weight loss, and a mediastinal mass strongly suggests lymphoma, and an excisional biopsy is required to preserve lymph node architecture, which is essential for distinguishing Hodgkin from non-Hodgkin lymphoma and for identifying diagnostic cells. Option A is inappropriate given the constellation of systemic B symptoms and a mediastinal mass, which should not be attributed to an infectious process without tissue diagnosis. Option C is wrong because fine needle aspiration does not preserve the architecture needed to reliably diagnose lymphoma. Option D is inappropriate given her significant systemic symptoms, which warrant prompt diagnostic evaluation rather than watchful waiting.
  2. The correct answer is A (Hodgkin lymphoma): the binucleate, owl-eye Reed-Sternberg cell within a mixed inflammatory background is the diagnostic hallmark of Hodgkin lymphoma, and her presentation with painless lymphadenopathy, B symptoms (fevers, night sweats, weight loss), and a mediastinal mass in a young adult is a classic clinical picture for this disease, which frequently has a bimodal age distribution favoring young adults and older adults. Option B would show a starry sky pattern of intermediate-sized lymphoid cells rather than Reed-Sternberg cells. Option C would occur in a patient with a preceding history of CLL, not a de novo presentation in a young woman. Option D would show a nodular, follicular growth pattern of small cleaved cells rather than Reed-Sternberg cells.
  3. The correct answer is A (The Ann Arbor staging system): the Ann Arbor staging system, based on the number and location of involved lymph node regions relative to the diaphragm and the presence or absence of extranodal extension and B symptoms, is the standard system used to stage both Hodgkin and non-Hodgkin lymphoma and directly guides treatment intensity and prognosis. Option B is a prognostic scoring tool used for non-Hodgkin lymphoma risk stratification rather than an anatomic staging system. Option C is the staging system used for chronic lymphocytic leukemia, an unrelated disease. Option D is the staging system used specifically for multiple myeloma, not lymphoma.
  4. The correct answer is B (Transfuse using a restrictive threshold around 8 g/dL given his cardiovascular disease): patients with active cardiovascular disease are generally given a somewhat higher transfusion threshold, around 8 g/dL, than the roughly 7 g/dL threshold used in otherwise healthy stable patients, since these patients have less physiologic reserve to compensate for reduced oxygen-carrying capacity. Option A sets the threshold far too low and does not account for his cardiovascular risk. Option C targets an unnecessarily high hemoglobin level that exposes the patient to needless transfusion risk without added benefit. Option D fails to account for his elevated-risk threshold and could allow clinically significant anemia to persist unnecessarily before surgery.
  5. The correct answer is B (Febrile nonhemolytic transfusion reaction): febrile nonhemolytic transfusion reaction is the most common transfusion reaction and results from cytokines accumulated in stored donor blood products, producing fever and chills without hemodynamic instability, hemolysis, or a clerical mismatch, exactly as described here, and it is prevented in future transfusions by using leukoreduced blood products. Option A would show evidence of hemolysis such as hemoglobinuria, flank pain, and hypotension, none of which are present here, and would typically reflect a clerical ABO mismatch. Option C presents with acute hypoxemia and pulmonary infiltrates rather than isolated fever with stable oxygen saturation. Option D presents with hypotension, urticaria, or respiratory compromise rather than isolated fever and chills.
  6. The correct answer is C (Using washed blood products that have had donor plasma proteins removed): patients with selective IgA deficiency can develop anti-IgA antibodies and experience severe anaphylactic reactions when exposed to IgA present in donor plasma, so washed blood products, which have donor plasma proteins removed, are used to prevent this reaction in future transfusions. Option A may reduce mild allergic symptoms but is not sufficient to reliably prevent a severe anaphylactic reaction driven by anti-IgA antibodies. Option B addresses volume-related reactions such as circulatory overload rather than an IgA-mediated anaphylactic reaction. Option D addresses ABO and Rh compatibility rather than the IgA-specific mechanism responsible for this reaction.
  7. The correct answer is B (Transfusion-associated circulatory overload from excessive volume infused too quickly): transfusion-associated circulatory overload results from infusing blood products faster or in greater volume than a patient's cardiovascular system, especially one already compromised by heart failure, can accommodate, producing hypertension and signs of volume overload such as elevated jugular venous pressure and pulmonary edema, in contrast to the hypotension typically seen with TRALI. Option A classically presents with hypotension rather than hypertension and occurs from an immune mechanism rather than simple volume overload. Option C would show hemolysis markers, fever, and hypotension rather than isolated volume overload. Option D would present with urticaria, wheezing, or hypotension rather than isolated pulmonary edema and hypertension.
  8. The correct answer is C (Stop the transfusion immediately and begin aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation): this presentation represents an acute hemolytic transfusion reaction from ABO incompatibility caused by a clerical error, and the immediate priority is to stop the transfusion right away to prevent further hemolysis, followed by aggressive fluid resuscitation to protect renal function and support blood pressure. Option A is dangerously inadequate because continuing to infuse incompatible blood, even slowly, continues the hemolytic process. Option B is wrong because the transfusion must be stopped entirely, not merely slowed, and corticosteroids are not the priority intervention here. Option D is dangerous because resuming transfusion once symptoms improve does not address the underlying incompatibility and risks further hemolysis.
  9. The correct answer is A (Elevated free plasma hemoglobin with a markedly reduced haptoglobin level): free plasma hemoglobin and a markedly reduced haptoglobin reflect active intravascular hemolysis, which is the hallmark of an acute hemolytic transfusion reaction from ABO incompatibility and is not seen in a febrile nonhemolytic reaction, which involves cytokine release without any hemolysis occurring. Option B reflects a nonspecific inflammatory response that can be seen with either type of reaction and does not distinguish between them. Option C would not be expected in an acute hemolytic transfusion reaction unless disseminated intravascular coagulation had also developed, and it is not the most specific finding for hemolysis itself. Option D would argue against, not support, ongoing hemolysis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Platelet transfusion should be reserved for active bleeding or given immediately before the surgical procedure itself): in ITP, circulating antiplatelet antibodies will rapidly destroy transfused platelets under ordinary circumstances, so platelet transfusion is generally reserved for active, significant bleeding or given immediately before an invasive procedure such as splenectomy, timed so that at least some transfused platelets are available during the highest-risk window. Option A is wrong because giving platelets well in advance of surgery allows antibody-mediated destruction to occur before they are needed. Option B is too absolute, since platelet transfusion can still provide transient benefit immediately before a procedure despite ongoing destruction. Option D is wrong because the procedure itself, before spleen removal, is exactly when bleeding risk and platelet support are most needed.
  11. The correct answer is C (Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate): four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate contains concentrated vitamin K-dependent clotting factors and reverses warfarin-associated coagulopathy far more rapidly and completely than fresh frozen plasma, making it the preferred agent for emergent reversal in life-threatening bleeding such as an intracranial hemorrhage. Option A replaces fibrinogen and factor VIII and is not the appropriate agent for reversing warfarin's effect on the vitamin K-dependent factors. Option B corrects anemia and does not address the underlying coagulopathy at all. Option D addresses platelet-related bleeding disorders, not a vitamin K-dependent factor deficiency from warfarin.
  12. The correct answer is D (Differentiation syndrome, treated promptly with corticosteroids while continuing therapy in most cases): differentiation syndrome is a well recognized complication of all-trans retinoic acid therapy in acute promyelocytic leukemia, caused by rapid differentiation and cytokine release from maturing promyelocytes, presenting with fever, dyspnea, pulmonary infiltrates, and edema, and it is treated promptly with corticosteroids, usually without permanently stopping the differentiating agent, since it directly treats the underlying leukemia. Option A does not match this specific clinical syndrome, which has a distinct and well described presentation and treatment separate from a drug allergy. Option B is unrelated, since no transfusion is mentioned and the presentation fits a known drug-specific syndrome. Option C does not explain the pulmonary and edema findings, which reflect a cytokine-mediated syndrome rather than a coagulopathy alone.
  13. The correct answer is B (Antiphospholipid antibody syndrome): recurrent pregnancy loss after ten weeks combined with venous thromboembolism and a prolonged aPTT that fails to correct on a mixing study is the classic laboratory and clinical picture of antiphospholipid antibody syndrome, in which circulating antiphospholipid antibodies interfere with the phospholipid-dependent aPTT assay in vitro while promoting thrombosis in vivo. Option A would present with a bleeding rather than a thrombotic and pregnancy-loss history. Option C would show additional evidence of factor and platelet consumption along with an identifiable underlying trigger, not this stable recurrent pattern. Option D would present with mucocutaneous bleeding rather than thrombosis and recurrent pregnancy loss.
  14. The correct answer is A (Heparin-induced thrombocytopenia): heparin-induced thrombocytopenia typically develops five to ten days after heparin exposure and is caused by antibodies against a platelet factor 4-heparin complex, producing a paradoxical combination of thrombocytopenia together with new arterial or venous thrombosis, which is the defining and dangerous feature of this condition and requires immediate discontinuation of all heparin products. Option B would show a more global consumptive coagulopathy with abnormal PT and aPTT, not an isolated thrombocytopenia with new thrombosis in this specific timeframe. Option C does not capture the distinctive paradoxical thrombosis that defines this heparin-specific syndrome. Option D would show microangiopathic hemolysis with schistocytes, which is not described here.
  15. The correct answer is B (Prompt intravenous factor VIII replacement): acute hemarthrosis in a patient with hemophilia A should be treated promptly with intravenous factor VIII replacement to stop ongoing bleeding into the joint and to prevent the chronic joint damage that results from repeated, undertreated bleeding episodes. Option A is dangerous because aspirin impairs platelet function and can worsen bleeding in a patient who already has a clotting factor deficiency. Option C is not the preferred replacement product for isolated factor VIII deficiency, since factor concentrate provides much more specific and concentrated correction. Option D risks progressive joint damage from repeated undertreated bleeding, which is a well known long-term complication of hemophilia when factor replacement is delayed or withheld.
Case BL3-615

A 38-year-old man with a history of intravenous drug use presents with two months of progressive weight loss, night sweats, and a dry cough. He has not seen a physician in over five years. Fourth-generation HIV antigen and antibody testing returns positive, and a subsequent CD4 count is 90 cells/microliter. Chest imaging reveals diffuse bilateral ground-glass opacities.

  1. The correct answer is A (Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia): a CD4 count below 200 cells per microliter defines a substantially increased risk for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, which classically presents with progressive dyspnea, a dry cough, and diffuse bilateral ground-glass opacities on imaging, exactly as described in this patient, and this combination is one of the most heavily tested opportunistic infections in HIV medicine. Option B typically presents more acutely with focal consolidation and productive cough rather than this subacute, diffuse ground-glass pattern. Option C typically occurs at CD4 counts below 50 and usually presents with disseminated symptoms such as fevers, weight loss, and diarrhea rather than this isolated pulmonary picture. Option D would present with characteristic cutaneous or endobronchial violaceous lesions rather than diffuse ground-glass infiltrates alone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Prophylaxis against both Pneumocystis jirovecii and Toxoplasma gondii, given his CD4 count below 200 and below 100): a CD4 count below 200 warrants Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia prophylaxis and a CD4 count below 100 additionally warrants Toxoplasma gondii prophylaxis, so at 90 cells per microliter both should be started, typically with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, which conveniently covers both organisms. Option A is wrong because cytomegalovirus prophylaxis is not routinely indicated at this CD4 level in the way Pneumocystis and Toxoplasma prophylaxis are. Option B is wrong because both Pneumocystis and Toxoplasma prophylaxis are already indicated at his current CD4 count, not only below 50. Option C incorrectly omits Pneumocystis prophylaxis, which is clearly indicated at this CD4 level, and Mycobacterium avium complex prophylaxis is not yet indicated until the count falls below 50.
  3. The correct answer is D (Antiretroviral therapy should be started promptly regardless of CD4 count, since early treatment reduces disease progression and transmission): current guidelines recommend starting antiretroviral therapy in essentially all patients diagnosed with HIV as soon as possible, regardless of CD4 count, since early initiation reduces the risk of disease progression, opportunistic infection, and onward transmission of the virus to others. Option A incorrectly suggests waiting for CD4 recovery through prophylaxis alone before treating the underlying infection, which is not the current standard of care. Option B is generally not required, since antiretroviral therapy is often started concurrently with treatment of an active opportunistic infection rather than delayed until full resolution. Option C incorrectly restricts therapy to only those with more advanced disease, contrary to current universal treatment recommendations.
  4. The correct answer is C (A deficiency of one of the terminal complement components, C5 through C9): the terminal complement components C5 through C9 assemble the membrane attack complex, which is particularly important for defending against Neisseria species, so a deficiency in one of these components specifically predisposes to recurrent Neisseria infections, including meningitis, while sparing patients from the broader recurrent sinopulmonary infections seen with antibody deficiencies. Option A would cause recurrent infections with a broad range of encapsulated organisms along with low immunoglobulin levels, not the isolated Neisseria pattern with normal immunoglobulins described here. Option B would cause severe, broad-spectrum infections including opportunistic pathogens starting in infancy, not an isolated recurrent Neisseria pattern in a young adult. Option D predisposes to catalase-positive organism infections such as Staphylococcus aureus and Aspergillus, not recurrent Neisseria meningitis.
  5. The correct answer is B (Variable T cell deficiency from thymic hypoplasia or aplasia): the 22q11 deletion causing DiGeorge syndrome disrupts development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia along with the characteristic cardiac defects, hypocalcemia from parathyroid hypoplasia, and distinctive facial features described here, and the resulting variable T cell deficiency predisposes affected infants to recurrent viral, fungal, and opportunistic infections. Option A describes a humoral immunodeficiency such as X-linked agammaglobulinemia, unrelated to the pharyngeal pouch developmental defect described in this vignette. Option C describes leukocyte adhesion deficiency, a distinct disorder not caused by 22q11 deletion. Option D describes a complement pathway defect, unrelated to DiGeorge syndrome's underlying mechanism.
  6. The correct answer is C (Leukocyte adhesion deficiency): leukocyte adhesion deficiency results from a defect in neutrophil surface integrins needed for firm adhesion to and migration across the vascular endothelium, so neutrophils cannot leave the bloodstream to reach sites of infection, producing delayed umbilical cord separation, recurrent infections without pus formation, and a persistently elevated peripheral white blood cell count since neutrophils accumulate in circulation rather than migrating into tissue. Option A produces recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms and granuloma formation, not this specific pattern of delayed cord separation and absent pus. Option B would present with much broader, more severe infections including opportunistic organisms starting very early in life. Option D would present with recurrent sinopulmonary infections related to low immunoglobulin levels, not this specific neutrophil migration defect.
  7. The correct answer is C (Chronic granulomatous disease): chronic granulomatous disease results from a defective NADPH oxidase complex that impairs the neutrophil oxidative burst, leaving phagocytes unable to kill catalase-positive organisms such as Staphylococcus aureus and Aspergillus, and it is confirmed with an abnormal dihydrorhodamine or nitroblue tetrazolium test showing reduced oxidative burst activity, consistent with this child's recurrent infections and the panel shown. Option A would present with much broader, more severe, and earlier-onset infections including viral and fungal opportunists beyond just catalase-positive organisms. Option B would present with recurrent sinopulmonary infections from encapsulated bacteria and low immunoglobulin levels, not this catalase-positive organism pattern. Option D would predispose specifically to recurrent Neisseria infections rather than Staphylococcus and Aspergillus.
  8. The correct answer is A (Mast cell and basophil degranulation triggered by IgE bound to the cell surface): this presentation is a classic type I hypersensitivity reaction, in which pre-formed IgE antibodies bound to mast cells and basophils bind the food allergen and trigger rapid degranulation, releasing histamine and other mediators that cause the immediate urticaria, angioedema, bronchospasm, and hypotension of anaphylaxis. Option B describes type III hypersensitivity, which produces a delayed, immune complex-mediated reaction rather than this immediate presentation. Option C describes type II hypersensitivity, a distinct antibody-mediated cytotoxic mechanism not responsible for anaphylaxis. Option D describes type IV hypersensitivity, which is delayed by one to several days and mediated by T cells rather than IgE, the opposite timeline from what is described here.
  9. The correct answer is C (Type II hypersensitivity): Goodpasture syndrome is a type II hypersensitivity reaction in which antibodies bind directly to a fixed tissue antigen, in this case type IV collagen within the glomerular and alveolar basement membranes, producing the characteristic linear immunofluorescence pattern and causing simultaneous pulmonary hemorrhage and glomerulonephritis. Option A describes an IgE-mediated immediate reaction, an entirely different mechanism from direct antibody binding to a fixed tissue antigen. Option B describes a delayed, T cell-mediated reaction without antibody deposition, which does not match the linear antibody staining described here. Option D describes immune complex deposition, which produces a granular rather than linear immunofluorescence pattern.
  10. The correct answer is B (Deposition of antigen-antibody immune complexes in tissues, activating complement): serum sickness is the prototypical type III hypersensitivity reaction, occurring roughly one to two weeks after exposure to a foreign protein such as horse serum-derived antivenom, once enough antibody has formed to create immune complexes with the circulating antigen; these complexes deposit in tissues such as joints and skin and activate complement, producing fever, urticaria, arthralgias, and lymphadenopathy. Option A describes an immediate reaction occurring within minutes, not the delayed one to two week timeline described here. Option C describes a type II mechanism targeting a specific cell type, not the multisystem immune complex process described. Option D describes a type IV reaction, which does not involve antibody or immune complex formation at all.
  11. The correct answer is C (Sensitized T lymphocytes releasing cytokines that recruit inflammatory cells over one to several days): allergic contact dermatitis, as with nickel exposure, is a type IV delayed hypersensitivity reaction in which T lymphocytes previously sensitized to the allergen are reactivated upon re-exposure and recruit additional inflammatory cells over one to several days, producing the characteristic delayed, localized, pruritic dermatitis pattern confirmed by patch testing. Option A describes an immediate IgE-mediated reaction, which would occur within minutes rather than days and would not be confirmed by patch testing. Option B describes a type III mechanism, which does not match this cell-mediated, delayed presentation. Option D describes a type II mechanism involving direct antibody-mediated cell destruction, which is not how contact dermatitis develops.
  12. The correct answer is D (X-linked agammaglobulinemia): X-linked agammaglobulinemia results from a mutation in Bruton tyrosine kinase that blocks B cell maturation, leading to absent circulating B cells and markedly low levels of all immunoglobulin classes, with normal T cell function, and it classically presents with recurrent infections by encapsulated organisms once maternal antibody protection wanes around six months of age, exactly as described in this infant. Option A involves a T cell, not a B cell, defect and would show abnormal rather than normal T cell numbers and function. Option B involves a neutrophil oxidative burst defect and would not cause absent B cells or low immunoglobulins. Option C involves a neutrophil adhesion and migration defect, unrelated to B cell development or immunoglobulin levels.
  13. The correct answer is A (Systemic lupus erythematosus can directly cause an antibody-mediated warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia): systemic lupus erythematosus is a well recognized cause of warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia, in which disease-associated autoantibodies bind red cell surface antigens and cause a positive direct Coombs test with hemolysis, illustrating how this multisystem autoimmune disease can directly produce hematologic manifestations rather than these findings being incidental. Option B is wrong because a positive Coombs test with hemolysis markers points specifically to an autoimmune hemolytic process rather than iron deficiency, which would not cause a positive Coombs test. Option C is wrong because anti-double-stranded DNA antibodies are directed against nuclear DNA and are not the antibodies responsible for red cell destruction in this condition. Option D is wrong because there is no transfusion history described, and this presentation reflects disease-related autoimmune hemolysis rather than a transfusion reaction.
  14. The correct answer is B (Felty syndrome): Felty syndrome describes the triad of rheumatoid arthritis, splenomegaly, and neutropenia, and it predisposes affected patients to recurrent bacterial infections due to the associated neutropenia, representing an important hematologic complication of long-standing rheumatoid arthritis to recognize on the exam. Option A describes an autoimmune exocrine gland disorder causing dry eyes and dry mouth, unrelated to neutropenia and splenomegaly. Option C describes a distinct hypercoagulable autoimmune condition, not a triad involving neutropenia and splenomegaly in a rheumatoid arthritis patient. Option D describes the combination of autoimmune hemolytic anemia and immune thrombocytopenia together, a different hematologic syndrome unrelated to rheumatoid arthritis specifically.
  15. The correct answer is A (Common variable immunodeficiency): common variable immunodeficiency is characterized by low immunoglobulin levels and impaired antibody responses to vaccination despite normal circulating B cell numbers, and unlike most primary antibody deficiencies it classically presents later, often in the second or third decade of life, with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, matching this patient's presentation closely. Option B would present in infancy with absent, not normal, circulating B cell numbers. Option C would show an isolated low IgA level with normal IgG and IgM, not the broader pattern described here, and often causes milder or no symptoms. Option D presents in early infancy with severe, broad-spectrum, often opportunistic infections rather than this later-onset, milder pattern.
  16. The correct answer is D (Overlapping autoimmune mechanisms, including antibody-mediated hemolysis, immune thrombocytopenia, and antiphospholipid antibodies, together explain her findings): systemic lupus erythematosus frequently produces overlapping autoimmune hematologic manifestations, including warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia from red cell-directed autoantibodies, immune thrombocytopenia from platelet-directed autoantibodies, and a prolonged aPTT from antiphospholipid antibodies interfering with the assay in vitro, and recognizing that all three findings can arise from one underlying autoimmune disease is the key teaching point tying this domain together. Option A does not explain a positive Coombs test or antiphospholipid-associated aPTT prolongation, both of which reflect immune rather than inherited factor mechanisms. Option B is not supported by the vignette, which describes findings entirely explained by known autoimmune disease rather than any evidence of marrow infiltration. Option C is less likely given the chronic autoimmune disease context and lack of any described acute infectious trigger.
  17. The correct answer is A (Empiric trial of a proton pump inhibitor with lifestyle modification): a patient under 60 with typical reflux symptoms and no alarm features such as dysphagia, weight loss, or bleeding can be started on empiric acid suppression without prior endoscopy. Option B is incorrect because endoscopy is reserved for patients with alarm features or those who fail empiric therapy. Option C is not the initial step since pH monitoring is used when the diagnosis is unclear or before an antireflux procedure. Option D evaluates motility disorders rather than typical reflux disease and is not indicated here.
  18. The correct answer is D (Continue proton pump inhibitor therapy with surveillance endoscopy at an interval based on the absence of dysplasia): this biopsy confirms nondysplastic Barrett esophagus, which is managed with ongoing acid suppression and periodic surveillance endoscopy rather than immediate intervention. Option A is far too aggressive since esophagectomy is reserved for invasive carcinoma, not nondysplastic metaplasia. Option B is wrong because acid suppression should continue to reduce ongoing esophageal injury. Option C is unnecessary since a single adequate biopsy showing no dysplasia does not require immediate repeat testing.
  19. The correct answer is A (Confirm eradication with a urea breath test after holding the proton pump inhibitor for one to two weeks): eradication testing is recommended after treatment, and acid suppressing medication must be held beforehand to avoid a false negative result. Option B is incorrect because routine repeat endoscopy is reserved for gastric ulcers, which carry malignant potential, not duodenal ulcers. Option C is wrong because confirming eradication is still recommended, particularly for a complicated or high-risk ulcer. Option D is incorrect because serologic antibody testing remains positive long after successful treatment and cannot be used to confirm cure.
  20. The correct answer is C (Upper endoscopy with biopsy of any suspicious lesion): an older patient with new dyspepsia, weight loss, and iron deficiency anemia has alarm features that raise concern for gastric malignancy and warrants prompt endoscopic evaluation with biopsy. Option D delays diagnosis in a patient who already has alarm features requiring endoscopy rather than empiric therapy. Option A addresses biliary disease, which does not explain this constellation of findings. Option B fails to investigate the underlying cause of the anemia and weight loss, which could represent a treatable malignancy.
  21. The correct answer is A (Intravenous octreotide and intravenous ceftriaxone): suspected variceal hemorrhage in a cirrhotic patient should be treated empirically with a splanchnic vasoconstrictor such as octreotide along with prophylactic antibiotics, both started before endoscopy to reduce bleeding and lower the risk of infection and rebleeding. Option B omits the antibiotic, which reduces mortality and rebleeding risk in this setting. Option C uses the oral route, which is inappropriate in an unstable, actively bleeding patient. Option D again omits the antibiotic, which is a standard part of empiric therapy for suspected variceal bleeding.
  22. The correct answer is B (Diverticular bleeding): painless, brisk hematochezia in an older adult without abdominal pain is the classic presentation of bleeding from an eroded vessel at the neck of a colonic diverticulum. Option C typically presents with severe abdominal pain out of proportion to examination findings, not painless bleeding alone. Option D would present with sudden severe abdominal pain and signs of peritonitis rather than isolated rectal bleeding. Option A is a complication of severe colitis with abdominal distension and systemic toxicity, not an isolated bleeding episode.
  23. The correct answer is C (Digestion and absorption of blood protein from an upper gastrointestinal source raises nitrogen load): blood protein sitting in the small bowel from an upper GI bleed is digested and its nitrogen absorbed, raising blood urea nitrogen out of proportion to creatinine, a classic clue favoring an upper GI source. Option D describes the opposite mechanism from what is happening and would not explain this specific ratio pattern. Option A is incorrect since a disproportionate BUN rise favors an upper, not lower, source. Option B describes a mechanism unrelated to the classic BUN to creatinine ratio finding in GI bleeding.
  24. The correct answer is B (Transfuse to maintain hemoglobin above approximately 7 g/dL): a restrictive transfusion strategy targeting a hemoglobin threshold near 7 g/dL is appropriate for a hemodynamically stable patient without active cardiac disease and has been shown to improve outcomes compared with a more liberal strategy. Option C describes a liberal threshold reserved for patients with active cardiac ischemia or ongoing uncontrolled bleeding, which does not apply here. Option D delays necessary transfusion in a patient who is already significantly anemic. Option A is not a standard transfusion trigger used in this clinical context.
  25. The correct answer is A (Crohn disease): skip lesions, transmural inflammation with granulomas, and perianal fistulizing disease are all classic features of Crohn disease, which can affect any segment of the gastrointestinal tract. Option B is characterized by continuous mucosal and submucosal inflammation beginning in the rectum, without granulomas or fistulas of this type. Option C is a functional disorder without mucosal inflammation or granulomas on biopsy. Option D typically presents acutely in older patients with vascular risk factors and shows a distinct histologic pattern of mucosal necrosis rather than granulomatous transmural disease.
  26. The correct answer is D (Begin surveillance colonoscopy with biopsies approximately eight to ten years after diagnosis): patients with long-standing ulcerative colitis have an increased colorectal cancer risk that rises with disease duration, and surveillance colonoscopy with biopsies is generally initiated around eight to ten years after diagnosis. Option A begins surveillance earlier than needed for a patient without additional risk factors. Option B is incorrect because even limited disease still carries elevated risk over time, though the risk profile differs from extensive colitis. Option C is inappropriate because surveillance is meant to detect dysplasia before symptoms develop, not to wait for a new symptom to appear.
  27. The correct answer is C (Urgent surgical evaluation for possible colectomy): this presentation is toxic megacolon, and failure to improve after 48 to 72 hours of intravenous corticosteroids in this setting is an indication for urgent surgical evaluation given the risk of perforation. Option D prolongs medical therapy in a patient who has already failed an adequate steroid trial and who is at risk of a life-threatening complication. Option A is not effective for an acute severe flare and would not address the emergency at hand. Option B is unsafe given the risk of colonic perforation and sepsis in untreated toxic megacolon.
  28. The correct answer is C (Transition to an anti-TNF biologic agent): 5-aminosalicylate agents have limited efficacy in Crohn disease, and a patient with ongoing symptoms and fistulizing disease despite mesalamine should be escalated to a more effective agent such as an anti-TNF biologic. Option D relies on a medication class known to be less effective for Crohn disease and delays appropriate escalation. Option A is not the first step for medically manageable fistulizing disease without evidence of an abscess or fixed stricture. Option B combines two agents from a class already known to be inadequate for this indication.
  29. The correct answer is A (Prior vaccination against hepatitis B): surface antibody positivity without core antibody indicates immunity from vaccination rather than natural infection, since the vaccine contains only surface antigen and does not generate a core antibody response. Option B would also show a positive core antibody, since natural infection generates antibody to the core antigen. Option C would show a positive surface antigen, indicating ongoing active infection, which is not present here. Option D, the window period, is characterized by a positive IgM core antibody with negative surface antigen and negative surface antibody, a different pattern from this one.
  30. The correct answer is B (Initiate direct acting antiviral therapy now): current guidance recommends treatment for essentially all patients with chronic hepatitis C regardless of fibrosis stage, since eradication prevents progression and transmission even in patients with minimal liver damage. Option C unnecessarily delays a highly effective, well-tolerated treatment and allows continued risk of progression and transmission. Option D is incorrect because normal liver enzymes do not exclude significant infection or the benefit of treatment. Option A waits far too long, since treatment is most beneficial before advanced liver disease develops.
  31. The correct answer is D (AST to ALT ratio greater than 2, favoring alcoholic hepatitis): the values given produce an AST to ALT ratio above 2, a classic pattern seen in alcoholic liver disease, distinct from most other causes of transaminase elevation where ALT typically exceeds AST. Option A describes a pattern more typical of viral hepatitis, not the ratio calculated from these values. Option B does not match the given values, which show clear AST elevation as well. Option C describes a different pattern not reflected by this specific ratio, and autoimmune hepatitis does not typically produce an AST-predominant pattern like this.
  32. The correct answer is B (Bilirubin, albumin, INR, ascites, and hepatic encephalopathy): these five variables make up the Child-Pugh classification, which is used to stratify severity of cirrhosis and estimate operative risk. Option C lists variables that are not part of this scoring system. Option D describes the components of the MELD score, which is used primarily to prioritize patients for liver transplantation rather than to estimate general surgical risk. Option A lists factors that are not part of any standard cirrhosis severity score used for operative planning.
  33. The correct answer is B (A serum-ascites albumin gradient of 2.6 g/dL indicates portal hypertension as the cause): subtracting the ascitic fluid albumin from the serum albumin gives a gradient of 2.6 g/dL, and a gradient of 1.1 g/dL or greater indicates a portal hypertensive cause such as cirrhosis. Option C describes the wrong interpretation, since a gradient this high favors portal hypertension rather than a malignant or infectious low-gradient cause. Option D states an incorrect gradient value that does not match these numbers and also mismatches the association, since cirrhosis produces a high, not low, gradient. Option A is unnecessary since the gradient is calculated directly from paired serum and ascitic fluid albumin values.
  34. The correct answer is B (Intravenous antibiotics with albumin infusion): an ascitic fluid neutrophil count of 250 cells/mm3 or higher confirms spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, which is treated with intravenous antibiotics, and albumin infusion is added in patients with significant liver or renal dysfunction to reduce the risk of hepatorenal syndrome. Option C uses an inadequate route and omits albumin, which provides additional benefit in appropriate patients. Option D fails to treat the underlying infection, which requires antibiotic therapy. Option A delays urgently needed treatment for a potentially life-threatening infection.
  35. The correct answer is C (Lactulose to reduce ammonia absorption): hepatic encephalopathy precipitated by constipation and opioid use is treated with lactulose, which acidifies the colon and traps ammonia as ammonium for excretion, along with addressing the precipitating factors. Option D treats cerebral edema from other causes, not the ammonia-mediated mechanism of hepatic encephalopathy. Option A is incorrect since a high protein diet would worsen ammonia production and encephalopathy in this setting. Option B is the opposite of appropriate management, since a bowel regimen such as lactulose should be started, not discontinued, given his constipation.
  36. The correct answer is C (Aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation with early enteral feeding as tolerated): initial management of acute pancreatitis centers on aggressive fluid resuscitation, pain control, and early enteral nutrition as tolerated, which improves outcomes compared to prolonged bowel rest. Option D reflects outdated practice, since early enteral feeding is now preferred over prolonged fasting and parenteral nutrition. Option A is not indicated in uncomplicated pancreatitis without infected necrosis or another surgical indication. Option B is not appropriate since antibiotics are reserved for confirmed or strongly suspected infection, not given empirically in uncomplicated pancreatitis.
  37. The correct answer is A (Delayed drainage of the collection once it is well organized, with antibiotics): infected pancreatic necrosis is suspected given gas within the collection and clinical deterioration, and in a stable patient, drainage is typically delayed until the collection has organized, paired with antibiotic therapy. Option B is not the preferred initial approach, since immediate aggressive surgery in an unorganized collection carries higher morbidity than a staged, delayed approach in a stable patient. Option C fails to address a likely infection requiring intervention. Option D is inadequate since drainage of an infected necrotic collection is typically required in addition to antibiotics.
  38. The correct answer is B (Biliary colic): transient right upper quadrant pain after a fatty meal that resolves completely, without fever or ongoing tenderness, is classic for biliary colic caused by a stone temporarily obstructing the cystic duct. Option C involves persistent inflammation with fever and a positive Murphy sign, not a transient, fully resolving episode. Option D presents with fever, jaundice, and pain together as part of the Charcot triad, which is not described here. Option A would present with epigastric pain radiating to the back along with elevated pancreatic enzymes, not a brief self-resolving episode.
  39. The correct answer is B (Intravenous antibiotics with cholecystectomy within 72 hours): this presentation is acute cholecystitis, confirmed by ultrasound findings and a positive Murphy sign, and standard management is intravenous antibiotics followed by early cholecystectomy, ideally within 72 hours in a patient who can tolerate surgery. Option C is inappropriate given the systemic signs of infection that warrant inpatient management. Option D is used for common bile duct stones causing obstruction or cholangitis, not for a stone impacted in the gallbladder neck causing cholecystitis. Option A risks progression to gallbladder gangrene or perforation without definitive treatment.
  40. The correct answer is A (Urgent biliary decompression, most often by ERCP, along with intravenous antibiotics): fever, jaundice, and right upper quadrant pain with new hypotension and confusion represent the Reynolds pentad of severe ascending cholangitis, a medical emergency requiring prompt biliary decompression and antibiotics. Option B is inadequate for a critically ill patient with evidence of sepsis and biliary obstruction. Option C delays urgently needed decompression and does not address the acute obstructing process directly. Option D risks clinical deterioration and death by delaying emergency intervention.
  41. The correct answer is D (Acalculous cholecystitis): gallbladder inflammation without stones in a critically ill patient with prolonged fasting and mechanical ventilation reflects bile stasis and ischemia, a presentation known as acalculous cholecystitis, which carries a higher risk of gangrene and perforation. Option A refers to a stone within the common bile duct, which is not described here since no stones are seen at all. Option B requires the presence of gallstones by definition, which are absent in this ultrasound. Option C would typically show a mass lesion rather than diffuse wall thickening in a critically ill patient with this clinical context.
  42. The correct answer is C (CT imaging of the abdomen and pelvis): this presentation is classic for appendicitis, and CT imaging is the standard diagnostic test in most adults with an equivocal presentation to confirm the diagnosis before proceeding to treatment. Option D skips confirmatory imaging in a patient whose presentation, while classic, still benefits from imaging confirmation in standard adult practice. Option A is not indicated in the acute evaluation of suspected appendicitis and carries perforation risk in an inflamed bowel segment. Option B is unsafe given the risk of appendiceal perforation if the diagnosis is missed.
  43. The correct answer is D (Abdominal ultrasound): ultrasound is often preferred as the first imaging test for suspected appendicitis in pregnant patients and children to avoid ionizing radiation exposure. Option A carries radiation exposure that is preferably avoided as a first-line test in pregnancy when an alternative is available. Option B has low sensitivity and specificity for appendicitis and is not the preferred initial test. Option C evaluates the biliary tree and pancreatic duct, not the appendix, and is not appropriate for this presentation.
  44. The correct answer is D (Hospitalization with intravenous antibiotics and percutaneous drainage of the abscess): complicated diverticulitis with an abscess generally requires hospitalization, intravenous antibiotics, and percutaneous drainage when the abscess is large enough or fails to resolve with antibiotics alone. Option A is appropriate for uncomplicated diverticulitis without an abscess, not for this complicated presentation. Option B is contraindicated during an acute diverticulitis episode because of the risk of perforation. Option C is not the first-line approach for a stable patient with a drainable abscess, since drainage and antibiotics are typically tried first.
  45. The correct answer is C (Colonoscopy in six to eight weeks after resolution of the acute episode): colonoscopy is recommended after resolution of an episode of diverticulitis to exclude an underlying colorectal malignancy that could mimic or coexist with diverticular disease. Option D is unsafe because colonoscopy during active diverticulitis carries an increased risk of perforation. Option A misses the opportunity to exclude malignancy, which is standard practice after a diverticulitis episode. Option B is not appropriate during the acute phase for the same perforation risk concern as colonoscopy.
  46. The correct answer is B (Colorectal malignancy or sigmoid volvulus): in a patient without prior abdominal surgery, large bowel obstruction is more often caused by colorectal malignancy, volvulus, or a diverticular stricture rather than adhesions. Option C is the most common cause of small bowel obstruction, not large bowel obstruction, and requires a history of prior surgery, which this patient does not have. Option D would typically cause a small bowel obstruction pattern with a palpable groin mass, not isolated sigmoid dilation. Option A presents with pain out of proportion to examination and is a vascular emergency, distinct from the mechanical obstruction pattern described on this CT.
  47. The correct answer is B (Urgent surgical exploration): new pneumatosis on imaging along with fever and worsening pain in the setting of bowel obstruction suggests bowel ischemia, which mandates urgent surgical evaluation rather than continued nonoperative management. Option C delays necessary intervention in a patient with signs of bowel compromise. Option D does not adequately address suspected ischemic bowel, which requires surgical assessment rather than antibiotics alone. Option A is unsafe given the risk of bowel necrosis and perforation with ongoing ischemia.
  48. The correct answer is C (Begin average-risk screening now using colonoscopy, stool-based testing, or CT colonography): average risk adults now begin colorectal cancer screening at age 45 using an accepted modality such as colonoscopy every ten years, annual stool-based testing, or CT colonography at an appropriate interval. Option D reflects an outdated starting age and would delay screening beyond current recommendations. Option A is inappropriate since screening is meant to detect disease before symptoms develop. Option B understates the ongoing testing schedule required, since a single negative test does not substitute for a full recommended screening program.
  49. The correct answer is A (Begin screening at age 40, since that is earlier than ten years before his father's age at diagnosis): a first degree relative diagnosed with colorectal cancer, particularly before age 60, warrants earlier screening, generally beginning at age 40 or ten years before the relative's age at diagnosis, whichever is EARLIER (the smaller, younger age). Ten years before his father's diagnosis at 52 is 42, but 40 is earlier than 42, so screening should begin at 40. Option B applies to average risk individuals and does not account for his elevated familial risk. Option C waits for symptoms rather than screening proactively, which defeats the purpose of early detection. Option D delays screening well beyond what is appropriate given his family history.
  50. The correct answer is C (IgA tissue transglutaminase antibody with a total IgA level): serologic testing with IgA tissue transglutaminase antibody, paired with a total IgA level to exclude selective IgA deficiency, is the preferred initial test for suspected celiac disease. Option D is used to confirm the diagnosis after positive serology rather than as the first-line test. Option A evaluates for parasitic infection, which is not suggested by this presentation and would not explain the iron deficiency pattern described. Option B evaluates the colon rather than the small intestine, which is the site of pathology in celiac disease.
  51. The correct answer is B (Advise reintroduction of gluten before duodenal biopsy to avoid a false negative result): early gluten avoidance can normalize both serology and duodenal histology, so gluten should ideally be reintroduced before biopsy confirmation to avoid a falsely reassuring result. Option C risks a false negative biopsy since her villous changes may already be resolving on a gluten-free diet. Option D does not provide histologic confirmation, which remains part of the standard diagnostic approach. Option A is not sufficient for diagnosis, since symptom improvement alone does not confirm celiac disease and could reflect a placebo effect or an unrelated dietary change.
  52. The correct answer is D (Percutaneous drainage of the abscess with intravenous antibiotics): an intra-abdominal abscess complicating Crohn disease is generally managed initially with percutaneous drainage and antibiotics, reserving surgery for cases that fail this approach or have an inaccessible collection. Option A is more invasive than needed as a first step when percutaneous drainage is feasible. Option B is not effective for an abscess and Crohn disease in general responds poorly to 5-aminosalicylate agents. Option C is unsafe since starting immunosuppressive biologic therapy in the setting of an undrained abscess can worsen the infection.
Case GI-C1

A 56-year-old man with alcohol use disorder presents with hematemesis. He is tachycardic and hypotensive. He has a known history of cirrhosis with prior variceal bleeding two years ago. On examination he has spider angiomata and a fluid wave.

  1. The correct answer is A (Intravenous octreotide and intravenous ceftriaxone, started before endoscopy): in a patient with known cirrhosis and prior variceal bleeding presenting with hematemesis, empiric therapy for suspected variceal hemorrhage includes a splanchnic vasoconstrictor and prophylactic antibiotics, both started before endoscopic confirmation. Option B omits the vasoactive agent and the antibiotic that reduce bleeding and infection risk in this setting. Option C skips endoscopic therapy, which is the first-line intervention, reserving TIPS for bleeding refractory to endoscopic control. Option D is unsafe for an actively bleeding, hemodynamically unstable patient who requires immediate resuscitation and treatment rather than observation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Endoscopic band ligation of the bleeding varix): band ligation is the preferred endoscopic technique for controlling and preventing recurrent esophageal variceal hemorrhage, with lower complication rates than older techniques. Option B describes a technique developed for nonvariceal ulcer bleeding rather than variceal disease. Option C is used for vascular ectasias such as angiodysplasia rather than as first-line therapy for esophageal varices. Option D was the older standard but has largely been replaced by banding as the preferred first-line technique because banding carries a lower complication rate.
  3. The correct answer is D (Transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt placement): a patient with variceal bleeding refractory to repeated endoscopic therapy despite adequate attempts requires portal decompression through a transjugular intrahepatic portosystemic shunt to control ongoing hemorrhage. Option A repeats a strategy that has already failed twice rather than escalating to a more definitive intervention. Option B would remove a therapy that continues to reduce splanchnic blood flow and should be continued, not stopped, in ongoing bleeding. Option C is unsafe in a hemodynamically unstable patient with uncontrolled active bleeding who requires urgent inpatient intervention.
Case GI-C2

A 27-year-old woman presents with a 5-month history of bloody diarrhea, tenesmus, and lower abdominal cramping. She has lost 3 kg. Colonoscopy shows continuous inflammation beginning in the rectum and extending to the mid-transverse colon, without any areas of normal intervening mucosa. Biopsy shows crypt abscesses confined to the mucosa and submucosa without granulomas.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ulcerative colitis): continuous mucosal and submucosal inflammation starting in the rectum and extending proximally without skip lesions, along with crypt abscesses and no granulomas, is the classic pattern of ulcerative colitis. Option A typically shows skip lesions, transmural inflammation, and granulomas, none of which are present in this biopsy description. Option B would show a distinct clinical history of antibiotic exposure and typically a different histologic and epidemiologic pattern rather than this continuous mucosal disease. Option C usually occurs in older patients with vascular risk factors and shows a different, often segmental, distribution correlating with watershed areas rather than a continuous rectosigmoid-to-transverse pattern.
  2. The correct answer is D (Escalate therapy with corticosteroids for induction of remission): a patient with ongoing moderate to severe symptoms despite an adequate trial of 5-aminosalicylate therapy should be escalated to corticosteroids to induce remission, with maintenance therapy adjusted afterward. Option A prolongs a strategy that has already proven inadequate rather than escalating appropriately. Option B is reserved for fulminant disease, toxic megacolon, or medically refractory disease after appropriate medical therapy has been tried, not as the next step after a single failed agent. Option C does not address the underlying inflammation and can be unsafe in active colitis by masking disease severity.
  3. The correct answer is C (Surveillance colonoscopy with biopsies beginning approximately eight to ten years after diagnosis): extensive ulcerative colitis carries an elevated colorectal cancer risk that rises with disease duration, so surveillance colonoscopy with biopsies is recommended beginning around eight to ten years after diagnosis regardless of symptomatic remission. Option D is incorrect because cancer risk relates to cumulative colonic inflammation over time, not current symptom control alone. Option A understates her risk, since her extensive disease requires an earlier and more frequent surveillance schedule than average-risk screening. Option B waits for a new symptom rather than proactively screening for dysplasia, which defeats the purpose of surveillance.
  4. The correct answer is B (Total colectomy given unresectable high-grade dysplasia): high-grade dysplasia in flat, endoscopically unresectable mucosa in a patient with ulcerative colitis is an indication for total colectomy given the substantial risk of coexisting or progressing malignancy. Option C is inadequate surveillance for a finding this concerning and risks progression to invasive cancer. Option D does not treat dysplasia, which is a precancerous change rather than active inflammation responsive to steroids. Option A leaves the remaining colon, which carries the same underlying disease and elevated cancer risk, in place rather than achieving definitive cancer risk reduction, which total colectomy is uniquely able to provide in ulcerative colitis.
Case GI-C3

A 61-year-old woman with known gallstones presents with severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, and vomiting. She has no fever. Laboratory studies show lipase 620 U/L (reference range 10-140 U/L) and total bilirubin 1.0 mg/dL.

  1. The correct answer is D (Gallstone pancreatitis): epigastric pain radiating to the back with a markedly elevated lipase in a patient with known gallstones and a normal bilirubin is consistent with gallstone-induced acute pancreatitis without concurrent biliary obstruction. Option A is a common alternative cause of pancreatitis but is not supported here, since the vignette specifies known gallstone disease rather than a history of alcohol use. Option B would typically show fever, jaundice, and an elevated bilirubin as part of the Charcot triad, none of which are present in this normal bilirubin, afebrile patient. Option C would present with sudden severe pain and peritoneal signs rather than an isolated markedly elevated lipase without free air or peritonitis described.
  2. The correct answer is C (A gallstone has migrated into the common bile duct, causing obstruction and cholangitis): new fever, right upper quadrant pain, and a rising bilirubin with a cholestatic pattern in a patient with known gallstones suggests a stone has passed into the common bile duct, causing obstruction and superimposed infection, that is, ascending cholangitis. Option D does not explain the new fever and cholestatic pattern of bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase elevation, which point toward a biliary rather than purely pancreatic process. Option A is not supported by the clinical history, which centers on known gallstone disease rather than peptic ulcer disease. Option B would not typically produce this specific cholestatic pattern with fever and rising bilirubin in the way that duct obstruction does.
  3. The correct answer is C (Intravenous antibiotics with urgent ERCP for biliary decompression): ascending cholangitis is a medical emergency requiring prompt intravenous antibiotics along with urgent biliary decompression, most often achieved through ERCP with stone extraction or stent placement. Option D is far too delayed and uses an inadequate route of antibiotics for a patient with an acute infected, obstructed biliary system. Option A fails to treat a new, potentially life-threatening infectious process that is unrelated to whether the earlier pancreatitis has resolved. Option B bypasses antibiotics, which should always be given promptly, and open surgical exploration is not the first-line approach when ERCP is available.
  4. The correct answer is A (Chronic NSAID use impairing mucosal prostaglandin-mediated protection): daily NSAID use is a major cause of peptic ulcer disease independent of H pylori infection, since NSAIDs reduce protective prostaglandin synthesis in the gastric and duodenal mucosa. Option B is typically suspected with multiple, refractory, or unusually distal ulcers, not a solitary duodenal ulcer in a patient with a clear NSAID exposure history. Option C is not supported since the patient does not report corticosteroid use and NSAID use already provides a sufficient explanation. Option D is unnecessary since a single negative test in a patient with an alternative clear explanation, chronic NSAID use, does not require repeated testing.
  5. The correct answer is D (Zollinger-Ellison syndrome): recurrent or refractory peptic ulcers with chronic diarrhea and a markedly elevated fasting gastrin level are classic for a gastrin-secreting tumor causing Zollinger-Ellison syndrome. Option A can elevate gastrin through loss of acid-producing parietal cells but does not typically cause recurrent refractory ulcers and diarrhea in this pattern. Option B would not explain markedly elevated gastrin or recurrent structural ulcer disease. Option C is not suggested by any history of self-harm behavior and does not explain the elevated gastrin level, which has a clear physiologic explanation.
  6. The correct answer is D (Repeat upper endoscopy in six to eight weeks to confirm healing): gastric ulcers require follow-up endoscopy after treatment to confirm healing and to exclude an underlying malignancy, since a malignant gastric ulcer can appear identical to a benign one at initial inspection. Option A is insufficient since biopsy alone at a single time point does not confirm healing or fully exclude malignancy if initial biopsies were nondiagnostic. Option B delays necessary confirmation of healing well beyond the recommended interval. Option C is far too aggressive without first confirming whether the biopsies show malignancy or whether the ulcer heals with medical therapy alone.
  7. The correct answer is C (Endoscopy is not routinely required since he lacks alarm features and responds well to therapy): a patient with typical reflux symptoms that are well controlled on therapy and no alarm features such as dysphagia, weight loss, or bleeding does not require routine endoscopy. Option D is incorrect since long-term proton pump inhibitor use alone, without alarm features, is not an automatic indication for annual endoscopy. Option A is not appropriate since gastric cancer screening is not routinely recommended in this population without specific risk factors. Option B is not an equivalent or preferred substitute for endoscopy and is not indicated in this well-controlled patient at all.
  8. The correct answer is B (Endoscopic eradication therapy such as radiofrequency ablation): high-grade dysplasia in Barrett esophagus is managed with endoscopic eradication therapy, which effectively reduces progression to invasive cancer while avoiding the morbidity of esophagectomy in appropriate candidates. Option C is inadequate for high-grade dysplasia, which requires active intervention rather than continued observation alone. Option D is reserved for cases with invasive carcinoma or those not amenable to endoscopic therapy, not as the first approach for high-grade dysplasia alone. Option A would remove acid suppression that helps prevent ongoing esophageal injury and should be continued, not stopped.
  9. The correct answer is D (Upper endoscopy with biopsy of any mass or stricture): progressive dysphagia with weight loss in an older patient with significant smoking history and chronic reflux represents alarm features concerning for esophageal malignancy and warrants prompt endoscopy with biopsy. Option A delays diagnosis in a patient who already has alarm features mandating endoscopy rather than empiric therapy. Option B can suggest a stricture or mass but does not provide tissue diagnosis, which is essential when malignancy is suspected. Option C dismisses a symptom that specifically requires urgent evaluation rather than reassurance.
  10. The correct answer is D (Multiphase contrast-enhanced CT or MRI of the liver): a hepatic lesion detected in a cirrhotic patient with an elevated alpha-fetoprotein warrants multiphase cross-sectional imaging, which can often diagnose hepatocellular carcinoma noninvasively based on characteristic enhancement patterns. Option A is not the routine first step, since noninvasive imaging can often establish the diagnosis and biopsy carries a small risk of tumor seeding. Option B delays the evaluation of a lesion with concerning features in a high-risk patient. Option C is incorrect since cirrhotic patients have a substantially elevated risk of hepatocellular carcinoma, which is precisely why they undergo surveillance.
  11. The correct answer is D (Progressive destruction of small intrahepatic bile ducts leading to cholestasis and potential cirrhosis): primary biliary cholangitis is an autoimmune condition causing progressive destruction of small intrahepatic bile ducts, leading to cholestasis and, if untreated or unresponsive to therapy, progression to cirrhosis. Option A describes primary sclerosing cholangitis, which involves large duct strictures rather than the small duct destruction seen in primary biliary cholangitis. Option B mischaracterizes this as an acute, self-limited process, when it is in fact a chronic, progressive condition. Option C is incorrect since primary biliary cholangitis occurs overwhelmingly in middle-aged women, not older men.
  12. The correct answer is A (Primary sclerosing cholangitis): a beaded appearance of multifocal strictures and dilations affecting both intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts in a patient with ulcerative colitis is the classic imaging and clinical presentation of primary sclerosing cholangitis. Option B involves small duct destruction rather than the large duct beading pattern described here and typically presents in middle-aged women rather than being strongly linked to ulcerative colitis. Option C presents with a hepatocellular, not cholestatic, injury pattern and does not produce this ductal beading on imaging. Option D would show a single, focal ductal obstruction rather than the multifocal beaded pattern described here.
  13. The correct answer is A (Corticosteroids, often combined with azathioprine): this presentation, positive autoantibodies with interface hepatitis and a lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate on biopsy, is characteristic of autoimmune hepatitis, which is treated with immunosuppression, typically corticosteroids with or without azathioprine. Option B is the first-line treatment for primary biliary cholangitis, a different cholestatic autoimmune liver disease, not autoimmune hepatitis. Option C treats viral hepatitis, which is not suggested by this serologic and histologic picture. Option D would be appropriate for alcohol-related liver disease, which is not indicated by this clinical and biopsy pattern.
  14. The correct answer is B (Therapeutic phlebotomy to reduce total body iron stores): regular therapeutic phlebotomy is the standard treatment for hereditary hemochromatosis, effectively reducing iron overload and preventing progression to organ damage. Option C is generally reserved for patients who cannot tolerate phlebotomy, such as those with significant anemia, rather than as first-line therapy. Option D alone is insufficient to meaningfully reduce the substantial iron burden present in this condition. Option A delays treatment that is most effective when started before significant organ damage such as cirrhosis develops.
  15. The correct answer is C (Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease): hepatic steatosis on imaging with mild transaminase elevation in a patient with obesity and diabetes, minimal alcohol use, and negative viral serologies is characteristic of nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, closely linked to metabolic risk factors. Option D requires a significant alcohol use history, which this patient does not have. Option A would present acutely with more marked transaminase elevation and typically a positive serologic test, not an incidental steatotic finding. Option B typically presents in younger patients with neuropsychiatric symptoms and distinct copper metabolism abnormalities, not this metabolic risk factor profile.
  16. The correct answer is A (Wilson disease): neuropsychiatric symptoms, a Kayser-Fleischer ring on slit-lamp examination, and low serum ceruloplasmin in a young patient are classic for Wilson disease, a disorder of copper metabolism causing copper accumulation in the liver and brain. Option B typically presents with iron overload findings rather than a corneal copper ring and neuropsychiatric symptoms. Option C presents with liver disease and early emphysema, not this pattern of neuropsychiatric findings and corneal deposition. Option D presents in middle-aged women with cholestatic symptoms rather than this neurologic and ophthalmologic presentation in a young man.
  17. The correct answer is B (Corticosteroid therapy): patients with severe alcoholic hepatitis, as indicated by a high Maddrey discriminant function score, may benefit from corticosteroid therapy in addition to supportive care and abstinence. Option C treats viral hepatitis and has no role in alcoholic liver disease. Option D is not the immediate next step, since transplantation evaluation involves specific criteria and is not appropriate as an immediate universal response to a severe Maddrey score. Option A has no role in alcoholic hepatitis and could be harmful, since iron overload is not the underlying pathology in this condition.
  18. The correct answer is D (Markedly elevated AST and ALT with a prolonged INR): acetaminophen-induced acute liver failure produces marked hepatocellular injury with very high transaminases and impaired hepatic synthetic function reflected by a prolonged INR, along with encephalopathy in severe cases. Option A describes a cholestatic pattern, which is not the classic pattern of acute toxic hepatocellular injury from acetaminophen. Option B would not explain the encephalopathy and coagulopathy described, which reflect significant hepatocellular dysfunction, not isolated bilirubin elevation. Option C describes a pancreatic enzyme pattern unrelated to acetaminophen hepatotoxicity.
  19. The correct answer is C (Bile acid and fat malabsorption from loss of terminal ileal reabsorption): the terminal ileum is the primary site of bile acid reabsorption, and its resection leads to bile acid loss into the colon along with fat malabsorption, producing greasy, foul-smelling, difficult-to-flush stools, a classic pattern of steatorrhea. Option D describes the opposite mechanism, since resection reduces rather than increases bile acid reabsorption. Option A is not supported, since pancreatic enzyme secretion is not increased by ileal resection and this would not explain steatorrhea through this mechanism. Option B is not the most likely explanation given her clear anatomic and surgical history directly explaining her malabsorption.
  20. The correct answer is C (Heyde syndrome, in which aortic stenosis is associated with acquired von Willebrand factor deficiency promoting bleeding from angiodysplasia): the combination of aortic stenosis and bleeding angiodysplasia, known as Heyde syndrome, arises from shear stress across the stenotic valve degrading von Willebrand factor multimers, impairing hemostasis at fragile angiodysplastic lesions. Option D understates a well-recognized clinical association between these two conditions. Option A is not the correct mechanism, since the relationship is hematologic rather than a direct ischemic effect of the valve on the cecum. Option B mischaracterizes the mechanism, which relates to acquired coagulopathy rather than embolization.
  21.
