The correct answer is A (Interventional radiology embolization or urgent surgical evaluation depending on institutional resources): a hemodynamically unstable patient with active extravasation localized on CT angiography requires prompt hemostatic intervention, typically through interventional radiology embolization, with surgery reserved for cases where this is not feasible or successful. Option B is unsafe given ongoing instability and active bleeding requiring urgent, not elective, management. Option C fails to address active, hemodynamically significant bleeding. Option D adds unnecessary delay and does not offer therapeutic intervention, unlike angiography with embolization capability.
  22. The correct answer is A (Clinical improvement along with a decline in celiac-specific antibody titers over time): response to a gluten-free diet in celiac disease is typically monitored through clinical improvement in symptoms along with a progressive decline in celiac-specific antibody titers, such as tissue transglutaminase antibody. Option B is more invasive and frequent than needed for routine monitoring in a child responding clinically and serologically. Option C is incorrect since serologic titers and symptom resolution provide a reliable, noninvasive way to follow response. Option D is used to interpret the validity of the initial antibody test rather than to monitor ongoing treatment response over time.
  23. The correct answer is B (Dermatitis herpetiformis): a pruritic, vesicular rash over extensor surfaces such as the elbows and knees in a patient with celiac disease is characteristic of dermatitis herpetiformis, an immune mediated skin manifestation directly linked to gluten sensitivity. Option C presents as tender subcutaneous nodules, typically on the shins, and is more classically an extraintestinal manifestation of inflammatory bowel disease rather than celiac disease. Option D presents as painful, rapidly enlarging ulcers rather than a pruritic vesicular rash. Option A presents with well-demarcated, silvery scaling plaques rather than the vesicular, intensely pruritic lesions described here.
  24. The correct answer is B (Ischemic colitis): severe abdominal pain out of proportion to examination findings along with bloody diarrhea in a patient with vascular risk factors, including prior aortic surgery, is characteristic of ischemic colitis affecting watershed areas of the colon. Option C typically presents with more localized left lower quadrant pain and fever rather than pain out of proportion to examination with bloody diarrhea. Option D presents with right lower quadrant pain that localizes over time, not diffuse pain out of proportion to examination with bloody diarrhea. Option A does not cause pain out of proportion to examination or bloody diarrhea, since it is a functional rather than an ischemic or inflammatory process.
  25. The correct answer is B (Approximately seven to ten years, as with average-risk screening given a single small low-risk adenoma): a single small tubular adenoma with low-grade dysplasia, completely removed, generally allows a return to an average-risk screening interval rather than more frequent surveillance. Option C is unnecessary and not indicated after a straightforward, completely removed low-risk polyp. Option D is incorrect since ongoing screening remains important given his adenoma history and continued colorectal cancer risk over time. Option A is more frequent than needed for a single low-risk polyp and does not reflect current risk-based surveillance recommendations.
  26. The correct answer is A (Colonoscopy beginning at a substantially younger age than average risk, repeated at a shorter interval such as every one to two years): patients with Lynch syndrome require earlier initiation of colonoscopy and more frequent surveillance intervals given their markedly elevated and accelerated colorectal cancer risk. Option B substantially understates his risk and screening needs given a hereditary cancer syndrome. Option C is inadequate since colonoscopy, not stool-based testing alone, is the recommended modality for this high-risk population given the need for polypectomy capability. Option D confuses genetic confirmation of risk with actual cancer surveillance, which remains essential precisely because the mutation confers ongoing risk.
  27. The correct answer is A (Prophylactic colectomy): familial adenomatous polyposis with hundreds of adenomas and areas of high-grade dysplasia carries a near-certain lifetime risk of colorectal cancer if the colon is not removed, making prophylactic colectomy the standard recommended management. Option B is not feasible or safe given the sheer number of polyps and the presence of high-grade dysplasia, which substantially raises cancer risk if the colon is left in place. Option C has no established role in reducing the polyp burden of familial adenomatous polyposis to a degree that would avoid colectomy. Option D delays definitive treatment in a condition with an extremely high risk of progression to invasive cancer without surgical intervention.
Case CASE-STROKE-1

A 68-year-old right-handed man is brought to the emergency department by his wife because 45 minutes ago he suddenly could not find the right words to answer her question and his right arm became weak. He has a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes and takes lisinopril and metformin. On examination he is alert, blood pressure is 168/94 mmHg, and he has expressive aphasia with right facial droop and right arm weakness (strength 2/5) greater than right leg weakness (strength 4/5). There is no headache, neck stiffness, or vomiting.

  1. The correct answer is A (Left middle cerebral artery): Expressive aphasia (a dominant hemisphere finding) together with contralateral face and arm weakness that is worse than leg weakness is the classic pattern of a middle cerebral artery occlusion, since that vessel supplies the lateral cortex where the face and arm are represented over a larger area than the leg. The anterior cerebral artery would cause leg weakness greater than arm weakness because it supplies the medial motor cortex, the posterior cerebral artery causes visual field loss rather than motor and language deficits, and a basilar artery occlusion causes bilateral or crossed brainstem signs rather than this isolated cortical pattern.
  2. The correct answer is D (Noncontrast head CT): A rapid noncontrast head CT is the essential first study in any patient with acute focal neurologic deficits because it quickly excludes intracranial hemorrhage, which would be a contraindication to thrombolysis, and it can be obtained faster than an MRI. MRI is more sensitive for early ischemia but takes longer to obtain and is not the first test when a time-critical treatment decision is pending. Lumbar puncture and carotid duplex have no role in the first minutes of an acute stroke evaluation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Intravenous alteplase or tenecteplase): This patient presents within 4.5 hours of clearly known onset with a disabling deficit, no hemorrhage on CT, and no contraindications, which is exactly the population that benefits from intravenous thrombolysis. Aspirin alone is used when thrombolysis is not given or after the thrombolysis window has passed, carotid endarterectomy is a secondary prevention procedure performed after the acute period once symptomatic stenosis is confirmed, and therapeutic heparin is not used in acute ischemic stroke because it increases hemorrhagic transformation risk without proven benefit.
  4. The correct answer is C (Left carotid endarterectomy): A symptomatic carotid stenosis of 70 percent or greater on the side corresponding to the patient's deficit is an indication for carotid endarterectomy, which reduces the risk of a future stroke more than medical therapy alone in this population. Warfarin has no proven benefit over antiplatelet therapy for large artery atherosclerotic disease, treating the asymptomatic contralateral vessel is not indicated based on the information given, and observation alone is inappropriate for a symptomatic, high grade stenosis.
  5. The correct answer is A (Lateral medulla, left posterior inferior cerebellar artery territory): The combination of ipsilateral facial sensory loss, Horner syndrome, and ataxia with contralateral body pain and temperature loss is the classic crossed sensory pattern of lateral medullary (Wallenberg) syndrome from a posterior inferior cerebellar artery infarct. A middle cerebral artery stroke produces face and arm weakness on one side rather than this crossed brainstem pattern, an anterior cerebral artery infarct causes leg predominant weakness, and bilateral occipital infarcts cause cortical blindness rather than these findings.
  6. The correct answer is B (Urgent evaluation including echocardiogram and initiation of anticoagulation): Complete resolution of focal deficits with a normal scan defines a transient ischemic attack, which carries a high short-term risk of a completed stroke, especially with atrial fibrillation as an identified cardioembolic source, so urgent risk stratification and anticoagulation are indicated promptly. Discharging without workup ignores this high recurrence risk, waiting six months is far too long for a TIA workup, and thrombolysis is not given once symptoms have already fully resolved.
  7. The correct answer is C (Careful blood pressure control and reversal of any anticoagulation): This presentation and CT appearance are diagnostic of a hypertensive intracerebral hemorrhage, and the priorities are lowering blood pressure to reduce ongoing bleeding and hematoma expansion and reversing any anticoagulant, along with close neurologic monitoring for signs of increasing mass effect. Lumbar puncture is unnecessary once hemorrhage is already seen on CT and could be dangerous with mass effect present, and both thrombolysis and antiplatelet therapy are contraindicated because they would worsen the bleeding.
  8. The correct answer is B (Lumbar puncture looking for xanthochromia): When a thunderclap headache raises concern for subarachnoid hemorrhage and the noncontrast CT is negative, a lumbar puncture evaluating for xanthochromia (from breakdown of red blood cells) is the next step because early CT can miss a small bleed, and a negative CT alone cannot safely exclude this life-threatening diagnosis. Discharging without further workup or treating empirically as migraine would risk missing a ruptured aneurysm, and lumbar spine imaging has no role in this evaluation.
  9. The correct answer is A (Cerebral vasospasm): Vasospasm is the classic delayed complication of subarachnoid hemorrhage, typically appearing between days 3 and 14 after the bleed, and it causes new focal neurologic deficits from delayed cerebral ischemia, which is why nimodipine and careful hemodynamic management are used during this window. Rebleeding is most likely to occur very early and much less likely after successful coiling, hydrocephalus more typically presents with gait and cognitive changes rather than an acute focal deficit, and there is nothing in this vignette suggesting a new cardioembolic event.
  10. The correct answer is D (GCS 8; airway protection should be strongly considered): Eye opening to pain scores 2, incomprehensible sounds score 2, and withdrawal from pain scores 4, giving a total Glasgow Coma Scale of 8, which is the widely used threshold below which airway protection with intubation should be strongly considered because of the risk of losing the ability to protect the airway from aspiration. The other option totals are simply incorrect additions of the same findings, and none of them changes the clinical principle that a GCS this low warrants airway assessment.
  11. The correct answer is C (Obtain an EEG to help estimate recurrence risk before deciding on treatment): After a first unprovoked seizure with a normal metabolic workup and a normal MRI, an EEG helps identify epileptiform abnormalities that would indicate a higher risk of recurrence and could change the decision about starting an antiepileptic drug, since medication is not routinely started after a single seizure with an otherwise low-risk workup. Starting an antiepileptic drug immediately is premature before this information is available, valproic acid should generally be avoided in women of childbearing potential, and lumbar puncture is not a routine part of the first-seizure workup absent signs of infection.
  12. The correct answer is C (Load a second line agent such as intravenous fosphenytoin, valproic acid, or levetiracetam): Status epilepticus that continues despite an adequate benzodiazepine dose calls for a second line intravenous antiepileptic load rather than repeating more benzodiazepine, which increases sedation and respiratory depression risk without added antiseizure benefit at this point. General anesthesia is reserved for refractory status epilepticus that persists despite an adequate second line agent, and observation alone is inappropriate while active seizure activity is ongoing.
  13. The correct answer is A (Absence seizure): Brief, frequent staring spells with abrupt onset and offset, eyelid fluttering, and no postictal confusion are classic for absence seizures, which are a generalized seizure type most common in school-age children and adolescents and are often mistaken for inattention. A focal impaired awareness seizure typically lasts longer and is followed by postictal confusion, myoclonic seizures are brief jerks rather than staring spells, and atonic seizures cause a sudden loss of muscle tone and falls rather than staring.
  14. The correct answer is B (Valproic acid): Valproic acid carries the highest teratogenic risk among commonly used antiepileptic drugs, including neural tube defects and other major malformations, so it is generally avoided in women of childbearing potential when an alternative agent is reasonable. Levetiracetam and lamotrigine are generally considered to have a more favorable safety profile in pregnancy, and while carbamazepine also carries some teratogenic risk, it is not the agent most strongly associated with this specific concern among these choices.
  15. The correct answer is D (Psychogenic nonepileptic seizure): Asynchronous, side-to-side movements, pelvic thrusting, forced eye closure with resistance to opening, a waxing and waning course, and a normal EEG during a captured event are the classic features that distinguish psychogenic nonepileptic seizures from true epileptic seizures, which typically show synchronous, stereotyped movements and a corresponding electrographic correlate. A true generalized tonic-clonic seizure would show synchronous tonic then clonic activity with an EEG correlate, a complex focal seizure typically has an EEG correlate as well, and absence status would show continuous epileptiform discharges rather than a normal EEG.
  16. The correct answer is B (Levetiracetam): Levetiracetam, along with valproic acid, is considered a broad spectrum antiepileptic effective for both focal and generalized seizure types, making it a reasonable choice when the seizure type could evolve from focal to generalized, as described here. Ethosuximide is effective only for absence seizures and has no role in focal epilepsy, and the other two options describe properties that do not correspond to any standard first-line agent for this presentation.
  17. The correct answer is B (Atonic seizure): A sudden loss of muscle tone causing a fall, sometimes called a drop attack, with rapid recovery is the defining feature of an atonic seizure, and the injury risk from repeated falls is why protective helmets are often recommended for children with this seizure type. A myoclonic seizure causes a brief muscle jerk rather than a loss of tone and fall, an absence seizure causes staring without a fall, and a focal aware seizure preserves awareness and does not typically cause sudden loss of postural tone.
  18. The correct answer is B (Carbamazepine can worsen absence and myoclonic seizures despite treating focal seizures effectively): Sodium channel blocking agents such as carbamazepine and phenytoin are effective for focal seizures but can paradoxically worsen absence and myoclonic seizure types, which is why correctly identifying the epilepsy syndrome before choosing therapy matters, and switching to a broad-spectrum agent such as valproic acid or levetiracetam would be appropriate here. Carbamazepine is specifically not well suited to generalized epilepsy syndromes with absence or myoclonic features, nonadherence would not typically produce a new seizure type rather than simply breakthrough of the original pattern, and the medication choice is directly relevant to this change.
  19. The correct answer is C (Induction of general anesthesia with continuous EEG monitoring in the intensive care unit): Refractory status epilepticus, defined as ongoing seizure activity despite an adequate benzodiazepine and a second-line agent, requires induction of general anesthesia (commonly with midazolam, propofol, or pentobarbital infusions) under continuous EEG monitoring in an intensive care setting. Repeating the same dose of a drug that has already failed is not appropriate, oral medication cannot be reliably absorbed or acted quickly enough during active status epilepticus, and discharge is clearly unsafe during ongoing seizure activity.
  20. The correct answer is C (Hyponatremia): Severe or rapidly developing hyponatremia is a classic metabolic cause of provoked seizures, along with hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia, uremia, and hepatic encephalopathy, all of which must be excluded before a seizure is classified as unprovoked epilepsy, since correcting the underlying abnormality is the primary treatment rather than starting a chronic antiepileptic drug. Mild iron deficiency anemia, isolated elevated LDL cholesterol, and mild dehydration alone are not recognized direct causes of acute provoked seizures.
  21. The correct answer is A (Migraine with aura): A unilateral, pulsatile headache lasting hours to a day with nausea, photophobia, phonophobia, worsening with physical activity, and a preceding visual aura is the classic presentation of migraine with aura. Tension-type headache is bilateral and band-like without these associated features or activity-related worsening, cluster headache is much shorter in duration with prominent ipsilateral autonomic features, and medication overuse headache requires a pattern of frequent analgesic use, which is not described here.
  22. The correct answer is B (High flow oxygen or a triptan): This presentation, with brief but severe unilateral periorbital pain, ipsilateral autonomic features, clustering of attacks, and restlessness during the episode, is classic for cluster headache, and the preferred acute treatments are high flow oxygen or a fast-acting triptan. Acetaminophen alone is generally ineffective for the severity of cluster headache, verapamil is used for prevention rather than acute abortive treatment, and mannitol has no role in primary headache management.
  23. The correct answer is B (Tension-type headache): Bilateral, band-like or pressure-type pain without nausea, photophobia, phonophobia, or activity-related worsening is the defining pattern of tension-type headache, the most common primary headache disorder overall. Migraine typically includes at least some of the associated features and is often unilateral, cluster headache is much more severe and briefer with autonomic features, and idiopathic intracranial hypertension usually presents with additional signs such as papilledema or visual disturbance.
  24. The correct answer is A (Empiric high dose glucocorticoids and urgent inflammatory markers with temporal artery biopsy): New headache after age 50 with jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, and transient vision loss is highly concerning for giant cell arteritis, and because the greatest risk is irreversible vision loss, empiric glucocorticoids should be started immediately while inflammatory markers are obtained and a temporal artery biopsy is arranged to confirm the diagnosis, rather than waiting for biopsy confirmation before treating. Reassurance would risk permanent blindness, a triptan does not address the underlying vasculitis, and sinus imaging does not address this presentation.
  25. The correct answer is D (Acetazolamide and weight loss with serial visual field monitoring): This presentation with headache worse lying down, papilledema, an elevated opening pressure, and normal cerebrospinal fluid composition and imaging aside from an empty sella is consistent with idiopathic intracranial hypertension, which is managed with acetazolamide to reduce cerebrospinal fluid production, weight loss, and close monitoring of visual fields to detect and prevent permanent optic nerve damage. Neurosurgical decompression and glucocorticoids are not first-line treatments for this condition, and a triptan does not address the underlying elevated intracranial pressure.
  26. The correct answer is C (Sudden thunderclap onset reaching maximum intensity within seconds): A thunderclap headache is a classic red flag requiring urgent evaluation for subarachnoid hemorrhage and other serious secondary causes, since its abrupt maximal intensity onset is fundamentally different from the gradual buildup typical of primary headache disorders. A long-standing stable pattern, a mild headache that responds to a simple analgesic, and a headache clearly tied to a recognizable stressor such as examinations are all reassuring features of a benign primary headache rather than red flags.
  27. The correct answer is D (Medication overuse headache): Frequent, near-daily use of acute headache medications, particularly combination analgesics and butalbital-containing products, is a well-recognized cause of medication overuse headache, in which the headache pattern worsens and becomes more refractory the more often the acute medication is used, and management requires carefully withdrawing the overused medication along with starting appropriate preventive therapy. A brain tumor and giant cell arteritis would typically show other systemic or focal findings, and a new cluster headache pattern would present with the distinctive unilateral autonomic features rather than a gradually worsening chronic migraine pattern.
  28. The correct answer is D (A beta blocker or topiramate taken daily): For patients with frequent, disabling migraines despite adequate acute treatment, daily preventive therapy with agents such as a beta blocker, topiramate, a tricyclic antidepressant, or a calcitonin gene-related peptide antagonist reduces attack frequency and severity. Triptans are acute abortive agents not intended for daily preventive use, and daily opioids or butalbital combinations are inappropriate for migraine prevention because they carry a high risk of dependence and can themselves cause medication overuse headache.
  29. The correct answer is C (Delirium): An acute onset over hours to days, a fluctuating course, and a core deficit in attention arising in the setting of an acute illness such as pneumonia are the defining features of delirium, which requires identification and treatment of the underlying medical cause. Alzheimer disease and frontotemporal dementia both have an insidious onset over months to years rather than an acute change, and this degree of new confusion and inattention is not part of normal aging.
  30. The correct answer is B (Alzheimer disease): A gradually progressive course over years with early prominent short-term memory loss followed by language and visuospatial difficulty, along with hippocampal-predominant cortical atrophy on imaging, is the classic pattern of Alzheimer disease, the most common cause of dementia. Delirium has an acute rather than gradual onset, normal pressure hydrocephalus presents with the triad of gait disturbance, incontinence, and cognitive decline with a different imaging pattern, and Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease causes a much more rapidly progressive dementia over weeks to months with characteristic myoclonus.
  31. The correct answer is B (Lewy body dementia): Fluctuating cognition, visual hallucinations, and parkinsonism arising around the same time as cognitive decline are the core features of Lewy body dementia, and patients with this disease have a well-documented severe, sometimes dangerous, sensitivity to antipsychotic medications, which explains his marked deterioration after receiving one. Vascular dementia typically shows a stepwise decline related to strokes, Alzheimer disease does not typically present with this early combination of hallucinations and parkinsonism, and major depressive disorder would not explain the parkinsonism or the antipsychotic sensitivity reaction.
  32. The correct answer is C (Frontotemporal dementia): Early, prominent personality change and disinhibition with relatively preserved memory, occurring at a younger age than typical Alzheimer disease, is the classic presentation of frontotemporal dementia, which affects the frontal and temporal lobes responsible for behavior and social conduct before memory becomes significantly involved. Alzheimer disease characteristically presents with early memory loss rather than personality change, delirium would have an acute rather than two-year course, and normal pressure hydrocephalus presents with gait disturbance and incontinence rather than primarily personality change.
  33. The correct answer is A (Improvement in gait after a large volume lumbar puncture): The classic triad of gait disturbance, urinary incontinence, and cognitive decline with disproportionate ventricular enlargement suggests normal pressure hydrocephalus, and transient improvement in gait after a large volume lumbar puncture supports the diagnosis and predicts a favorable response to ventriculoperitoneal shunting, making this one of the few reversible causes of a dementia-like syndrome. A normal B12 level and a normal EEG are nonspecific findings that do not support this diagnosis, and a family history of Alzheimer disease is not relevant to normal pressure hydrocephalus.
  34. The correct answer is C (Search for an acute precipitating medical cause of delirium superimposed on his dementia): A sudden, acute worsening of cognition with fluctuating attention in a patient with baseline dementia should always prompt a search for a new acute medical cause, such as infection, medication effect, or a metabolic derangement, rather than being attributed to gradual progression of the underlying dementia, since dementia itself does not cause this kind of acute fluctuating change. Sedating medications should generally be avoided as first-line management of agitation in this setting, and adjusting his baseline dementia medication does not address an acute superimposed process.
  35. The correct answer is B (Vitamin B12 level and thyroid function tests): Vitamin B12 deficiency and thyroid dysfunction are well-recognized, treatable contributors to cognitive impairment, and screening for them along with basic metabolic studies and structural brain imaging is a standard part of the initial dementia workup to identify reversible causes before attributing decline solely to a neurodegenerative process. Apolipoprotein E4 genotyping and amyloid imaging are research or specialized tools rather than routine initial screening, and waiting a year to repeat testing delays identification of a potentially treatable cause.
  36. The correct answer is D (Identify and treat any underlying precipitant while reorienting him and minimizing deliriogenic medications): The cornerstone of delirium management is finding and correcting the underlying medical cause while implementing nonpharmacologic measures such as reorientation, promoting a normal sleep-wake cycle, and reducing exposure to deliriogenic drugs, particularly benzodiazepines and anticholinergic agents, which generally worsen delirium rather than help it. Scheduled benzodiazepines are not first-line therapy and typically worsen delirium, prolonged physical restraint without further evaluation is not appropriate management, and discharging a medically unstable, delirious patient is unsafe.
  37. The correct answer is A (Multiple sclerosis): Neurologic episodes separated in time and space, here optic neuritis followed months later by a separate sensory episode, together with multiple periventricular white matter lesions of different ages on MRI, is the classic pattern that defines multiple sclerosis. Guillain-Barre syndrome presents as an acute, monophasic ascending weakness rather than episodes separated over months, myasthenia gravis produces fatigable weakness without this pattern of white matter lesions, and vitamin B12 deficiency causes a subacute combined degeneration pattern rather than this relapsing, lesion-separated course.
Case CASE-MG-1

A 45-year-old woman presents with several weeks of drooping eyelids and double vision that are worse by the end of the day and improve somewhat after resting, and she has recently also noticed difficulty chewing tough foods and mild difficulty swallowing.

  1. The correct answer is A (Elevated serum acetylcholine receptor antibodies): Fatigable ptosis and diplopia that worsen with use and improve with rest, along with bulbar symptoms such as difficulty chewing and swallowing, is the classic presentation of myasthenia gravis, and elevated acetylcholine receptor antibodies are the most specific confirmatory test. Albuminocytologic dissociation is a finding in Guillain-Barre syndrome, oligoclonal bands support a diagnosis of multiple sclerosis, and a markedly elevated creatine kinase would suggest a primary muscle disease rather than a neuromuscular junction disorder.
  2. The correct answer is D (Chest imaging to evaluate for a thymoma): Nearly all patients newly diagnosed with myasthenia gravis should undergo chest imaging (CT or MRI of the chest) to evaluate for a thymoma, since thymic abnormalities are common in this disease and thymectomy improves outcomes even in many patients without a thymoma. Lumbar spine MRI, renal ultrasound, and echocardiogram are not part of the standard initial myasthenia gravis workup and do not address the disease's underlying pathophysiology.
  3. The correct answer is D (Plasmapheresis or intravenous immunoglobulin): A myasthenic crisis with respiratory compromise is a neurologic emergency treated with rapid-acting therapies such as plasmapheresis or intravenous immunoglobulin, along with mechanical ventilatory support as needed, because these therapies act faster than chronic immunosuppression. A single dose of pyridostigmine alone would not be sufficient for this degree of weakness, glucocorticoids alone can even transiently worsen weakness early in treatment and are not the immediate rescue therapy, and observation without escalation is unsafe with impending respiratory failure.
  4. The correct answer is D (Cerebrospinal fluid with elevated protein and a normal white blood cell count): This presentation of ascending, symmetric weakness with areflexia following a preceding gastrointestinal illness is classic for Guillain-Barre syndrome, and the characteristic cerebrospinal fluid finding is albuminocytologic dissociation, an elevated protein level with a normal white blood cell count. A neutrophilic pleocytosis with low glucose is seen in bacterial meningitis, periventricular white matter plaques are seen in multiple sclerosis, and a decremental response on repetitive nerve stimulation is characteristic of myasthenia gravis rather than Guillain-Barre syndrome.
  5. The correct answer is C (Forced vital capacity and negative inspiratory force): Because respiratory muscle weakness is the most dangerous complication of Guillain-Barre syndrome, serial bedside measurement of forced vital capacity and negative inspiratory force allows clinicians to anticipate the need for elective intubation before the patient develops overt respiratory failure. Creatine kinase and erythrocyte sedimentation rate do not predict respiratory decline in this disease, and acetylcholine receptor antibody titer is relevant to myasthenia gravis, not Guillain-Barre syndrome.
  6. The correct answer is B (Glucocorticoids): Glucocorticoids have been studied extensively in Guillain-Barre syndrome and have not been shown to improve outcomes, so they are not recommended despite being a mainstay of treatment in other autoimmune neurologic diseases such as multiple sclerosis relapses. Intravenous immunoglobulin and plasmapheresis are both proven effective therapies for Guillain-Barre syndrome, and mechanical ventilation remains an essential supportive measure when respiratory failure develops regardless of the specific immunotherapy chosen.
  7. The correct answer is D (Symmetric, distal, sensory-predominant polyneuropathy in a stocking-glove distribution): Diabetes mellitus is the most common cause of peripheral neuropathy in the outpatient setting, and it classically produces a gradually progressive, symmetric, distal, sensory-predominant polyneuropathy beginning in the feet and slowly ascending, described as a stocking-glove distribution. An acute ascending demyelinating polyneuropathy describes Guillain-Barre syndrome rather than a chronic diabetic process, fatigable weakness of ocular and bulbar muscles describes myasthenia gravis, and a single mononeuropathy would not explain this bilateral, symmetric pattern.
  8. The correct answer is C (Pyridoxine, vitamin B6): Isoniazid can cause a peripheral neuropathy by interfering with pyridoxine metabolism, so co-administration of pyridoxine is standard practice to prevent this complication in patients being treated for tuberculosis. Vitamin B12, folic acid, and vitamin D do not prevent isoniazid-associated neuropathy, since the mechanism specifically involves pyridoxine depletion rather than these other nutrients.
  9. The correct answer is C (Medial longitudinal fasciculus, internuclear ophthalmoplegia): Impaired adduction of one eye on attempted lateral gaze with nystagmus of the opposite abducting eye is the classic sign of internuclear ophthalmoplegia, caused by a lesion in the medial longitudinal fasciculus connecting the abducens and oculomotor nuclei, and it is a common and highly suggestive finding in multiple sclerosis, especially in a young patient with periventricular lesions. Isolated optic nerve involvement would cause monocular vision loss rather than this gaze abnormality, a full oculomotor nerve palsy would cause ptosis and a fixed, dilated pupil in addition to adduction weakness, and neuromuscular junction disease produces fatigable weakness rather than this specific brainstem sign.
  10. The correct answer is A (Empiric intravenous acyclovir): Fever, altered mental status, seizures, and temporal lobe changes on MRI are highly suggestive of herpes simplex virus encephalitis, and because delayed treatment substantially worsens outcomes, empiric intravenous acyclovir should be started immediately once this diagnosis is suspected, without waiting for confirmatory cerebrospinal fluid polymerase chain reaction results. Fluconazole targets fungal infection rather than herpes encephalitis, vancomycin alone does not treat a viral process, and delaying treatment while awaiting results risks irreversible neurologic injury.
Case CASE-MEN-1

A 24-year-old previously healthy man is brought to the emergency department with two hours of fever, severe headache, photophobia, and neck stiffness. He appears acutely ill and is somewhat confused.

  1. The correct answer is D (Draw blood cultures and start empiric intravenous antibiotics without delay): Suspected bacterial meningitis is a true emergency, and empiric antibiotics should be started immediately after blood cultures are drawn if there will be any meaningful delay in obtaining cerebrospinal fluid, since delayed antibiotic therapy is directly associated with increased mortality. Waiting for lumbar puncture results before treating risks a fatal delay, outpatient imaging scheduled for the following week is entirely inappropriate for this acute presentation, and oral antibiotics with discharge would be dangerously inadequate for suspected bacterial meningitis.
  2. The correct answer is D (Vancomycin plus a third generation cephalosporin such as ceftriaxone): This cerebrospinal fluid profile, with a neutrophil-predominant pleocytosis, low glucose, and high protein, confirms bacterial meningitis, and standard empiric therapy in an adult of this age combines vancomycin (for resistant pneumococcus) with a third generation cephalosporin, reserving added ampicillin for patients at risk for Listeria (those under 1 month or over 50 years old, pregnant, or immunocompromised), which this 24-year-old does not describe. A single dose of oral azithromycin is grossly inadequate, omitting the cephalosporin would leave gaps in coverage, and acyclovir alone would not treat bacterial infection.
  3. The correct answer is C (Dexamethasone given with the first antibiotic dose): Adjunctive dexamethasone, given with or shortly before the first dose of antibiotics, has been shown to reduce neurologic complications and improve outcomes in bacterial meningitis, particularly pneumococcal meningitis, and its benefit is lost if it is delayed until after antibiotics have already been running for some time. Intravenous immunoglobulin and plasmapheresis have no established role in bacterial meningitis, and delaying the glucocorticoid until well after antibiotics begin loses the timing-dependent benefit that makes this intervention effective.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Lymphocyte-predominant, modest pleocytosis with normal glucose): Viral meningitis typically produces a more modest, lymphocyte-predominant cerebrospinal fluid pleocytosis with a normal glucose level, in contrast to the marked neutrophilic pleocytosis and low glucose typical of bacterial meningitis. Grossly bloody fluid that fails to clear suggests subarachnoid hemorrhage rather than infection, and markedly elevated protein with a completely normal white blood cell count describes the albuminocytologic dissociation of Guillain-Barre syndrome rather than a meningeal infectious process.
  2. The correct answer is D (A new focal neurologic deficit or papilledema): A head CT before lumbar puncture is indicated in patients with an immunocompromised state, a history of central nervous system disease, new seizure, papilledema, or a focal neurologic deficit, because these findings raise concern for a mass lesion or elevated intracranial pressure where lumbar puncture could risk herniation. Fever alone, a normal exam in an otherwise healthy young adult, and isolated mild neck stiffness do not meet criteria for CT before lumbar puncture and should not delay obtaining cerebrospinal fluid and starting antibiotics.
  3. The correct answer is D (A 65-year-old man): Patients over 50 years of age, along with those under 1 month old, pregnant patients, and immunocompromised patients, are at increased risk for Listeria monocytogenes meningitis and require empiric ampicillin in addition to standard coverage. A healthy 30-year-old man, a 20-year-old immunocompetent college student, and a previously healthy 10-year-old child do not fall into any of the recognized higher risk groups for Listeria and do not routinely require added ampicillin.
  4. The correct answer is D (Urgent lowering of intraocular pressure with topical and systemic agents followed by laser peripheral iridotomy): This presentation, precipitated by a dark environment with a fixed mid-dilated pupil, a firm globe, halos, and severe pain, is classic for acute angle closure glaucoma, an ophthalmologic emergency requiring urgent pressure-lowering treatment (such as a topical beta blocker, an alpha agonist, and acetazolamide) followed by laser peripheral iridotomy to prevent permanent vision loss. Topical antibiotics do not address elevated intraocular pressure, observation risks permanent blindness within hours, and acetazolamide alone without topical therapy is not the complete standard urgent regimen.
  5. The correct answer is A (Chronic open angle glaucoma): Gradual, typically asymptomatic peripheral visual field loss discovered incidentally, with elevated intraocular pressure and no acute pain, is the classic presentation of chronic open angle glaucoma, managed with topical prostaglandin analogs or beta blockers to gradually lower intraocular pressure and preserve remaining vision. Acute angle closure glaucoma presents with sudden severe pain rather than a painless, gradual course, and retinal detachment and uveitis present with acute visual symptoms rather than this chronic, painless field loss.
  6. The correct answer is A (Urgent ophthalmology referral for likely retinal detachment): A sudden shower of floaters, flashes of light, and a curtain-like visual field defect, especially in a patient with high myopia (a risk factor), is classic for retinal detachment, which is a surgical emergency because delay risks permanent vision loss, particularly if the macula becomes involved. While some floaters can be benign, this combination of symptoms should never simply be reassured away, and neither oral glucocorticoids nor topical antibiotics address a retinal detachment.
  7. The correct answer is B (Anterior uveitis): A painful red eye with photophobia, blurred vision, and cells and flare in the anterior chamber on slit lamp exam defines anterior uveitis, which is frequently associated with systemic autoimmune conditions such as ankylosing spondylitis, inflammatory bowel disease, and sarcoidosis, and is treated with topical glucocorticoids and cycloplegic agents along with management of the underlying systemic disease. Acute angle closure glaucoma would show a fixed mid-dilated pupil and a firm globe rather than anterior chamber cells and flare, bacterial conjunctivitis typically causes discharge without significant pain or photophobia, and retinal detachment presents with floaters and a visual field defect rather than a painful red eye with anterior chamber inflammation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Prompt oral glucocorticoid therapy): Sudden sensorineural hearing loss is an otologic emergency because early treatment with oral glucocorticoids improves the chance of meaningful hearing recovery, so prompt evaluation and treatment should not be delayed. Sensorineural hearing loss does not reliably resolve on its own without treatment, cerumen removal addresses conductive rather than sensorineural hearing loss and would not be expected to help here, and waiting months before treating eliminates the window during which glucocorticoids are most likely to be beneficial.
  9. The correct answer is A (Conductive hearing loss in the right ear): In conductive hearing loss, the Weber test lateralizes to the affected ear because bone conduction is relatively enhanced when the air conduction pathway is impaired, and the Rinne test in that ear shows bone conduction better than air conduction, which is the reverse of the normal pattern. Sensorineural hearing loss would instead cause the Weber test to lateralize away from the affected ear toward the better hearing ear, with air conduction remaining better than bone conduction on Rinne testing in both ears, so this pattern does not fit either sensorineural option, and it clearly is not normal hearing given the abnormal Rinne result.
  10. The correct answer is B (Meniere disease): Episodic vertigo lasting hours together with fluctuating sensorineural hearing loss, tinnitus, and aural fullness is the classic tetrad of Meniere disease, thought to result from excess endolymphatic fluid, and it is managed with a low sodium diet and diuretics. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo causes brief, position-triggered episodes lasting seconds without hearing loss or aural fullness, and acute angle closure glaucoma and giant cell arteritis are unrelated conditions that do not present with this vertigo and hearing symptom combination.
  11. The correct answer is D (Dix-Hallpike maneuver for diagnosis and the Epley maneuver for treatment): Brief, intense, position-triggered vertigo without hearing loss is classic for benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, which is diagnosed with the Dix-Hallpike maneuver (provoking characteristic nystagmus that fatigues with repeated testing) and treated with the Epley repositioning maneuver, which repositions the displaced otoconia. Weber and Rinne tests assess hearing rather than positional vertigo, caloric testing evaluates vestibular function but is not itself therapeutic, and the Valsalva maneuver has no established diagnostic or therapeutic role in this condition.
  12. The correct answer is C (Additional brainstem signs such as dysarthria, diplopia, or limb ataxia, with nystagmus that does not fatigue): Central causes of vertigo, such as a posterior circulation stroke, are suggested by accompanying brainstem signs and by nystagmus that is persistent rather than fatiguing with repeated testing, which helps distinguish a dangerous central cause from benign peripheral vertigo. A fatiguing nystagmus pattern and a brief, position-triggered course are instead features that support a benign peripheral cause such as benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, and a complete absence of nystagmus does not by itself indicate a central process.
  13. The correct answer is C (Pupillary dilation from the sympathomimetic agent precipitating acute angle closure in a susceptible eye): Sympathomimetic and anticholinergic agents can dilate the pupil, which in an anatomically susceptible eye (typically one with a narrow anterior chamber angle) can precipitate acute angle closure glaucoma, producing this combination of severe pain, halos, a mid-dilated poorly reactive pupil, and a firm globe. This mechanism does not directly cause retinal detachment, does not describe a simple allergic flare since intraocular pressure is clearly affected here, and does not describe a bacterial corneal infection, which would more typically present with discharge and a corneal infiltrate rather than this pressure-related picture.
  14. The correct answer is A (Amaurosis fugax from transient retinal ischemia): A transient, painless, curtain-like monocular vision loss that resolves completely is the classic description of amaurosis fugax, typically caused by a transient embolic or ischemic event affecting the retinal circulation, often arising from an ipsilateral carotid artery source, and it should prompt the same urgent stroke and carotid risk evaluation as a transient ischemic attack elsewhere in the brain. Acute angle closure glaucoma and anterior uveitis both present with a painful eye rather than this painless transient pattern, and Meniere disease causes vertigo and hearing symptoms rather than monocular vision loss.
  15. The correct answer is A (Bacterial keratitis related to contact lens use): A painful red eye with a visible corneal infiltrate in the setting of extended or improper contact lens wear is characteristic of bacterial keratitis, a sight-threatening infection that requires prompt ophthalmologic evaluation and targeted antimicrobial therapy, since contact lens wear is a major risk factor for this condition. Chronic open angle glaucoma is typically painless, and Meniere disease and benign paroxysmal positional vertigo are vestibular conditions unrelated to a painful red eye with a corneal infiltrate.
  16. The correct answer is B (A visible impacted cerumen plug seen on otoscopic exam): Cerumen impaction is one of the most common causes of conductive hearing loss, and a visible obstructing plug on otoscopic exam directly supports a conductive mechanism, which is confirmed by a Weber test lateralizing to the affected ear and a Rinne test showing bone conduction better than air conduction in that ear. Sudden onset with a normal-appearing canal, associated vertigo and tinnitus, and a family history of a progressive sensorineural disorder are all features that point toward a sensorineural rather than conductive cause.
  17. The correct answer is B (Otosclerosis): Otosclerosis is an inherited disorder of abnormal bone remodeling around the stapes footplate that causes progressive conductive hearing loss, often with a family history and a normal-appearing tympanic membrane, and it is a classic cause of conductive hearing loss distinct from cerumen impaction. Acoustic neuroma and presbycusis both typically cause sensorineural rather than conductive hearing loss, and Meniere disease presents with episodic vertigo and fluctuating hearing loss rather than this gradually progressive conductive pattern.
  18. The correct answer is B (The much shorter attack duration, prominent ipsilateral autonomic features, and cyclical clustering pattern): Cluster headache is distinguished from migraine by its much shorter individual attack duration (minutes to a few hours versus 4 to 72 hours), prominent ipsilateral autonomic features such as tearing, conjunctival injection, and nasal congestion, and its characteristic pattern of clustering into periods of frequent attacks separated by long symptom-free intervals. Migraine can also recur in a pattern over time, migraine is frequently unilateral as well so this alone does not distinguish the two, and nausea is actually a prominent feature of migraine rather than being unique to cluster headache.
  19. The correct answer is A (Acetaminophen, generally preferred as safer in pregnancy): Acetaminophen is generally considered the safest first-line option for acute headache treatment in pregnancy, since NSAIDs are best avoided, particularly in the third trimester, because of risks including premature closure of the ductus arteriosus, and ergotamine is contraindicated in pregnancy because of its uterotonic and vasoconstrictive effects. Triptans are not considered unrestricted first-line therapy in pregnancy and are used more selectively with individualized risk discussion, making acetaminophen the more broadly preferred initial choice among these options.
  20. The correct answer is A (A recognized side effect of topical prostaglandin analog therapy): Increased iris pigmentation and eyelash growth are well-recognized, generally cosmetic side effects of topical prostaglandin analog medications used to treat open angle glaucoma, and they do not indicate worsening disease control. This finding is not a sign of dangerously uncontrolled glaucoma, does not indicate acute angle closure, which would instead present with pain and a firm globe, and is not a sign of an unrelated corneal infection.
  21. The correct answer is C (Begin a daily inhaled corticosteroid): her symptom pattern of daily daytime symptoms and frequent nocturnal awakenings, along with a documented reversible obstructive pattern on spirometry, places her at a persistent asthma severity that requires a daily controller medication rather than reliance on a rescue inhaler alone. An inhaled corticosteroid reduces airway inflammation and is the preferred first controller for persistent asthma. A long-acting beta agonist should never be used without a concurrent inhaled corticosteroid because monotherapy increases the risk of severe exacerbation and death. Oral steroids and bronchial thermoplasty are reserved for more severe or refractory disease, not initial persistent asthma.
  22. The correct answer is C (Smoking cessation counseling and pharmacotherapy): this patient has chronic obstructive pulmonary disease confirmed by a fixed obstructive pattern on spirometry with a postbronchodilator FEV1/FVC ratio below 0.70. Among all available interventions for COPD, smoking cessation is the only one demonstrated to slow the decline in lung function and reduce overall mortality. Inhaled corticosteroid monotherapy is not standard COPD therapy and is typically added to a bronchodilator regimen only in select patients with frequent exacerbations. Theophylline and azithromycin have narrower roles for exacerbation reduction in specific subgroups, and CPAP treats obstructive sleep apnea rather than COPD.
  23. The correct answer is C (Prescribe long-term supplemental home oxygen): long-term home oxygen therapy improves survival in COPD patients with resting hypoxemia defined as a PaO2 at or below 55 mmHg, or an oxygen saturation at or below 88%, confirmed on stable outpatient testing. This patient meets that threshold on repeated measurement while clinically stable, so oxygen should be prescribed regardless of the absence of cor pulmonale. There is no indication of an acute infectious exacerbation requiring antibiotics. Simply adjusting bronchodilator dosing or deferring to repeat testing without addressing the hypoxemia would delay a mortality-reducing intervention, and nocturnal ventilation is not the first-line treatment for daytime resting hypoxemia in stable COPD.
  24. The correct answer is D (Impending respiratory failure from respiratory muscle fatigue): a normalizing or rising PaCO2 in a tachypneic asthmatic who initially had a low PaCO2 from hyperventilation is a dangerous sign, not a reassuring one, because it indicates the patient can no longer sustain the increased minute ventilation that had been driving carbon dioxide down. Combined with decreased air movement and fatigue, this pattern signals impending respiratory failure and should prompt urgent reassessment for possible intubation. It does not represent resolution of bronchospasm, a new independent metabolic process, or an oxygen-related overcorrection, and dismissing the value as a lab error would delay necessary escalation of care.
  25. The correct answer is B (Acute respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation, consistent with an early severe exacerbation): the blood gas shows an elevated pH with a low PaCO2 and a normal bicarbonate, consistent with acute respiratory alkalosis from tachypnea-driven hyperventilation, which is the expected early pattern in a severe asthma exacerbation as the patient works hard to compensate for airway obstruction. This is not yet respiratory acidosis, which would indicate a much later and more ominous stage of fatigue. There is no evidence of a chronic compensated process given the acute presentation and normal bicarbonate, and while a mild metabolic component can appear later, the primary driver here is respiratory alkalosis, not lactic acidosis.
  26. The correct answer is D (Omalizumab, an anti-IgE monoclonal antibody): her elevated IgE level with a positive allergen sensitization pattern identifies an allergic asthma phenotype, for which omalizumab, a monoclonal antibody that binds circulating IgE and prevents it from triggering mast cell degranulation, is an appropriate add-on biologic. Antibiotics have no role since there is no evidence of infection driving her symptoms. Theophylline and additional muscarinic antagonist therapy are reasonable in refractory asthma more generally but do not specifically target the IgE-mediated allergic pathway suggested here, and high-dose short-acting beta agonist use as scheduled maintenance therapy is inappropriate and unsafe.
  27. The correct answer is B (Initiate noninvasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP)): this presentation reflects an acute COPD exacerbation complicated by acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis, and in a patient who remains alert and able to protect the airway, noninvasive positive pressure ventilation is the first-line intervention because it reduces the work of breathing, improves alveolar ventilation, and often avoids the need for invasive intubation. Immediate intubation is not necessary when the patient can tolerate a noninvasive trial and has intact mental status. Simple observation would allow ongoing respiratory acidosis to worsen, and high-flow nasal cannula alone does not provide the positive pressure support needed to unload the respiratory muscles as effectively as BiPAP in this setting.
  28. The correct answer is A (Serum alpha-1 antitrypsin level): lower lobe predominant panacinar emphysema in a young or nonsmoking patient, especially with a family history of early liver disease, is a classic presentation of alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency, an inherited condition in which the abnormal protein accumulates in hepatocytes causing liver disease while its deficiency in the lungs leaves neutrophil elastase unopposed, destroying alveolar walls. A serum alpha-1 antitrypsin level is the appropriate initial test. Sweat chloride testing evaluates for cystic fibrosis, which presents differently with bronchiectasis, ACE level is used in sarcoidosis, and antinuclear antibody and tuberculin testing do not address this clinical picture.
  29. The correct answer is B (Continue as-needed albuterol alone without a daily controller): her symptom frequency, roughly once every two months with no nighttime symptoms and no functional limitation, is consistent with intermittent rather than persistent asthma, which is appropriately managed with an as-needed short-acting beta agonist alone rather than a scheduled daily controller. Starting a daily inhaled corticosteroid is reserved for persistent asthma with more frequent symptoms. A long-acting beta agonist should never be used as monotherapy at any severity level because of the risk of masking worsening inflammation, and daily oral steroids or biologic therapy would be substantial overtreatment for infrequent, well-controlled symptoms.
Case case_copd_1

A 68-year-old man with a 50 pack-year smoking history and known severe COPD presents to the emergency department with three days of worsening dyspnea, increased sputum production, and new yellow-green sputum. He is afebrile, respiratory rate 28, heart rate 110, and oxygen saturation 88% on room air. He is using accessory muscles but is alert and oriented, able to answer questions in short sentences.

  1. The correct answer is D (Nebulized albuterol and ipratropium plus systemic corticosteroids and an antibiotic): this represents an acute COPD exacerbation with the classic triad of increased dyspnea, increased sputum volume, and increased sputum purulence, all of which support adding an antibiotic to the standard bundle of short-acting bronchodilators (albuterol and ipratropium) and a short course of systemic corticosteroids. Bronchodilators or corticosteroids alone would omit a component of care that is indicated given the purulent sputum change. There is no indication for antifungal therapy in this typical bacterial exacerbation picture.
  2. The correct answer is B (Initiate noninvasive positive pressure ventilation (BiPAP)): the blood gas demonstrates an acute respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia superimposed on a chronically elevated bicarbonate consistent with COPD, and because he remains alert and able to protect his airway, noninvasive positive pressure ventilation is the appropriate first-line escalation to reduce work of breathing before considering invasive ventilation. Jumping directly to intubation is not necessary when a noninvasive trial is appropriate and feasible. Discharging him or leaving his respiratory support unchanged would be unsafe given his worsening acidosis, and there is no clinical information here suggesting pulmonary embolism as the primary process.
  3. The correct answer is D (Proceed to endotracheal intubation and invasive mechanical ventilation): a declining level of consciousness and reduced respiratory effort on noninvasive ventilation are signs of failure of the noninvasive approach and impending respiratory arrest, which mandate prompt endotracheal intubation rather than further adjustment of noninvasive settings. Switching to high-flow nasal cannula would provide less ventilatory support, not more, and is inappropriate at this point. Administering sedation to a somnolent, fatiguing patient would be dangerous by further blunting respiratory drive, and observation on a general floor is unsafe given his trajectory toward respiratory failure.
  4. The correct answer is C (Daily low-dose azithromycin): in patients with frequent COPD exacerbations and a chronic bronchitis phenotype who remain symptomatic despite optimized inhaler therapy, chronic low-dose macrolide therapy such as azithromycin has been shown in clinical trials to reduce exacerbation frequency, likely through anti-inflammatory and antimicrobial effects. Adding more scheduled short-acting beta agonist does not address exacerbation prevention. Chronic oral corticosteroids carry substantial long-term toxicity and are not recommended as routine maintenance therapy, and there is no indication to stop his inhaled corticosteroid or to treat empirically for tuberculosis based on chronic sputum production alone.
  5. The correct answer is C (It improves exercise capacity and quality of life, and is recommended after a hospitalization for exacerbation): pulmonary rehabilitation, a structured program combining exercise training, education, and behavioral support, has been shown to improve exercise tolerance, reduce dyspnea, and improve quality of life in COPD patients, and is specifically recommended following hospitalization for an exacerbation because this period carries a high risk of readmission. It does not reverse the fixed airflow obstruction that defines COPD physiologically. It is beneficial across disease severities, not only mild disease, and avoiding it after a recent hospitalization would forgo a period when its benefit is greatest, since its mechanism relates to improved conditioning rather than a direct change in spirometric FEV1.
  6. The correct answer is C (Amoxicillin or a macrolide): this presentation is classic for lobar community-acquired pneumonia most likely caused by Streptococcus pneumoniae, and for a previously healthy outpatient without comorbidities, amoxicillin or a macrolide is the appropriate first-line empiric regimen per current outpatient CAP guidelines. Broad-spectrum agents with antipseudomonal or MRSA coverage such as vancomycin plus piperacillin-tazobactam or a carbapenem are reserved for patients with specific risk factors for resistant organisms or hospital-acquired infection, which this patient does not have. Antituberculous therapy and antifungal therapy are not indicated for this acute bacterial lobar pneumonia presentation.
  7. The correct answer is A (Admit to the hospital given a CURB-65 score of 3): the CURB-65 score assigns one point each for confusion, elevated BUN (urea), respiratory rate at or above 30, systolic blood pressure below 90 or diastolic at or below 60, and age 65 or older; this patient has a respiratory rate over 30 (1 point), elevated BUN (1 point), and age 65 or older (1 point), for a total of 3 points, since he is not confused and his systolic blood pressure remains above 90. A score of 3 or more supports inpatient admission and consideration of intensive care, so outpatient management or a single ED dose without admission would understate his severity, and withholding antibiotics until a pathogen is confirmed would inappropriately delay empiric treatment of a potentially severe pneumonia.
  8. The correct answer is B (Urinary antigen test for Legionella): the combination of high fever with relative bradycardia, gastrointestinal symptoms, hyponatremia, and exposure to a potential aerosolized water source such as a cooling tower strongly suggests Legionella pneumonia, which is most efficiently diagnosed with a urinary antigen test. Legionella is a fastidious organism that does not grow well on standard blood culture media and Gram stain does not reliably identify it either, since it stains poorly with conventional methods. Influenza antigen testing and cold agglutinin titers, the latter more associated with Mycoplasma, would not be expected to confirm this specific clinical picture.
  9. The correct answer is B (A macrolide or fluoroquinolone, because Legionella is an intracellular organism not effectively treated by cell wall active agents): Legionella is an intracellular pathogen, and beta-lactam antibiotics, which act on bacterial cell wall synthesis, do not achieve adequate intracellular concentrations to eradicate it, which is why macrolides or fluoroquinolones, which penetrate host cells effectively, are the treatment of choice. Legionella does not have an unusually thick cell wall requiring cell wall agents alone, it is not a gram-positive organism appropriately treated with vancomycin, it possesses ribosomes like other bacteria, and it is not a strict anaerobe requiring metronidazole.
  10. The correct answer is A (The right lower lobe, because the right mainstem bronchus is more vertical and in line with the trachea): aspiration pneumonia in a patient with impaired airway protection, such as after a stroke with dysphagia and a witnessed or presumed aspiration event, characteristically involves the right lower lobe because the right mainstem bronchus branches off the trachea at a less acute angle and is more vertically oriented, directing aspirated material preferentially into that lobe rather than the left side. The left upper lobe and lingula are not the characteristic sites, and while any lobe can theoretically be involved depending on body position at the time of aspiration, the right lower lobe remains the classic and most frequently tested answer.
  11. The correct answer is D (Broad-spectrum antipseudomonal coverage, with additional MRSA coverage if risk factors are present): pneumonia developing after 48 hours of mechanical ventilation is classified as ventilator-associated pneumonia, which carries a substantially higher risk of resistant organisms including Pseudomonas aeruginosa and methicillin resistant Staphylococcus aureus compared with community-acquired pneumonia, so empiric therapy should include antipseudomonal coverage with vancomycin or linezolid added when MRSA risk factors are present. Amoxicillin alone or a macrolide alone would be inadequate coverage for this higher-risk nosocomial setting. Withholding antibiotics for 48 to 72 hours while awaiting cultures would be dangerous in a critically ill patient with an active infection, and antifungal monotherapy is not the appropriate initial empiric choice for a typical ventilator-associated pneumonia presentation.
  12. The correct answer is C (Mycoplasma pneumoniae): this presentation, a gradual onset of dry cough and constitutional symptoms in a young, otherwise healthy patient with a chest radiograph that appears more severe than the clinical picture suggests, along with a positive cold agglutinin test, is classic for atypical pneumonia caused by Mycoplasma pneumoniae. Streptococcus pneumoniae typically causes a more acute lobar pneumonia with a productive cough and higher fever. Staphylococcus aureus and Klebsiella pneumoniae are more associated with severe, necrotizing, or postinfluenza pneumonia, and Pseudomonas aeruginosa is more typical of hospital-acquired or structural lung disease-associated pneumonia, none of which fit this mild atypical presentation.
  13. The correct answer is B (Outpatient treatment with a respiratory fluoroquinolone or a beta-lactam plus macrolide given her comorbidities): her CURB-65 score is 1, from age 65 or older alone, since she has no confusion, a respiratory rate below 30, and a systolic blood pressure above 90, which supports outpatient management; however, because she has comorbidities including hypertension and chronic kidney disease, guidelines recommend a respiratory fluoroquinolone or a beta-lactam plus macrolide rather than amoxicillin monotherapy, which is reserved for previously healthy patients without comorbidities or recent antibiotic exposure. ICU admission and broad intravenous double coverage would be excessive for this low CURB-65 score, and withholding antibiotics pending culture results would inappropriately delay treatment of a likely bacterial pneumonia.
  14. The correct answer is C (Streptococcus pneumoniae): Streptococcus pneumoniae remains the most common bacterial cause of community-acquired pneumonia across most age groups, including in children, making it the organism most likely to be implicated in this scenario. Mycoplasma pneumoniae is more typical in school-age children and young adults with a milder, atypical presentation. Legionella and Pseudomonas are less common in previously healthy young children without specific risk factors or exposures, and Chlamydophila psittaci is a rare cause associated specifically with bird exposure, none of which are as broadly prevalent as Streptococcus pneumoniae.
  15. The correct answer is D (A beta-lactam plus either a macrolide or a respiratory fluoroquinolone): severe community-acquired pneumonia requiring ICU admission is treated with combination therapy consisting of a beta-lactam plus either a macrolide or a respiratory fluoroquinolone, providing coverage for both typical organisms such as Streptococcus pneumoniae and atypical organisms such as Legionella, since this population carries higher mortality risk and narrower single-agent regimens used for milder outpatient disease would be inadequate. Amoxicillin monotherapy, a macrolide alone, and oral doxycycline alone are all appropriate only for milder presentations, and topical mupirocin has no role in treating pneumonia.
  16. The correct answer is D (Anaerobic coverage such as clindamycin or a beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination): a cavitary lung lesion with an air-fluid level and foul-smelling sputum following aspiration in a patient with impaired airway protection is characteristic of a lung abscess caused by oral anaerobic flora, which requires anaerobic antimicrobial coverage such as clindamycin or a beta-lactam combined with a beta-lactamase inhibitor in addition to standard therapy. Antifungal or antiviral therapy would not address this typical bacterial anaerobic process, and simply continuing standard aspiration coverage without specifically ensuring anaerobic activity, or starting empiric antituberculous therapy without supporting evidence such as chronic symptoms or risk factors, would not appropriately treat this abscess.
  17. The correct answer is A (He should be treated according to standard community-acquired pneumonia guidelines given his preserved CD4 count): with a CD4 count of 550 cells per microliter, well above the threshold of 200 cells per microliter associated with markedly increased risk for opportunistic infections such as Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, this patient's presentation should be managed according to standard community-acquired pneumonia guidelines based on his clinical severity, similar to a patient without HIV. Empiric Pneumocystis or antituberculous therapy is not warranted based on HIV status alone without a suggestive CD4 count or clinical picture, and his HIV status alone does not preclude outpatient management if his CURB-65 score and clinical stability support it; CD4 count is directly relevant to risk stratification rather than irrelevant.
  18. The correct answer is A (D-dimer testing, with CT pulmonary angiography reserved for a positive result): in a patient with a low pretest probability for pulmonary embolism by Wells criteria, a D-dimer test is an appropriate initial step, since a negative D-dimer in this low-probability setting has a high enough negative predictive value to effectively exclude pulmonary embolism without further imaging, while a positive result should prompt CT pulmonary angiography. Proceeding directly to CT pulmonary angiography or empiric anticoagulation would expose her to unnecessary radiation, contrast, or bleeding risk when a d-dimer could reasonably rule out the diagnosis first. Ventilation-perfusion scanning is not the preferred first-line test in most patients and stopping the workup entirely based on a low Wells score alone, without any diagnostic test, would risk missing a true PE.
  19. The correct answer is D (Proceed directly to CT pulmonary angiography regardless of the D-dimer result): in a patient with a high pretest probability for pulmonary embolism, a negative or even a mildly elevated D-dimer is not sufficiently reliable to exclude the diagnosis, because the test's negative predictive value is inadequate in this population, so he should proceed directly to CT pulmonary angiography regardless of the D-dimer result. Relying on the D-dimer alone in this high-probability setting could miss a true pulmonary embolism. Repeating the D-dimer in a week would dangerously delay diagnosis and treatment of a potentially life-threatening condition, and starting empiric antibiotics or discharging him without addressing the high suspicion for PE would be inappropriate given his risk profile.
  20. The correct answer is B (Ventilation-perfusion scanning): ventilation-perfusion scanning is often preferred over CT pulmonary angiography in pregnancy because it delivers less radiation to maternal breast tissue, which is particularly radiosensitive, while CT pulmonary angiography is more commonly favored in nonpregnant patients or when V/Q scanning is not readily available or is nondiagnostic due to underlying lung disease. Conventional invasive pulmonary angiography is not first-line imaging given its invasiveness. A plain chest radiograph alone cannot reliably diagnose or exclude pulmonary embolism, and while lower extremity Doppler ultrasound can support the diagnosis if a DVT is found, it does not substitute for direct pulmonary imaging when clinical suspicion for PE itself remains high.
Case case_pe_1

A 54-year-old woman is postoperative day 6 after total hip arthroplasty. She develops acute onset dyspnea, pleuritic right-sided chest pain, and tachycardia to 118 beats per minute. Her oxygen saturation is 91% on room air, and her blood pressure is 108/70 mmHg.

  1. The correct answer is B (High pretest probability given her surgery, tachycardia, and no more likely diagnosis): recent major surgery, tachycardia, and an acute presentation with no clearly more likely alternative diagnosis place her in a high pretest probability category by Wells criteria, and in this setting a D-dimer is not reliable enough to exclude pulmonary embolism, so she should proceed directly to definitive imaging such as CT pulmonary angiography. Treating her as low probability would be inconsistent with her clear risk factors and clinical findings. Pretest probability is estimated directly from clinical criteria before any D-dimer is obtained, recent orthopedic surgery is one of the most significant risk factors for venous thromboembolism, and Wells criteria are specifically validated and commonly applied in postoperative populations.
  2. The correct answer is B (CT pulmonary angiography): CT pulmonary angiography is the diagnostic test of choice for most patients with suspected pulmonary embolism, offering direct visualization of thrombus within the pulmonary arterial tree and being widely available and rapid, which is important in a patient with a high pretest probability who needs prompt diagnosis. Ventilation-perfusion scanning is generally reserved for patients with contraindications to contrast, such as significant renal impairment, or in pregnancy, rather than being preferred here. Repeating a D-dimer would not add useful information given her already high pretest probability, and while echocardiography can suggest right heart strain, it does not confirm the diagnosis and a plain chest radiograph is insufficient on its own.
  3. The correct answer is D (Consider systemic thrombolysis or catheter-directed therapy given hemodynamic instability): her new hypotension indicates that she has progressed to a hemodynamically unstable, or massive, pulmonary embolism, and in this setting systemic thrombolysis or catheter-directed therapy should be considered in addition to anticoagulation to rapidly restore right ventricular function and improve pulmonary blood flow, since standard anticoagulation alone acts too slowly to address acute hemodynamic collapse. Continuing anticoagulation alone or planning for outpatient management would not adequately address her current instability. An inferior vena cava filter alone does not treat the existing clot burden or improve hemodynamics and is not a substitute for definitive therapy in this acute unstable presentation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Placement of an inferior vena cava filter): an inferior vena cava filter is indicated in patients who develop recurrent pulmonary embolism despite adequate, confirmed therapeutic anticoagulation, or in those with an absolute contraindication to anticoagulation, since it mechanically prevents further emboli from lower extremity thrombi from reaching the pulmonary circulation. Discontinuing anticoagulation entirely would leave her without any protection against further clot formation and propagation. Aspirin is not an adequate substitute for therapeutic anticoagulation in venous thromboembolism, and continuing the identical regimen unchanged after a documented recurrence on adequate anticoagulation ignores a clear treatment failure; surgical embolectomy is reserved for specific unstable situations rather than being the routine next step after a confirmed recurrence.
  5. The correct answer is B (Transudative effusion; treat the underlying heart failure): none of Light's criteria are met since the pleural fluid to serum protein ratio is below 0.5, the pleural fluid to serum LDH ratio is below 0.6, and the pleural fluid LDH is below two-thirds the upper limit of normal serum LDH, classifying this as a transudative effusion, which typically reflects a systemic process such as heart failure and is managed by treating the underlying condition rather than pursuing invasive pleural procedures. Exudative-pattern interventions such as pleural biopsy or chest tube placement are not indicated for a transudate, and empiric antituberculous therapy would not be appropriate without supporting clinical or fluid findings suggestive of tuberculosis.
  6. The correct answer is D (Chest tube drainage in addition to antibiotics): a pleural fluid pH below 7.2, low glucose, and a positive Gram stain indicate a complicated parapneumonic effusion, which requires chest tube drainage in addition to antibiotics because antibiotics alone will not adequately sterilize an acidic, loculated collection with organisms already present on Gram stain. Antibiotics alone, observation alone, or oral-only therapy would all be insufficient given these high-risk fluid characteristics, and a lobectomy is an unnecessarily aggressive and inappropriate initial intervention for a parapneumonic process that should first be addressed with drainage and antibiotics.
  7. The correct answer is A (Transudative effusion from movement of ascitic fluid across diaphragmatic defects (hepatic hydrothorax)): hepatic hydrothorax occurs when ascitic fluid moves through small diaphragmatic defects into the pleural space, producing a transudative effusion consistent with her underlying portal hypertension and hypoalbuminemia from cirrhosis, similar in mechanism to how ascites itself forms. This is not primarily an infectious or malignant process based on the information given, so labeling it exudative from infection or presuming an occult pulmonary embolism would not be supported by the described transudative mechanism, and malignant effusions are exudative rather than transudative; furthermore, a transudative effusion does not automatically require chest tube drainage the way a complicated parapneumonic effusion does.
  8.
