The correct answer is C (Observation with supplemental oxygen and outpatient follow-up imaging): a small, asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic primary spontaneous pneumothorax, typically from rupture of a subpleural bleb in a tall, thin young patient, without signs of tension physiology or significant respiratory compromise can be safely managed with observation, supplemental oxygen to help promote resorption of the pleural air, and outpatient follow-up imaging. Needle decompression and chest tube placement are reserved for larger, symptomatic, or tension pneumothoraces, not this small, stable presentation, and bilateral chest tube placement or emergency thoracotomy would be markedly excessive interventions for this scenario.
  9. The correct answer is B (Immediate needle decompression at the second intercostal space, midclavicular line): tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis based on absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, distended neck veins, and hypotension, and it requires immediate needle decompression at the second intercostal space, midclavicular line, without waiting for confirmatory imaging, since delaying treatment to obtain a chest radiograph, CT scan, or echocardiogram in a hemodynamically unstable patient with this clinical picture could be fatal. Intravenous fluids alone would not address the underlying mechanical problem of a tension pneumothorax obstructing venous return and cardiac output.
  10. The correct answer is C (Secondary spontaneous pneumothorax in the setting of underlying lung disease, generally requiring more aggressive intervention due to reduced pulmonary reserve): a pneumothorax occurring in a patient with underlying lung disease such as COPD is classified as a secondary spontaneous pneumothorax, and because these patients have substantially reduced pulmonary reserve, even a moderate pneumothorax is more likely to cause significant respiratory compromise and generally warrants more aggressive intervention such as chest tube placement compared with an equivalent-sized primary spontaneous pneumothorax in an otherwise healthy young person. It is not clinically identical to primary spontaneous pneumothorax and should not be managed the same way in all cases, it is not appropriate to reflexively attribute it to malignancy without further workup, and given his known severe COPD it is directly related to his underlying disease rather than coincidental.
  11. The correct answer is D (Pleurodesis or placement of an indwelling pleural catheter): for recurrent, symptomatic malignant pleural effusions, definitive management options such as chemical or mechanical pleurodesis, or placement of a tunneled indwelling pleural catheter, are appropriate to reduce the need for repeated thoracentesis procedures and improve quality of life. Simply discontinuing further intervention would leave his symptomatic effusions unaddressed, diuretics are not an effective treatment for a malignant exudative effusion, performing only a single additional thoracentesis without a longer-term plan does not address the recurrent nature of his effusions, and there is no indication here suggesting tuberculosis as the underlying cause.
  12. The correct answer is B (Restrictive pattern, most consistent with an interstitial lung process): the findings show a reduced FEV1 and reduced FVC with a preserved or increased FEV1/FVC ratio, along with a reduced diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide, which together define a restrictive ventilatory pattern most consistent with an interstitial lung process rather than an obstructive pattern, which would instead show a reduced FEV1/FVC ratio. This is not a normal study, it does not represent a mixed pattern given the preserved ratio, and while pulmonary vascular disease can also reduce diffusing capacity, the combined reduction in both FEV1 and FVC with a preserved ratio points specifically toward a restrictive parenchymal process.
  13. The correct answer is A (Clinical stability and symptom severity, more than the specific radiographic size, primarily determine whether observation is appropriate): in managing a spontaneous pneumothorax, clinical stability, symptom severity, and the presence or absence of tension physiology are the primary drivers of management decisions, and a small, asymptomatic pneumothorax in a hemodynamically stable patient can typically be observed with follow-up imaging rather than immediate invasive intervention. Reflexively placing a chest tube or performing needle decompression in every case regardless of symptoms would expose a stable patient to unnecessary procedural risk, and completely omitting follow-up imaging to confirm resolution would not be appropriate monitoring; bilateral chest tubes have no role in a unilateral, asymptomatic finding.
  14. The correct answer is A (Small cell lung cancer): small cell lung cancer is the type most classically associated with paraneoplastic syndromes including syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion, along with ectopic ACTH production and Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome, reflecting its neuroendocrine origin and central location. Adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype overall and in nonsmokers but is not classically linked to SIADH. Squamous cell carcinoma is more associated with hypercalcemia from PTHrP secretion and cavitary lesions, and large cell carcinoma and bronchoalveolar carcinoma are not the classic associations for this specific paraneoplastic syndrome.
  15. The correct answer is D (Secretion of parathyroid hormone-related protein (PTHrP) by the tumor): squamous cell lung carcinoma is classically associated with hypercalcemia mediated by tumor secretion of parathyroid hormone-related protein, which mimics the action of parathyroid hormone at the bone and kidney while appropriately suppressing endogenous parathyroid hormone secretion, explaining his low PTH level despite hypercalcemia. Direct bony destruction can contribute to hypercalcemia in some malignancies but is not the classic mechanism specifically linked to squamous cell lung cancer. Antidiuretic hormone and adrenocorticotropic hormone secretion cause hyponatremia and Cushing syndrome respectively, not hypercalcemia, and unregulated vitamin D activation by granulomas is characteristic of sarcoidosis rather than squamous cell lung cancer.
  16. The correct answer is A (Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome improves with repeated muscle use, whereas myasthenia gravis worsens with repeated use): Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome, caused by autoantibodies against presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels that impair acetylcholine release, characteristically shows improvement in strength with repeated muscle contraction as calcium accumulates presynaptically, along with autonomic symptoms such as dry mouth, which contrasts with myasthenia gravis, an autoantibody-mediated postsynaptic acetylcholine receptor disorder that characteristically worsens with repeated use due to receptor fatigue. The two conditions have a distinguishable clinical pattern rather than being indistinguishable, myasthenia gravis is not caused by presynaptic calcium channel antibodies, and prominent ocular symptoms are a more classic early feature of myasthenia gravis rather than Lambert-Eaton syndrome.
  17. The correct answer is D (Surgical resection): for early-stage non-small cell lung cancer in a patient with adequate pulmonary reserve and no evidence of metastatic disease, surgical resection is the preferred primary treatment approach, offering the best chance for cure, unlike small cell lung cancer, which is rarely resectable at diagnosis due to early spread and is managed primarily with chemotherapy and radiation. Immunotherapy alone as first-line therapy is more typically used in advanced or metastatic disease based on molecular markers, and observation alone or palliative care referral without disease-directed treatment would be inappropriate for a potentially curable early-stage cancer in an operative candidate.
  18. The correct answer is D (Targeted therapy with an EGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor): identification of an actionable EGFR mutation in metastatic non-small cell lung cancer allows for targeted therapy with an EGFR tyrosine kinase inhibitor, which often provides better response rates and tolerability than standard cytotoxic chemotherapy in mutation-positive tumors, reflecting the modern molecularly guided approach to advanced non-small cell lung cancer treatment. Standard chemotherapy without considering this actionable mutation would not optimize his treatment, surgical resection is not appropriate for metastatic disease, antituberculous therapy has no role here, and observation alone would forgo an effective, targeted systemic treatment option.
  19. The correct answer is A (Pancoast tumor with associated Horner syndrome): an apical lung tumor invading the brachial plexus, causing shoulder and arm pain, and the sympathetic chain, causing ptosis, miosis, and anhidrosis (Horner syndrome), is the classic description of a Pancoast tumor. Superior vena cava syndrome instead presents with facial and upper extremity swelling and distended neck and chest wall veins from venous obstruction, and Lambert-Eaton syndrome, SIADH, and Cushing syndrome are paraneoplastic endocrine or neuromuscular syndromes that do not explain this localized structural invasion pattern with Horner syndrome.
  20. The correct answer is A (No further follow-up imaging is needed given the low risk of malignancy for this size and appearance): a pulmonary nodule under 6 mm in a low-risk patient, such as this nonsmoker with smooth margins and no other risk factors, generally requires no further follow-up imaging given the very low probability of malignancy at this size. Proceeding immediately to PET imaging and biopsy, or to empiric treatment or surgical resection without tissue confirmation, would represent significant overtreatment for a nodule with such a low pretest probability of cancer, and routine serial imaging every three months for five years is not the recommended follow-up interval or duration for a nodule this small and low risk.
Case case_ards_1

A 45-year-old man is admitted to the intensive care unit with septic shock from a urinary source. Over the next 18 hours he develops worsening hypoxemia requiring intubation. Chest radiograph shows new bilateral pulmonary opacities, and echocardiography shows normal left ventricular function with no evidence of volume overload.

  1. The correct answer is C (Acute respiratory distress syndrome; respiratory failure must not be fully explained by cardiac failure or fluid overload): the Berlin definition of ARDS requires onset within one week of a known clinical insult (here, sepsis), bilateral opacities not fully explained by effusion, lobar collapse, or nodules, and respiratory failure not fully explained by cardiac failure or fluid overload, which his normal echocardiogram and left ventricular function help support. Cardiogenic pulmonary edema would be expected to show evidence of volume overload or reduced cardiac function rather than the normal echocardiogram described, and elevated BNP is not the confirmatory criterion for ARDS. Community-acquired pneumonia typically presents with a single or focal infiltrate rather than the bilateral pattern described, pulmonary embolism is not excluded by these findings but a positive D-dimer is not part of the Berlin ARDS criteria, and this acute presentation over 18 hours does not fit chronic interstitial lung disease.
  2. The correct answer is C (Severe ARDS (P/F ratio below 100); low tidal volume ventilation at approximately 6 mL per kilogram of predicted body weight): his PaO2 of 72 mmHg on an FiO2 of 1.0 yields a P/F ratio of 72, placing him in the severe ARDS category (P/F ratio below 100), and the cornerstone of ventilator management in ARDS across severities is a lung-protective strategy using low tidal volumes of approximately 6 mL per kilogram of predicted body weight, which minimizes ventilator-induced lung injury from overdistension of already compromised alveoli. High or unrestricted tidal volumes would increase the risk of further lung injury rather than reduce it, and large tidal volumes are specifically avoided rather than preferred in ARDS management; this blood gas, combined with his clinical scenario, is entirely consistent with severe ARDS rather than an alternative diagnosis.
  3. The correct answer is D (Prone positioning): prone positioning has been shown to improve oxygenation and reduce mortality in patients with moderate to severe ARDS by improving ventilation-perfusion matching and recruiting previously dependent, poorly aerated lung regions. High tidal volume ventilation would worsen rather than help ventilator-induced lung injury in ARDS, discontinuing sedation for spontaneous breathing trials is not the appropriate next step in a patient with this degree of hypoxemia, aggressive fluid administration would worsen pulmonary edema in ARDS since conservative fluid management is instead preferred once hemodynamically stable, and targeting supranormal oxygen levels (permissive hyperoxia) is not an evidence-based strategy and can itself be harmful.
  4. The correct answer is A (Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis): idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis is the most common interstitial lung disease, occurring predominantly in older men with a smoking history, and this presentation of progressive dyspnea, Velcro-like crackles, clubbing, and a usual interstitial pneumonia pattern with peripheral, basal-predominant reticular changes and honeycombing on high resolution CT is the classic description. Sarcoidosis more typically shows bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy rather than this fibrotic pattern, hypersensitivity pneumonitis is linked to a specific organic antigen exposure, silicosis shows upper lobe predominant nodules with eggshell calcification, and bronchiolitis obliterans organizing pneumonia typically presents with a more subacute course and patchy consolidative changes rather than this classic fibrotic pattern.
  5. The correct answer is A (Antifibrotics can slow progression, but transplant is the only survival-extending option): antifibrotic medications such as pirfenidone and nintedanib have been shown to slow the rate of decline in lung function in idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis, but they do not reverse existing fibrosis, and lung transplantation remains the only intervention demonstrated to substantially extend survival in patients with advanced disease. Systemic corticosteroids have not been shown to reliably reverse fibrosis in this condition and are not standard therapy, treatment options beyond palliative care do exist as described, antibiotics are not curative for this noninfectious fibrotic process, and bronchodilators alone have not been shown to halt the progression of pulmonary fibrosis.
  6. The correct answer is C (Silicosis, with increased susceptibility to tuberculosis): silicosis, caused by inhalation of crystalline silica dust in occupations such as sandblasting, mining, and quarry work, characteristically produces upper lobe predominant nodular opacities with eggshell calcification of hilar lymph nodes, and it is well established to increase susceptibility to tuberculosis by impairing macrophage function. Asbestosis instead produces lower lobe predominant fibrosis with pleural plaques and increases risk of mesothelioma and bronchogenic carcinoma rather than being defined by this eggshell calcification pattern, coal worker's pneumoconiosis is not specifically linked to increased fungal infection risk, hypersensitivity pneumonitis relates to organic antigen exposure rather than mineral dust, and byssinosis is associated with cotton dust exposure rather than this radiographic pattern.
  7. The correct answer is A (His combined asbestos exposure and smoking history synergistically and dramatically increase his risk of bronchogenic carcinoma): asbestos exposure and cigarette smoking act synergistically to dramatically increase the risk of bronchogenic carcinoma, meaning the combined risk is substantially greater than the additive risk from either exposure alone, which is an important reason to counsel him strongly on smoking cessation. Asbestos exposure alone without smoking still increases cancer risk but not to the same degree as combined exposure. Pleural plaques themselves are a marker of asbestos exposure and are typically benign findings rather than indicating active malignancy requiring immediate biopsy, and mesothelioma risk in a patient with a clear asbestos exposure history is directly related to that exposure rather than independent of it.
  8. The correct answer is D (Avoidance of the causative antigen exposure, such as moldy hay): this presentation, recurrent respiratory symptoms temporally related to hay exposure with improvement away from the farm, is classic for hypersensitivity pneumonitis, known in this context as farmer's lung, caused by an immune-mediated reaction to inhaled organic antigens such as those found in moldy hay, and the cornerstone of management is identification and avoidance of the causative antigen exposure. Antituberculous therapy, lung transplantation, and antifibrotic therapy are not appropriate initial treatments for this exposure-related hypersensitivity process, and while secondary bacterial infection can occasionally complicate respiratory illness, the primary driver of his recurrent symptoms described here is antigen exposure rather than bacterial pneumonia.
  9. The correct answer is B (Sarcoidosis, with hypercalcemia caused by unregulated conversion of vitamin D by macrophages within granulomas): this presentation of bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy, erythema nodosum, uveitis (blurred vision and eye pain), hypercalcemia, an elevated angiotensin converting enzyme level, and noncaseating granulomas on biopsy with negative infectious workup is classic for sarcoidosis, a multisystem granulomatous disease in which macrophages within granulomas unregulatedly convert vitamin D to its active form, driving hypercalcemia. Tuberculosis would be expected to show caseating granulomas and a positive infectious workup rather than the negative studies described here, silicosis and hypersensitivity pneumonitis do not typically present with this extrapulmonary constellation, and idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis is not a granulomatous disease and does not explain the hypercalcemia or extrapulmonary findings.
  10. The correct answer is A (Observation with periodic monitoring, given her asymptomatic stage I presentation): asymptomatic or minimally symptomatic sarcoidosis with stage I disease, characterized by isolated bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy without significant pulmonary infiltrates, extrapulmonary involvement, or functional impairment, is often managed with observation and periodic monitoring rather than immediate pharmacologic treatment, since many such cases remit spontaneously. Immediate systemic corticosteroids are reserved for more symptomatic or progressive disease. Antituberculous therapy is not indicated once infectious causes including tuberculosis have been excluded, and lung transplantation and antifibrotic therapy are not appropriate for this early, asymptomatic presentation of sarcoidosis.
  11. The correct answer is C (Treatment for latent tuberculosis infection, such as isoniazid for several months): a positive interferon gamma release assay in the absence of symptoms and with a normal chest radiograph indicates latent tuberculosis infection rather than active disease, and treatment is recommended to reduce the risk of future reactivation, commonly with isoniazid for several months or an appropriate shorter rifamycin-based regimen. Withholding treatment entirely would leave him at ongoing risk of reactivation. The full four-drug regimen used for active tuberculosis is not necessary for latent infection, airborne isolation is not required since he does not have active, transmissible pulmonary disease, and there is no indication of pneumonia requiring that alternative treatment.
  12. The correct answer is B (Simple coal worker's pneumoconiosis, with potential progression to progressive massive fibrosis): chronic coal dust inhalation produces coal worker's pneumoconiosis, which in its simple form shows small nodular opacities, often upper zone predominant, and carries the risk of progressing over time to progressive massive fibrosis, characterized by larger conglomerate masses and more significant impairment. Silicosis is specifically caused by crystalline silica exposure rather than coal dust, though the two can occasionally coexist in certain mining exposures, this presentation is not classic asbestosis, which would show lower lobe predominant fibrosis with pleural plaques, hypersensitivity pneumonitis relates to an organic antigen rather than mineral dust exposure, and bronchiectasis is a distinct process not primarily driven by this occupational history.
  13. The correct answer is B (Confirm the temporal relationship to bird antigen exposure and recommend avoidance of the exposure): her presentation, recurrent respiratory symptoms that improve when away from her home environment where she keeps birds, is classic for hypersensitivity pneumonitis from avian antigen exposure, known as bird fancier's lung, and the appropriate approach is to confirm this temporal pattern and recommend avoidance of the antigen exposure, which often leads to significant symptomatic improvement. Antituberculous therapy is not supported by this exposure-related presentation, continuing her current exposure unchanged without further evaluation would not address the likely cause of her recurrent symptoms, long-term antibiotics do not address this immune-mediated rather than infectious process, and lung transplantation would be a premature and excessive intervention before a trial of antigen avoidance.
  14. The correct answer is C (Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an obligate aerobe that thrives in the higher oxygen tension of the upper lung zones): reactivation tuberculosis characteristically involves the upper lung zones because Mycobacterium tuberculosis is an obligate aerobe, and the upper lobes have relatively higher oxygen tension and lower blood flow compared with the more perfused lower lobes, favoring growth and reactivation of the organism in this region. The upper lobes do not have a richer blood supply, in fact they are relatively less perfused than the lower lobes, they do not have lower oxygen tension, and while inhalation patterns can influence initial deposition, the classic explanation tested for reactivation TB's upper lobe predominance relates to oxygen tension and relative perfusion rather than gravity or an unrelated lymphatic explanation.
  15. The correct answer is B (Isoniazid, which can be prevented with concurrent pyridoxine (vitamin B6) supplementation): isoniazid can cause peripheral neuropathy by interfering with pyridoxine (vitamin B6) metabolism, and this complication can be prevented or treated with concurrent pyridoxine supplementation, which is why it is often co-administered with isoniazid therapy. Rifampin is more associated with orange discoloration of body fluids and cytochrome P450 induction rather than peripheral neuropathy prevented by B12, ethambutol is associated with optic neuritis and red-green color blindness rather than a neuropathy prevented by thiamine, and pyrazinamide is more associated with hepatotoxicity and hyperuricemia rather than a neuropathy addressed by folate; vitamin C supplementation does not address isoniazid-induced neuropathy.
  16. The correct answer is C (Ethambutol): ethambutol is classically associated with optic neuritis, presenting with decreased visual acuity and red-green color blindness, which requires prompt recognition and typically discontinuation of the drug along with ophthalmologic evaluation. Isoniazid is more associated with peripheral neuropathy and hepatotoxicity, rifampin with orange discoloration of body fluids and enzyme induction, pyrazinamide with hepatotoxicity and hyperuricemia, and while streptomycin (an aminoglycoside sometimes used in specific tuberculosis regimens) is associated with ototoxicity and nephrotoxicity rather than this specific visual disturbance.
  17. The correct answer is D (Continue monitoring closely; oxygen therapy is not yet indicated since his values are still above threshold): long-term home oxygen is indicated for a resting PaO2 at or below 55 mmHg or oxygen saturation at or below 88%, or a PaO2 at or below 59 mmHg with evidence of cor pulmonale or polycythemia, and his stable, repeated values here do not yet meet these thresholds, so ongoing monitoring for future qualification is appropriate rather than either immediate prescription or complete discontinuation of future reassessment. Prescribing oxygen now would not be supported by his current, above-threshold values, waiting a full year without reassessment could delay identification of a future qualifying change, restricting to exercise-only oxygen is a distinct consideration from this resting oxygen assessment, and permanently discontinuing future assessments is not appropriate given that COPD is a progressive disease.
  18. The correct answer is A (Severe obstructive sleep apnea; continuous positive airway pressure is first-line treatment): an apnea-hypopnea index of 42 events per hour, well above the threshold of 30, classifies this as severe obstructive sleep apnea, and continuous positive airway pressure, which splints the upper airway open during sleep, is the first-line treatment for OSA across severities including severe disease. Mild OSA (AHI 5 to 15) would not apply here, and even in moderate disease CPAP rather than oral appliance therapy is generally preferred as first-line treatment. Immediate surgery is not the routine first-line approach and is reserved for specific anatomic obstruction or CPAP intolerance, and the clinical description of obesity, snoring, and witnessed apneas along with a clearly elevated AHI is consistent with obstructive rather than central sleep apnea, which would not be the primary consideration here.
  19. The correct answer is B (Further evaluation with PET imaging and consideration of tissue biopsy given high-risk features): a pulmonary nodule with spiculated margins, larger size, documented interval growth on serial imaging, and significant smoking history all represent high-risk features that warrant further evaluation with PET imaging and consideration of tissue biopsy to evaluate for malignancy, since findings such as irregular or spiculated margins and growth over time suggest malignancy rather than a benign process. Interval growth of a nodule is a concerning feature rather than a reassuring one, so simple reassurance, discharge from follow-up, or deferring further imaging for years would risk delaying diagnosis of a potentially malignant lesion, and there is no clinical information here supporting empiric antituberculous therapy as the appropriate next step.
  20. The correct answer is A (It suggests a benign process such as a hamartoma rather than malignancy): a popcorn-like, dense, central pattern of calcification within a pulmonary nodule is a classic benign feature most often associated with a pulmonary hamartoma, and along with other benign patterns such as central or laminated calcification, this pattern argues against malignancy and generally supports a more conservative follow-up approach rather than immediate invasive intervention. This calcification pattern does not suggest malignancy requiring urgent biopsy, it is not irrelevant to malignancy risk assessment, it is not a pattern exclusive to tuberculosis, and it does not mandate immediate surgical resection without further imaging follow-up given its benign implications.
  21. The correct answer is A (It is the most common subtype of lung cancer overall and is the most common subtype among nonsmokers): lung adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype of lung cancer overall and is specifically the most common subtype seen in nonsmokers, which fits this patient's presentation despite her lack of smoking history. It is not exclusive to smokers, since it is the predominant subtype in never-smokers as well. It typically arises peripherally rather than centrally, in contrast to squamous cell and small cell carcinoma, which tend to be more centrally located, it is small cell carcinoma rather than adenocarcinoma that is most classically linked to SIADH, and many cases of adenocarcinoma, particularly when detected at an early stage, are resectable rather than universally unresectable.
Case MULTI-C1

A 26-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a high-speed motorcycle collision. He was found unresponsive at the scene by paramedics, who report he was breathing spontaneously but had obvious deformity of the left thigh and a large scalp laceration. On arrival his blood pressure is 88/54 mmHg, heart rate is 128/min, respiratory rate is 26/min, and oxygen saturation is 91% on a nonrebreather mask. He opens his eyes to painful stimuli, makes incomprehensible sounds, and withdraws from pain in all four extremities. Breath sounds are absent on the left with tracheal deviation to the right and distended neck veins.

  1. The correct answer is D (Perform needle decompression of the left chest followed by tube thoracostomy): The findings of absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, distended neck veins, and hypotension in a trauma patient describe a tension pneumothorax, which is a clinical diagnosis that should never wait for imaging confirmation. Needle decompression provides immediate temporizing relief of the mechanical obstruction to venous return and cardiac output, followed promptly by definitive tube thoracostomy. Fluids and further imaging are reasonable later steps but do not address the immediately life-threatening obstructive physiology present here.
  2. The correct answer is B (Perform endotracheal intubation to protect the airway): A Glasgow Coma Scale score of 8 or less has traditionally been used as the threshold for definitive airway management, but a score of 9 with this mechanism of injury, ongoing altered mentation, and inability to reliably protect the airway from aspiration still warrants intubation in this unstable multi-trauma patient. Waiting for further deterioration risks aspiration and hypoxia. A nasopharyngeal airway and observation alone are insufficient when the patient cannot reliably protect the airway.
  3. The correct answer is B (Apply a traction splint and obtain orthopedic consultation for definitive fixation): A closed femur fracture with a deformed, swollen thigh but an intact distal pulse is managed with realignment and splinting (traction splint) to reduce ongoing blood loss, pain, and further soft tissue injury, followed by definitive orthopedic fixation once the patient is stabilized. Femur fractures can cause significant occult blood loss even without external bleeding, so early stabilization is important, but amputation is not indicated with an intact distal pulse, and outpatient management is inappropriate for a patient requiring surgical fixation.
Case MULTI-C2

A 68-year-old woman underwent an open sigmoid colectomy for diverticular disease six days ago. Her postoperative course had been unremarkable until this morning, when she develops a temperature of 39.1 C, heart rate 118/min, blood pressure 92/58 mmHg, and respiratory rate 24/min. She reports increasing abdominal pain and the surgical incision appears mildly erythematous but without drainage. Laboratory studies show a white blood cell count of 19,000/mm3 and a lactate of 3.8 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is D (An anastomotic leak with resulting intra-abdominal infection): Fever occurring on postoperative day 5 through 7 after a bowel resection, accompanied by hemodynamic instability, worsening abdominal pain, leukocytosis, and an elevated lactate, is highly concerning for an anastomotic leak with resulting peritonitis or intra-abdominal abscess. Atelectasis typically presents earlier (the first 24 to 48 hours) and would not explain this degree of hemodynamic compromise. A urinary source or drug fever would not typically produce worsening localized abdominal pain at the surgical site.
  2. The correct answer is C (Begin broad-spectrum empiric antibiotics and start intravenous fluid resuscitation): This patient meets criteria for septic shock (suspected infection with hypotension and lactate above 2 mmol/L), so immediate management requires prompt broad-spectrum empiric antibiotics and aggressive fluid resuscitation, ideally within the first hour of recognition, followed by source identification and control. Waiting for a specific culture result before starting antibiotics would dangerously delay treatment in an unstable patient, and imaging can proceed in parallel with, not instead of, initial resuscitation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Begin a norepinephrine infusion to maintain adequate mean arterial pressure): When hypotension persists despite adequate fluid resuscitation, the patient meets criteria for septic shock and requires vasopressor support to maintain a mean arterial pressure of at least 65 mmHg. Norepinephrine is the first-line vasopressor in septic shock because it provides balanced alpha and beta adrenergic support with a lower arrhythmia risk than dopamine, which is no longer preferred as first-line therapy. A diuretic would be inappropriate and dangerous in a hypotensive, likely volume-depleted patient.
  4. The correct answer is B (Begin N-acetylcysteine therapy based on the Rumack-Matthew nomogram): The Rumack-Matthew nomogram plots serum acetaminophen concentration against time since a single acute ingestion (validated starting at 4 hours) to determine the need for N-acetylcysteine. A level of 180 mcg/mL at 6 hours falls above the treatment line, indicating a significant risk of hepatotoxicity, so N-acetylcysteine should be started promptly regardless of current symptoms, since liver injury develops silently over the following 24 to 72 hours. Activated charcoal alone is insufficient once this treatment threshold is met, and hemodialysis is reserved for select severe cases, not routine management.
  5. The correct answer is D (Accumulation of the toxic metabolite NAPQI after glutathione stores are depleted): Acetaminophen is normally metabolized safely through glucuronidation and sulfation, with only a small fraction converted by CYP2E1 to the reactive, hepatotoxic metabolite NAPQI, which is ordinarily detoxified by conjugation with glutathione. In overdose, glutathione stores become depleted, allowing NAPQI to accumulate and cause centrilobular hepatocellular necrosis. N-acetylcysteine works precisely by repleting glutathione stores (and providing an alternate sulfate source), which is why early administration is protective.
  6. The correct answer is B (Distributive shock with low systemic vascular resistance): Septic shock is a form of distributive shock characterized by profound peripheral vasodilation, producing a low systemic vascular resistance, often with a normal or elevated cardiac output, particularly in the early warm phase before myocardial depression sets in. This contrasts with hypovolemic shock, which shows low cardiac output and high systemic vascular resistance from compensatory vasoconstriction, and cardiogenic or obstructive shock, both of which show low cardiac output with elevated filling pressures.
  7. The correct answer is B (Atropine to counteract excess muscarinic stimulation): This presentation, with excessive secretions, miosis, bradycardia, and fasciculations, is classic for organophosphate poisoning producing a cholinergic toxidrome from acetylcholinesterase inhibition. Atropine is the initial treatment to counteract the muscarinic effects (secretions, bradycardia), and pralidoxime is added to reactivate acetylcholinesterase before it becomes permanently bound. Naloxone, physostigmine, and flumazenil address entirely different toxidromes and would not address this cholinergic excess.
  8. The correct answer is D (A rising anti-double-stranded DNA antibody titer with falling complement levels): Anti-double-stranded DNA antibody levels tend to rise and complement levels (C3 and C4) tend to fall during active lupus flares, particularly lupus nephritis, because complement is being consumed by immune complex deposition in the glomeruli. A low-titer antinuclear antibody is highly sensitive but not specific for SLE and does not track disease activity, an elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate is nonspecific, and rheumatoid factor is more associated with rheumatoid arthritis and is not specific for lupus activity.
  9. The correct answer is D (Begin high-dose glucocorticoids immediately based on clinical suspicion): This presentation (jaw claudication, new headache, elevated inflammatory markers, and transient vision loss in an older adult) is classic for giant cell arteritis, which risks permanent blindness if untreated. High-dose glucocorticoids should be started immediately based on clinical suspicion without waiting for temporal artery biopsy confirmation, since biopsy findings can remain positive for one to two weeks after starting steroids. Delaying treatment for biopsy or additional imaging risks irreversible vision loss.
  10. The correct answer is C (His functional capacity and low-risk profile make further cardiac testing unnecessary): A patient who can achieve at least 4 metabolic equivalents of activity, such as climbing two flights of stairs without symptoms, and who has none of the major risk factors captured by the Revised Cardiac Risk Index, generally does not require further preoperative cardiac testing before moderate-risk surgery. Reflexively ordering stress testing, angiography, or echocardiography in a patient with good functional capacity and a low-risk profile adds cost and delay without meaningful benefit.
  11. The correct answer is B (The normal postoperative inflammatory response and possible atelectasis): Fever in the first 24 to 48 hours after surgery is most commonly attributed to the normal systemic inflammatory response to surgery itself, with atelectasis also traditionally implicated, though its direct causal role has been increasingly questioned. Surgical site infections typically present later (around day 5 or beyond), deep venous thrombosis is more typical after day 7, and this patient has no signs suggesting an anastomotic complication, which would also be far less expected after a cholecystectomy than after a bowel resection.
  12. The correct answer is B (A mixed picture with early respiratory alkalosis and evolving metabolic acidosis): Early salicylate toxicity classically produces a primary respiratory alkalosis from direct stimulation of the medullary respiratory center, evidenced by the elevated pH and low PaCO2. However, the bicarbonate is already low, more than expected compensation for a pure acute respiratory alkalosis would produce, indicating a concurrent evolving anion gap metabolic acidosis from salicylate uncoupling oxidative phosphorylation. This classic mixed picture is a high-yield salicylate toxicity finding.
  13. The correct answer is A (Intravenous or intranasal naloxone with attention to the risk of re-sedation): Miosis, respiratory depression, and altered mental status form the classic opioid toxidrome, and naloxone is the specific antidote. Because naloxone's duration of action is typically shorter than that of many opioids, repeat dosing or a continuous infusion may be necessary to prevent recurrent respiratory depression as the naloxone wears off. Flumazenil, physostigmine, and fomepizole are antidotes for unrelated toxidromes and would not reverse opioid-induced respiratory depression.
  14. The correct answer is A (Propranolol for heart rate and blood pressure control): Beta blockers, including nonselective agents like propranolol, are generally avoided in cocaine toxicity because blocking beta-2 mediated vasodilation while leaving alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed can worsen hypertension and coronary vasospasm. Benzodiazepines are first-line for the agitation, hypertension, and tachycardia of sympathomimetic toxicity, and supportive measures like cooling and fluids are appropriate adjuncts.
  15. The correct answer is D (Administer intravenous calcium gluconate to stabilize the cardiac membrane): Hyperkalemia with electrocardiographic changes (peaked T waves progressing to QRS widening) represents an immediate cardiac emergency, and intravenous calcium should be given first to stabilize the cardiac membrane and reduce the risk of a fatal arrhythmia, even though it does not lower the serum potassium level itself. Measures to shift potassium intracellularly (insulin with glucose, beta agonists) and to remove potassium from the body (resins, diuretics, dialysis) follow but should not precede membrane stabilization when ECG changes are present.
  16. The correct answer is D (Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis): The combination of asthma, peripheral eosinophilia, sinus disease, and a vasculitic neuropathy (mononeuritis multiplex) with a positive perinuclear antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody strongly suggests eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis typically involves the upper and lower respiratory tract with cytoplasmic antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies rather than prominent eosinophilia or asthma, microscopic polyangiitis lacks the granulomatous and asthmatic features, and polyarteritis nodosa is a medium-vessel vasculitis without ANCA association or asthma.
  17. The correct answer is B (Infection, malignancy, and autoimmune or inflammatory disease): Fever of unknown origin, defined as a documented temperature above 38.3 C lasting more than three weeks without a diagnosis despite reasonable evaluation, has a differential traditionally organized into infections (such as tuberculosis, endocarditis, or occult abscess), malignancy (such as lymphoma), autoimmune or inflammatory conditions (such as adult-onset Still disease or vasculitis), and miscellaneous causes. Self-limited viral illnesses, uncomplicated urinary tract infections, and dehydration would not typically produce a persistent, unexplained fever of this duration.
  18. The correct answer is A (Immune complex deposition producing painful nodules): The tender nodules on the finger and toe pads described are Osler nodes, which result from immune complex deposition rather than direct septic embolization. This distinguishes them from Janeway lesions, which are painless erythematous macules on the palms and soles caused by septic microemboli. Both findings occur in infective endocarditis, a classic multisystem disease with fever plus embolic and immunologic phenomena affecting distant organs.
  19. The correct answer is B (Airway patency, which is already suggested by his clear speech): The ATLS primary survey always begins with airway assessment, and a patient who is speaking clearly in full sentences has, by definition, a patent airway at that moment, though ongoing reassessment remains important. Even though his complaint is abdominal pain, the primary survey sequence (airway, breathing, circulation, disability, exposure) must be systematically confirmed before moving to a focused abdominal exam, secondary survey, or definitive imaging, which come later in the evaluation.
  20. The correct answer is D (Class III hemorrhage): Class III hemorrhage, corresponding to roughly 30 to 40 percent blood volume loss, is characterized by overt hypotension, tachypnea, and early mental status changes, matching this presentation. Class I (up to 15 percent) shows minimal vital sign change, Class II (15 to 30 percent) shows tachycardia with a narrowing pulse pressure but typically preserved blood pressure, and Class IV (over 40 percent) shows more profound hypotension and significant mental status depression, representing an immediately life-threatening degree of hemorrhage.
  21. The correct answer is C (Physostigmine to reverse anticholinergic symptoms): This presentation describes the anticholinergic toxidrome. While physostigmine can reverse pure anticholinergic toxicity, it is generally avoided when tricyclic antidepressant co-ingestion is suspected or confirmed, because it can worsen cardiac conduction abnormalities and precipitate seizures or arrhythmias in that setting. Supportive fluids, cooling, and benzodiazepines for agitation remain appropriate regardless of a potential tricyclic co-ingestion.
  22. The correct answer is C (Venous thromboembolism with pulmonary embolism): Fever occurring after postoperative day 7, combined with pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, and a swollen tender calf, strongly suggests deep venous thrombosis with pulmonary embolism, which classically presents in this later postoperative window, particularly after orthopedic procedures involving prolonged immobility. Surgical site infection would more typically show localized wound findings, atelectasis presents earlier in the postoperative course, and there is no described new medication exposure to suggest drug fever.
  23. The correct answer is D (Fomepizole to block alcohol dehydrogenase): This presentation, with an elevated osmolar gap, an anion gap metabolic acidosis, and calcium oxalate crystalluria, is classic for ethylene glycol (antifreeze) poisoning. Fomepizole blocks alcohol dehydrogenase, preventing the formation of the toxic metabolites responsible for the acidosis and renal injury, and hemodialysis is used for severe cases. Deferoxamine is used for iron overdose, digoxin-specific antibody fragments for digoxin toxicity, and N-acetylcysteine for acetaminophen toxicity, none of which apply here.
  24. The correct answer is A (Continue the beta blocker perioperatively as he has been taking it): Patients who are already established on beta blocker therapy should generally continue it through the perioperative period, since abrupt discontinuation can precipitate rebound tachycardia, hypertension, or myocardial ischemia. In contrast, acutely starting a beta blocker for the first time on or immediately before the day of surgery has been associated with increased perioperative stroke and mortality despite reducing nonfatal myocardial infarction, so new initiation should not occur acutely.
  25. The correct answer is A (Scleroderma renal crisis): Scleroderma renal crisis presents with abrupt-onset severe hypertension and acute kidney injury in a patient with systemic sclerosis, and prompt recognition is critical because early treatment with an angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor significantly improves outcomes. Sjogren syndrome more classically causes dryness and occasionally tubulointerstitial disease rather than this abrupt hypertensive crisis, lupus nephritis occurs in SLE rather than systemic sclerosis, and Goodpasture syndrome involves anti-glomerular basement membrane antibodies with pulmonary hemorrhage, an unrelated disease process.
  26. The correct answer is C (Intravenous glucagon): Glucagon is a relatively specific antidote for beta blocker toxicity because it increases intracellular cyclic AMP through a receptor pathway that bypasses the blocked beta-adrenergic receptor, improving heart rate and contractility. High-dose insulin with glucose (euglycemia therapy) and calcium are more specifically used for calcium channel blocker toxicity, and sodium bicarbonate is more relevant to sodium channel blockade toxicity such as tricyclic antidepressant overdose, though it can occasionally be used adjunctively in mixed overdoses.
  27. The correct answer is B (Ketoacidosis, lactic acidosis, toxic alcohol ingestion, uremia, or salicylates): An elevated anion gap metabolic acidosis has a differential that classically includes ketoacidosis (diabetic, alcoholic, or starvation), lactic acidosis, toxic alcohol ingestions (methanol, ethylene glycol), uremia, and salicylate toxicity, all of which generate unmeasured anions. Gastrointestinal bicarbonate losses and renal tubular acidosis instead classically produce a normal anion gap (hyperchloremic) metabolic acidosis, and excessive saline administration also typically produces a normal anion gap dilutional acidosis rather than a high anion gap picture.
  28. The correct answer is D (A positive anti-Smith antibody): Anti-Smith antibodies are highly specific for systemic lupus erythematosus, even though they are less sensitive than antinuclear antibodies, and their presence strongly supports the diagnosis in a patient with a compatible clinical picture. Rheumatoid factor and anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibodies are associated with rheumatoid arthritis rather than lupus, and antimitochondrial antibodies are associated with primary biliary cholangitis, none of which are specific confirmatory findings for SLE.
  29. The correct answer is A (Remove or exchange the catheter and begin appropriate antibiotic therapy): This presentation, with fever and urinary symptoms on postoperative day 3 to 5 in a patient with an indwelling catheter and pyuria with bacteriuria, is consistent with a catheter-associated urinary tract infection. Appropriate management includes removing or exchanging the catheter when feasible along with directed antibiotic therapy. Observation without treatment would be inappropriate given clear evidence of infection, imaging is not the first step for an uncomplicated catheter-associated urinary tract infection, and empiric antifungal therapy is not indicated without evidence of fungal infection.
  30. The correct answer is D (A balanced ratio of packed red blood cells, fresh frozen plasma, and platelets): Current trauma resuscitation practice favors a balanced transfusion strategy, using packed red blood cells, fresh frozen plasma, and platelets in roughly equal ratios, over crystalloid-heavy resuscitation or red cells alone. This approach better replaces the components actually being lost in hemorrhage and reduces dilutional coagulopathy, which worsens ongoing bleeding and contributes to the lethal triad of hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy.
  31. The correct answer is B (Sympathomimetic toxidrome, distinguished from anticholinergic toxicity by diaphoresis rather than dry skin): Sympathomimetic toxicity, from agents such as cocaine or amphetamines, produces tachycardia, hypertension, agitation, and mydriasis similar to the anticholinergic toxidrome, but the presence of diaphoresis (sweaty skin) rather than dry, flushed skin distinguishes sympathomimetic toxicity from anticholinergic toxicity. The other toxidromes listed feature entirely different vital sign patterns (bradycardia and miosis for cholinergic, respiratory depression and miosis for opioid, and drowsiness with hypotension for sedative-hypnotic toxicity) and do not match this presentation.
  32. The correct answer is B (Blood cultures should be obtained first, if this does not meaningfully delay prompt antibiotic administration): In suspected sepsis, blood cultures should ideally be obtained before starting antibiotics, since antibiotics can reduce culture sensitivity, but this should not meaningfully delay the prompt administration of broad-spectrum antibiotics, which is a time-sensitive intervention within the first hour of recognition. Waiting for final culture results, delaying antibiotics for hours, or drawing cultures only after prolonged antibiotic exposure would all inappropriately delay effective care in a septic patient.
  33. The correct answer is D (Correcting the sodium by 20 mEq/L or more over the first 24 hours): In chronic, severe hyponatremia, correcting the serum sodium too rapidly (generally more than about 8 to 10 mEq/L in 24 hours) risks osmotic demyelination syndrome, a serious and often irreversible neurologic injury affecting the pons and other brain regions. A slower, carefully monitored correction rate, along with frequent sodium checks and specialist input, is the safer approach to avoid this complication.
  34. The correct answer is A (Perform emergent needle decompression of the right chest): This clinical picture, with absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, and jugular venous distension, describes a tension pneumothorax, a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate needle decompression rather than confirmatory imaging. A central venous catheter and diuretics do not address the underlying mechanical obstruction to venous return and would not relieve the life-threatening physiology present here.
  35. The correct answer is C (Increased risk of lymphoma): Sjogren syndrome, characterized by dry eyes and dry mouth from lymphocytic infiltration of exocrine glands, carries an increased long-term risk of lymphoma, which patients should be counseled about and monitored for. Pulmonary fibrosis is more classically associated with systemic sclerosis, renal crisis is specific to systemic sclerosis, and aortic aneurysm risk is more associated with large-vessel vasculitis or connective tissue disorders like Marfan syndrome, not Sjogren syndrome.
  36. The correct answer is C (Polyarteritis nodosa): Polyarteritis nodosa is a medium-vessel vasculitis that classically produces multiple aneurysms of medium-sized arteries (mesenteric, renal) visible on angiography, typically spares the lungs, and is not associated with antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibodies, matching this presentation. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis and microscopic polyangiitis are small-vessel, ANCA-associated vasculitides, and giant cell arteritis is a large-vessel vasculitis affecting older adults with cranial symptoms, none of which fit this angiographic and serologic picture.
  37. The correct answer is A (Use mechanical prophylaxis plus low molecular weight heparin when her bleeding risk allows it): Postoperative venous thromboembolism prophylaxis typically combines mechanical measures (such as intermittent pneumatic compression devices) with pharmacologic prophylaxis, such as low molecular weight heparin, when the patient's bleeding risk allows it. Withholding all prophylaxis in an immobile postoperative patient increases venous thromboembolism risk, relying on ambulation alone is insufficient in a sedated, intubated patient, and aspirin alone is not the standard of care for surgical thromboprophylaxis in this setting.
  38. The correct answer is C (Balancing his thromboembolic risk from the mechanical valve against the bleeding risk of the planned procedure): Perioperative anticoagulation bridging decisions are guided by weighing the patient's thromboembolic risk, which is elevated with a mechanical mitral valve, recent venous thromboembolism, or a high stroke risk score, against the bleeding risk associated with the specific planned procedure, such as a colonoscopy with polypectomy. Neither polyp appearance, anesthetic preference, nor equipment choice factor into this risk-based decision about anticoagulation management.
  39. The correct answer is A (Deferoxamine): Deferoxamine is the chelating agent used for significant iron overdose, binding free iron to facilitate its excretion and reduce ongoing tissue toxicity. Fomepizole is used for toxic alcohol ingestions, pralidoxime is used alongside atropine for organophosphate poisoning, and digoxin-specific antibody fragments are used for digoxin toxicity, none of which apply to this iron ingestion.
  40. The correct answer is A (A systematic search for and correction of reversible contributing factors): Postoperative delirium, especially common in elderly patients, is best managed by systematically identifying and correcting reversible contributors such as pain, infection, medication effects, sleep disruption, hypoxia, or electrolyte derangement, rather than reflexive sedation, which can worsen delirium, particularly with benzodiazepines. Physical restraints alone without evaluation and simple reassurance without any workup both fail to address potentially treatable underlying causes.
  41. The correct answer is C (Immediate empiric antibiotics along with aggressive hemodynamic support, without delay for confirmatory testing): Fulminant meningococcemia can progress to septic shock within hours and carries high mortality, so empiric antibiotics and aggressive hemodynamic support must begin immediately based on clinical suspicion, without waiting for lumbar puncture or imaging results, which would dangerously delay life-saving treatment. This case illustrates how a multisystem infectious process can cause simultaneous neurologic, hematologic, and hemodynamic collapse, requiring immediate, parallel intervention rather than a sequential diagnostic-then-treatment approach.
  42. The correct answer is B (Emergent surgical debridement along with broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics): Rapidly spreading skin changes with bullae, pain out of proportion to examination findings, and systemic toxicity in a surgical wound raise concern for necrotizing soft tissue infection, a surgical emergency requiring immediate operative debridement in addition to broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics. Oral antibiotics, simple observation, or topical dressing changes alone would allow rapidly progressive, life-threatening tissue destruction to continue unchecked.
  43. The correct answer is C (Cardiogenic shock from acute pump failure): A low cardiac output combined with elevated filling pressures, such as an elevated pulmonary capillary wedge pressure, reflects cardiogenic shock, in which the heart itself has failed as a pump, in this case likely from a perioperative myocardial event. Distributive shock would show low systemic vascular resistance rather than elevated filling pressures, hypovolemic shock would show low, not elevated, filling pressures, and obstructive shock from tension pneumothorax would typically present with additional findings such as absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation.
  44. The correct answer is C (An antiphospholipid antibody that interferes with phospholipid-dependent coagulation testing in vitro while promoting thrombosis in vivo): The lupus anticoagulant is an antiphospholipid antibody that paradoxically prolongs phospholipid-dependent clotting assays in vitro, producing a prolonged partial thromboplastin time that fails to correct with mixing studies, while clinically promoting thrombosis rather than bleeding in vivo as part of antiphospholipid syndrome, which is associated with systemic lupus erythematosus and recurrent pregnancy loss. This pattern is distinct from a true factor deficiency, vitamin K deficiency, or a simple laboratory artifact, none of which would explain the combination of this specific antibody and the associated clinical thrombotic risk.
  45. The correct answer is A (Renal cell carcinoma): Renal cell carcinoma is a classic malignant cause of fever of unknown origin, sometimes presenting with paraneoplastic fever along with the more commonly recognized triad of flank pain, hematuria, and a palpable abdominal mass, though the full triad is actually uncommon at presentation. A simple renal cyst, benign prostatic hyperplasia, and uncomplicated cystitis would not typically be associated with a prolonged, otherwise unexplained fever of this nature.
  46. The correct answer is A (She has one of the six risk factors captured by the index, insulin-dependent diabetes): The Revised Cardiac Risk Index includes high-risk surgery, ischemic heart disease, congestive heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and a preoperative creatinine above 2 mg/dL. This patient has only one of those six factors, insulin-dependent diabetes, since she lacks the other listed comorbidities and has normal renal function; whether bariatric surgery itself qualifies as high-risk would need to be considered separately, but the index framework applies broadly across noncardiac surgeries.
  47. The correct answer is A (Emergent surgical exploration given hemodynamic instability with a positive FAST examination): A hemodynamically unstable trauma patient with a positive FAST examination showing free intra-abdominal fluid should proceed to emergent surgical exploration rather than further diagnostic imaging, since the instability itself indicates ongoing significant hemorrhage requiring definitive surgical control. Discharge, elective imaging the next day, and observation alone would all inappropriately delay necessary intervention in a patient who is actively decompensating from presumed intra-abdominal hemorrhage.
Case endo_case_dka

A 19-year-old woman with a 6-year history of type 1 diabetes is brought to the emergency department by her roommate after two days of nausea, vomiting, and abdominal pain. She reports running out of insulin four days ago. Temperature is 37.4 C, blood pressure is 98/60 mmHg, pulse is 118/min, and respirations are 28/min and deep. She appears lethargic but arousable, with dry mucous membranes and a fruity odor on her breath. Initial labs show glucose 512 mg/dL, sodium 132 mEq/L, potassium 3.1 mEq/L, chloride 96 mEq/L, bicarbonate 8 mEq/L, and arterial pH 7.12. Urinalysis is strongly positive for ketones.

  1. The correct answer is B (Accumulation of beta-hydroxybutyrate and acetoacetate from unchecked lipolysis and ketogenesis): This patient has classic diabetic ketoacidosis: absolute insulin deficiency after missing doses allows unrestrained lipolysis, generating free fatty acids that the liver converts to ketoacids. These ketoacids, beta-hydroxybutyrate and acetoacetate, consume bicarbonate buffer and produce the high anion gap metabolic acidosis seen here (gap = 132 minus (96 plus 8) = 28). Vomiting-related bicarbonate loss and lactic acidosis can coexist but do not explain the strongly positive ketones and the markedly elevated anion gap that dominates this picture.
  2. The correct answer is D (Begin intravenous potassium repletion before starting the insulin infusion): Her potassium is 3.1 mEq/L, below the 3.3 mEq/L threshold at which insulin should not yet be started, because insulin drives potassium into cells and can precipitate dangerous hypokalemia and arrhythmia in a patient who already has depleted total body potassium despite this borderline lab value. Potassium repletion must begin first, with insulin added once the level is verified safe. Bicarbonate is not indicated at this pH, and delaying all therapy pending a repeat panel is unsafe.
  3. The correct answer is A (Add dextrose to the intravenous fluids while continuing the insulin infusion at a reduced rate): The ketoacidosis, reflected by the persistently elevated anion gap, has not yet resolved even though glucose has fallen. Stopping the insulin infusion at this point would allow ketogenesis to continue unchecked. The correct approach is to add dextrose to the fluids once glucose approaches 200 to 250 mg/dL, which allows the insulin infusion to continue clearing the ketoacidosis without causing hypoglycemia, rather than stopping insulin or transitioning to subcutaneous dosing prematurely.
  4. The correct answer is C (Aggressive intravenous isotonic saline resuscitation before initiating an insulin infusion): This presentation, marked hyperglycemia with near-normal pH, absent ketones, and markedly elevated osmolality in an elderly type 2 diabetic with an infectious trigger, is hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state. Patients with HHS are typically more profoundly volume depleted than those with DKA, so aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation is the essential first step, with an insulin infusion added once fluids are underway. Bicarbonate is not indicated since the pH is near normal, and oral rehydration is inadequate for this degree of dehydration and altered mental status.
  5. The correct answer is B (Impaired fasting glucose, which does not yet meet diagnostic criteria for diabetes mellitus): A fasting plasma glucose between 100 and 125 mg/dL defines impaired fasting glucose, a prediabetic state, whereas diabetes requires a fasting glucose of 126 mg/dL or higher confirmed on two occasions, an A1c of 6.5 percent or higher, or other criteria described in this chapter. Her values fall short of the diagnostic threshold, so she has prediabetes rather than established diabetes, and there is no indication whatsoever for insulin therapy or concern for ketoacidosis at this stage.
  6. The correct answer is A (A glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist added to his current metformin regimen): In a patient with established atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease and inadequate control on metformin, both GLP-1 receptor agonists and SGLT2 inhibitors have demonstrated reductions in major adverse cardiovascular events independent of their glucose-lowering effect, making a GLP-1 agonist the preferred addition here. Sulfonylureas and alpha-glucosidase inhibitors lower glucose without this cardiovascular benefit, and thiazolidinediones can worsen fluid retention and are used cautiously in patients with cardiac disease.
  7. The correct answer is D (Duloxetine, an agent with demonstrated efficacy for diabetic peripheral neuropathic pain): This patient has classic diabetic peripheral neuropathy, a symmetric, distal, sensory-predominant process related to chronic hyperglycemia. First-line pharmacologic options for painful diabetic neuropathy include gabapentin, pregabalin, and duloxetine, all of which have trial evidence supporting their use. Corticosteroids and intravenous immunoglobulin are not indicated since this is not an inflammatory or autoimmune neuropathy, and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs are not effective for this type of neuropathic pain and carry renal risk in diabetic patients.
  8. The correct answer is C (Refer for a dilated eye examination now, and continue annual screening thereafter regardless of symptoms): Diabetic retinopathy can progress silently without symptoms until advanced, vision-threatening disease is present, so guidelines recommend a dilated eye examination at the time of type 2 diabetes diagnosis and annually thereafter regardless of visual symptoms or the status of other complications such as nephropathy. Waiting for symptoms or linking screening to the albumin-to-creatinine ratio would miss treatable early disease, and laboratory tests alone cannot substitute for direct examination of the retina.
  9. The correct answer is A (Anti-thyroid peroxidase antibody, consistent with Hashimoto thyroiditis as the underlying cause): This vignette describes primary hypothyroidism, the most common cause of which in an iodine-sufficient country like the United States is Hashimoto thyroiditis, an autoimmune process associated with anti-thyroid peroxidase and anti-thyroglobulin antibodies and a firm, diffusely enlarged gland. Thyroid-stimulating immunoglobulin is associated with Graves disease, which causes hyperthyroidism rather than this hypothyroid picture, and anti-GAD and anti-centromere antibodies are unrelated to thyroid disease.
  10. The correct answer is B (Stop methimazole and obtain a complete blood count promptly to assess for agranulocytosis): Methimazole carries a risk of agranulocytosis, and patients are specifically counseled to seek immediate evaluation for fever or sore throat while on this medication, since these can be the only early signs of a dangerously low neutrophil count. The correct approach is to hold the medication and urgently check a complete blood count rather than assuming a benign viral illness, adjusting the dose for symptom control, or treating empirically without first excluding this serious drug effect.
  11. The correct answer is D (Propylthiouracil, preferred specifically during the first trimester of pregnancy): Methimazole is associated with a rare but serious embryopathy when used in the first trimester, so propylthiouracil is preferred during this early period despite its own small risk of hepatotoxicity, with a switch back to methimazole often made after the first trimester. Radioactive iodine is absolutely contraindicated in pregnancy because it crosses the placenta and can ablate the fetal thyroid, and a beta blocker alone does not address the underlying hyperthyroidism.
  12. The correct answer is C (Subacute (de Quervain) thyroiditis with a transient thyrotoxic phase from release of stored hormone): A painful, tender thyroid gland following a viral illness, combined with a thyrotoxic biochemical picture but low radioactive iodine uptake, is classic for subacute (de Quervain) thyroiditis, in which inflammatory damage releases preformed hormone rather than reflecting new hormone synthesis. Graves disease and toxic multinodular goiter both show high, not low, uptake, and thyroid carcinoma does not typically present with an acutely tender gland or a thyrotoxic laboratory pattern.
  13. The correct answer is B (Clinical monitoring with periodic repeat thyroid testing rather than starting levothyroxine now): This patient has subclinical hypothyroidism, a mildly elevated TSH with a normal free T4. Treatment is generally reserved for TSH values above 10, symptomatic patients, or those who are pregnant or trying to conceive, none of which apply here, so periodic monitoring is the appropriate approach rather than starting levothyroxine now. A radioactive iodine uptake scan is used to evaluate hyperthyroidism, not hypothyroidism, and glucocorticoids have no role in this condition.
  14. The correct answer is C (Propranolol and propylthiouracil first, followed by iodine solution given at least one hour later): Thyroid storm is treated with beta blockade and an antithyroid drug such as propylthiouracil started first, followed by iodine solution given at least one hour later so the antithyroid drug can block new hormone synthesis before iodine is administered, since giving iodine first could otherwise be used as substrate for additional hormone production. Levothyroxine would worsen thyrotoxicosis, and radioactive iodine ablation is not an acute treatment and is not used in an unstable, critically ill patient.
  15. The correct answer is A (Fine needle aspiration biopsy of the nodule given its suspicious sonographic features): When TSH is normal or elevated, ultrasound features guide the next step, and this nodule shows several classic features of malignancy risk: microcalcifications, irregular margins, and an increased height-to-width ratio. Fine needle aspiration biopsy is the appropriate next step to obtain a tissue diagnosis. A radioactive iodine uptake scan is reserved for patients with a low TSH suggesting a hyperfunctioning nodule, and proceeding directly to surgery without a biopsy, or simply observing, would bypass necessary diagnostic information.
  16. The correct answer is D (Psammoma bodies, a classic finding associated with papillary thyroid carcinoma): Papillary thyroid carcinoma, the most common thyroid malignancy, is classically associated with psammoma bodies (laminated calcified structures) and characteristic nuclear features often described as 'Orphan Annie eye' nuclei, both described in this vignette. Amyloid deposits are associated with medullary thyroid carcinoma, Hurthle cell change is seen in a distinct follicular-patterned neoplasm, and colloid nodules describe benign nodular hyperplasia rather than a malignant process.
  17. The correct answer is B (A 24-hour urinary free cortisol collection or a late-night salivary cortisol level): This clinical picture of central obesity, wide striae, easy bruising, plethora, hypertension, and menstrual irregularity is classic for Cushing syndrome. Appropriate initial screening tests include a 24-hour urinary free cortisol, a late-night salivary cortisol, or an overnight low-dose dexamethasone suppression test, since cortisol has a diurnal pattern that makes a single random morning value unreliable for screening. A plasma ACTH level is used later to localize the source once excess cortisol is confirmed, and growth hormone suppression testing is used to evaluate acromegaly, not Cushing syndrome.
  18. The correct answer is A (Ectopic ACTH secretion from a small cell lung carcinoma driving adrenal cortisol overproduction): An elevated ACTH in the setting of confirmed hypercortisolism indicates ACTH-dependent disease, and in a heavy smoker with a new lung mass, hypokalemia, and markedly elevated cortisol, ectopic ACTH secretion from a tumor such as small cell lung carcinoma is the most likely mechanism, often producing more severe hypokalemia than pituitary Cushing disease. An adrenal adenoma would suppress rather than elevate ACTH, and adrenal insufficiency would present with low, not elevated, cortisol.
Case endo_case_addison

A 46-year-old woman with a history of autoimmune thyroiditis is brought to the emergency department after two days of worsening nausea, vomiting, and severe abdominal pain. Her husband reports she has felt increasingly fatigued and has lost 12 pounds over the past two months. On examination, blood pressure is 78/48 mmHg, pulse is 122/min, and she has noticeably darkened skin over her knuckles, elbows, and gum line. Laboratory studies show sodium 126 mEq/L, potassium 6.1 mEq/L, and glucose 62 mg/dL.

  1. The correct answer is C (Primary adrenal insufficiency, most likely from autoimmune adrenalitis): The combination of hypotension, hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, hypoglycemia, weight loss, and hyperpigmentation strongly suggests primary adrenal insufficiency, and in a patient with an existing autoimmune condition such as autoimmune thyroiditis, autoimmune adrenalitis (Addison disease) is the most likely cause. Secondary adrenal insufficiency would not cause hyperkalemia or significant hyperpigmentation since aldosterone is preserved and ACTH is low rather than high. Isolated SIADH does not explain the hyperkalemia, hypotension, or skin findings, and nothing suggests an infectious source.
  2. The correct answer is D (Elevated ACTH and its precursor proopiomelanocortin, which shares a melanocyte-stimulating fragment): In primary adrenal insufficiency, loss of negative feedback from cortisol leads to markedly elevated ACTH, which is cleaved from the larger precursor proopiomelanocortin that also contains a melanocyte-stimulating hormone fragment, producing the characteristic hyperpigmentation seen at pressure points, skin creases, and mucous membranes. This mechanism is specific to primary, not secondary, adrenal insufficiency, since ACTH is low rather than elevated in the secondary form.
  3. The correct answer is A (Intravenous hydrocortisone and aggressive isotonic fluid resuscitation without delay): This presentation represents an adrenal crisis, a medical emergency requiring immediate intravenous hydrocortisone and aggressive fluid resuscitation without waiting for confirmatory testing such as a cosyntropin stimulation test, since delay can be fatal. Oral fludrocortisone is not appropriate in this acute, unstable setting, and an insulin infusion is not indicated since her glucose is already low, not elevated, and insulin would worsen both the hypoglycemia and the hyperkalemia-related risk.
  4. The correct answer is C (Plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urinary fractionated metanephrines): Episodic headache, palpitations, diaphoresis, and severe paroxysmal hypertension are classic for pheochromocytoma, and the appropriate initial biochemical test is plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urinary fractionated metanephrines, both of which have high sensitivity for catecholamine-secreting tumors. Cortisol testing evaluates Cushing syndrome, growth hormone suppression testing evaluates acromegaly, and a cosyntropin stimulation test evaluates adrenal insufficiency, none of which fit this episodic hyperadrenergic presentation.
  5. The correct answer is B (A dopamine agonist such as cabergoline to lower prolactin and shrink the tumor): This patient has a prolactinoma, the most common functioning pituitary tumor, presenting with galactorrhea and hypogonadism from prolactin-mediated suppression of gonadotropin-releasing hormone. Her normal TSH argues against hypothyroidism as the cause of elevated prolactin. First-line treatment for a prolactinoma is a dopamine agonist such as cabergoline or bromocriptine, which shrinks the tumor and normalizes prolactin in most patients, reserving surgery for those who fail or cannot tolerate medical therapy, and radiation is not a first-line option.
  6. The correct answer is D (An insulin-like growth factor 1 (IGF-1) level drawn at any time): This presentation of acral enlargement, coarsened facial features, and joint pain over years suggests acromegaly from excess growth hormone, almost always due to a pituitary somatotroph adenoma. Because growth hormone is secreted in pulses, a single random level is unreliable, so an IGF-1 level, which reflects integrated growth hormone activity over time, is the preferred initial screening test. Cortisol and metanephrine testing evaluate unrelated conditions that do not match this presentation.
  7. The correct answer is C (An oral glucose tolerance test with measurement of growth hormone suppression): Once an elevated IGF-1 raises suspicion for acromegaly, the confirmatory test is an oral glucose tolerance test with growth hormone measurement, since growth hormone should be suppressed after a glucose load in a normal individual but fails to suppress in acromegaly. A cosyntropin stimulation test evaluates adrenal insufficiency, a water deprivation test evaluates diabetes insipidus, and urinary free cortisol evaluates Cushing syndrome, none of which confirm acromegaly.
  8. The correct answer is D (Sheehan syndrome, causing postpartum pituitary necrosis from severe obstetric hemorrhage): Failure to lactate, amenorrhea, and fatigue following severe postpartum hemorrhage are classic for Sheehan syndrome, in which the pituitary gland, physiologically enlarged during pregnancy, becomes particularly vulnerable to ischemic necrosis during an episode of severe hemorrhage and hypotension. Primary ovarian insufficiency and isolated postpartum thyroiditis would not explain the failure to lactate, and lymphocytic hypophysitis is a distinct autoimmune process rather than one directly tied to hemorrhagic shock.
  9. The correct answer is A (Glucocorticoid replacement first, followed by levothyroxine once cortisol replacement is established): In panhypopituitarism, glucocorticoid replacement must always be started before levothyroxine, because thyroid hormone accelerates cortisol metabolism and can precipitate an adrenal crisis if an underlying, unrecognized cortisol deficiency is not already addressed. Starting levothyroxine first, or starting both simultaneously, risks this dangerous sequence, and sex hormone replacement is not the priority when cortisol deficiency has not yet been corrected.
  10. The correct answer is B (The optic chiasm, where fibers from the nasal retina of each eye cross): Bitemporal hemianopsia, loss of the outer visual field in both eyes with preserved central vision, is the classic finding when a pituitary mass compresses the optic chiasm from below, affecting the crossing nasal retinal fibers that carry temporal field information. A unilateral optic tract lesion would produce a homonymous, not bitemporal, field defect, oculomotor nerve compression affects eye movement rather than visual fields, and bilateral occipital cortex disease is not explained by a single midline pituitary mass.
  11.
