The correct answer is D (Primary hyperparathyroidism, most commonly due to a single parathyroid adenoma): An elevated PTH in the setting of confirmed hypercalcemia defines primary hyperparathyroidism, most commonly caused by a single parathyroid adenoma, and the low-normal phosphate fits this pattern since PTH increases renal phosphate excretion. Malignancy-associated hypercalcemia from PTH-related peptide would show an appropriately suppressed, not elevated, standard PTH level, and secondary hyperparathyroidism and vitamin D toxicity typically present with low or normal, rather than elevated, calcium at the time PTH rises.
  12. The correct answer is B (A calcium level more than 1 mg/dL above the upper limit of normal): Surgical criteria for asymptomatic primary hyperparathyroidism include a calcium level more than 1 mg/dL above the upper limit of normal, significantly reduced bone mineral density, reduced renal function, or age under 50, and this patient's calcium of 12.1 mg/dL meets that threshold. Undetectably low urine calcium would instead suggest familial hypocalciuric hypercalcemia rather than typical primary hyperparathyroidism, and normal bone density or preserved renal function would not themselves be indications for surgery.
  13. The correct answer is C (Secondary hyperparathyroidism driven by chronic kidney disease impairing phosphate excretion and vitamin D activation): Chronic kidney disease impairs both phosphate excretion and renal activation of vitamin D, producing hyperphosphatemia and low calcium that together drive a sustained, appropriate rise in PTH, known as secondary hyperparathyroidism, which fits this patient's low calcium, high phosphate, and markedly elevated PTH. Primary hyperparathyroidism and malignancy-associated hypercalcemia would both produce elevated, not low, calcium, and isolated vitamin D deficiency alone would not produce phosphate retention of this degree in the absence of renal disease.
  14. The correct answer is A (Low calcium, low parathyroid hormone, and high phosphate): This patient has postsurgical hypoparathyroidism from inadvertent removal or devascularization of the parathyroid glands during thyroidectomy, demonstrating positive Chvostek and Trousseau signs from hypocalcemia. The expected laboratory pattern is low calcium, low PTH (since the source of PTH has been damaged), and high phosphate, since PTH normally increases renal phosphate excretion and this effect is lost. The other option patterns describe primary hyperparathyroidism or unrelated combinations that do not fit postsurgical hypoparathyroidism.
  15. The correct answer is C (Secretion of PTH-related peptide by the tumor, mimicking PTH action but undetected by a standard assay): Squamous cell lung cancer classically causes hypercalcemia of malignancy through secretion of PTH-related peptide, which activates the same receptor as PTH on bone and kidney but is not measured by a standard intact PTH assay, explaining the appropriately suppressed PTH level seen here. A coexisting parathyroid adenoma would raise, not suppress, PTH, osteolytic metastases alone are more characteristic of breast cancer or myeloma than squamous cell lung cancer, and secondary hyperparathyroidism from kidney disease would lower, not raise, calcium.
  16. The correct answer is A (Aggressive intravenous isotonic saline hydration as the first step in management): Severe symptomatic hypercalcemia is a medical emergency, and the first step in management is aggressive intravenous isotonic saline hydration, both to correct the dehydration typically present and to promote renal calcium excretion, often followed by additional therapies such as calcitonin or bisphosphonates. Calcitriol would worsen hypercalcemia by increasing intestinal calcium absorption, immediate surgery is not appropriate before the acute crisis is stabilized, and withholding fluids would delay essential emergency treatment.
  17. The correct answer is D (Initiate a moderate-intensity statin given his age, diabetes, and calculated cardiovascular risk): Patients with diabetes between 40 and 75 years old are considered clear candidates for statin therapy regardless of a specific LDL threshold, and moderate-intensity statin therapy is the appropriate starting point for primary prevention in this group, with high-intensity statins generally reserved for those with established cardiovascular disease or very high LDL. Deferring therapy because LDL is below 190 ignores the diabetes-based indication, and fibrates are not first-line therapy for cardiovascular risk reduction in this context.
  18. The correct answer is B (Familial hypercholesterolemia, an autosomal dominant disorder of LDL receptor function): Achilles tendon xanthomas, corneal arcus in a young patient, markedly elevated LDL cholesterol, and a strong family history of premature myocardial infarction together point to familial hypercholesterolemia, an autosomal dominant disorder caused by defects in the LDL receptor pathway that presents with these features from a young age. Metabolic syndrome, hypothyroidism, and nephrotic syndrome can each raise lipid levels but do not typically produce tendon xanthomas or this degree of LDL elevation at such a young age with this family history.
  19. The correct answer is A (A fibrate or other triglyceride-lowering therapy, since statins alone have limited effect at this level): Severe hypertriglyceridemia, generally above 500 to 1,000 mg/dL, carries a real risk of acute pancreatitis, which this patient appears to have developed. Fibrates and other triglyceride-lowering agents are used to treat severe hypertriglyceridemia independent of the LDL-focused statin decision, since statins have only a modest effect on triglycerides at this magnitude. Bile acid sequestrants can actually worsen hypertriglyceridemia and are not appropriate here, and this degree of hypertriglyceridemia is a well-recognized cause of acute pancreatitis rather than an unrelated finding.
  20. The correct answer is D (Metabolic syndrome, since she meets at least three of the five diagnostic criteria): Metabolic syndrome is diagnosed when at least three of five criteria are met: abdominal obesity, triglycerides of 150 mg/dL or higher, HDL below 40 in men or below 50 in women, blood pressure of 130/85 mmHg or higher, and fasting glucose of 100 mg/dL or higher. This patient meets abdominal obesity, elevated blood pressure, elevated triglycerides, and elevated fasting glucose, and her HDL of 42 mg/dL is below the 50 mg/dL threshold used for women, so she meets multiple criteria and has metabolic syndrome, which does not require an established diagnosis of diabetes.
  21. The correct answer is B (Continue the statin at the same or a reduced dose while monitoring symptoms and creatine kinase): Mild statin-associated myalgia with only modestly elevated creatine kinase, in the absence of significant weakness or a markedly elevated creatine kinase suggesting rhabdomyolysis, is generally managed by continuing the statin at the same or a reduced dose, or trying an alternative statin, given the substantial secondary prevention benefit in a patient with recent myocardial infarction. Permanently stopping all statin therapy would remove a critical secondary prevention medication, a fibrate alone is not an adequate substitute for LDL-lowering secondary prevention, and an electromyogram is not the appropriate first step for this common, generally benign presentation.
  22. The correct answer is C (Structured weight loss through dietary change and increased physical activity): Metabolic syndrome reflects an underlying state of insulin resistance, and weight loss through dietary modification and increased physical activity favorably affects blood pressure, glucose, triglycerides, and HDL cholesterol simultaneously, making it the single most effective intervention for the overall syndrome. A thiazide diuretic, a fibrate, or metformin each targets only one component in isolation and does not address the shared underlying mechanism the way lifestyle intervention does.
  23. The correct answer is B (Redate the pregnancy to the ultrasound estimate since it is more accurate than uncertain menstrual dates): a first-trimester crown-rump length measurement is the single most accurate method available for dating a pregnancy, more accurate than even a certain last menstrual period, and is especially preferred when cycles are irregular and the exact date of conception is unclear. When the ultrasound estimate differs from menstrual dating by more than about one week in the first trimester, the pregnancy should be redated to the ultrasound estimate. Option A is wrong because irregular cycles and uncertain timing of conception make menstrual dating unreliable here. Option C describes an arbitrary averaging method with no basis in obstetric dating standards. Option D unnecessarily delays establishing a due date that guides all subsequent screening and management timelines.
  24. The correct answer is A (Cell-free fetal DNA testing from a maternal blood sample drawn as early as 9 to 10 weeks): cell-free fetal DNA testing is the most sensitive noninvasive screening method currently available for trisomy 21, 18, and 13, and can be drawn as early as 9 to 10 weeks gestation, matching both her stated priority for sensitivity and her current gestational age. It remains a screening test, so any positive result still requires diagnostic confirmation with chorionic villus sampling or amniocentesis, consistent with her stated willingness to accept that step. Option B describes a second-trimester test that is less sensitive and typically used when a patient presents too late for earlier screening. Option C is an invasive diagnostic procedure carrying a small procedural miscarriage risk and is not first-line before noninvasive screening. Option D alone, without serum analytes, is a less sensitive combined first-trimester approach than the test she has requested.
  25. The correct answer is C (Begin intravenous penicillin now and continue dosing at intervals throughout active labor): a positive Group B Streptococcus culture mandates intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis once labor begins, and intravenous penicillin is the preferred first-line agent given her documented lack of allergy, continued at intervals through active labor to reduce vertical transmission and early-onset neonatal sepsis. Option A is wrong because oral dosing does not achieve adequate intrapartum drug levels for this indication. Option B is wrong because prophylaxis exists specifically to prevent neonatal sepsis before it can occur, not to treat it reactively afterward. Option D defeats the purpose of prophylaxis entirely, since protective drug levels must already be present in maternal and fetal circulation before delivery occurs.
  26. The correct answer is D (Obtain a detailed anatomy ultrasound to evaluate for a neural tube or abdominal wall defect): an isolated elevated maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein, once dating is confirmed accurate as it is here, should prompt a detailed anatomy ultrasound to look specifically for a neural tube defect or an abdominal wall defect such as gastroschisis or omphalocele before any invasive testing is considered. Option A is wrong because an elevated result of this magnitude carries real structural risk that ultrasound can often clarify directly and noninvasively. Option B skips the noninvasive imaging step that frequently explains the finding without any procedural risk to the pregnancy. Option C simply delays a structural evaluation that should occur promptly given the abnormal result already obtained.
  27. The correct answer is A (Start low-dose aspirin now, since it was not yet started and she has one high-risk factor): a prior history of preeclampsia is a single high-risk factor that alone justifies starting low-dose aspirin between 12 and 28 weeks, ideally before 16 weeks, to reduce the risk of recurrent preeclampsia, and at 14 weeks she still falls within that recommended window. Option B is wrong because prior preeclampsia, particularly severe disease requiring early delivery, meaningfully raises recurrence risk in a subsequent pregnancy. Option C is inappropriate since scheduled antihypertensive therapy is not indicated for a patient with entirely normal blood pressure today. Option D misses the preventive window entirely, since aspirin's benefit depends on starting before disease onset rather than after warning signs appear.
  28. The correct answer is B (Gestational hypertension, since it is new-onset after 20 weeks without proteinuria or severity features): gestational hypertension is defined as new-onset blood pressure of 140/90 mm Hg or higher after 20 weeks gestation, in a patient without prior hypertension documented earlier in pregnancy, and without proteinuria or other features of preeclampsia, which matches this presentation exactly given her normal early blood pressure and normal current labs. Option A is wrong because chronic hypertension requires elevated pressure documented before 20 weeks, which was clearly not the case at her 9-week visit. Option C requires either significant proteinuria or a defined severe feature, neither of which is present in her current evaluation. Option D is a seizure disorder superimposed on preeclampsia and does not apply here at all.
  29. The correct answer is D (Severe-range blood pressure of 168/112 mm Hg together with a platelet count below 100,000): severe features of preeclampsia include severe-range blood pressure, systolic 160 or higher or diastolic 110 or higher on two occasions at least four hours apart, and thrombocytopenia with a platelet count below 100,000 per microliter, both of which are clearly documented here on two separate readings and independently qualify as severe features. Option A describes the baseline definition of preeclampsia itself rather than a marker of severity. Option B is incorrect because gestational age alone, independent of blood pressure or laboratory findings, does not define disease severity. Option C is a distractor, since the absence of headache does not rule out severe disease once other severe criteria have already been met on laboratory testing.
  30. The correct answer is C (Stop the magnesium infusion and prepare to administer calcium gluconate if toxicity progresses): magnesium toxicity progresses in a predictable, ascending fashion, with loss of deep tendon reflexes occurring before respiratory depression and cardiac arrest at progressively higher serum magnesium levels, so the absent reflexes found on this examination are an early warning sign that should prompt stopping the infusion and preparing calcium gluconate, the antidote, even though her respiratory status and mental status remain normal for now. Option A dangerously continues an infusion despite a clear early sign of toxicity already documented. Option B would further worsen toxicity rather than improve seizure prophylaxis. Option D is unnecessary, since magnesium sulfate remains first-line therapy and simply needs to be held and reassessed rather than replaced with a different agent.
  31. The correct answer is B (Intravenous labetalol given as a first-line agent for acute severe hypertension in pregnancy): intravenous labetalol is one of the first-line agents, along with intravenous hydralazine and oral immediate-release nifedipine, for acute treatment of severe-range hypertension in pregnancy, and she has an intravenous line already available for its administration. Option A and option C are both incorrect because ACE inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers are contraindicated throughout pregnancy due to fetal renal toxicity, regardless of the intended route or titration schedule described. Option D is a diuretic rather than an antihypertensive and is not an appropriate first-line agent for acute severe hypertension in this clinical setting.
Case PREG-C1

A 27-year-old primigravida at 35 weeks gestation is admitted to the labor and delivery unit with a blood pressure of 166/108 mm Hg confirmed on two readings four hours apart, a new-onset headache that has not responded to two doses of acetaminophen, and 2+ proteinuria on urine dipstick. Laboratory studies drawn on admission reveal a platelet count of 95,000 per microliter, aspartate aminotransferase 210 U/L, alanine aminotransferase 195 U/L, and lactate dehydrogenase elevated above the reference range with schistocytes and evidence of hemolysis noted on peripheral blood smear. Serum creatinine is within normal limits. Fetal heart rate tracing is category I throughout admission, and fetal growth by ultrasound is appropriate for gestational age. She is started on a continuous magnesium sulfate infusion for seizure prophylaxis and an antihypertensive agent for her severe-range blood pressure.

  1. The correct answer is A (Proceed with delivery now, since severe features are present regardless of gestational age): preeclampsia with severe features, especially with laboratory evidence of hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets consistent with HELLP syndrome, is generally an indication for prompt delivery regardless of gestational age once maternal stabilization with magnesium sulfate and antihypertensive therapy is already underway, as it is here. Option B is inappropriate and unsafe given the severity of her laboratory findings and symptoms. Option C incorrectly treats severe features as though they do not change the recommended timing of delivery. Option D would apply to stable severe features earlier in gestation without hemolytic laboratory findings, which does not accurately describe this patient's presentation.
  2. The correct answer is D (Loss of the patellar deep tendon reflex on serial neurologic examination): magnesium toxicity develops in a predictable, ascending sequence, and loss of deep tendon reflexes is the earliest expected clinical sign, occurring before respiratory depression and well before cardiac arrest at progressively higher serum magnesium levels, which is exactly why serial reflex checks are performed during the infusion. Option A describes the very condition magnesium sulfate is meant to prevent, not a sign of its own toxicity. Option B is a late, severe toxicity finding that should already be preceded by detectable earlier examination findings. Option C is incorrect because magnesium sulfate is not an antihypertensive agent and does not predictably raise or lower blood pressure at all.
  3. The correct answer is C (HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of preeclampsia with hemolysis and hepatic and platelet involvement): the combination of Hemolysis, Elevated Liver enzymes, and Low Platelets defines HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of preeclampsia that can occur with or without frank hypertension and carries additional risks including hepatic rupture and disseminated intravascular coagulation, and her full laboratory pattern fits this diagnosis precisely. Option A describes a distinct condition with a different laboratory and clinical profile, typically without this specific hemolytic smear pattern. Option B would not explain either the hemolysis or the markedly elevated liver enzymes documented here. Option D is a separate microangiopathic process and is not the best fit given her clear preeclampsia features and the timing at 35 weeks gestation.
  4. The correct answer is A (Ensure maternal airway and safety first, then start intravenous magnesium sulfate promptly): eclampsia is managed first by protecting the maternal airway and preventing further injury during and immediately after the seizure, followed promptly by intravenous magnesium sulfate, which is the treatment of choice both to prevent recurrent seizures and to stabilize the patient before further steps are taken. Option B is incorrect because delivery, while often ultimately necessary, is not the very first priority before maternal stabilization and seizure prophylaxis have been addressed. Option C treats blood pressure but does not address the seizure disorder itself, which magnesium sulfate specifically targets. Option D would delay life-saving treatment and is not routinely required emergently in a classic eclamptic seizure without focal neurologic findings.
  5. The correct answer is C (Proceed to a 100 gram three-hour oral glucose tolerance test to confirm the diagnosis): in the two-step approach to gestational diabetes screening, an abnormal 50 gram one-hour glucose challenge test is a screening result only and must be confirmed with a 100 gram three-hour oral glucose tolerance test, with two or more abnormal values on that confirmatory test needed to establish the diagnosis. Option A skips the required confirmatory testing step that this two-step protocol depends on. Option B unnecessarily repeats a screening test rather than proceeding directly to the appropriate confirmatory diagnostic test. Option D is not the preferred diagnostic method for gestational diabetes, since hemoglobin A1c is less reliable during the normal physiologic changes of pregnancy.
  6. The correct answer is B (Add pharmacologic therapy, with insulin generally preferred as the first-line agent): when glycemic targets, generally a fasting glucose under 95 mg/dL, are not met despite adequate, well-documented lifestyle measures as in this case, pharmacologic therapy should be added, and insulin is generally preferred as first-line treatment because it does not cross the placenta in clinically significant amounts. Option A delays needed treatment when her fasting targets are clearly and consistently not being met over a full two-week trial. Option C is not an appropriate dietary strategy for improving glycemic control in gestational diabetes. Option D is incorrect since her fasting values consistently exceed the accepted target threshold for adequate control.
  7. The correct answer is D (No, since gestational diabetes develops after the period of organogenesis is already complete): gestational diabetes typically develops or is first recognized in the late second trimester, well after the period of organogenesis is complete, so unlike pregestational diabetes with poor periconceptional control, it does not raise the risk of congenital anomalies such as cardiac defects or neural tube defects, and her documented normal earlier screening supports this timeline. Option A incorrectly equates gestational diabetes risk with the distinct risk profile of pregestational diabetes. Option B incorrectly ties anomaly risk to third-trimester control rather than to the timing of disease onset relative to organogenesis. Option C incorrectly attributes an anomaly risk that is specific to poorly controlled pregestational diabetes around conception.
  8. The correct answer is C (A 75 gram two-hour oral glucose tolerance test to confirm resolution of glucose intolerance): all patients with gestational diabetes should undergo a 75 gram two-hour oral glucose tolerance test at 6 to 12 weeks postpartum to confirm resolution of glucose intolerance, since gestational diabetes substantially raises lifetime risk of developing type 2 diabetes and ongoing surveillance is warranted regardless of how well controlled the pregnancy itself was. Option A uses the wrong test entirely, since that screening protocol is designed for use during pregnancy rather than for postpartum evaluation. Option B incorrectly assumes permanent resolution without any confirmatory testing being performed. Option D is not sufficiently standardized or sensitive for this specific postpartum purpose compared with the recommended oral glucose tolerance test.
  9. The correct answer is D (Congenital cardiac and neural tube defects related to hyperglycemia during organogenesis): poor glycemic control around the time of conception and during early organogenesis, exactly as described in this patient with an elevated hemoglobin A1c at 6 weeks, raises the risk of congenital anomalies including cardiac defects and neural tube defects, a risk that gestational diabetes developing later in pregnancy does not share since organogenesis is already complete by the time it typically appears. Options A, B, and C describe complications that can occur with either pregestational or gestational diabetes when glycemic control is poor later in pregnancy, and none of them distinguish the two conditions the way early anomaly risk clearly does in this case.
  10. The correct answer is A (Her prior history of tubal surgery and pelvic inflammatory disease affecting the fallopian tubes): prior tubal surgery and prior pelvic inflammatory disease are well-established risk factors for ectopic pregnancy because both processes can distort or scar the fallopian tube, impairing normal transport of the fertilized egg into the uterine cavity, and this patient has both risk factors clearly documented in her history. Option B is incorrect because age 30 alone is not considered a major independent risk factor for ectopic pregnancy in the way tubal disease is. Option C describes a presenting symptom rather than an underlying risk factor for developing the condition itself. Option D is a comparatively weak and nonspecific historical detail when set against her own well-documented tubal disease and prior ectopic pregnancy.
  11. The correct answer is B (Because her hCG is above the classic discriminatory zone without a visible sac, ectopic pregnancy cannot yet be excluded): the discriminatory zone is historically cited as 1,500 to 2,000 mIU/mL, though current guidance favors a higher, more conservative threshold around 3,500 mIU/mL to avoid mistaking a normal but slightly early intrauterine pregnancy for an abnormal one. Because a gestational sac is not seen here, ectopic pregnancy cannot be excluded and must remain on the differential, warranting serial hCG measurements and repeat ultrasound rather than a single decisive test. Option A incorrectly assumes the absence of a visible intrauterine sac rules out ectopic pregnancy entirely. Option C is incorrect because a normal intrauterine pregnancy at a substantially higher hCG level should already demonstrate a visible gestational sac on transvaginal imaging. Option D unnecessarily delays evaluation of a finding that already warrants close follow-up.
  12. The correct answer is D (Methotrexate therapy, since she meets standard criteria for medical management of ectopic pregnancy): methotrexate is appropriate for a hemodynamically stable patient with a confirmed, unruptured ectopic pregnancy who meets criteria including no fetal cardiac activity, hCG generally below about 5,000 mIU/mL, mass size under about 3.5 to 4 cm, and no contraindication such as hepatic or renal disease, breastfeeding, or peptic ulcer disease, all of which this patient clearly satisfies. Option A is unnecessarily invasive when medical management is appropriate, available, and preferred by the patient herself. Option B is inadequate, since an ectopic pregnancy still requires active treatment even when the patient is currently hemodynamically stable. Option C is more invasive than necessary given that she fully qualifies for medical therapy instead.
  13. The correct answer is B (Proceed immediately to emergency surgical management regardless of her exact hCG level): a ruptured ectopic pregnancy with hemodynamic instability and free fluid consistent with hemoperitoneum, exactly as seen in this patient, is a surgical emergency requiring immediate operative intervention regardless of the specific hCG level, since ongoing intra-abdominal hemorrhage must be controlled promptly to prevent further deterioration. Option A is contraindicated, since methotrexate is reserved for stable, unruptured ectopic pregnancies and would not address her active hemorrhage. Option C dangerously delays lifesaving treatment in a patient who is already unstable. Option D is unsafe given her clear hemodynamic instability, falling hemoglobin, and ultrasound evidence of intra-abdominal bleeding already present.
  14. The correct answer is C (Missed abortion, since there is fetal demise without expulsion of tissue or cervical dilation): a missed abortion describes fetal demise without expulsion of tissue and without cervical dilation, often discovered incidentally exactly as in this asymptomatic patient with no cardiac activity detected and a closed, firm cervix on examination. Option A requires ongoing bleeding in the setting of a viable pregnancy, which does not match a demised fetus with no cardiac activity. Option B requires an open cervical os with some tissue already expelled, neither of which is described in this case. Option D requires an open cervical os as well, whereas this patient's cervix remains closed throughout her examination.
  15. The correct answer is A (Testing for antiphospholipid syndrome along with evaluation of uterine anatomy and parental karyotype): recurrent pregnancy loss, generally defined as two or more consecutive clinical losses, prompts evaluation for antiphospholipid syndrome, uterine anatomic abnormalities, parental chromosomal translocations, and uncontrolled thyroid or diabetic disease, all of which are reasonable and standard components of a thorough workup for this patient given her history. Option B understates the clinical significance of three consecutive losses, a pattern that clearly warrants formal evaluation rather than reassurance alone. Option C is a premature recommendation made without first completing a standard diagnostic evaluation that might identify a treatable cause. Option D skips an evaluation that could identify a modifiable cause before she and her partner attempt another pregnancy.
Case PREG-C2

A 29-year-old woman, gravida 1 para 1, undergoes an uncomplicated vaginal delivery of a 4,100 gram infant after a prolonged second stage of labor that was augmented with oxytocin for several hours. Fifteen minutes after delivery of the placenta, which appeared grossly intact on inspection, the bedside nurse notes continued heavy vaginal bleeding, now estimated at 900 mL total and still actively increasing. On bimanual examination the uterine fundus is soft, enlarged well above the umbilicus, and poorly contracted despite fundal massage already attempted by the nurse. Careful visual inspection of the cervix and vaginal walls under adequate lighting reveals no lacerations. Her blood pressure has drifted down slightly to 102/64 mm Hg with a heart rate of 108 beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is B (Uterine atony, given the soft, poorly contracted uterus found on bimanual examination): a soft, enlarged, poorly contracted, boggy uterus on bimanual examination is the classic finding of uterine atony, which accounts for the large majority of postpartum hemorrhage cases and is more likely here given the clear risk factors present, including fetal macrosomia, a prolonged second stage, and oxytocin augmentation. Option A would typically show retained tissue on ultrasound or a grossly incomplete placenta rather than a diffusely boggy uterus with an intact-appearing placenta. Option C is less likely given a careful, well-lit inspection of the cervix and vagina that found no laceration. Option D is possible in theory but far less likely than atony as the initial explanation without any supporting bleeding history before this delivery.
  2. The correct answer is D (Methylergonovine or carboprost, escalating since she has no hypertension or asthma): when oxytocin alone fails to control uterine atony, escalation proceeds to additional uterotonic agents such as methylergonovine, which is avoided in hypertension, or carboprost, a prostaglandin analog avoided in asthma, and since this patient has neither contraindication, either agent is an appropriate next step. Option A is not a uterotonic medication at all and has no established role in treating uterine atony. Option B unnecessarily withholds an appropriate injectable escalation option when one is clearly indicated and available given her ongoing hemorrhage. Option C is incorrect because magnesium sulfate actually relaxes rather than contracts the uterus and would worsen her atony further.
  3. The correct answer is A (Intrauterine balloon tamponade to apply direct pressure while further options are arranged): when pharmacologic measures for uterine atony fail to control ongoing hemorrhage, as they have here, escalation proceeds through mechanical interventions such as intrauterine balloon tamponade, followed by uterine artery embolization if available and feasible, and ultimately surgical management if bleeding still continues, making balloon tamponade the appropriate next step at this point. Option B is clearly unsafe given her ongoing significant blood loss and now-declining blood pressure. Option C inappropriately delays needed intervention in a patient who is actively and progressively hemorrhaging. Option D addresses future anemia only and does nothing to treat her active, ongoing postpartum hemorrhage right now.
  4. The correct answer is C (Proceed to expedited delivery given a category III tracing that has not improved): a category III tracing, defined by absent variability with recurrent late decelerations, recurrent variable decelerations, bradycardia, or a sinusoidal pattern, requires immediate intervention, and when intrauterine resuscitative measures have already been attempted and fail to improve the tracing, as they clearly have here, expedited delivery is the appropriate next step. Option A inappropriately delays action on a tracing that already meets criteria for immediate intervention after conservative measures have failed. Option B would likely worsen fetal status further by increasing uterine contraction frequency. Option D delays necessary action and is not the priority once conservative measures have already clearly failed to resolve the tracing.
  5. The correct answer is D (Umbilical cord compression producing an abrupt, variably timed deceleration pattern): variable decelerations have an abrupt onset and abrupt offset that are unrelated in timing to the uterine contraction, exactly as described here, and they reflect umbilical cord compression, making them the most common deceleration pattern seen during labor. Option A describes the mechanism of early decelerations, which mirror the contraction in a gradual, uniform pattern rather than the abrupt, variably timed pattern described. Option B describes the mechanism of late decelerations, which begin consistently after the contraction peaks rather than varying independently in timing as this pattern does. Option C could theoretically contribute to late decelerations but does not explain this specific abrupt, variably timed pattern seen on the tracing.
  6. The correct answer is C (Continue labor and reassess, since adequate contractions with no progress for four hours defines arrest): an arrest disorder in the active phase is generally defined as no cervical change for four hours with adequate contractions already confirmed by intrauterine pressure catheter, which this patient clearly meets, and the most common underlying cause is still addressed through continued observation and reassessment as management proceeds toward considering cesarean delivery only if arrest truly persists further, since cephalopelvic disproportion remains a diagnosis of exclusion. Option A moves to a diagnosis of exclusion prematurely without allowing further reassessment of this labor pattern. Option B dismisses a labor pattern that already meets formal criteria for arrest given the documented findings. Option D incorrectly assumes her contractions are inadequate when they have already been confirmed as adequate by pressure catheter.
  7. The correct answer is B (Tocolysis to delay delivery 48 hours along with antenatal corticosteroids and magnesium sulfate): preterm labor with confirmed cervical change, exactly as documented in this patient over the observation period, is managed with tocolysis to delay delivery for 48 hours, allowing time for antenatal corticosteroids to promote fetal lung maturity and, since she is under 32 weeks gestation, magnesium sulfate for fetal neuroprotection as well. Option A is incorrect because tocolysis and corticosteroid administration are specifically intended to safely delay delivery when appropriate and no contraindication is present. Option C fails to offer proven interventions shown to improve neonatal outcomes at this early gestational age. Option D omits both tocolysis and corticosteroids, both of which are indicated here in the absence of infection or another contraindication.
  8. The correct answer is A (Expectant inpatient management with antibiotics and corticosteroids given the gestational age): preterm premature rupture of membranes at 30 weeks without signs of infection, labor, or nonreassuring fetal status, exactly as described in this reassuring presentation, is generally managed expectantly in an inpatient setting with antibiotics, typically covering both a macrolide such as azithromycin and ampicillin, and corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity, balancing the risks of prematurity against ascending infection. Option B is not appropriate at this gestational age without an indication such as infection, labor, or nonreassuring fetal status developing. Option C is unsafe, since this diagnosis specifically requires inpatient monitoring rather than outpatient discharge given the risk of infection and cord complications. Option D omits antibiotics, an important standard component of management shown to reduce infectious morbidity in this setting.
  9. The correct answer is C (The McRoberts maneuver, hyperflexing the maternal hips, combined with suprapubic pressure): the McRoberts maneuver, which hyperflexes the maternal hips sharply against the abdomen, combined with suprapubic pressure applied by an assistant, is the appropriate first-line approach to shoulder dystocia, and additional maneuvers such as delivery of the posterior arm are reserved for cases where this initial approach fails. Option A is specifically discouraged in this scenario, since fundal pressure actually worsens impaction of the shoulder behind the maternal symphysis. Option B is not feasible once the fetal head has already delivered, as described here. Option D risks excessive traction injury to the fetal brachial plexus and is not an appropriate management step for this obstetric emergency.
  10. The correct answer is B (Administer an appropriate antibiotic regimen for prophylaxis, since GBS status still requires treatment): intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis remains indicated for a documented positive Group B Streptococcus culture regardless of current membrane status, and a reported low-risk penicillin allergy history such as hers, without features of anaphylaxis, does not eliminate the need for prophylaxis, since an appropriate alternative or modified regimen can be selected based on allergy severity. Option A incorrectly assumes that any reported penicillin allergy history precludes all antibiotic prophylaxis regardless of its severity. Option C incorrectly ties the timing of prophylaxis to membrane rupture rather than to the onset of active labor itself, which has already begun. Option D incorrectly reserves treatment for a reactive indication rather than the established preventive indication already present.
  11. The correct answer is D (Intravenous clindamycin plus gentamicin continued until she is afebrile for 24 to 48 hours): postpartum endometritis, presenting with fever, marked uterine tenderness, and foul-smelling lochia, and more common after cesarean than vaginal delivery exactly as in this patient, is treated with broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics, classically clindamycin plus gentamicin, continued until she has been afebrile for 24 to 48 hours. Option A uses an inadequate oral outpatient regimen for a clinical presentation that warrants inpatient intravenous therapy instead. An LR- this far below 0.1 produces a large, often conclusive decrease in the probability of disease when the test is negative. The correct answer is D (A case-control study, because it efficiently studies a rare disease by starting with...): the investigators begin with the outcome (cancer diagnosis versus no diagnosis) and look backward for prior exposures, the defining structure of a case-control study, which is especially efficient for rare diseases like this one since it does not require following a large cohort forward for years to accumulate enough cases. Option A is incorrect because a prospective cohort study would follow exposed and unexposed children forward in time, which this design does not do, and would be extremely inefficient for a disease this rare.   34. The correct answer is A (Intention-to-treat analysis, because it preserves the benefit of randomization by...): analyzing patients according to their original randomized assignment, even if they did not complete or adhere to that assignment, is the definition of intention-to-treat analysis. Its main advantage is that it preserves the balance of known and unknown confounders created by randomization and better reflects real-world effectiveness, since nonadherence happens in practice too. Option B describes the opposite approach, per-protocol analysis, which only counts patients who actually completed the assigned treatment and can overestimate efficacy. Option C describes a different type of analysis entirely, comparing predefined patient subgroups, which is not what is described here. Option D describes a technique used to test the robustness of conclusions to alternative assumptions, not the scenario described here.
  35. The correct answer is C (Berkson bias, a form of selection bias from using hospitalized patients as controls,...): using hospitalized controls introduces Berkson bias, since patients admitted to a hospital, by definition, tend to have more comorbid conditions and different exposure patterns than the general population, which can create or distort an apparent association between two conditions that are both associated with hospitalization itself. Option A describes a different bias entirely, involving differential memory of past exposures, which is not the issue described here. Option B applies to screening and early detection, not to this hospital-based selection problem. Option D describes confounding related to why a treatment was prescribed, which does not match this scenario about control selection.
  36. The correct answer is B (Recall bias, since mothers of an affected child are more likely to have searched their...): this is the classic setup for recall bias, a problem specific to retrospective studies like this one, in which the group with the outcome of interest (an affected child) has been strongly motivated to search their memory for possible causes, leading to more complete or more prominent reporting of past exposures compared with controls, independent of any true difference in what actually happened during pregnancy. Option A describes a bias in how subjects entered the study, not in how they reported information after enrollment.   37. The correct answer is A (Cigarette smoking is a confounder, since people who smoke are more likely to carry a...): a confounder is a third variable that is independently associated with both the exposure being studied (lighter carrying) and the outcome (lung cancer), without being a step on the causal pathway between them. Smoking fits this definition perfectly here, since smokers both carry lighters more often and independently have a much higher risk of lung cancer, creating a spurious association between lighter carrying and cancer once smoking is not accounted for. Option B misapplies effect modification, which describes a real difference in the strength of an association across strata, not a spurious association created by a third factor. Option C proposes an implausible causal direction with no supporting mechanism. Option D misapplies a bias specific to screening and early detection, which does not fit this scenario.
  38. The correct answer is D (Age is an effect modifier here, and the drug's effect should be reported separately...): when the true magnitude of an association or treatment effect genuinely differs across levels of a third variable, and that difference persists after adjustment for confounding, the third variable is an effect modifier rather than a confounder. The correct response to true effect modification is to report the effect separately within each stratum (here, by age group), since averaging it into one overall number would obscure a real, clinically important difference. Option A incorrectly treats a real biological difference in effect as if it were a nuisance variable to be adjusted away, which is only appropriate for confounding, not effect modification. Option B describes an unrelated bias that does not explain a differential treatment effect observed within a randomized trial.   40. The correct answer is C (Length-time bias, because screening preferentially identifies slower-growing, less...): length-time bias occurs precisely as described, when a screening program is more likely to catch indolent, slow-growing disease that remains detectable for a long window, while aggressive, fast-growing disease is more likely to become symptomatic and get diagnosed clinically between screening intervals, skewing the screen-detected group toward a biologically better prognosis. Option A describes a related but distinct bias about the timing of diagnosis relative to death, not about which biological subtype of tumor gets detected. The correct answer is D (If there were truly no difference between the two treatments, a difference this large...): a p-value is the probability of observing a result at least as extreme as the one obtained, assuming the null hypothesis is true, which is exactly what this option describes. Because 0.04 is below the prespecified alpha of 0.05, the trial would reject the null hypothesis and call the result statistically significant.   42. The correct answer is A (The result is not statistically significant at the conventional threshold, since the...): whenever the 95% confidence interval for a relative risk or odds ratio includes 1.0, the result is not statistically significant at the conventional p less than 0.05 threshold, since a true value of 1.0 would mean no association between the exposure and outcome at all. Option B incorrectly focuses on the point estimate alone while ignoring the width of the interval. Option C overstates the conclusion; a wide, nonsignificant interval does not prove there is definitively no effect, it simply means the study could not rule out a true value of 1.0 or even a small harmful effect. Option D fundamentally misinterprets what a confidence interval represents, which is a range of plausible values for the true population parameter, not a statement about the proportion of individual patients who benefit.
  43. The correct answer is C (Inadequate statistical power, since a small sample size increases the risk of a type...): power is the probability of correctly rejecting the null hypothesis when it is truly false, and power increases with larger sample size, larger true effect size, and reduced variability in the outcome measured. A small trial like this one is underpowered and therefore especially prone to a type II (false-negative) error, meaning a nonsignificant result may simply reflect too few patients rather than a true absence of benefit. Option A describes the opposite error, a false-positive result, which does not match a trial that failed to reach significance. Option B describes a bias that would distort the direction or validity of a result, not the statistical power issue that best explains this scenario. Option D describes a bias that would distort the direction or validity of a result, not the statistical power issue that best explains this scenario.
  44. The correct answer is B (Including the unpublished trials would likely reduce or eliminate the apparent...): publication bias occurs because studies with positive, statistically significant results are more likely to be submitted and accepted for publication than studies with null or negative results. When a meta-analysis pools only published trials, and a substantial number of unpublished, likely null trials exist, the pooled estimate is skewed toward showing a benefit that would shrink or disappear if the missing negative trials were included. Option A misapplies the term effect modification, which describes genuine variation in effect across subgroups, not a bias from selective publication. Option C misdescribes how a forest plot and meta-analysis actually work, since every included trial contributes to the pooled estimate weighted by its precision, not just the largest study. Option D misapplies length-time bias, a screening-specific concept unrelated to this scenario.

The correct answer is D (Referring him to sources of social support that address food insecurity and...): the vignette explicitly identifies economic instability, specifically food insecurity and inability to afford medication copays, as the direct cause of his skipped meals and doses. Addressing this social determinant of health directly, for example through referral to food assistance programs, patient assistance programs, or a social worker, is far more likely to prevent further hospitalizations than any purely medical intervention, since the medication regimen itself is already documented as appropriate. The correct answer is A (Using the teach-back method, asking the patient to restate the instructions in her own...): the teach-back method is a well-supported communication strategy specifically because it verifies actual comprehension rather than passive acknowledgment, allowing the clinician to identify and correct specific misunderstandings before discharge. This approach is particularly valuable for patients with low health literacy or limited proficiency in the language of instruction, since low health literacy is an independent risk factor for medication errors and hospital readmission. Option B describes simply asking if the patient understands, which research shows is an unreliable way to confirm true comprehension, since patients often say yes out of politeness or embarrassment regardless of actual understanding. Option C ignores the patient's stated reading level and does not verify comprehension at all. Option D incorrectly assumes health literacy and language barriers are solved by volume and pace alone, which does not address the underlying comprehension gap.
